# THE PARROT OF PARADISE from Cuba. <note>original Latin: *PSITTICUS PARADISI ex Cuba*. This bird is now identified as the Cuban Amazon (*Amazona leucocephala*).</note>

## The Parrot of Paradise of Cuba. The Parrot of Paradise of Cuba. <note>translated from French: *Perroquet du Paradis de Cuba*.</note>

[THIS BIRD] IS somewhat less than the common African gray Parrot: the Bill white, the Eyes red: the upper-part of the Head, Neck, Back and Wings, of a bright yellow, except the Quill-feathers <note>The primary feathers used for flight.</note> of the Wing, which are white: the Neck and Breast scarlet; below which is a wide space of yellow; the remainder of the under-part of the Body scarlet; half way of the under-part of the Tail next the Rump red, the rest yellow. All the yellow, particularly the Back and Rump, have the ends of the feathers tinged with red: the Feet and Claws white. The figure of this Bird has the disadvantage of all the rest, it being painted only from the Case <note>original: "Case," meaning a stuffed specimen or taxidermy mount.</note>: for as every different Bird have gestures peculiar to them, it is requisite they should be drawn from the living Birds, otherwise it is impracticable to give them their natural air; which method, except in a few Birds, has been practis'd through the whole Collection. It was shot by an *Indian* <note>A member of the indigenous peoples of the Caribbean.</note>, on the Island *Cuba*; and being only disabled from flying, he carried it to the Governour of the *Havana*, who presented it to a Gentlewoman of *Carolina*, with whom it lived some Years, much admir'd for its uncommoness and beauty.

<column-break/>

THIS Bird is a little smaller than the *gray Parrots* which are common in *Africa*: it has a white beak, red eyes, the top of the head, the neck, the back, and the wings of a bright yellow, except the large feathers of the wing which are white: the neck and the breast are a scarlet red: there is below a large yellow space, the rest of the lower part of the body is scarlet. Half of the underside of the tail near the rump is red, the rest yellow. All the yellow feathers, especially those of the back and the rump, have tips tinged with red: the feet are white, and the talons also: the figure of this bird has not been drawn to its advantage, like that of all the others, because it was only painted from the dead bird; now as all birds have an appearance which is particular to them, it is proper to paint them from life, otherwise it is not possible to give them their natural air. This is the method that has been followed throughout the course of this collection, with the exception of a few birds. This one received an arrow-shot from an *Indian* on the Island of *Cuba*, and as he had only put it out of a state to fly, he carried it to the Governor of *Havana*, who made a present of it to a gentleman <note>The French text uses *gentil-homme* (gentleman), whereas the English text specifies "Gentlewoman."</note> of *Carolina*, with whom it lived for several years, admired because of its rarity and its beauty.

---

## A laurel-leaved shrub with drooping foliage, three-seeded fruit, and shining black seeds. <note>original Latin: *Frutex Lauri folio pendulo, fructu tricocco, semine nigro splendente*. This plant is likely the Strongback or Juniper-Berry (*Bourreria succulenta*).</note>

**RED-WOOD.**

THIS Tree usually grows from sixteen to twenty foot high, with a small Trunk, and slender Branches; the Leaves shaped not unlike those of the Bay-tree <note>The Laurel or Bay leaf tree (*Laurus nobilis*).</note>; three black Seeds are contained in every capsula <note>A seed pod or dry fruit that splits open.</note>: the Bark of a russet colour, and smooth: the Grain of a fine red; but being exposed a little time to the air, fades, and loses much of its lustre. They grow plentifully on the Rocks in most of the *Bahama* Islands.

<column-break/>

**Red-wood.** <note>translated from French: *Bois-rouge*.</note>

THIS tree, of which the trunk is small and the branches slender, is ordinarily sixteen to twenty feet high; the shape of the leaves is not different from those of the laurel: each capsule contains three grains of black seed: the bark is smooth and reddish; the wood-grain <note>The French text says "la graine" (the seed), but the context of "fine red" and "exposed to the air" in the English column indicates Catesby was referring to the wood grain.</note> is of a beautiful red, but when it has been a little exposed to the air it withers and loses much of its luster. These trees grow in abundance upon the rocks in most of the Bahama Islands.

# THE PARROT OF PARADISE from Cuba. <note>original Latin: *PSITTICUS PARADISI ex Cuba*. This bird is now identified as the Cuban Amazon (*Amazona leucocephala*), specifically a color variant or potentially an individual with xanthochroism (excessive yellow pigment).</note>

## The Parrot of Paradise of Cuba. <note>translated from French: *Perroquet du Paradis de Cuba*.</note>

[THIS BIRD] IS somewhat less than the common African gray Parrot: the Bill white, the Eyes red: the upper-part of the Head, Neck, Back and Wings, of a bright yellow, except the Quill-feathers <note>The primary feathers used for flight.</note> of the Wing, which are white: the Neck and Breast scarlet; below which is a wide space of yellow; the remainder of the under-part of the Body scarlet; half way of the under-part of the Tail next the Rump red, the rest yellow. All the yellow, particularly the Back and Rump, have the ends of the feathers tinged with red: the Feet and Claws white. 

The figure of this Bird has the disadvantage of all the rest, it being painted only from the Case <note>original: "Case," meaning a stuffed specimen or taxidermy mount.</note>: for as every different Bird have gestures peculiar to them, it is requisite they should be drawn from the living Birds, otherwise it is impracticable to give them their natural air; which method, except in a few Birds, has been practis'd through the whole Collection. It was shot by an *Indian* <note>A member of the indigenous peoples of the Caribbean.</note>, on the Island *Cuba*; and being only disabled from flying, he carried it to the Governour of the *Havana*, who presented it to a Gentlewoman of *Carolina*, with whom... <note>The text concludes on the following page.</note>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the description of the Parrot of Paradise (Plate 10) to Plate 11, which illustrates the Carolina Parrot (now known as the Carolina Parakeet) and the American Cypress. The text continues Catesby’s pattern of providing parallel descriptions in English and French, though both are translated here into English for accessibility.</meta>

# THE CAROLINA PARROT. <note>original Latin: *Psitticus Caroliniensis*. This bird is the Carolina Parakeet (*Conuropsis carolinensis*), the only parrot species native to the eastern United States; it was declared extinct in the early 20th century.</note>

## The Parrot of Carolina.

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut drop cap 'T' depicting a bird perched among leafy branches and flowers.</image-desc>
THIS Bird is of the size, or rather smaller than a Blackbird, weighing three ounces and a half: the fore-part of the Head is Orange-colored; the hind-part of the Head and Neck are yellow. All the rest of the Bird appears green; but upon nearer scrutiny the interior <term>vanes</term> <note>The flat part of a feather on either side of the central shaft.</note> of most of the wing-feathers are dark-brown: the upper-parts of the exterior vanes of the larger Wing or <term>Quill-feathers</term> <note>The primary feathers used for flight.</note> are yellow, proceeding gradually to a deeper color toward the end, from yellow to green, and from green to blue: the edge of the Shoulder of the Wing, for about three inches down, is bright Orange-color. The Wings are very long, as is the Tail; having the two middle feathers longer than the others by an inch and a half, ending in a point; the rest are gradually shorter. The Legs and Feet are white: the small Feathers covering the Thighs are green, ending at the Knees with a border of Orange-color. They feed on Seeds and Kernels of Fruit; particularly those of Cypress and Apples. The Orchards in Autumn are visited by numerous flights of them; where they make great destruction for their Kernels only: for the same purpose they frequent *Virginia*; which is the furthest North I ever heard they have been seen. Their Guts are certain and speedy poison to Cats. This is the only one of the Parrot kind in *Carolina*: some of them breed in the Country; but most of them retire further South.

## The Cypress of America.

THE Cypress <note>The Bald Cypress (*Taxodium distichum*).</note> (except the Tulip-tree) is the tallest and largest in these parts of the world. Near the ground some of them measure 30 feet in circumference, rising in a pyramid shape for six feet, where it is about two-thirds smaller; from which to the limbs, which is usually 60 or 70 feet, it grows in the same proportion as other trees. Four or five feet around this Tree (in a singular manner) rise many Stumps, some a little above ground, and others from one to four feet high, of various shape and size, their tops round, covered with a smooth red Bark. These Stumps <note>Now commonly called "cypress knees," these woody projections from the root system help the tree breathe in waterlogged soil.</note> shoot from the roots of the Tree, yet they produce neither Leaf nor Branch, the Tree increasing only by seed, which in form are like the common Cypress, and contain a balsamic substance of a fragrant smell. The Timber this Tree affords is excellent, and particularly for covering Houses, it being light, of a <term>free Grain</term> <note>Wood that is easy to split or work along its fibers.</note>, and resisting the injuries of the weather better than any other here. It is an <term>Aquatic</term> <note>A plant that grows in or near water.</note>, and usually grows from one, five, and six feet deep in water; which secure situation seems to invite a great number of different Birds to breed in its lofty branches; amongst which this Parrot delights to make their Nests, and in *October* (at which Time the Seed is ripe) to feed on their Kernels.

<column-break/>

## The Carolina Parrot. <note>translated from French: *Perroquet de la Caroline*.</note>

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut drop cap 'C' featuring a human figure or face within elaborate decorative scrollwork.</image-desc>
THIS Bird is of the size of a blackbird, or even smaller, and weighs three and a half ounces. It has the front of the head orange-colored; the back of the head and the neck are yellow; all the rest of the bird appears green, but after a more exact search I found that the interior barbs of most of the wing feathers are a dark brown, and the top of the exterior barbs of the largest wing feathers are yellow, becoming by degrees darker until the end, shifting from yellow to green, and from green to blue. The edge of the top of the wing is, for about three inches down, a beautiful orange color: the wings are long as well as the tail, of which the two middle feathers are an inch and a half longer than the others, and end in a point: the others are shorter, and that by degrees. The legs and feet are white; the small feathers that cover the thighs down to the joint of the leg are green and bordered with orange color. They feed on seeds and the pits of fruits, and especially on Cypress seeds and apple pits. In autumn, innumerable flocks of these birds come into the orchards where they cause great damage because they eat only the pits; it is also the pits that attract them into *Virginia*, which is the most distant place to the North where I have heard it said that these birds have been seen. Their guts are a prompt and certain poison for cats. This is the only species of parrot that there is in *Carolina*. Some have their young in the countryside, but most retire further South.

## The Cypress of America. <note>translated from French: *Cyprès de l'Amerique*.</note>

THIS tree is the tallest and the largest that there is in this part of the world, except the tree that bears tulips <note>The Tulip Poplar (*Liriodendron tulipifera*).</note>. Some have thirty feet of circumference near the ground; they rise while always diminishing until the height of six feet, where, reduced to two-thirds of the thickness they are at the foot, they continue to grow ordinarily sixty or seventy feet up to the branch-line with the same proportion as other trees. There issue in a singular manner, at four or five feet around this tree, several stumps of different shape and different size, some a little above the ground and others from one foot high up to four. Their head is covered with a red and smooth bark. These stumps come out of the roots of the tree; however, they produce neither leaves nor branches, for the tree comes only from the seed-grain which is of the same form as that of ordinary cypresses and which contains a balsamic and fragrant substance. The structural wood that is made from this tree is excellent, especially for covering houses because it is light, because it has a fine grain, and because it resists the injuries of the weather better than any other that we have in this country here. It is aquatic, and grows ordinarily from one foot up to five and six deep in the water. It seems that its situation invites a great number of different sorts of birds to lodge on its branches to multiply their species; the parrot among others willingly makes its nest there, and feeds on the seeds in October, which is the time of their maturity.

<summary>This page describes the Carolina Parrot (the now-extinct Carolina Parakeet) and the American Bald Cypress. Catesby details the bird's colorful plumage and its habit of eating fruit kernels, while also describing the unique "knees" and water-dwelling nature of the cypress tree.</summary>
<keywords>Carolina Parakeet, Bald Cypress, cypress knees, Virginia, extinct birds, Mark Catesby, ornithology, botany, fruit kernels, colonial natural history</keywords>

# THE PURPLE JACK-DAW <note>original Latin: *Monedula Purpurea*. Based on the description and behavior, this bird is the Common Grackle (*Quiscalus quiscula*), a member of the blackbird family native to North America.</note>

## The Purple Jack-Daw.

THIS is not so big by one third part as the common Jack-Daw <note>A small, black, crow-like bird common in Europe.</note>, weighing six ounces: the Bill black; the Eyes gray; the Tail long, the Middle-feather longest, the rest gradually shorter. At a distance they seem all black, but at a nearer view, they appear purple, particularly the Head and Neck has most lustre.

THE Hen <note>The female bird.</note> is all over brown, the Wing, Back and Tail being darkest. They make their Nests on the branches of trees in all parts of the country, but most in remote and unfrequented places; from whence in Autumn, after a vast increase, they assemble together, and come amongst the Inhabitants in such numbers that they sometimes darken the air, and are seen in continued flights for miles together, making great devastation of grain where they light. In winter they flock to barn-doors. They have a ranck <note>Strong and unpleasant.</note> smell; their flesh is course, black, and is seldom eat.

<column-break/>

## Purple-colored Jack-Daw <note>original French: *Choucas couleur de pourpre*</note>

*THIS bird, which weighs six ounces, is smaller than the ordinary Jack-Daw by one third. It has a black beak, gray eyes, and a long tail; in the middle of the tail, it has a feather that is longer than the others, which decrease in size on each side. From a distance, they appear entirely black, but from close up one sees they are the color of purple; the head and the neck especially have more luster.*

*THE female is entirely brown; her wings, back, and tail are of a darker brown. They make their nests on the branches of trees in every part of the countryside, but even more so in remote places that are not frequented. It is from there that, after having multiplied greatly, they come into inhabited areas in autumn in such great numbers that they sometimes darken the air, and they are seen thus flying together for several miles. They cause great damage to grain in the places where they stop. They come in crowds to the barn doors during the winter. They have a strong odor; their flesh is coarse, black, and is rarely eaten.*

<summary>This page describes the Purple Jack-Daw (Common Grackle), detailing its iridescent purple plumage, its distinction from the European Jackdaw, and its behavior of forming massive autumnal flocks that damage grain crops.</summary>
<keywords>Common Grackle, Jackdaw, bird migration, North American ornithology, agricultural pests, iridescent plumage</keywords>

# THE PURPLE JACKDAW <note>original Latin: *Monedula Purpurea*. Based on the description and behavior, this bird is the Common Grackle (*Quiscalus quiscula*), a member of the blackbird family native to North America.</note>

## The Purple Jackdaw.

THIS bird is about one-third smaller than the common Jackdaw <note>A small, black, crow-like bird common in Europe.</note>, weighing six ounces. The beak is black; the eyes are gray; the tail is long, with the middle feathers being the longest and the rest becoming gradually shorter. From a distance, they appear entirely black, but upon a closer view, they appear purple; the head and neck, in particular, have the most brilliant luster.

THE female <note>original: "Hen"</note> is brown all over, with the wings, back, and tail being the darkest. They build their nests on the branches of trees in all parts of the country, but mostly in remote and unfrequented places. In the autumn, after a vast increase in their numbers, they assemble together and come among the inhabitants in such numbers that they sometimes darken the air; they are seen in continuous flights for miles together, causing great devastation to grain wherever they land. In winter, they flock to barn doors. They have a rank <note>Strong and unpleasant.</note> smell; their flesh is coarse, black, and is seldom eaten.

<page-num>P. 13</page-num>

# The Black Starling with red upper wings.
<note>original Latin: *STURNUS niger alis superne rubentibus*. This bird is now known as the Red-winged Blackbird (*Agelaius phoeniceus*).</note>

<margin>The broad-leaved candle-berry Myrtle</margin>

## The red-winged Starling.

<image-desc>Decorative initial A depicting a landscape scene with a house and a tree.</image-desc>

**A** male <note>Catesby uses the term "Cock" for the male bird.</note> weighed between three and four ounces, resembling our European Starling in shape and size. The entire bird (except for the upper part of the wings) is black, and it would possess little beauty if not for the shoulders of the wings, which are a bright scarlet. This bird and the Purple Jackdaw <note>Referred to here as the "Purple-Daw."</note> belong to the same genus and are extremely greedy eaters of corn. They seem to work together to cause as much trouble as possible; to make themselves even more intimidating, both species join into a single flock and are always found together, except during the breeding season, spreading destruction across the countryside. When they are shot at, individuals of both species usually fall; yet, before a person can even reload their gun, more birds will often gather in that same spot than were there before the shots. They are the boldest and most destructive birds in the country.

This appears to be the bird that Francisco Hernandez <note>A 16th-century Spanish physician and naturalist who documented the flora and fauna of Mexico.</note> calls the ACOLCHICHI <note>A Nahuatl name for the bird.</note> (*Willughby's Ornithology, p. 391*). They build their nests in Carolina and Virginia upon trees, but always positioned over water among reeds or sedge. They artfully interweave the tops of these plants and fix their nests underneath; they are so well-secured from moisture that even where the tides rise, it is observed that the water never reaches them. They are friendly and active birds, and can be taught to speak and sing.

The hens <note>The females.</note> are considerably smaller than the males, colored in a mottled gray, and the red on their wings is not nearly as bright.

---

# A lower-growing Carolina plant similar to the Brabant Myrtle; with broader and more serrated leaves.
<note>original Latin: *Myrtus Brabanticæ similis caroliniensis humilior; foliis latioribus & magis serratis*. This refers to the Southern Wax Myrtle (*Morella cerifera*), used by early settlers to make aromatic candles.</note>

## The broad-leaved Candle-berry MYRTLE.

<image-desc>Small decorative initial T in a square frame.</image-desc>

**T**HIS plant usually grows no taller than three feet. It differs from the tall Candle-berry Myrtle primarily in its height and in having a broader leaf.

<column-break/>

<note>The following column is the translation of the original French text provided in the manuscript, which mirrors the English text in the first column.</note>

## Red-winged Starling.
<note>original French: *Etourneau a aile rouge*.</note>

<image-desc>Decorative initial L depicting a landscape with a bridge over water.</image-desc>

**T**HE male weighs between three and four ounces. It is of the same shape and size as our Starlings. The entire bird, except for the upper part of the wings, is black, and it would hardly be beautiful if the top of its wings were not a vivid scarlet. This bird and the Jackdaw, which is purple-colored, are of the same genus and eat a prodigious amount of grain. They appear to act in concert to do all the mischief they can; and to make themselves very formidable, the two species join together and fly in company, except during the time they nest, and cause much damage throughout the country. When they are shot, some of both species usually fall, and before one has time to reload, there are often more in the same place than there were before the shot. These birds are the boldest and most pernicious in the country.

It seems that this is the bird *Hernandez* calls *Acolchichi* (*Willughby’s Ornithology, p. 391*). They make their nests in Carolina and Virginia, not in trees, but always above the water among the reeds, of which they interweave the tips with great art and fix their nests underneath; they place them at such a precise height that they have nothing to fear from the tides. These birds are familiar and active; they learn to speak and to sing.

The female is much smaller than the male; she is of a mixed gray, and the red on her wings is not so bright.

---

## Candle Myrtle.
<note>original French: *Myrte à chandelle*.</note>

<image-desc>Small decorative initial C in a square frame.</image-desc>

**T**HIS shrub usually grows only three feet high, in which it differs from the Great Myrtle, and also in that its leaves are wider.

<meta>This page continues Mark Catesby’s accounts of the birds of the American Southeast, specifically those that impact the agriculture of the colonies. Having previously described the destructive Red-winged Blackbird on page 13, he now focuses on the Rice-Bird, noting its migration patterns and sexual dimorphism.</meta>

# THE CAROLINA BUNTING.
<note>original Latin: *HORTULANUS CAROLINIENSIS*. This bird is the Bobolink (*Dolichonyx oryzivorus*).</note>

<column-break/>

## The Rice-Bird.

In the beginning of *September*, while the Grain of Rice is yet soft and milky, innumerable Flights of these Birds arrive from some remote Parts, to the great Detriment of the inhabitants. In the year 1724, an inhabitant near the *Ashley* river <note>A major river in South Carolina.</note> had forty acres of Rice so devoured by them, that he was in doubt whether what they had left was worth the expense of gathering in.

They are esteemed in *Carolina* the greatest delicacy of all other Birds. When they first arrive, they are lean, but in a few days become so excessively fat that they fly sluggishly and with difficulty; and when shot, they frequently break apart upon the fall. They continue for about three weeks, and retire by the time the Rice begins to harden.

There is something so singular and extraordinary in this Bird, that I cannot pass it over without notice. In *September*, when they arrive in infinite swarms to devour the Rice, they are all Hens <note>Catesby's term for female birds.</note>, not being accompanied by any Cock <note>Catesby's term for male birds.</note>. Observing them to be all feathered alike, I imagined they were the young of both sexes, not yet perfected in their colors; but by opening some scores prepared for the spit, I found them to be all Females. And so that I would leave no room for doubt, I repeated the search often on many of them, but could never find a Cock at that time of the year.

Early in the Spring, both Cocks and Hens make a transient visit together, at which time I made the same search as before, and both sexes were plainly distinguishable. The Hen, which is properly the Rice-Bird, is about the size of a Lark, and colored not unlike it on the Back; the Breast and Belly are pale-yellow, the Bill strong and sharp-pointed, and shaped like most others of the grain-eating <note>original: "graniverous"</note> kind. This seems to be the Bird described by the name of **Maia**, in *Willughby’s Appendix, page 386.* <note>Referring to the 1678 work *The Ornithology of Francis Willughby*.</note> In *September*, 1725, while lying upon the deck of a Sloop <note>A small sailing vessel.</note> in a Bay at *Andros Island* <note>In the Bahamas.</note>, I and the Company with me heard for three nights successively Flights of these Birds (their Note being plainly distinguishable from others) passing over our heads northerly, which is their direct way from *Cuba* to *Carolina*; from which I conceive that after partaking of the earlier crop of Rice at *Cuba*, they travel over the sea to *Carolina* for the same intent, the Rice there being at that time fit for them.

The Cock's Bill is lead-colored, the fore-part of the Head black, the hind-part and the Neck of a reddish yellow, the upper-part of the Wing white, the Back next the Head black, lower down grey, the Rump white, the greatest part of the Wing and the whole Tail black, and the Legs and Feet brown in both sexes.

<column-break/>

## The Carolina Ortolan, or Rice-Bird.
<note>original French: *Ortolan de la Caroline, ou Oiseau à Ris*.</note>

There come from some distant countries innumerable flocks of these birds at the beginning of September, during the time when the rice is still tender and milky, and these birds cause great damage to the inhabitants. In the year 1724, they ravaged forty acres of rice that one of the inhabitants had near the *Ashley* river, and ravaged them so severely that the owner did not know if what remained was sufficient to compensate him for the expenses required to harvest it.

They are considered in Carolina to be the most delicious of all birds. They are lean upon arrival, but they become so fat in a few days that they fly slowly and with difficulty; and when one shoots them, they even often break apart when falling. They stay for about three weeks, and then retire at the time when the rice begins to harden.

It is a singular and extraordinary thing that the infinite multitude of these birds that arrive in the month of September is composed only of females; I have tested this on several scores <note>original French: "plusieurs vingtaines," meaning groups of twenty.</note> that I opened for this purpose. Because I had noticed that they were all of a similar plumage, I believed that they were the young of one or the other sex whose color was not yet in its perfection; but in order to leave no room for doubt, I often performed the same experiment on many, and I could never find any male in this season of the year.

At the beginning of spring, the males and the females come together, but they are only passing through; I then made the same search as before, and one could easily distinguish the two sexes. The female, which is properly the Rice-Bird, is about the size of a lark; she has a back of a similar color, the breast and the belly of a pale yellow, the beak strong and pointed, and of the same shape as that of most other species of birds that live on grain. It seems that this is the bird that has been described under the name of **Maia**, in *Willughby’s Appendix, page 386.* Being in the month of September 1725, lying on the deck of a small boat in a bay at *Andros* Island, we heard, for three nights in a row, flocks of these birds (their song being easily distinguished from that of others) which passed over our heads going towards the North, which is their direct path from *Cuba* to Carolina. From this, I conceive that after having eaten the rice which is first ripe in *Cuba*, they cross the sea and go to Carolina for the same purpose, the rice there being then just as they need it.

The males have a lead-colored beak, the front of the head and the neck of a reddish yellow. The males and females have the top of the wing white, the upper part of the back black, the lower part all grey, the rump white, the largest part of the wing and the entire tail black, and the legs and feet brown.

<sig>A</sig>

### The Blue Jay
This lively bird is native to North America and was frequently observed by early naturalists for its bold behavior and loud calls. In this illustration, the jay is depicted with its characteristic crest raised, a posture it often adopts when excited or defending its territory.

<column-break/>

### The Smooth Greenbrier
The plant shown here is a climbing vine. Catesby specifically notes that the leaves are "not serrated" (smooth-edged) to distinguish it from other varieties of *Smilax* that possess thorny stems or jagged leaves. The clusters of black berries provide a vital food source for many forest birds during the winter months.

# Blue Crested Jay <note>original Latin: *PICA glandaria cærulea cristata*</note>

## The Blue Jay

This bird is fully as large as, or larger than, a Starling. Its beak is black; above the base of the upper mandible are black feathers which run in a narrow stripe across the eyes, meeting a broad black stripe that circles the head and throat. The feathers on its crown are long, and it can raise them whenever it likes. Its back is a dark, dusky purple. The inner vanes of the larger wing feathers are black, while the outer parts are blue, marked with black horizontal lines across every feather, and their tips are edged with white. The tail is blue, marked with the same cross-lines as the wings. These birds have the same jerking, rhythmic motion as our English Jay, though their call is more melodic.

The female is not quite as bright in color, but other than that, there appears to be no difference between the sexes.

# Smooth Laurel-Leaved Smilax with Black Berries <note>original Latin: *Smilax lævis Lauri folio baccis nigris*</note>

## THE BAY-LEAVED SMILAX
<note>This plant is likely *Smilax laurifolia*, commonly known as Bamboo Vine or Laurel-leaved Greenbrier.</note>

This plant is usually found in damp places. It sends forth many green stems from its root, and its branches spread over whatever stands near it to a very great distance. It frequently climbs over sixteen feet in height, growing so thick that in the summer it creates an impenetrable shade, and in winter it provides a warm shelter for livestock. The leaves have the color and thickness of Laurel, but in shape they more closely resemble the Bay <note>referring to *Laurus nobilis*</note>, with no visible veins except for the central rib.

The flowers are small and whitish. The fruit grows in round clusters and consists of a black berry containing a single hard seed. The fruit ripens in October and serves as food for several kinds of birds, particularly this Jay.

<column-break/>

## Blue Jay <note>original French: *Geai bleu*</note>

This bird is as large as, and even larger than, a Starling. It has a black beak, and above the base of the upper mandible there are black feathers which form a small stripe across the eyes; this joins a larger stripe that surrounds the head and the throat. The feathers of its crest are long, and it raises them when it wishes. It has a dark purple back. The inner barbs of the large wing feathers are black; the outer ones are blue, with black stripes across each feather, the ends of which are bordered with white. Its tail is blue and marked with the same stripes as its wings. This Jay has the same mannerisms as ours, but its cry is not so disagreeable.

The female does not have such vivid colors, but otherwise there appears to be no difference.

## Laurel-Leaved Smilax <note>original French: *Smilax à feüille de Laurier*</note>

This plant is ordinarily found in humid places. It pushes several green stems from its root, the branches of which cover everything around it for a very considerable distance. They often climb more than sixteen feet high and become so thick that in summer they form an impenetrable shade, and in winter a warm retreat for cattle. The leaves of this plant are of the same color and consistency as those of the "male" laurel, but they have more the shape of the "female" laurel <note>Historically, "male" and "female" were used to distinguish between different species of similar-looking plants, such as the Bay Laurel and the Cherry Laurel</note>, and have no visible vein except for the one in the middle.

The flowers are small and whitish. The fruit comes in round clusters and is nothing more than a black berry containing a single hard seed grain, which is ripe in October and serves as food for several sorts of birds, but principally for the Blue Jay.

<summary>This page describes the physical characteristics and behaviors of the Blue Jay alongside the botanical details of the Bay-leaved Smilax, noting how the plant provides both shelter for cattle and food for the birds.</summary>
<keywords>Blue Jay, Smilax, Greenbrier, bird behavior, colonial botany, animal-plant interactions, 18th-century natural history</keywords>

## The Greatest Woodpecker with a white beak <note>original Latin: *PICUS Maximus rostro albo*</note>

#### *The largest white-bill Wood-pecker.* <note>This bird is now known as the Ivory-billed Woodpecker (*Campephilus principalis*), a species currently feared to be extinct.</note>

Weighs twenty ounces; about the size, or somewhat larger than a Crow. The Bill white, as Ivory, three inches long, and channelled from the basis to the point: the Iris of the eye yellow: the hind-part of the Head adorned with a large peaked crest of scarlet-feathers: a crooked white Stripe runs from the eye on each side the Neck, towards the Wing: the lower part of the Back and Wings (except the large Quill-feathers) are white, all the rest of the Bird is black.

The Bills of these Birds are much valued by the Canada Indians <note>A historical term for the Indigenous peoples of the northern regions</note>, who make Coronets of 'em for their Princes and great warriors, by fixing them round a Wreath, with their points outward. The Northern Indians having none of these Birds in their cold country, purchase them of the Southern People at the price of two, and sometimes three Buck-skins a Bill.

These Birds subsist chiefly on Ants, Wood-worms, and other Insects, which they hew out of rotten trees; nature having so formed their Bills, that in an hour or two's time they will raise a bushel of chips; for which the Spaniards call 'em the Carpenters <note>original Spanish: *Carpenteros*</note>.

<column-break/>

#### The Woodpecker of the first magnitude with a white beak. <note>Translated from the original French: *Pic de la premiere grandeur au bec blanc*</note>

This Bird weighs twenty ounces; it is of the size of a crow and even a little larger. It has a beak as white as ivory, three inches long, and grooved from the base to the tip, the iris of the eye is yellow, the back of the head is adorned with a large crest of scarlet feathers: a hooked white stripe on each side of the neck from the eyes to the wing, the lower part of the body, and the wings (except the large feathers) are white, all the rest of the bird is black.

The beak of these birds is highly esteemed by the Indians of Canada, who make crowns for their Princes and for their great warriors by setting them so that the points rise outwards: the Northern Indians, having none of these birds in their cold country, buy them from the Southern Indians, and give up to two and even three deer skins for a beak.

These birds feed on ants, worms, and other insects that they take from old rotten trees, nature having formed their beak in such a way that in an hour or two they can make a bushel of chips; it is for this reason that the Spaniards call them the Carpenters <note>original Spanish: *Carpenteros*</note>.

---

### Narrow-leaved Oak; Maryland Holm-oak with the long narrow leaf of a willow. From Ray’s History. <note>original Latin: *Quercus Angustifolia; Ilex Marilandica folio longo angusto salicis. Raij Hist.* "Ray's History" refers to the *Historia Plantarum* by the English naturalist John Ray.</note>

#### The WILLOW OAK. <note>Now known scientifically as *Quercus phellos*.</note>

This Oak is never found but in low moist land; the Leaves are long, narrow and smooth-edged, in shape like the Willow: the wood is soft and coarse-grained, and of less use than most of the other kinds of Oak. In mild Winters they retain their Leaves in Carolina; but in Virginia they drop.

<column-break/>

#### The Willow Oak. <note>Translated from the original French: *Le Chêne Saule*</note>

One never finds this oak except in humid bottoms; the leaves are long, narrow and smooth at the edges, of the same shape as those of the willow; the wood is soft, and the grain is coarse, and it is less good for use than that of most other species of oak: when the winters are temperate, the leaves of these trees do not fall at all in Carolina, but they fall in Virginia.

## The Greatest Woodpecker with a white beak <note>original Latin: *PICUS Maximus rostro albo*</note>

#### *The largest white-bill Wood-pecker.* <note>This bird is now known as the Ivory-billed Woodpecker (*Campephilus principalis*). Once the largest woodpecker in North America, it is now critically endangered and widely considered to be extinct due to habitat loss.</note>

It weighs twenty ounces; it is about the size of, or somewhat larger than, a Crow. The Bill is white, like Ivory, three inches long, and channeled from the base to the point. The Iris of the eye is yellow. The back part of the Head is adorned with a large peaked crest of scarlet feathers. A crooked white Stripe runs from the eye on each side of the Neck, toward the Wing. The lower part of the Back and Wings (except for the large primary feathers) are white; all the rest of the Bird is black.

The Bills of these Birds are much valued by the Canada Indians <note>A historical term for the Indigenous peoples of the northern regions and Great Lakes</note>, who make Coronets of them for their Princes and great warriors by fixing them round a Wreath with their points facing outward. Because the Northern Indians have none of these Birds in their cold country, they purchase them from the Southern People at the price of two, and sometimes three, buckskins per Bill.

These Birds subsist chiefly on Ants, wood-worms, and other Insects, which they hew out of rotten trees. Nature has so formed their Bills that in an hour or two's time they will raise a bushel of chips; for this reason, the Spaniards call them the **Carpenters** <note>original Spanish: *Carpenteros*</note>.

### T. 17.

*Ever-green Oak with oblong leaves that are not lobed.* <note>original Latin: *Quercus Sempervirens folijs oblongis non sinuatis*. In botanical terms, "non sinuatis" means the edges of the leaves are smooth rather than having the deep indentations or "sinuses" typical of many oak species.</note>
*Mr. Banister* <note>John Banister (1650–1692) was a pioneering English naturalist and clergyman who settled in Virginia; his specimens and drawings were vital to early European understanding of American nature.</note>
**Live Oak.** <note>The Southern Live Oak (*Quercus virginiana*) is iconic for its sprawling branches and the fact that it remains green throughout the winter, unlike deciduous oaks.</note>

# The Largest Black Woodpecker with a Red Head. <note>original Latin: *Picus Niger maximus capite rubro*</note>

## *The larger red-crested Woodpecker.*

WEIGHS nine ounces: the Bill is angular, two inches long, and of a lead colour: the Neck is small; the <term>Iris</term><note>The colored part of the eye surrounding the pupil.</note> of the eye is gold colour, encompassed with a lead-colored skin: the whole Crown of the head is adorned with a large scarlet crest; under which, and from the eyes back, runs a narrow white line, and under that a broad black lift: a patch of red covers some of the lower mandible of the Bill and Neck; the rest of the Neck (except the hind-part, which is black) is of a pale yellow, with a small stripe of black dividing it: the upper-part of the exterior <term>vanes</term><note>The flat, spread-out part of a feather on either side of the central shaft.</note> of the <term>Quill-feathers</term><note>The primary flight feathers of the wing.</note> is white; above which on the edge of the Wing is a white spot or two; on the middle of the Back is a broad white spot: all the rest of the upper part of the Body and Tail are black: the under part of the Body is of a dusky black.

That which distinguishes the Cock from the Hen, is the red which covers some part of his under jaw, which in the Hen is black. And whereas the whole Crown of the Cock is red, in the Hen the Fore-head is brown. These Birds (besides Insects, which they get from rotten trees, their usual food) are destructive to <term>Maiz</term><note>Maize, or Indian corn.</note>, by pecking Holes through the husks that enclose the grain, and letting in wet.

---

### Evergreen Oak with oblong leaves, not lobed. Mr. Banister. <note>original Latin: *Quercus sempervivens foliis oblongis non sinuatis*. John Banister was an English clergyman and naturalist who provided many early specimens of American plants to European scholars.</note>

## THE LIVE OAK.

THE usual Height of the Live Oak is about 40 foot; the Grain of the wood is coarse, harder and tougher than any other Oak. Upon the edges of Salt-Marshes (where they usually grow) they arrive to a large size. Their Bodies are irregular, and generally lying along, occasioned by the looseness and moisture of the soil, and tides washing their Roots bare.

On higher lands they grow erect, with a regular pyramidal-shaped Head, retaining their leaves all the year. The Acorns are the sweetest of all others; of which the Indians <note>A historical reference to the Indigenous peoples of the Southeastern North American coast, such as the Yamasee or Cusabo.</note> usually lay up store, to thicken their venison-soup, and prepare them other ways. They likewise draw an Oil, very pleasant and wholesome, little inferior to that of Almonds.

<column-break/>

## *Great Red-headed Woodpecker.* <note>original French: *Grand Piverd à tête rouge*</note>

THIS Bird weighs nine ounces. It has an angular beak, two inches long, and lead-colored; the neck is small, the iris of the eye is gold-colored, surrounded by lead-colored skin. All the top of its head is adorned with a large scarlet crest, under which there is a small white stripe that extends from the eyes backward, and under that another large black one. A red spot covers part of the lower mandible of the beak and the neck; the rest of the neck, except the back which is black, is a pale yellow with a small black stripe dividing it. The top of the exterior barbs of the large wing feathers is white, above which and on the edge of the wing there are one or two white spots, and on the middle of the back there is a large one of the same color; all the rest of the upper body is black as well as the tail; the underside of the body is a dark black.

What distinguishes the male from the female is the red that covers part of the lower mandible of the male, whereas it is black that covers that of the female; and furthermore, the entire top of the male's head is red, while the front of the female's head is brown. These birds (not content with the insects they pull from rotten trees, which are their ordinary food) also destroy much <term>Maiz</term><note>Corn.</note>, because the moisture that enters through the holes they make in the husk spoils the grain enclosed within.

---

## Green Oak with oblong leaves. <note>original French: *Chêne verd à feüilles oblongues*</note>

THE ordinary height of this tree is about forty feet. The grain of the wood is coarse, harder, and rougher than that of any other oak. They grow to a larger size at the edges of the salt marshes where they ordinarily grow. The trunk is irregular and for the most part leaning or, so to speak, lying down; this comes from the fact that the ground, being humid, has little consistency, and the tides carry away the earth that should cover the roots. In a more elevated soil, this kind of tree is straight, and has a regular and pyramidal crown, and keeps its leaves all year. The acorns they bear are sweeter than those of all other oaks: the Indians ordinarily make a provision of them, and use them to thicken the soups they make with venison; they also prepare them in several other ways. They extract from them a very pleasant and very healthy oil which is almost as good as that of Almonds.

## The Great Woodpecker with Golden Wings. <note>original Latin: *Picus major alis aureis*</note>

### The Gold-winged Wood-pecker.

THIS Bird weighs five ounces: the Bill is black, an inch and a half long, and a little bending: from the angles of the Mouth on each side runs down a broad black <term>list</term><note>A stripe or band of color.</note>, about an inch long: the upper part of the Head and Neck are of a lead-colour <note>a bluish-grey</note>. On the hind part of the Head is a large scarlet spot. The hind part of the Neck, Throat and the area about the Eyes are of a <term>bay colour</term><note>A reddish-brown, similar to the color of a chestnut horse.</note>; the Back and the part of the Wing next to it are intermixed with black spots, in the form of half-moons. The larger Wing-Feathers are brown. What adds to the elegance of this bird, and what alone is sufficient to distinguish it, is that the <term>beams</term><note>The central shafts or rachis of the feathers.</note> of all the Wing-Feathers are of a bright gold-color. The Breast has in the middle of it a large black spot, in the form of a crescent, from which to its <term>vent</term><note>The area on the underside of the bird, near the tail.</note> it is a dusky white, and spotted with round and some heart-shaped black spots. The Rump is white, and the Tail is black, which, along with the Feet, are formed like others of this kind. It differs from other Wood-peckers in the curvature of its Bill and its manner of feeding, which is usually on the ground, out of which it draws worms and other insects; neither do they alight on the bodies of trees in an upright posture as Wood-peckers usually do, but perch like other birds.

THE Hen <term>wants</term><note>In this context, "wants" means "lacks" or is without.</note> the black stripe which is at the Throat of the Cock; except for this, she does not differ from him in colour.

<column-break/>

### The Great Woodpecker with Golden Wings. <note>original French: *Grand Piverd aux ailes d'or*</note>

THIS Bird weighs five ounces; it has a black bill, an inch and a half long and a little curved. There is on each side a large black stripe, which begins at the angles of the bill and descends about an inch; the top of the head and the neck of this bird is the color of lead; it has on the back of the head a large scarlet spot: the back of its neck, its throat, and the area around its eyes are of a brown-red; the back and that part of the wing which touches it are intermingled with black spots in the shape of a crescent. The largest feathers of the wing are brown, but what increases the beauty of this bird and which alone suffices to distinguish it is that the <term>rib</term><note>The central shaft of the feather; original French: *la côte*.</note> of all the wing feathers is of a bright gold color. It has in the middle of the breast a large black spot in the shape of a crescent, and that part which is between this spot and the <term>anus</term><note>original French: *l'anus*; referring to the vent or under-tail area.</note> is of a dirty white marked with black spots, some of which are round and others in the shape of a heart. It has a white rump, a black tail, and the same form as that of other woodpeckers, as well as the feet. It differs from other woodpeckers in that its bill is curved and that its way of feeding is usually on the ground, from which it pulls worms and other insects; it does not climb on the trunks of trees as other woodpeckers do, but perches there like other birds.

THE throat of the female does not have that black stripe seen on that of the male, but otherwise she is of the same color.

---

## The tall Virginian tree, an oak with chestnut-like leaves. <note>original Latin: *Quercus castaneæ foliis, procera arbor virginiana*. The abbreviation "Pluk. Alma." refers to Leonard Plukenet's *Almagestum Botanicum*, a renowned 17th-century botanical catalog.</note>

### The CHESNUT-OAK.

THIS Oak grows only in low and very good land, and is the tallest and largest of the Oaks in these parts of the World: the Bark is white and scaly; the <term>Grain</term><note>The texture or pattern of the wood fibers.</note> of the Wood is not fine, though the Timber is of great use: the Leaves are large, indented round the edges like those of the Chestnut. None of the other Oaks produce such large Acorns.

<column-break/>

### Oak with chestnut-like leaves. <note>original French: *Chêne à feüilles de chataigner*</note>

THIS Oak only grows in low-lying areas and in good soil; it is the largest and thickest of the oaks that grow in this part of the world: the bark is white and scaly; the grain of the wood is not beautiful, although it is used much for timber; the leaves are wide and indented like those of the chestnut tree. There is no other oak that produces acorns as large as this one.

<summary>This page describes the physical characteristics and behaviors of the Gold-winged Woodpecker (Northern Flicker) and the Chestnut Oak, highlighting the bird's unique golden feather shafts and the tree's massive size and chestnut-like leaves.</summary>

# WOODPECKER with a red belly <note>original Latin: *PICUS ventre rubro*</note>

## The Red-bellied Wood-pecker.

WEIGHS two ounces six penny weight <note>a "penny weight" or dwt is a historical unit of mass used in the troy system, equal to 24 grains or 1.55 grams</note>: the Bill black; the Eyes of a hazel colour; all the upper part of the Head and Neck bright red; below which it is Ash colour, as is the under part of the Body, except the Belly near the Vent, which is stained with red: the upper part of the Body, including the Wings, is marked regularly with transverse black and white lines: the Tail black and white; the Feet black.

The Hen's Fore-head is brown; which is all the difference between them.

<column-break/>

## Red-bellied Woodpecker. <note>translated from French: *Piverd a ventre rouge*</note>

THIS Bird weighs two ounces and six pennyweights. It has a black beak, hazel-colored eyes, and the entire top of the head and the neck are a bright red. The underside of the neck is ash-colored, as is the underside of the body, except for that part of the belly near the vent which is marked with red. The upper part of the body and the wings are regularly marked with black and white stripes across them: the tail is black and white, and the feet are black.

The female is the same color as the male, with the difference that the front of her head is brown.

---

# WOODPECKER of medium size, appearing hairy. <note>original Latin: *PICUS medius quasi villosus*</note>

## The Hairy Wood-pecker.

WEIGHS two ounces: the Crown of the Head black; a red spot covers the back part of the Head, between which and the Eye it is white; the rest of the Head and Neck black, with a white line in the middle: the Back is black, with a broad white stripe of hairy feathers, extending down the middle to the Rump: the Wings are black, with both vanes of the Feathers, spotted with large white spots: the Tail black; all the under part of the Body white.

The Hen differs from the Cock, only in not having the red spot at the Back of the Head.

<column-break/>

## Hairy Woodpecker. <note>translated from French: *Piverd velu*</note>

THIS Bird weighs two ounces. The top of its head, the back of which is covered with a red spot, is black, and the part between this spot and the eye is white; the rest of the head and the neck are black, and there is a white stripe in the middle; its back is black, with a large white stripe composed of hairy feathers, which extend to the rump. The wings are black, and the barbs of the feathers that compose them are mottled with large white spots. The tail is black, and the entire underside of the body is white.

The female differs from the male only in that she has no red spot at the back of the head.

---

Black Oak.
</margin>

# Oak (perhaps) of Maryland, with a three-lobed leaf resembling sassafras. From Ray’s History. <note>original Latin: *Quercus (forte) Marilandica, folio trifido ad sassafras accedente. Raij Hist.*</note>

## The BLACK OAK.

Usually grows on the poorest land, and is small: the colour of the Bark black, the Grain course <note>coarse</note>; and the Wood of little use but to burn: some of these Oaks produce leaves ten inches wide.

<column-break/>

## Black Oak. <note>translated from French: *Chêne noir*</note>

THIS tree usually grows in poor soil. It is small, and has black bark, a coarse grain, and the wood is hardly used for anything but burning: some of these oaks have leaves ten inches wide.

# WOODPECKER with a completely red head <note>original Latin: *PICUS capite toto rubro*</note>

## The Red-headed Wood-pecker.

THIS Bird weighs two ounces: the Bill sharp, somewhat compressed sideways, of a lead colour: the whole Head and Neck deep red: all the under part of the Body and Rump white; as are the smaller Wing-feathers; which, when the Wings are closed, joines to the white on the Rump, and makes a broad white patch cross the lower part of the back; the upper part of which is black, as are the Quill-feathers <note>the primary flight feathers of the wing</note> and Tail, which is short and stiff. In *Virginia* very few of these Birds are to be seen in winter: in *Carolina* there are more, but not so numerous as in summer; wherefore I conceive they retire Southward, to avoid the cold. This is the only one of the Wood-peckers that may be termed domestick, frequenting Villages and Plantations, and takes a peculiar delight in ratling <note>the drumming behavior Woodpeckers use to communicate or find insects</note> with their Bill on the boarded houses: they are great devourers of fruit and grain.

The Hen <note>the female bird</note> in colour differs little or nothing from the Cock <note>the male bird</note>.

<column-break/>

## Red-headed Woodpecker <note>translated from French: *Piverd a tete rouge*</note>

THIS Bird weighs two ounces: its beak, which is pointed and a little flattened on the sides, is the color of lead. It has the entire head and the neck of a deep red, all the underside of the body and the rump <note>original: *croupion*</note> white, likewise the small feathers of the wing, which when they are closed, join the white which is on the rump, and together form a large white spot which crosses the lower back; the top of the back is black as well as the large feathers of the wing and the tail, which is short and stiff. One sees in *Virginia* only very few of these birds during the winter: there are more in *Carolina*, but not in such great numbers as in summer, which makes me believe that they retreat toward the South to avoid the cold. It is the only Woodpecker that one can call domestic, for it frequents villages and plantations, and takes much pleasure in making noise with its beak on the boards with which the houses are built. It eats a prodigious amount of fruit and grain.

The female differs little or not at all from the male in color.

---

# OAK with a non-serrated leaf, nearly triangular at the tip <note>original Latin: *Quercus folio non serrato, in summitate quasi triangulo*</note>

## The WATER-OAK.

THESE grow no where but in low waterish lands: the Timber not durable, therefore of little use, except for fenceing in fields. In mild winters they retain most of their leaves. Their Acrons <note>acorns</note> are small and bitter, and are rejected by the hogs, while others are to be found.

<column-break/>

## Water Oak <note>translated from French: *Chêne d'eau*</note>

THIS Oak only grows in bottomlands full of water; the timber that one makes from it is not durable, thus it is hardly used except for enclosing fields. When the winters are mild, it preserves most of its leaves. The acorns it bears are small and bitter; the pigs do not eat them at all when they can find others.

# WOODPECKER with a completely red head <note>original Latin: *PICUS capite toto rubro*</note>

## The Red-headed Wood-pecker.

THIS Bird weighs two ounces: the Bill <note>beak</note> sharp, somewhat compressed sideways, of a lead colour: the whole Head and Neck deep red: all the under part of the Body and Rump white; as are the smaller Wing-feathers; which, when the Wings are closed, joines to the white on the Rump, and makes a broad white patch cross the lower part of the back; the upper part of which is black, as are the Quill-feathers <note>the primary flight feathers of the wing</note> and Tail, which is short and stiff. In *Virginia* very few of these Birds are to be seen in winter: in *Carolina* there are more, but not so numerous as in summer; wherefore I conceive they retire Southward, to avoid the cold. This is the only one of the Wood-peckers that may be termed domestick, frequenting Villages and Plantations, and takes a peculiar delight in ratling <note>the drumming behavior Woodpeckers use to communicate or find insects</note> with their Bill on the boarded houses: they are great devourers of fruit and grain.

The Hen <note>the female bird</note> in colour differs little or nothing from the Cock <note>the male bird</note>.

<column-break/>

## Red-headed Woodpecker <note>translated from French: *Piverd a tete rouge*</note>

THIS Bird weighs two ounces: its beak, which is pointed and a little flattened on the sides, is the color of lead. It has the entire head and the neck of a deep red, all the underside of the body and the rump <note>original: *croupion*</note> white, likewise the small feathers of the wing, which when the wings are closed, join with the white of the rump, and form a large white spot across the lower back. The upper part of the back is black, as are the large wing feathers and the tail, which is short and firm. In *Virginia* one sees very few of these birds during the winter; they are more common in *Carolina*, though less numerous than in the summer, which leads me to believe they withdraw to the South to avoid the cold. This is the only Woodpecker that one might call domestic; it frequents villages and plantations, and takes a particular pleasure in drumming its beak against the wood of the houses. These birds are great consumers of fruits and grain.

The female differs little or not at all in color from the male.

# SMALLER VARIEGATED WOODPECKER, WITH A YELLOW BELLY.
<note>original Latin: *Picus varius minor, ventre luteo*; this describes the Yellow-bellied Sapsucker (*Sphyrapicus varius*).</note>

->The yellow belly'd Wood-pecker<-

WEIGHS one ounce thirteen penny weight <note>A pennyweight is an old unit of mass; 13 pennyweights is approximately 20 grams.</note>. Its Bill is of a lead-colour; all the upper part of the Head is red, bordered below with a list <note>a stripe or band</note> of black, under which runs a list of white, parallel with which runs a black list from the Eyes to the back of the Head, under which it is pale yellow: The throat is red, and bordered round with black: On the Neck and Back the feathers are black and white, with a tincture of greenish yellow: The Breast and Belly are of a light yellow, with some black feathers intermix’d: The Wings are black, except towards the shoulders, where there are some white feathers; and both edges of the Quill-feathers are spotted with White: the Tail is black and white.

The Hen <note>the female bird</note> is distinguishable by not having any red about her.

# SMALLEST VARIEGATED WOODPECKER.
<note>original Latin: *Picus varius minimus*; this describes the Downy Woodpecker (*Dryobates pubescens*).</note>

->The smallest spotted Wood-pecker<-

WEIGHS fourteen penny-weight <note>approximately 21.7 grams</note>. It so nearly resembles the Hairy Wood-pecker, *Table 19.* in its mark, and colour, that were it not for disparity of size, they might be thought to be the same. The Breast and Belly of this are light gray: The four uppermost feathers of the Tail are black: the rest are gradually shorter, and transversely marked with Black and White: the Legs and Feet are black. Thus far this differs from the description of the above mentioned.

The Hen differs from the Cock in nothing but wanting the red spot on its Head.

# *White Virginian Oak, as described by Parkinson.*
<note>original Latin: *Quercus alba Virginiana, Park.*; refers to the American White Oak (*Quercus alba*). "Park." refers to the botanist John Parkinson.</note>

->THE WHITE OAK.<-

<margin>
White Oak.
</margin>
THIS nearest resembles our common English Oak in the shape of its Leaves, Acorns, and Manner of growing; the Bark is white, the grain of the Wood fine, for which and its durableness it is esteem’d the best Oak in *Virginia* and *Carolina*. It grows on all kind of Land; but most on high barren Ground amongst Pine Trees.

There is another kind of white Oak, which in *Virginia* is called the *Scaly white Oak*, with Leaves like this, the Bark white and scaly, the Wood of great use in building. They grow on rich Land both high and low.

# *Carolina Oak with prickly green veins.*
<note>original Latin: *Quercus Caroliniensis virentibus venis muricata*; likely a variation of the White Oak or a similar species like the Post Oak.</note>

->THE WHITE OAK, with pointed Notches.<-

<margin>
The white Oak with sharp notches.
</margin>
THE leaves of this Oak are notched, and have sharp points. The Bark and Wood is white, but has not so close a grain as the precedent. *Dr. Pluknet* <note>Leonard Plukenet, an English botanist</note> has figured a leaf shaped like this by the Name of *Virginian Oak with prickly red veins* <note>original Latin: *Quercus Virginiana rubris venis muricata*</note>. This has no red Veins. *See Plukenet's Phytographia, Plate* LIV. *figure 5.*

# [See plate 20]

# *Berry-bearing Lilac. Myrtle-like with nearly round leaves and twin white flowers, from the province of Florida.*
<note>original Latin: *Syringa Baccifera. Myrti subrotundis folijs, floribus albis gemellis ex provincia Floridana*; this is the Partridgeberry (*Mitchella repens*), a creeping evergreen herb.</note>

THIS Plant grows in moist Places, usually under trees, on which it sometimes creeps a little way up, but most commonly trails on the Ground, many Stems rising close together near the Ground, about six inches long, which have some side Branches: the Leaves are small, in form of a heart, and grow opposite to each other on very small foot-stalks: it’s Flowers are four-petaled <note>original Latin: *tetrapetalous*</note>, very small, and in colour like those of the white Lilach, and are succeeded by red berries of an Oval form and of the size of large peas, having two small holes, and contain many small Seeds. It retains the leaves all the Year.

<column-break/>

->Yellow-bellied Woodpecker.<-

THIS bird weighs a little more than an ounce and a half: its bill is the color of lead: the entire top of its head is red and finished with a black stripe, above which there is another white one: a black stripe parallel to this last goes from the eyes to the back of the head, which below is a pale yellow: its throat is red bordered with black: on its neck and back the feathers are black and white, with a mixture of greenish yellow: its breast and belly are a light yellow, with some black feathers here and there. Its wings are black except toward the shoulders, where there are some white feathers; and the edges of the large feathers are spotted with white: its tail is black and white.

One knows the Female because she has no red at all.

->Small spotted Woodpecker.<-

IT weighs a little more than half an ounce. It resembles the Hairy Woodpecker (*Table 19*) so much by its markings and colors, that if it were not for their different size one could believe it is the same species. The breast and belly of this one are of a light gray; the four highest feathers of the tail are black; the others decrease in length as they move away: they are marked transversely with black and white; the legs and feet are black. This is how it differs from the one described above.

The female differs from the Male in that she has no red spots on the head.

->White Oak of Virginia.<-

IT is this one which best resembles the common Oak of England in the shape of its leaves, its acorns, and its manner of growing; its bark is white, the grain of its wood fine, and it is for that reason as well as for its durability that it is regarded in *Carolina* and *Virginia* as the best species of Oak. It grows on all types of soil, and principally among the Pines in high and sterile places.

There is another species of white oak that is named in *Virginia*, the Scaly White Oak; its leaves are similar to those of the preceding; its bark is white and scaly, its wood is of very great use for building; it grows in good soil, low or high.

->White Oak with leaves armed with points.<-

THE leaves of this Oak have deep notches and very sharp points; its bark and wood are white; but the grain is not as tight as that of the preceding. DR. PLUKNET has noted a leaf of the same shape as this by the name of *Virginian Oak with leaves armed with points, streaked with red veins*. The leaves of this one have no red veins.

->[See the 20th Plate.]<-

THIS plant grows ordinarily in damp places, and under trees, which sometimes serve it as support to raise itself a little; but most often it crawls on the earth. It pushes out several stems at once, very close to one another as they come out of the ground. They are about six inches long, and have some lateral branches: its leaves are small: they have the shape of a heart, and resemble those of the Myrtle. They are arranged opposite one another on the stems, and attached by very small stalks: its flowers have four petals, are white, and very small: they much resemble those of the Jasmine or the Lilac: they are followed by red Berries, oval, of the size of a large pea: they each have two small holes, and contain several small seeds. This plant keeps its leaves throughout the year.

--- Page 82 ---
<meta>This page continues Mark Catesby’s descriptions of American flora and fauna, moving from the woodpeckers of the previous page to nuthatches and a specific variety of oak tree. As before, these descriptions correspond to his hand-colored etchings in "The Natural History of Carolina, Florida and the Bahama Islands."</meta>

# BLACK-HEADED NUTHATCH
<note>original Latin: *Sitta Capite Nigro*; this is the White-breasted Nuthatch (*Sitta carolinensis*).</note>

## The Nuthatch

WEIGHS 13 Penny weight 5 grains <note>A pennyweight is an old English unit of mass; 13 pennyweights and 5 grains is approximately 20.5 grams.</note>. The Bill and upper part of the Head and Neck are black, the Back gray; The Wings are of a dark brown, edged with light gray; the uppermost two Feathers of the Tail are gray; the rest are black and white. At the vent <note>the area around the bird's excretory opening</note> is a reddish Spot, the Legs and Feet are brown. The back claw is remarkably bigger and longer than the rest, which seems necessary to support their body in creeping down as well as up Trees, in which action they are usually seen pecking their food (which are Insects) from the chinks or crevices of the Bark.

The Hen <note>the female bird</note> differs but little from the Cock <note>the male bird</note> in the colour of her feathers. They breed and continue the whole year in Carolina.

# BROWN-HEADED NUTHATCH
<note>original Latin: *Sitta Capite Fusco*; this is the Brown-headed Nuthatch (*Sitta pusilla*).</note>

## The small Nuthatch.

THIS weighs six Penny weight <note>approximately 9.3 grams</note>; the Bill is black; the upper part of the Head brown; behind which is a dusky white spot; the Back is gray; as are the two uppermost tail feathers; the rest being black; the Wings are dark brown; the throat and all the under part of the Body are dusky white; the tail is short; the back Toe is largest. They abide all the Year in Carolina. Their Food, and Manner of taking it, is the same as that of the larger Nuthatch.

# LOW-GROWING OAK WITH SHORTER WILLOW-LIKE LEAVES
<note>original Latin: *Quercus humilior salicis folio breviore*; identified as a variety of the Willow Oak (*Quercus phellos*).</note>

## The HIGHLAND WILLOW OAK.

THIS is usually a small Tree, having a dark coloured Bark with Leaves of a pale green, and shaped like those of a Willow. It grows on dry poor land, producing but few Acorns, and those small. Most of these Oaks are growing at Mr. Fairchild’s <note>Thomas Fairchild (1667–1729) was a celebrated London gardener and nurseryman who successfully grew many of the North American plants Catesby sent back to England.</note>.

<column-break/>

## Small black-headed Wood-pecker.
<note>original French: *Piverd petit à la tête noire*. Although the French title uses "Piverd" (Woodpecker), the description clearly refers to the Nuthatch described in the English column.</note>

THIS Bird weighs four drams and twenty-five grains <note>original French: *quatre dragmes & vint cinq grains*; a dram is an old unit of weight approximately equal to 3.9 grams.</note>. Its bill, the top of its head, and its neck are black; its back is gray; its wings are a dark brown, bordered with light gray; the two middle feathers of its tail are gray; all the rest are black and white. It has a reddish spot near the anus. Its legs and its feet are brown. It has the rear spur <note>claw</note> much larger and longer than the others, which seems necessary to support its body as well when descending as when climbing up trees. It is seen continually doing one or the other: for it feeds on Insects which it pulls from between the cracks and crevices of the bark of trees.

The Female is almost similar to the Male in her plumage. They produce their young in Carolina, and remain there all the year.

## Small brown-headed Wood-pecker.
<note>original French: *Pivert petit à la tête brune*.</note>

THIS Bird weighs two drams <note>approximately 7.8 grams</note>. Its bill is black; the top of its head is brown; it has a dull white spot at the back of the head; its back and the two middle feathers of its tail are gray, the other feathers are black; its wings are a dark brown; its throat and all the underside of its body are a dull white; its tail is short; it has the rear spur larger and thicker than the others; it remains all the year in Carolina; it feeds in the same manner as the Wood-pecker described above.

## Oak with Willow leaves.
<note>original French: *Chêne aux feuilles de Saule*.</note>

THIS tree is usually small. Its bark is of a dark colour, and its leaves are of a pale green in the same shape as those of a Willow. It grows in a dry and thin soil; it produces only a few acorns, and even those are very small. Most of these Oaks are in the Gardens of Mr. Fairchild.

# BLACK-HEADED NUTHATCH
<note>original Latin: *Sitta Capite Nigro*; this bird is known today as the White-breasted Nuthatch (*Sitta carolinensis*).</note>

## The Nuthatch

WEIGHS 13 Penny weight 5 grains <note>A pennyweight is an old English unit of mass; 13 pennyweights and 5 grains is approximately 20.5 grams.</note>. The Bill and upper part of the Head and Neck are black, the Back gray; The Wings are of a dark brown, edged with light gray; the uppermost two Feathers of the Tail are gray; the rest are black and white. At the vent <note>the area around the bird's excretory opening</note> is a reddish Spot, the Legs and Feet are brown. The back claw is remarkably bigger and longer than the rest, which seems necessary to support their body in creeping down as well as up Trees, in which action they are usually seen pecking their food (which are Insects) from the chinks or crevices of the Bark.

The Hen <note>the female bird</note> differs but little from the Cock <note>the male bird</note> in the colour of her feathers. They breed and continue the whole year in Carolina.

# BROWN-HEADED NUTHATCH
<note>original Latin: *Sitta Capite Fusco*; this is the Brown-headed Nuthatch (*Sitta pusilla*).</note>

## The small Nuthatch.

THIS weighs six Penny weight <note>approximately 9.3 grams</note>; the Bill is black; the upper part of the Head brown; behind which is a dusky white spot; the Back is gray; as are the two uppermost tail feathers; the rest being black; the Wings are dark brown; the throat and all the under part of the Body are dusky white; the tail is short; the back Toe is largest. They abide all the Year in Carolina. Their Food, and Manner of taking it, is the same as the former. <note>Catesby notes that their behavior mirrors that of the larger Nuthatch, foraging for insects within tree bark.</note>

# THE PASSENGER PIGEON
<note>Known scientifically as *Ectopistes migratorius*, this bird was once the most numerous bird in North America, traveling in massive flocks that could darken the sky for days. It was hunted to extinction by the early 20th century.</note>

## The Pigeon of Passage

This bird is remarkable for its incredible numbers and its migratory habits, moving across the continent in search of food, particularly "mast" (the nuts of forest trees like oaks and beeches). Its plumage is characterized by a delicate mauve-pink breast and a long, tapering tail, which distinguishes it from other common doves. 

# RED OAK
<note>original Latin: *Quercus Esculi*; likely referring to a member of the red oak group, such as *Quercus rubra* or *Quercus falcata*.</note>

## The Oak Tree

The tree illustrated is a variety of Oak known for its deeply lobed, "prickly" leaves, ending in sharp points. The acorns serve as a vital food source for the pigeons described above. The artist has carefully rendered the cluster of acorns to show both the protective cap (cupule) and the nut, providing a clear reference for identification in the American wilderness.
