P. 23.

# THE PASSENGER PIGEON
<note>original Latin: *Palumbus migratorius*. This bird, the Passenger Pigeon, was once the most numerous bird in North America but was driven to extinction by the early 20th century.</note>

### *The Pigeon of Passage.*

It is about the size of our English Wood-Pigeon; the Bill black; the iris <note>the colored part</note> of the Eye red; the Head a dusty blue; the breast and belly a faint red. Above the shoulder of the Wing is a patch of feathers that shines like Gold; the wing is colored like the head, having some few spots of black (except that the larger feathers of it are dark brown) with some white on their exterior vanes <note>the flat part of the feather</note>. The Tail is very long, covered with a black feather; under which the rest are white; the Legs and Feet are red.

Of these, there come in Winter to *Virginia* and *Carolina*, from the North, incredible numbers; insomuch that in some places where they roost (which they do on one another’s backs) they often break down the limbs of Oaks with their weight, and leave their dung some inches thick under the trees they roost on. Where they light, they so effectually clear the woods of acorns and other <term>mast</term> <note>forest nuts used as food for animals</note>, that the hogs that come after them—to the detriment of the planters—fare very poorly. In *Virginia*, I have seen them fly in such continued trains for three days successively, that there was not the least interval in losing sight of them; somewhere or other in the air, they were to be seen continuing their flight South. In mild winters, there are few or none to be seen. A hard winter drives them South for the greater plenty and variety of mast, berries, etc., which they are deprived of in the North by continual frost and snow.

In their passage, the people of *New York* and *Philadelphia* shoot many of them as they fly, from their balconies and tops of houses; and in *New England*, there are such numbers that with long poles they knock them down from their roosts in the night in great numbers. The only information I have had regarding where they come from and their breeding places was from a *Canada Indian*, who told me he had seen them make their nests in rocks by the sides of rivers and lakes far North of the *St. Lawrence River*, where he said he shot them. It is remarkable that none are ever seen to return, at least this way, and what other route they may take is unknown.

<column-break/>

### The Pigeon of Passage
<note>original French: *Pigeon de Passage*</note>

It is approximately the size of the English Wood-Pigeon. Its beak is black, the iris of its eyes is red, its head is a dark blue, and its breast and belly are a pale red. Above the shoulder, it has a round spot that shines like Gold: its wings are the same color as its head, with a small number of black spots (except that the large feathers are a dark brown), and they have a little white on their exterior fringe; the tail is very long, covered with a black feather; those below are white, and its legs and feet are red.

Incredible numbers of these pigeons come from the North into *Carolina* and *Virginia*, so that in the places where they perch—which they do on one another’s backs—they often break the branches of Oaks by their weight, and leave several inches of their droppings under the trees where they have rested. In the places where they stop, they so thoroughly strip the Oaks of their acorns that none remain for the pigs, which is no small loss for the inhabitants. I have seen them in *Virginia* for three consecutive days flying South in bands, which followed so closely that it was not possible to find a moment where one did not see some in the air following the same route. During mild winters, none or very few are seen. Harsh winters drive them toward the South, where they find a great abundance and more varieties of <term>mast</term> <note>original: *glandées*; acorns and forest nuts</note>, berries, etc., of which they are absolutely deprived in the North because of the continual snows and frosts.

At *New York* <note>original: *Nieuporf*, likely an error for New York in the French text</note> and *Philadelphia*, while they pass, they are shot from balconies and the roofs of houses; and in *New England*, there is such a great number that they are knocked down with long poles from the places where they roost during the night. I have not been able to learn anything of the places they come from or where they raise their young, except from a *Canada Indian* who told me he had seen them make their nests on the banks of rivers and lakes far to the North of the *St. Lawrence River*, where he had killed them with gunshots. It is surprising that one never sees any return, at least on this side. It is absolutely unknown what path they take.

---

## The Oak of the Esculus division, with larger prickly leaves.
<note>original Latin: *Quercus Esculi divisura foliis amplioribus aculeatis*. Referring to Leonard Plukenet’s *Phytographia*, Plate 54.</note>

<margin>The Oak of the Esculus division, with larger prickly leaves. Red Oak.</margin>

### **THE RED OAK.**

The leaves of this Oak retain no certain form, but sport into various shapes more than other Oaks do. The bark is dark-colored, very thick and strong, and for tanning <note>the process of making leather</note> is preferable to any other kind of Oak; the grain is coarse, the wood spongy, and not durable. They grow on high land; the acorns vary in shape, as appears by the figures of them, though they come from the same kind of Oak.

<column-break/>

### Red Oak
<note>original French: *Chêne rouge*</note>

The leaves of this Oak have no determined shape; but they are much more varied among themselves than those of other Oaks. The bark of this tree is a dark brown, very thick and very strong; it is preferable to all others for tanning. Its wood has a coarse grain; it is spongy and not very durable. It grows in elevated soil: its acorns are of different shapes, as appears by the Plate. All those represented there belong to the Red Oak.

# THE MOURNING DOVE.
<note>original Latin: *Turtur Carolinensis*. Today known as the Mourning Dove (*Zenaida macroura*).</note>

## The Turtle of Carolina.

THIS is somewhat less than a Dove house Pigeon: the Eyes black, compassed with a blue skin: the Bill black: the upper part of the Head, Neck, Back, and upper part of the Wings brown: the small feathers of the Wing, next the Back, have large black spots: the lower part of the Wing and <term>Quill-feathers</term> <note>the large, stiff feathers of the wings and tail necessary for flight</note> of a lead colour, three or four of the longest being almost black: The Breast and Belly of a pale Carnation colour <note>a soft, flesh-toned pink</note>. On each side the Neck, the breadth of a Man’s Thumb, are two spots of the colour of burnished Gold, with a tincture of crimson and green; between which and its Eye is a black spot. The Wings are long, the Tail much longer, reaching almost five Inches beyond them, and hath fourteen feathers, the two middle longest, and of equal length, and all brown; the rest are gradually shorter, having their upper part lead colour, the middle black, and the end white. The Legs and Feet are red. They breed in Carolina, and abide there always. They feed much on the Berries of Poke, i. e. *Virginian Blite* <note>original Latin: *Blitum Virginianum*; commonly known as Pokeweed. Though toxic to humans, many birds eat the berries safely.</note>, which are Poison. They likewise feed on the Seeds of this Plant; and they are accounted good Meat.

<column-break/>

## The Turtle Dove of Carolina.
<note>This column translates the original French text.</note>

IT is a little smaller than a domestic Pigeon; its eyes are black, surrounded by a blue skin; its beak is black; the top of the head, its neck, its back, and the part of its wings closest to the shoulders, are brown; the small feathers of the wings closest to the back have large black spots; the other wing feathers, large and small, are lead-colored; the three or four largest are almost black; its breast and its belly are of a pale Rose color; on each side of its neck there is a spot the width of a thumb, the color of burnished gold mixed with Crimson and Green. Between this spot and the eye there is another black one; its wings are long; its tail extends past them by nearly five inches: it is composed of fourteen feathers; the two in the middle are equal, longer than the others, and entirely brown; the others become shorter as they move away from the middle feathers; they are white at the tip, black in the middle, and lead-colored at the top. Its feet and its legs are red. These birds raise their young in Carolina, and remain there always. They feed themselves on the berries of the Virginian Blite <note>Pokeweed</note> which are poisonous. They also feed on the seeds of the Plant described below; its seeds are not poisonous.

---

->Canadian Duck’s-foot [Mayapple] of Morinus. Tournefort. A foreign plant with the appearance of a Buttercup from the Royal Garden at Paris. Low-growing, with monkshood-like leaves, a single white bell-shaped flower, and fruit like a rose-hip. Mentzel Plate 11. Tournefort’s Institutions, page 239.<-
<note>This section translates the Latin botanical citations: *Anapodophyllon Canadense Morini. Turnef. Ranunculi facie planta peregrina H. R. Par. Aconitifolia humilis, flore albo, unico campanulato Fructu Cynosbati Mentz. Tab. 11. Turnef. inst. p. 239.*</note>

---

## THE MAY APPLE.
<note>The May Apple (*Podophyllum peltatum*) is a woodland plant native to North America.</note>

THIS Plant grows about a Foot and half high; the Flower consisting of several <term>Petala</term> <note>petals</note>, with many yellow <term>Stamina</term> <note>the pollen-producing parts of a flower</note> surrounding the <term>seed-vessel</term> <note>the ovary or fruit</note>, which is oval, <term>unicapsular</term> <note>having a single seed-holding chamber</note>, and contains many roundish seeds. The Leaves of the Plant resemble the Yellow Wolfsbane <note>original Latin: *Aconitum lycoctomum luteum*</note>. The Root is said to be an excellent <term>Emetic</term> <note>a substance that causes vomiting</note>, and is used as such in Carolina; which has given it there the Name of *Ipecacuana* <note>a reference to the Brazilian medicinal root Ipecac, used for the same purpose</note>, the stringy Roots of which it resembles. It flowers in March; the Fruit is ripe in May; which has occasioned it in Virginia to be called May-Apple.

<column-break/>

## The May Apple.
<note>This column translates the original French text.</note>

THIS Plant rises to the height of a foot and a half. Its flower is composed of several leaves <note>petals</note> and several yellow stamens which surround the ovary: it is oval and has only a single pod filled with nearly round seeds. The leaves of this plant are quite similar to those of the Yellow Wolfsbane. It is said that its root is an excellent emetic; and it is used in Carolina to induce vomiting; this has caused it to be given the name Ipecacuana in that country, additionally because it resembles the fibrous roots of the latter. This plant flowers in the month of March, its fruit is ripe in May, which is why in Virginia it is called the May Apple.

<vocab>Turtur Carolinensis, Turtle of Carolina, May Apple, Anapodophyllon Canadense, Ipecacuana, Blitum Virginianum, Aconitum lycoctomum luteum, Carolina, Virginia</vocab>

# THE MOURNING DOVE
<note>original Latin: *Turtur Caroliniensis*. Today this species is known as the Mourning Dove (*Zenaida macroura*).</note>

## The Turtle of Carolina

THIS bird is somewhat smaller than a domestic pigeon. Its eyes are black, surrounded by a blue skin, and its beak is black. The upper part of the head, neck, back, and the upper part of the wings are brown. The small feathers of the wing closest to the back are marked with large black spots. The lower part of the wing and the **quill-feathers** <note>the large, stiff feathers of the wings and tail necessary for flight</note> are a leaden color, with the three or four longest feathers being almost black.

The breast and belly are a pale **carnation** <note>a soft, flesh-toned pink or peach color</note> color. On each side of the neck, about the width of a man’s thumb, are two spots the color of burnished gold, shimmering with hints of crimson and green; a black spot is located between these iridescent patches and the eye. The wings are long, and the tail is much longer, reaching almost five inches beyond the wingtips. The tail has fourteen feathers: the two in the middle are the longest and are entirely brown; the rest are gradually shorter, being leaden-colored at the base, black in the middle, and white at the tips. The legs and feet are red. 

These birds breed in Carolina and remain there throughout the year. They feed extensively on the berries of the **Poke** <note>original Latin: *Blitum Virginianum*; commonly known as Pokeweed. While these berries are toxic to humans, they are a primary food source for many North American birds.</note>. They also feed on the seeds of the Mayapple plant, and they are considered good to eat.

<column-break/>

## The Turtle Dove of Carolina
<note>This column translates the parallel French text from the original manuscript.</note>

IT is a little smaller than a domestic pigeon; its eyes are black, surrounded by a blue skin; its beak is black. The top of the head, the neck, the back, and the top of the wings are brown. The small feathers of the wings near the back have large black spots; the bottom of the wing and the flight feathers are a lead color, the three or four longest being almost black. The breast and the belly are of a pale flesh color. 

On each side of the neck, the width of a thumb, there are two spots the color of polished gold, mixed with crimson and green; between these and the eye is a black spot. The wings are long, the tail much longer, exceeding the wings by five inches; it has fourteen feathers, the two in the middle being the longest and of a single brown color. The others are shorter, of a lead color at the top, black in the middle, and white at the end. The legs and feet are red. They live in Carolina all year. They eat the berries of the **Virginian Blite** <note>Pokeweed</note>, which are a poison to others. They also eat the seeds of the plant shown here.

->**THE MAYAPPLE**<-
<note>original Latin: *Anapodophyllon Canadense*. Literally "Canadian Duck's-foot leaf," now known as the Mayapple (*Podophyllum peltatum*).</note>

This plant consists of a single tall stem supporting a very large green leaf, deeply lobed and shaped like an umbrella. A single white flower with a yellow center grows from the stem. It produces a fruit that, while the plant itself contains toxins, is sometimes consumed when fully ripe.

# PLATE 25

### A shrub with leaves like the Venetian Sumach, thick, etc.
<note>original Latin: *Frutex cotini folio, craſſo, &c.* The author compares the leaves of this American plant to the *Cotinus* (Smoke Tree) common in European gardens.</note>

## **The Coco Plum**
<note>Today known scientifically as *Chrysobalanus icaco*. It is a hardy shrub found near beaches and coastal swamps in the tropical Americas and the Caribbean.</note>

### The white-headed pigeon
<note>original Latin: *Columba capite albo*. Today known as the White-crowned Pigeon (*Patagioenas leucocephala*).</note>

## **The White-crown Pigeon**

->**Description of the Illustration**<-
The accompanying hand-colored engraving depicts a White-crowned Pigeon perched gracefully upon a branch of the Coco Plum. The bird is shown in profile, highlighting its distinctive plumage: a body of deep slate-blue that contrasts sharply with the snowy-white crown atop its head. Its legs are a vibrant red.

The branch of the **Coco Plum** <term>Coco Plum: A coastal fruit-bearing shrub whose fruit is often used for jams or eaten raw by wildlife</term> is shown with thick, leathery green leaves that are oval in shape. Small white flowers appear among the foliage. The plant bears fruit in various stages of ripeness: three are a bright, cheerful red, while one has reached a deep, purple-blue maturity. At the bottom right, a single brown, oval seed is illustrated, showing the characteristic longitudinal ridges of the plum’s stone.

<note>Catesby often illustrated birds alongside the specific plants they frequented or fed upon, providing an early ecological view of the American wilderness.</note>

# THE WHITE-HEADED PIGEON.
## *History of Jamaica, Page 303, Plate 261, Volume II.*
### The White-crowned Pigeon. <note>original French: *Pigeon à la Couronne blanche*</note>

It is as large as the common tame pigeon. The base of the bill is purple; the tip is a dusky white; the iris of the eye is yellow, surrounded by a dusky white skin. The crown of the head is white, below which the plumage is purple. The back of the neck is covered with shimmering, iridescent green feathers, each edged with black. All the rest of the bird is a dark, dusky blue; the legs and feet are red. They breed in great numbers throughout all the Bahama Islands and are very useful to the inhabitants, especially when they are young. They are gathered in great quantities from the rocks where they make their nests.

<column-break/>

It is of the same size as ordinary domestic pigeons. The base of its beak is purple; the iris of its eyes is yellow, surrounded by a whitish skin. The top of its head is white, and lower down it is purple. The back of its neck is covered with changing green feathers, bordered with black. All the rest of its body is a deep blue. Its legs and its feet are red. It multiplies greatly in all the Bahama Islands and provides great relief to the inhabitants, especially when the birds are still young; for an infinite number of them are taken from the rocks where they make their nests.

---

# A shrub with leaves like the Smoke Tree, thick, drooping at the top, with an oval blue fruit containing an angular stone.
<note>original Latin: *Frutex cotini fere folio crasso, in summitate deliquium patiente, fructu ovali cæruleo ossiculum angulosum continente.* The author compares the foliage to the *Cotinus*, known as the Smoke Tree or Venetian Sumach.</note>

## THE COCO PLUM. <note>original French: *Prune de Cacao*</note>

This is a shrub which grows from five to ten feet high; it does not have a single trunk, but rather several small stems rising from the ground, growing together in thickets. The flowers grow in bunches and are small and white, with many <term>stamens: the pollen-producing parts of a flower</term>. They produce a succession of fruit through most of the summer, which are the size and shape of a large <term>damson: a small, tart variety of plum</term>; most of them are blue, though some trees produce pale yellow fruit, and others red. Each plum contains a stone shaped like a pear, <term>chanulated: fluted or grooved</term> with six ridges. They usually grow in low, moist ground near the seaside. The leaves are as broad as a <term>crown: a large silver coin of the era</term>, thick, stiff, and shaped somewhat like a heart. The fruit is considered wholesome and has a sweet, luscious taste. The Spaniards in Cuba make a preserve of them by simmering them in sugar.

<column-break/>

This is a bush that grows from five up to ten feet in height. It pushes out several stems at once, which rise together and form a thicket. Its flowers come in bouquets. They are white, small, and have many stamens. It produces fruit almost all summer long, which follow one after another. They resemble a large Damson plum and are mostly blue. Some trees produce red ones; some others produce them in a pale yellow. Each plum contains a stone shaped like a pear, with six grooves. They grow ordinarily in a low and humid soil near the edge of the sea. Their leaves are as wide as a crown coin, thick and stiff. They have roughly the shape of a heart. The fruit is believed to be very healthy; its taste is sweet and mild. The Spaniards at Cuba make a preserve of them.

<sig>G</sig>

<summary>This page describes the White-crowned Pigeon and the Coco Plum, two species common to the Bahama Islands and the Caribbean. Catesby notes the pigeon's importance as a food source and the Spanish practice of preserving the Coco Plum in sugar.</summary>
<keywords>White-crowned Pigeon, Coco Plum, Bahama Islands, Cuba, colonial foodways, tropical botany, ornithology, Chrysobalanus icaco, Patagioenas leucocephala</keywords>

# THE SMALLEST SPOTTED TURTLE-DOVE. <note>original Latin: *TURTUR MINIMUS GUTTATUS*</note>

## The Ground-Dove.
THE weight of this Dove was an ounce and half: In size about the same as a Lark. The Bill is yellow, except the end, which is black. The iris of the Eye red. The Breast and whole Front of the Bird is of a changeable purple colour, with dark purple spots. The large Quill-Feathers and Tail are of a muddy purple. The Legs and Feet dirty yellow. In short, the whole Bird has such a Composition of Colours, so blended together, that no perfect Description by words can be given of it. And I have observed some of them to differ in colour from others: From which Causes probably may be the reason why Nieremberg <note>Juan Eusebius Nieremberg (1595–1658), a Spanish Jesuit and naturalist.</note>, Margravius <note>Georg Marcgraf (1610–1644), a German naturalist who co-authored a famous study of Brazilian nature.</note>, and others who have described it, have varied in their descriptions of it. They fly many of them together, and make short flights from place to place, lighting generally on the ground. They are natives of most Countries in America lying between the Tropics. They sometimes approach so far North as Carolina, and visit the lower parts of the Country near the Sea, where these Trees grow, and feed on the Berries, which gives their flesh an Aromatic flavour.

<column-break/>

## Little Spotted Turtle-Dove. <note>original French: *Petite Tourterelle tachetée*</note>
THE weight of this Bird is an ounce and a half, and its size that of a Lark. Its beak is yellow except for the tip which is black. The Iris of its eyes is red. The breast and the whole front of this bird is of a changing purple color with dark purple spots. The large feathers of the wings and the tail are of a dark purple: The legs and the feet are a dirty yellow. In a word, this bird has so many different colors and so mixed, that it is not possible to describe them. I have even observed that they do not all have precisely the same colors; which may well be the cause of the differences one observes in the descriptions that Nieremberg, Margravius, and some others have given of them. These Birds fly in flocks. They stop often, and usually rest on the Earth. They are found in almost all the Countries of America that are between the Tropics. Sometimes they advance toward the North as far as Carolina: They come into the lower part of this Country toward the Sea, where the trees described below grow, whose berries they eat, which gives their flesh an aromatic taste.

---

### *Prickly Yellow-wood, with Mastic-like leaves, longer Spindle-tree-like leaves and capsule-bearing fruit, from the island of Jamaica. Mr. Banister. Phytographia.* <note>original Latin: *Zanthoxylum spinosum, Lentisci, longioribus foliis Euonimi fructu capsulari ex Insula Jamaicensi. D. Banister. Phytogr.*</note>

## The Pellitory, or Tooth-ach Tree.
THIS Tree seldom grows above a Foot in thickness, and about sixteen feet high. The Bark is white, and very rough. The Trunc and large Limbs are in a singular manner thick set, with pyramidal-shaped protuberances, pointing from the Tree; at the end of every one of which is a sharp thorn. These protuberances are of the same consistence with the Bark of the Tree, of various sizes, the largest being as big as Walnuts: The smaller Branches are beset with prickles only. The leaves are pennated <note>pinnate: feathershaped, with leaflets arranged on either side of a central stem.</note>, standing on a Rib six inches long, to which the Lobes <note>leaflets</note> are set one against another, with foot-stalks half an Inch long. The Lobes are awry, their greatest Vein not running in the middle, but on one side, being bigger than the other. From the ends of the Branches shoot forth long stalks of small Pentapetalous <note>five-petaled</note> white Flowers with reddish Stamina: Every flower is succeeded by four shining black Seeds, contained in a round green capsula <note>seed pod</note>. The leaves smell like those of Orange; which, with the Seeds and Bark, is aromatic, very hot and astringent, and is used by the People inhabiting the Sea Coasts of Virginia and Carolina for the Tooth-ach, which has given it its name.

<column-break/>

## Tree for the Tooth-ache. <note>original French: *Arbre pour le Mal des Dents*</note>
THIS Tree rarely has more than sixteen feet of height by one foot in diameter. Its bark is white and very rough. Its trunk and its large branches have this peculiarity: they are almost all covered in pyramidal protuberances, the point of which is terminated by a very sharp spine. These protuberances are of the same consistency as the bark of the Tree. The largest are as big as Walnuts. The small branches have only thorns. The leaves are arranged two by two, one across from the other, on a stem six inches long, to which they are attached by stalks of half an inch. These leaves are skewed; their largest veins do not divide them through the middle. They sprout at the ends of the branches long stems which support small white five-leaved flowers, with red Stamens. They form small bouquets. Each flower is followed by four seeds of a shining black, enclosed in a green and round capsule. The leaves have the same odor as those of the Orange tree. They are aromatic as well as the bark and the seed, very hot and astringent. The Peoples who inhabit the Coasts of Virginia and Carolina use it for tooth-ache, and it is from this that the Tree took its name.

<summary>This page describes the Ground-Dove (Columbigallina passerina) and the Toothache Tree (Zanthoxylum clava-herculis), noting the bird's iridescent plumage and the tree's medicinal use for dental pain.</summary>

# THE SMALLEST SPOTTED TURTLE-DOVE. <note>original Latin: *TURTUR MINIMUS GUTTATUS*</note>

## The Ground-Dove.
THE weight of this Dove was an ounce and half: In size about the same as a Lark. The Bill is yellow, except the end, which is black. The iris of the Eye red. The Breast and whole Front of the Bird is of a changeable purple colour, with dark purple spots. The large Quill-Feathers and Tail are of a muddy purple. The Legs and Feet dirty yellow. In short, the whole Bird has such a Composition of Colours, so blended together, that no perfect Description by words can be given of it. And I have observed some of them to differ in colour from others: From which Causes probably may be the reason why Nieremberg <note>Juan Eusebius Nieremberg (1595–1658), a Spanish Jesuit and naturalist who wrote on the natural history of the New World.</note>, Margravius <note>Georg Marcgraf (1610–1644), a German naturalist who co-authored the *Historia Naturalis Brasiliae*, a foundational study of South American nature.</note>, and others who have described it, have varied in their descriptions of it. They fly many of them together, and make short flights from place to place, lighting generally on the ground. They are natives of most Countries in America lying between the Tropics. They sometimes approach so far North as Carolina, and visit the lower parts of the Country near the Sea, where these Trees grow, and feed on the Berries, which gives their flesh an Aromatic flavour.

<column-break/>

## Little Spotted Turtle-Dove. <note>original French: *Petite Tourterelle tachetée*</note>
THE weight of this Bird is an ounce and a half, and its size that of a Lark. Its beak is yellow except for the tip which is black. The Iris of its eyes is red. The breast and the whole front of this bird is of a changing purple color, with spots of a deep purple. The large flight feathers and the tail are of a dull purple. The legs and feet are a dirty yellow. In summary, this bird is composed of such a mixture of colors, so blended together, that it is impossible to give a perfect description in words. And I have observed that some differ in color from others; which is probably the reason why Nieremberg, Marcgrave, and others who have described it, differ in their accounts. They fly together in great numbers, and make only short flights, usually landing on the ground. They are found in most of the countries of America between the Tropics. They sometimes go as far north as Carolina, where they frequent the lower parts of the country near the sea, where these trees <note>referring to the Prickly Ash or Yellow-wood shown in the illustration</note> grow, and feed on the berries, which gives their flesh an aromatic flavor.

THE SMALLER ASH-WHITE THRUSH, NOT SPOTTED. <note>original Latin: *Turdus minor cinereo-albus non maculatus*</note>. History of Jamaica, page 306. Plate 256. Figure 3.

## *The Mock-Bird.*

**THIS** Bird is about as big or rather less than a Blackbird, and of a slenderer build. The bill is black; the iris of the eye is a brownish yellow; the back and tail are dark-brown; the breast and belly are light-gray; the wings are brown, except that the upper part of the quill-feathers have their exterior vanes white; and some of the small feathers, near the shoulder of the wing, are edged with white. The males and females are so alike that they are not easily distinguished by the color of their feathers.

*Hernandez* <note>Francisco Hernández de Toledo (1514–1587), a physician and naturalist who studied the flora and fauna of Mexico.</note> justly calls it the Queen of all singing birds. The native *Indians*, by way of eminence or admiration, call it *Cencontlatolly*, or "four hundred tongues"; and we call it (though not by so elevated a name, yet very properly) the *Mock-bird*, from its wonderful mocking and imitating the notes of all birds, from the *Hummingbird* to the *Eagle*. From March till August it sings incessantly day and night with the greatest variety of notes; and, to complete its compositions, it borrows from the whole choir, and repeats to them their own tunes with such artful melody that it is equally pleasing and surprising. They may be said not only to sing but dance, by gradually raising themselves from the place where they stand, with their wings extended, and falling with their head down to the same place; then turning round, with their wings continuing spread, they perform many pretty, playful gestures along with their melody.

They are familiar and sociable birds, usually perching on the tops of chimneys or trees among the inhabitants, who are entertained with their tuneful airs for most part of the summer. Their food consists of haws, berries, and insects. In winter, when there is least variety and plenty, they will eat the berries of the Dogwood.

## Virginian Male Dogwood with small flowers arranged in a cluster, surrounded by a white four-petaled perianth arranged radially. <note>original Latin: *Cornus mas Virginiana flosculis in corymbo digestis perianthio tetrapetalo albo radiatim cinctis*</note>. Plukenet, Almagestum 120. <note>Refers to Leonard Plukenet’s *Almagestum Botanicum*, a major botanical compendium of the era.</note>

### THE DOGWOOD TREE.

**THIS** is a small tree, the trunk being seldom above eight or ten inches thick. The leaves resemble our common European Dogwood, but are fairer and larger, standing opposite to each other on foot-stalks of about an inch long, from among which many flowers branch forth in the following manner. In the beginning of March the blossoms break forth; and though perfectly formed and wide open, they are not as wide as a six-pence; they increase gradually to the breadth of a man's hand, not reaching their full size until about six weeks after they began to open. Each flower consists of four greenish-white leaves <note>In modern botany, these "leaves" are known as bracts, while the true flowers are the small yellow cluster in the center.</note>, every leaf having a deep notch at the end. From the bottom of the flower rises a tuft of yellow *stamina*; every one of which opens at the top into four small leaves or petals. The wood is white, has a close grain, and is very hard, like that of Boxwood. The flowers are succeeded by clusters of berries, having from two to six in a cluster, closely joined, and set on foot-stalks an inch long. These berries are red, of an oval form, and of the size of large haws, containing a hard stone. Just as the flowers are a great ornament to the woods in summer, so are the berries in winter, as they usually remain full on the trees until the approach of spring; and being very bitter, they are little desired by birds except in times of scarcity. I have observed Mock-birds and other kinds of Thrushes feeding on them. In Virginia, I found one of these Dogwood trees with rose-colored flowers, which was luckily blown down, and many of its branches had taken root, which I transplanted into a garden. The variety with the white flower Mr. Fairchild <note>Thomas Fairchild (1667–1729), a celebrated London gardener and nurseryman.</note> has in his garden.

<column-break/>

## *The Mockingbird.* <note>This column is a translation of the French text, which served as a parallel description for international readers.</note>

**THIS** bird is nearly the size of a Blackbird, but more slender. Its beak is black. The iris of its eyes is a dull yellow; its back and tail are a dark brown; its breast and belly are a light gray. Its wings are brown, except for the top of the large feathers, whose outer edges are white, and some of the small feathers near the shoulder, which are bordered with white. It is difficult to distinguish the male from the female by the color of their feathers.

*Hernandez* is right to call it the King of all singing birds. The *Indians*, to express the admiration it causes them, have given it the name *Cencontlatolli*, which is to say, "four hundred tongues." The English have not given it such a magnificent name, but one which suits it perfectly. They have named it the *Mock-Bird*, that is to say, the "Mocking Bird," for it possesses to a surprising degree the talent of counterfeiting the warbling of all birds, from the *Hummingbird* to the *Eagle*. From the month of March until the month of August, it sings without ceasing, day and night. Its song is infinitely varied. It incorporates the songs of all birds into the composition of its airs, and repeats their warbling with such accuracy and melody that one is equally surprised and charmed. One can say of this bird not only that it sings, but also that it dances. It rises little by little, wings extended, from the place where it stops to sing, and then it falls back there head-first. Then, turning in a circle, still with wings extended, it seems to match its grotesque movements to the sound of its voice.

These birds are familiar and love the company of men. They have a habit of coming to place themselves on the tops of chimneys, or perching on trees in the middle of settlements. Thus, one has the pleasure of hearing them during the best part of the summer. The fruits of the Hawthorn, cherries, and some insects are their food. In winter, when they find nothing else, they eat the berries of the Male Dogwood.

### Virginian Male Dogwood. <note>original French: *Cornier Mâle de la Virginie*</note>

**THIS** tree is not large. Its trunk is hardly more than eight or ten inches in diameter. Its leaves resemble those of our ordinary Dogwood, but they are larger and more beautiful. They are arranged opposite one another on stems an inch long. Several flowers grow from between them in the following manner. At the beginning of the month of March they begin to appear; and although they are entirely formed and open, they are not as wide as a six-sou piece <note>A small French coin; Catesby uses this as a familiar size reference for French readers, just as he used the six-pence for the English.</note>. They then increase up to the size of a hand. These flowers do not reach their perfection until six weeks after they have begun to open. They are composed of four greenish-white leaves. Each leaf has a deep notch at its extremity. From the center of the flower rises a tuft of yellow *stamens*, divided at the top into four small leaves. The wood of this tree is white. Its grain is tight. It is as hard as Boxwood. Its flowers are followed by berries arranged in clusters. There are from two to six in the same cluster, very tight against one another. They are attached by stems an inch long. These berries are red, of an oval shape, and the size of the fruit of the Hawthorn. They contain a very hard stone. Just as the flowers served as a great ornament to the forests during the summer, the berries embellish them in turn during the winter. They usually remain on the trees until the approach of spring; for as they are very bitter, birds are not very fond of them except when they lack other food. I have noticed that the Mockingbird and some other species of Thrushes eat them. I found in Virginia one of these Dogwoods whose flowers were the color of roses. The wind had fortunately knocked it down, and I transplanted into a garden several of its branches which had taken root. Mr. Fairchild has in his garden the one whose flowers are white.

<summary>This page describes the Mockingbird (Mimus polyglottos) and the Flowering Dogwood (Cornus florida), highlighting the bird's extraordinary mimetic abilities and the tree's seasonal transformation and importance as a winter food source.</summary>
<keywords>Mockingbird, Cencontlatolli, Flowering Dogwood, bird mimicry, Thomas Fairchild, Virginia flora, 18th-century ornithology, Mark Catesby</keywords>

# THE RED THRUSH <note>original Latin: *Turdus Ruffus*; today known as the Brown Thrasher (*Toxostoma rufum*).</note>

### The Fox coloured Thrush.

THIS is somewhat larger than the Mockbird, and of a more clumsy shape. Its Bill somewhat long, and a little hooked. The Eyes yellow. All the upper part of its Body of a muddy red, or Fox-colour, except the interior vanes of the Quill-Feathers which are dark-brown, and the ends of the covert Wing-Feathers which are edged with dusky white. Its Tail is very long, and of the same colour of the Back and Wings. The Neck, Breast, and all the under part of the Body, of a dusky white, spotted with dark-brown. The Legs and Feet are brown. This Bird is called in *Virginia* the *French Mockbird* <note>A common historical name for the Brown Thrasher, perhaps distinguishing its less varied song from the "True" Mockingbird.</note>. It remains all the year in *Carolina* and *Virginia*. It sings with some variety of Notes, though not comparable to the Mockbird.

---
->*A Maryland shrub-like cherry tree, with the leaf of the Bird-cherry, and small white clustered flowers.* <note>original Latin: *Cerasi similis arbuscula Mariana. Pædi folio, flore albo parvo racemoso.*</note><-
->Plukenet, *Mantissa* 43. Table CCCXXXIX. <note>Refers to the botanical works of Leonard Plukenet (1642–1706), an English botanist.</note><-
---

### THE CLUSTER’D BLACK CHERRY.

THIS Tree, in the Manner of its growing, resembles much our common black Cherry, in the thick Woods of *Carolina*, where these Trees most abound. They seldom grow bigger than a Man's leg; but by being removed to more open Places, they become large, some of them being two Feet in Diameter. In March it produces pendulous Bunches of white flowers, which are succeeded by small black Cherries of a greenish Cast, hanging in Clusters of five inches long, in the manner of Currants. The Fruit of some of these Trees is sweet and pleasant: Others are bitter. They are esteemed for making the best Cherry Brandy of any other, and also for stocks to graft other Cherries upon. They are much coveted by Birds, particularly those of the Thrush-kind.

<column-break/>

### Red Thrush <note>translated from the French: *Grive rousse*</note>.

*IT is a little larger than the Mockingbird, and is not as slender. Its beak is long, and a little hooked; its eyes yellow. All the upper part of its body is red, or Fox-colored, except the exterior fringes of the large wing feathers which are of a dark brown, and the tips of the small feathers that cover the wings, which are a dull white. Its tail is very long and of the same color as its back; and its wings, its neck, its breast, and the entire underside of its body are a dull white, spotted with dark brown. Its legs and its feet are brown. In Virginia this Bird is called the French Mockingbird. It stays in Carolina and Virginia throughout the year. Its song has some variety; but it is not comparable to that of the Mockingbird.*

### Shrub resembling the Black Cherry <note>translated from the French: *Arbrisseau ressemblant au Cerisier noir*</note>.

*THIS tree, in its manner of growth, much resembles our black Cherry tree. One hardly finds any larger than a leg in the woods of Carolina, where this tree is very common; but when it is transplanted to a more open place, it grows larger. One sees some that are up to two feet in diameter. In the month of March it produces drooping clusters of white flowers. These are followed by small black cherries, a little greenish. They form clusters five inches long, similar to those of Redcurrants. The fruits of some of these trees are sweet and pleasant; others are bitter. The cherry-water <note>original French: *l'eau de Cerise*; likely referring to cherry brandy or spirits.</note> made from them is considered the best; as are ordinary cherries that have been grafted onto one of these trees. Birds, and especially Thrushes, are very fond of these cherries.*

# THE RED THRUSH <note>original: *Turdus ruffus*; today known as the Brown Thrasher (*Toxostoma rufum*).</note>

### The Fox-colored Thrush.

This bird is somewhat larger than the Mockingbird, and of a more clumsy shape. Its beak is somewhat long and slightly hooked. The eyes are yellow. All the upper part of its body is of a dull red, or fox-color, except for the interior vanes of the flight feathers <term>Quill-Feathers; the primary feathers of a bird's wing</term> which are dark brown, and the ends of the wing coverts <term>covert Wing-Feathers; the feathers covering the bases of the flight feathers</term> which are edged with a dusky white. Its tail is very long, and of the same color as the back and wings. The neck, breast, and all the underside of the body are a dusky white, spotted with dark brown. The legs and feet are brown. 

This bird is called in *Virginia* the *French Mockingbird* <note>A common historical name for the Brown Thrasher, perhaps distinguishing its song—which repeats phrases—from the "True" Mockingbird, or suggesting a perceived "foreign" or lesser quality.</note>. It remains throughout the year in *Carolina* and *Virginia*. It sings with some variety of notes, though it is not comparable to the Mockingbird.

---
->*A small Maryland tree resembling a cherry, with the leaf of a Bird-cherry, and small, white, clustered flowers.* <note>original Latin: *Cerasi similis arbuscula Mariana, Pædi folio, flore albo parvo racemoso.*</note><-
->Plukenet, *Mantissa* 43. Table CCCXXXIX. <note>A reference to the botanical works of Leonard Plukenet (1642–1706), an influential English botanist.</note><-
---

### THE CLUSTERED BLACK CHERRY.

This tree, in the manner of its growth, closely resembles our common Black Cherry. In the thick woods of *Carolina*, where these trees are most abundant, they grow to a great height and size. The leaves are a bright green, shaped like those of the common cherry but with finely serrated edges. The fruit grows in long, drooping clusters, turning from green to a deep, dark purple or black when ripe. These berries provide a favorite food for many birds, including the Red Thrush.

# THE FIELDFARE <note>original Latin: *Turdus pilaris, migratorius*. While *Turdus pilaris* is the scientific name for the European Fieldfare, Catesby used it here to describe the American Robin (*Turdus migratorius*), noting its similar appearance and migratory behavior.</note>

### The Fieldfare.

# THE SNAKE-ROOT

### *Aristolochia* and others. <note>original Latin: *Aristolochia &c.* A genus of plants known as birthworts or pipevines. In colonial America, various species were known as "Snake-root" due to their supposed medicinal properties in treating venomous bites.</note>

The Snake-root.

<note>This illustration demonstrates Catesby’s signature style: placing a bird in a dynamic, life-like pose—here, clinging to a vine—integrated with a botanical specimen. This provided a more complete picture of the animal's natural environment than traditional stiff, profile-only drawings of the era.</note>

--- Page 105 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of North American birds and plants by Mark Catesby. Following the Red Thrush on the previous page, Catesby now describes the "Fieldfare" (American Robin) and the Virginia Snake-root plant.</meta>

<page-num>29</page-num>

# THE MIGRATORY FIELDFARE <note>original: *Turdus pilaris, migratorius*. Though Catesby uses the name of the European Fieldfare, the description of the red breast and behavior identifies this as the American Robin (*Turdus migratorius*).</note>

## The Fieldfare of Carolina

WEIGHS two ounces three quarters; about the size and shape of the European Fieldfare. That part of the Bill, next the Head, is yellow. Over and under the Eye are two white streaks. The upper part of the Head is black, with a mixture of Brown. The Wings and upper part of the Body brown: The Tail dark-brown: The Throat black and white: The Breast and Belly red: The Legs and Feet brown. In Winter they arrive from the North in *Virginia* and *Carolina*, in numerous Flights, and return in the Spring as ours in *England*. They are <term>canorous</term> <note>Melodious or song-filled.</note>, having a loud Cry like our <term>Missel-bird</term> <note>The Mistletoe Thrush (*Turdus viscivorus*), a common European bird known for its loud song.</note>, which the following Accident gave me an opportunity of knowing: Having some Trees of *Alaternus* <note>A species of buckthorn (*Rhamnus alaternus*).</note> full of Berries (which were the first that had been introduced in *Virginia*) a single Fieldfare seemed so delighted with the Berries, that he tarried all the Summer feeding on them. In *Maryland*, I am told, they breed and abide the whole year.

<column-break/>

## Brown Thrush of Passage. <note>original French: *Grive brune de Passage*.</note>

*IT weighs two ounces and three quarters. It is of nearly the same size as the European species, and resembles it greatly. The base of its beak is yellow. It has a white stripe above, and another below the eyes. The top of its head is a black mixed with brown; its wings and its back are brown: its tail of an obscure brown; its throat black and white; its breast and its belly red; its legs and its feet brown. During the Winter they come in flocks from the North into Virginia and Carolina, and return in the Spring, like those that we see in England. They sing well; they have a strong voice, much like our Brown Thrush, which feeds on <term>Mistletoe</term> <note>original French: *Guy*.</note>, which I only discovered by chance. I had several Alaternus trees loaded with berries. They were the first that had been planted in Virginia. A Thrush took such a liking to these berries, that she remained during the entire Summer to eat them. I have been told that they remain during the whole year in Maryland, and raise their young there.*

---

->The Virginia Snakewort or Snake-root with a knotted stem. <note>original Latin: *Aristolochia pistolochia seu Serpentaria Virginiana caule nodoso*.</note><-
->Plukenet's Almagestum, page 50, Plate 148. <note>original: *Pluk. Alma. p. 50. Tab. 148.* A reference to the botanical work of Leonard Plukenet.</note><-

### The Snake-Root of Virginia.

THIS Plant rises out of the Ground in one, two, and sometimes three pliant Stalks, which at every little distance are crooked, or undulated. The Leaves stand alternately, and are about three inches long, in form somewhat like the *Smilax aspera* <note>Commonly known as Rough Bindweed or Sarsaparilla.</note>. The Flowers grow close to the ground on foot-stalks an inch long, of a singular shape, though somewhat resembling those of the <term>Birthworts</term> <note>Plants of the genus *Aristolochia*, historically used in childbirth and for treating snakebites.</note>, of a dark purple colour. A round granulated <term>capsula</term> <note>Seed pod.</note> succeeds the Flower, containing many small Seeds, which are ripe in May. The usual price of this excellent Root, both in *Virginia* and *Carolina*, is about six pence a Pound when dryed, which is Money hardly earned. Yet the Negro Slaves (who only dig it) employ much of the little time allowed them by their Masters in search of it; which is the Cause of there being seldom found any but very small Plants. By planting them in a Garden they increased so in two years time, that one's hand could not grasp the stalks of one Plant. It delights in shady Woods, and is usually found at the Roots of great Trees.

<column-break/>

### Virginia Snakewort. <note>original French: *Serpentaire de la Virginie*.</note>

*THIS Plant pushes up one, two, and sometimes three stems, flexible and very twisted. Its leaves are arranged alternately on its stems, and are about three inches long. It resembles those of the Smilax aspera. Its flowers are born near the ground on pedicles one inch long. They are of a singular shape, although they approach those of the Birthwort. Their color is dark purple. They are succeeded by a round, ribbed Capsule. It contains several small seeds, which are ripe in the month of May. This excellent root is only sold in Virginia and Carolina for <term>six sous</term> <note>equivalent to six pence.</note> a pound, even when it is dry. This is very little; however the Negroes, who alone take this trouble, employ in searching for these roots, and pulling them from the earth, the greater part of the little time that their Masters leave them; which is why one hardly finds anything but very small Snakewort. After having transplanted some into a Garden, they increased so much in two years, that one could not grab all the stems of a single Plant at once. The Snakewort thrives in shaded places. It is commonly found on the roots of large trees.*

<sig>H</sig>

# LEAD-COLORED MISTLETOE THRUSH <note>original: *Turdus Viscivorus Plumbeus*. This is the Red-legged Thrush (*Turdus plumbeus*), native to the Caribbean.</note>

## The red leg'd Thrush

WEIGHS two ounces and an half; has a dusky black Bill: The inside of the Mouth is more red than usual: The Iris of the Eye red, with a circle of the same colour encompassing it: The Throat black: All the rest of the Body of a dusky blue, except that the interior <term>Vanes</term> <note>The flat, web-like part of the feather on either side of the central shaft.</note> of the large wing-feathers are black, as is the Tail when closed; but when spread, the outermost Feathers appear to have their Ends white, and are gradually shorter than the two middlemost: The Legs and Feet are red.

The Hen differs from the Cock no otherwise than in being about a third part less. In the <term>Gizzard</term> <note>A muscular part of a bird's stomach used for grinding food.</note> of one were the Berries of the Tree described below. In its Singing, Gestures, etc. this Bird much resembles other Thrushes. I saw many of them on the Islands of Andros and <term>Ilathera</term> <note>Now known as Eleuthera in the Bahamas.</note>.

---

# LARGE TURPENTINE TREE WITH BIRCH-LIKE BARK AND TRIANGULAR FRUIT <note>original: *Teribinthus major Betulæ cortice, fructu triangulari*</note>
## History of Jamaica, Volume 2, page 89, Table 199 <note>original: *Hist. Jam. Vol. 2. p. 89. Tab. 199*. This refers to Hans Sloane’s earlier natural history work.</note>

---

## THE GUM-ELIMY TREE <note>Now commonly known as the Gumbo-limbo tree (*Bursera simaruba*).</note>

THIS is a large Tree; the Bark remarkably red and smooth. The Leaves are <term>pennate</term> <note>Arranged like a feather, with leaflets on both sides of a common stalk.</note>; the middle rib five or six inches long, with the <term>Pinnæ</term> <note>Leaflets.</note> set opposite to one another, on foot-stalks half an inch long. The Blossoms (which I did not see) are succeeded by purple-coloured Berries bigger than large Peas, hanging in clusters on a stalk of about five inches long, to which each Berry is joined by a Foot-stalk of an inch long. The Seed is hard, white, and of a triangular figure, inclosed within a thin <term>capsula</term> <note>A seed-pod or casing.</note>, which divides in three parts, and discharges the Seed: This Tree produces a large quantity of Gum, of a brown colour, and of the Consistence of Turpentine. It is esteemed a good <term>Vulnerary</term> <note>A medicine or substance used for healing wounds.</note>, and is much used for Horses. Most of the Bahama Islands abound with these Trees.

<column-break/>

## Red-legged Thrush <note>original French: *Grive aux jambes rouges*.</note>

IT weighs two ounces and a half. Its beak is a dark black on the outside, and a brighter red than usual on the inside. The iris of its eyes is red, as is a circle by which it is surrounded. Its throat is black; and all the rest of its body is a dark blue, except for the interior fringes of the large wing feathers, which are black. The tail also appears black when it is closed; when it opens, the feathers that terminate it on each side seem to have white tips, and are always shorter as they move away from the two middle feathers: Its legs and its feet are red.

The Female differs from the Male only in that she is about a third smaller than him. In the Gizzard of one of these Thrushes were found the Berries of the Tree described below. This Bird closely resembles other Thrushes in its Song and its manner. I have seen a great number of them on the Islands of Andros and <term>Ilathera</term>.

---

## Tree that produces Elemi Gum <note>original French: *Arbre qui produit la Gomme Elemi*.</note>

THIS tree is large; its bark is extremely red and smooth, its leaves are arranged in pairs on a rib of five to six inches, and supported by stalks of half an inch. Following the flowers (which I have not seen) are Berries larger than the largest peas: they form clusters on a stem of about five inches in length: Each Berry is attached there by a stalk one inch long: The seed is hard, white, and triangular, enclosed in a thin Capsule that opens in three places and lets it fall. This tree produces a quantity of Gum of a brown color and of the same consistency as Turpentine: It is believed to be a <term>vulnerary</term>; and it is used much for Horses. Most of the Bahama Islands have these trees.

## The Red-legged Thrush

# LARGE TURPENTINE TREE WITH BIRCH-LIKE BARK AND TRIANGULAR FRUIT <note>original: *Terebinthus major Betulæ cortice, fructu triangulari*. This is the Gumbo-limbo tree.</note>

## History of Jamaica, Volume 2, page 89, Table 199 <note>original: *Hist. Jam. Vol. 2. p. 89. Tab. 199*. This refers to Sir Hans Sloane’s seminal work on Jamaican natural history.</note>

---

## THE GUM-ELIMY TREE <note>Now commonly known as the Gumbo-limbo tree (*Bursera simaruba*), famous for its peeling red bark.</note>

THIS is a large Tree; the Bark remarkably red and smooth. The Leaves are <term>pennate</term> <note>Arranged like the components of a feather, with leaflets on both sides of a common stalk.</note> and grow in clusters. The fruit is small, hanging in bunches, and contains a three-sided seed which serves as a vital food source for many birds. The tree produces a fragrant resin or gum, used historically for medicinal purposes and as a waterproof sealant for canoes.

# THE CAROLINA HOLLY <note>original: *Agrifolium Carolinense*. This plant is the Dahoon Holly (*Ilex cassine*), an evergreen native to the coastal plains of the southeastern United States.</note>

## The Dahoon Holly.

<meta>This page continues Mark Catesby’s Natural History of Carolina, Florida and the Bahama Islands. # THE SMALLEST THRUSH <note>original: *Turdus minimus*. This species is currently identified as the Gray-cheeked Thrush (*Catharus minimus*), though Catesby’s description of it staying "all the Year" suggests he may have confused it with a similar-looking resident species or observed a Hermit Thrush.</note>

### The little Thrush.

IN Shape and Colour it agrees with the Description of the European *Mavis* <note>The Song Thrush (*Turdus philomelos*).</note>, or *Song-Thrush*, differing only in Bigness; this weighing no more than one Ounce and a quarter. It never sings, having only a single Note, like the Winter-Note of our *Mavis*. It abides all the Year in *Carolina*. They are seldom seen, being but few, and those abiding only in dark Recesses of the thickest Woods and Swamps. Their Food is the Berries of Holly, Haws, *&c.*

---

# CAROLINA HOLLY WITH TOOTHED LEAVES AND RED BERRIES <note>original: *Agrifolium Carolinense folijs dentatis baccis rubris*. This is the Dahoon Holly (*Ilex cassine*).</note>

### THE DAHOON HOLLY.

THIS Holly usually grows erect, sixteen Feet high; the Branches shooting straighter, and being of quicker Growth than the common Kind. The Leaves are longer, of a brighter green, and more pliant; not prickly, but serrated only. The Berries are red, growing in large Clusters. This is a very uncommon Plant in *Carolina*, I having never seen it but at Col. *Bull's* <note>Colonel William Bull (1683–1755), a prominent landowner and later Lieutenant Governor of South Carolina.</note> Plantation on *Ashley* River, where it grows in a Bog.

<column-break/>

### Little Thrush <note>original: *Petite Grive*</note>

*THIS Bird perfectly resembles, in its shape and color, the European Song-Thrush: It differs only in its size: For it weighs only an ounce and a quarter. It never sings. Its cry is not varied: It is the same as that which our Song-Thrush makes in Winter. It remains all year in Carolina. One sees it rarely, because there is only a very small number: Furthermore, they hide themselves in the thickest of the Woods, and toward the most shaded Swamps. They feed on Berries of Holly, Hawthorn, &c.*

---

### Dahoon Holly <note>original: *Houx de Dahon*</note>

*THIS Holly usually rises quite straight, to the height of sixteen feet. Its branches are straighter and grow faster than those of the common Holly. Its leaves are longer, more flexible, and of a lighter green: They are not armed with spines, but only serrated. Its Berries are red and form very large Clusters. This Plant is very rare in Carolina. I have never seen it there except in the Plantation of Colonel Bull on the Ashley River, where it grows in a bog.*

<meta>This page continues Mark Catesby’s Natural History of Carolina, Florida and the Bahama Islands. Having previously described the Smallest Thrush and the Dahoon Holly on page 31, Catesby here moves to the Shore Lark and the iconic Sea Oats of the Atlantic coast.</meta>

# THE YELLOW-THROATED LARK <note>original: *Alauda gutture flavo*. This bird is now known as the Shore Lark or Horned Lark (*Eremophila alpestris*). Catesby’s name refers to its distinctive yellow throat.</note>

### The Lark.
In size and shape this resembles our Sky-Lark <note>The Eurasian Skylark (*Alauda arvensis*), which Catesby uses as a familiar reference for his European readers.</note>. The Crown of the Head is mixed with black and yellow feathers. Through the Eyes runs a stripe of yellow. From the Angle of the Mouth runs a black stripe, inclining downward; except for which, the Throat and Neck are yellow. The upper part of the Breast is covered with a Patch of black feathers, in the form of a Crescent. The remaining part of the Breast and Belly are of a brown straw-color. It has a long Heel <note>term: *heel* — referring to the elongated hind claw or "hallux" typical of larks, which helps them walk on open ground.</note>. It has a single Note, like that of our Sky-Lark in Winter; at which time, and in cold Weather only, they appear in *Virginia* and *Carolina*. They come from the North in great Flights, and return early in the Spring. From their near resemblance to our Sky-Lark, I conceive they mount up and sing as ours do; but as they appear here only in Winter, I cannot determine it. They frequent the Sand-Hills upon the Sea-shore of *Carolina*, and there feed on these Oats, which they find scattered on the Sands.

---

## CAROLINIAN SHARP-LEAVED MILL-SEED GRASS <note>original: *Gramen Myloicophoron Oxyphyllon Carolinianum*. This is the Sea Oat (*Uniola paniculata*), a vital plant for stabilizing coastal dunes.</note>
->Plukenet, *Almagestum Botanicum*, page 137. Plate 32.<-

### THE SEA-SIDE OAT.
This Plant I observed growing nowhere but on Sand-Hills; so near the Sea, that at high Tides the Water flows to it. Its Height is usually four and five feet.

<column-break/>

### The Lark <note>original: *L'Alloüette*</note>
It resembles, in its form and size, our singing Lark. Its head is covered with a mixture of black and yellow feathers. Its throat and its body are yellow, except for a black stripe which begins on each side at the corner of the beak, and descends to the middle of the neck. The top of its breast is covered with black feathers, which form a Crescent. The rest of its breast and its belly are of a dark straw color. It has a long spur <note>original: *éperon*. Catesby uses the term "heel" in English for this anatomical feature.</note>. Its song consists of only one Note, like that of our singing Lark in Winter. It is only in this season, and when it is very cold, that these Birds show themselves in *Virginia* and *Carolina*. They come from the North in large flocks, and return early in the Spring. I judge by the resemblance they have to our Lark, that they rise and sing like her; but as they only appear in Winter, I cannot affirm it positively. They frequent the Dunes which are on the shores of the Sea of *Carolina*; and they feed on the Oats that they find here and there in the sands.

---

### Oat of the Seaside <note>original: *Avoine du Bord de la Mer*</note>
I have observed that this Plant only grows on the Dunes, and so close to the Sea, that during high Tides the water comes right up to it. It ordinarily rises to the height of four or five feet.

## CAROLINIAN SHARP-LEAVED MILL-SEED GRASS <note>original: *Gramen Mylcicophoron oxyphyllon Carolinianum*. This plant is the Sea Oat (*Uniola paniculata*), a hardy grass essential for stabilizing the sand dunes of the American Southeast.</note>

## THE YELLOW-THROATED LARK <note>original: *Alauda gutture flavo*. This bird is now known as the Horned Lark (*Eremophila alpestris*). It is the only lark species native to North America.</note>
### The Lark.

In size and shape this resembles our Sky-Lark <note>Catesby refers to the Eurasian Skylark (*Alauda arvensis*), a bird familiar to his European audience, to provide a point of comparison for the new American species.</note>. The Crown of the Head is mixed with black and yellow feathers. Through the Eyes runs a stripe of yellow. From the Angle of the Mouth runs a black stripe, inclining downward; except for which, the Throat and Neck are yellow. The upper part of the Breast is covered with a Patch of black feathers, in the form of a Crescent. The remaining part of the Breast and Belly are of a brown straw-color. It has a long <term>Heel</term> <note>The "heel" refers to the bird's hind claw, or hallux. In larks, this claw is unusually long and straight, which helps them walk and run across open, sandy ground.</note>. It has a single Note, like that of our Sky-Lark in Winter; at which time, and in cold Weather only, they appear in *Virginia* and *Carolina*. They come from the North in great Flights, and return early in the Spring. From their near resemblance to our Sky-Lark, I conceive they mount up and sing as ours do; but as they appear here only in Winter, I cannot determine it. They frequent the Sand-Hills upon the Sea-shore of *Carolina*, and there feed on these Oats, which they find scattered on the Sands.

## YELLOW STAR-OF-BETHLEHEM <note>original: *Ornithogalum luteum*. Currently known to botanists as *Gagea lutea*, this is a spring-flowering plant in the lily family. Catesby often included European plants in his plates if he felt they provided a beautiful or appropriate backdrop for his American birds.</note>

<column-break/>

## THE LARGE LARK <note>original: *Alauda Magna*. This bird is now known as the Eastern Meadowlark (*Sturnella magna*). Although Catesby classified it as a "Lark" because of its terrestrial habits and long hind claw, it is actually a member of the Icterid family, which includes blackbirds and orioles.</note>

### The Meadowlark.

This bird is considerably larger than the common Lark of Europe. Its most striking feature is its bright yellow throat and breast, which is marked by a prominent black <term>gorget</term> <note>A "gorget" refers to a patch of color on the throat or upper chest, named after the piece of armor that protected a knight's throat.</note> or crescent. The crown of the head is divided by a pale stripe, and a similar yellow stripe runs above the eye. The feathers on its back and wings are a complex mosaic of brown, black, and buff, providing excellent camouflage when the bird is nestled in the tall grass of meadows and fields. 

Like the European larks Catesby was familiar with, this bird possesses a long <term>heel</term> <note>Referring to the hallux or hind claw, which is elongated in ground-dwelling birds to provide stability while walking or running through grass.</note>. However, unlike the Sky-Lark, it rarely sings while soaring high in the air, preferring instead to whistle its clear, flute-like melody from a fence post or a low bush. It is a permanent resident in the warmer climates of *Virginia* and *Carolina*, where it frequents open pastures and savannas.

<summary>This plate depicts the Eastern Meadowlark (misidentified as a large lark) alongside the Yellow Star-of-Bethlehem, highlighting the bird's distinctive yellow breast and black V-shaped marking.</summary>

<keywords>Eastern Meadowlark, Alauda Magna, Yellow Star-of-Bethlehem, Ornithogalum luteum, Mark Catesby, North American birds, botanical illustration, Virginia, Carolina</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Mark Catesby’s Natural History of Carolina, Florida, and the Bahama Islands. Following the description of the Shore Lark (Horned Lark) and Sea Oats on page 32, Catesby here presents the Eastern Meadowlark and the Yellow Star-of-Bethlehem. A circular library stamp in the top left margin reads: "EX LIBRIS MILDRED".</meta>

-># THE GREAT LARK <note>original: *ALAUDA MAGNA*</note><-

### The large Lark.

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'T' within a square frame, depicting a bird perched on a branch among foliage.</image-desc>THIS Bird weighs three ounces and a quarter. The Bill is straight, sharp, and somewhat flat towards the End. Between the Eye and the Nostril is a yellow Spot. The Crown of the Head is brown, with a dusky white <term>list</term> <note>an archaic term for a stripe or band</note> running from the Bill along the middle of it. A black stripe, about an inch long, extends downwards from the Eye. The sides of the Head are light gray. The Wings and upper part of the Body are of a Partridge-colour <note>referring to a mottled brown and buff camouflage pattern</note>. The Breast has a large black Mark, in form of a horse-shoe; except which, the Throat and all the under part of the Body is yellow. It has a jetting Motion with its Tail, sitting on the tops of small Trees and Bushes in the manner of our Bunting; and, in the Spring, sings musically, though not many Notes. They feed mostly on the Ground on the seed of Grasses: Their flesh is good Meat. They inhabit *Carolina*, *Virginia* and most of the Northern Continent of *America*.

## Small yellow Star-of-Bethlehem with smooth grassy leaves <note>original Latin: *Ornithogalum luteum parvum folijs gramineis glabris*</note>.

### THE LITTLE YELLOW STAR-FLOWER.

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'T' within a square frame with simple floral and line patterns.</image-desc>THIS Plant grows usually not above five inches in height, producing many grassy leaves, from which rises a slender stalk bearing a yellow Star-like <term>pentapetalous</term> <note>having five petals</note> Flower. It has five <term>Stamina</term> <note>the pollen-producing parts of a flower</note>, every leaf of the flower having one growing opposite to it. The Flower is succeeded by a small long <term>capsula</term> <note>a seed pod or case</note>, containing many little black Seeds. This plant grows plentifully in most of the open Pasture lands in *Carolina* and *Virginia*, where these Larks most frequent and feed on the Seed of it.

<column-break/>

### The Large Lark <note>original French: *Grande Alloüette*</note>.

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'C' within a square frame featuring foliage and a small figure or bird in profile.</image-desc>THIS Bird weighs three ounces and a quarter. Its beak is straight, sharp, and a little flattened towards the tip. Between the eye and the nostril, it has a yellow spot. The top of its head is brown, divided by a dull white stripe which begins at the beak. A black stripe descends from its eye to about an inch lower along the neck. The sides of its head are a light gray. Its wings and the upper part of its body are the color of a Partridge. It has on its breast a large black mark in the shape of a horseshoe: apart from this, its throat and the entire underside of its body are yellow. It has a very brisk up-and-down motion in its tail. When it is still, it perches on the tops of small trees or bushes much like our <term>Whinchat</term> <note>original: *Traquet*. This is a small European songbird Catesby uses as a comparison for his readers</note>. It sings harmoniously in the Spring, although its song consists of few notes. These birds feed almost exclusively on the seeds of grasses that they find on the ground. Their flesh is good to eat. They are found in *Virginia* and *Carolina* and in almost the entire Northern Continent of *America*.

### The Yellow Star-of-Bethlehem <note>original: *L'Ornithogalum jaune*</note>.

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'C' within a square frame with ornate floral scrollwork.</image-desc>THIS Plant does not ordinarily rise more than five inches in height. It produces several leaves similar to those of grass. From the middle of these leaves, it pushes out a very thin stem which supports a yellow flower with five petals. When the flower has passed, it is succeeded by a small long capsule which contains several small black seeds. This plant grows in great abundance in most of the open pastures of *Carolina* and *Virginia*. The Larks described above frequent these pastures often and feed on these seeds.

<sig>I</sig>

<vocab>Alauda Magna (Great Lark), Lark, Alloüette (Lark), Ornithogalum (Star-of-Bethlehem), Star-Flower, Carolina, Virginia, America, pentapetalous (five-petaled), capsula (seed capsule)</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the Eastern Meadowlark (referred to as the "Large Lark") and the Yellow Star-of-Bethlehem plant found in colonial Carolina and Virginia. Catesby details the bird's distinctive yellow breast with its black "horseshoe" mark and notes that it feeds on the seeds of the accompanying plant.</summary>

## BLACK SPARROW WITH RED EYES. <note>original Latin: *PASSER NIGER, OCULIS RUBRIS*</note>

### The Towhee Bird. <note>now known as the Eastern Towhee (*Pipilo erythrophthalmus*)</note>

THIS Bird is about the size of, or rather bigger than a Lark: The Bill black and thick: The <term>iris</term> <note>the colored part of the eye surrounding the pupil</note> of the Eye red: The Head, Neck, Breast, Back, and Tail, black; as are the Wings, with the larger Quill-Feathers edged with white. The lower part of the Breast and Belly white; which, on each side, is of a muddy red, extending along its Wings. The Legs and Feet are brown.

The Hen is brown, with a <term>tincture</term> <note>a slight trace or tint</note> of red on her Breast. It is a solitary Bird; and one seldom sees them but in Pairs. They breed and abide all the Year in *Carolina* in the shadiest Woods.

---

## BROWN SPARROW. <note>original Latin: *PASSER FUSCA*</note>

### The Cowpen Bird. <note>now known as the Brown-headed Cowbird (*Molothrus ater*)</note>

THIS Bird is entirely brown, the Back being darkest, and the Breast and Belly the lightest part of it. In Winter they associate with the Red-winged Starling <note>the Red-winged Blackbird</note> and Purple Jack-daw <note>the Common Grackle</note> in <term>Flights</term> <note>large flocks</note>. They delight much to feed in the Pens of Cattle, which has given them their Name. Not having seen any of them in Summer, I believe they are Birds of Passage <note>migratory birds</note>. They inhabit *Virginia* and *Carolina*.

---

## Black poplar with very large leaves and buds yielding a most fragrant balsam. <note>original Latin: *Populus nigra folio maximo gemmis Balsamum odoratissimum fundentibus*</note>

### The BLACK POPLAR of Carolina. <note>likely the Eastern Cottonwood (*Populus deltoides*)</note>

THIS Tree grows only near Rivers, above the inhabited parts of *Carolina*. They are large and very tall. In April, at which time only I saw them, they had dropt their Seeds; which, by the Remains, I could only perceive to hang in Clusters, with a Cotton-like Consistence covering them. Upon the large swelling Buds of this Tree sticks a very Odoriferous <note>sweet-smelling</note> Balsam. The leaves are indented about the edges, and very broad, resembling in shape the Black Poplar, described by <term>Parkinson</term> <note>John Parkinson (1567–1650), a celebrated English botanist and herbalist to King Charles I</note>.

<column-break/>

### Black sparrow with red eyes

THIS Bird is nearly the size of a Lark, or even a little larger. Its beak is black and thick; the Iris of its eyes red; its head, its neck, its breast, its back and its tail black; its wings are also black, except the large feathers which are bordered with white. The underside of its breast and its belly are white in the middle, and on each side under the wings, of a dark red. Its legs and its feet are brown.

The Female is brown, with a light tint of red on the breast. This Bird is solitary. One hardly sees them except in pairs. It remains throughout the year in *Carolina* in the thickest Woods.

---

### Brown sparrow.

THIS Bird is entirely brown: Its back is of a darker brown: Its breast and its belly of a lighter brown than the rest. In Winter it associates and forms bands with the Red-winged Starling, and the Jack-daw. It enjoys itself very much and feeds in the Cattle Pens, and it is from there that it has taken its English name. I have seen none of them in Summer: Thus I believe that it is a Bird of Passage. It is found in *Virginia* and in *Carolina*.

---

### Black Poplar of Carolina.

THESE trees grow only near Rivers above the part of *Carolina* which is inhabited. They are very tall, and their branches extend far. In the month of April (it is the only time when I saw them), the harvest of their seeds had already been made. I judged by what remained of them, that they were arranged in Clusters, and wrapped in a cottony substance. A very fragrant Balsam is found attached to the largest buds of this tree. Its leaves are indented, very large, and similar in shape to those of the Black Poplar described by Parkinson.

### The Towhee Bird. <note>now known as the Eastern Towhee (*Pipilo erythrophthalmus*), named for its distinctive "to-whee" call</note>

THIS Bird is about the size of, or rather bigger than a Lark: The Bill is black and thick: The <term>iris</term> <note>the colored part of the eye surrounding the pupil</note> of the Eye is red: The Head, Neck, Breast, Back, and Tail are black; as are the Wings, with the larger Quill-Feathers edged with white. The lower part of the Breast and Belly are white; which, on each side, is of a muddy red, extending along its Wings. The Legs and Feet are brown.

The female <note>original: "Hen"</note> is brown, with a <term>tincture</term> <note>a slight trace or tint</note> of red on her Breast. It is a solitary Bird; and one seldom sees them but in Pairs. They breed and remain all the Year in *Carolina* in the shadiest Woods.
