# THE LITTLE SPARROW <note>original Latin: *Passerculus*</note>

### The little Sparrow.

THIS Bird is entirely of a brown Colour; less than our Hedge-Sparrow <note>the European Dunnock, *Prunella modularis*</note>, but partaking much of the Nature of it.

They are not numerous, being usually seen single, hopping under Bushes. They feed on Insects, and are seen most common near Houses in *Virginia* and *Carolina*, where they breed and abide the whole Year.

### The Carolina Bindweed with narrow arrow-shaped leaves, a very large purple flower, and a thick root <note>original Latin: *Convolvulus Caroliniensis angusto sagittato folio, flore amplissimo purpureo, radice crassa*</note>

### THE PURPLE BIND-WEED of Carolina.

THE Flower of this *Bindweed* <term>Convolvulus<span>A genus of climbing or creeping vines in the morning glory family.</span></term> is of a reddish purple, and of the size and shape of common white Bind-weed. They blow <note>an archaic term for blooming</note> in *June*. The Leaves are shaped like the head of an Arrow. Colonel *Moore* <note>Colonel James Moore, a prominent colonial official and governor in Carolina</note>, a Gentleman of good Reputation in *Carolina*, told me, that he has seen an Indian <note>a historical reference to the Indigenous peoples of the Southeastern Woodlands</note> daub himself with the Juice of this Plant; immediately after which, he handled a Rattle-Snake with his naked Hands without receiving any harm from it, though thought to be the most venomous of the Snake-kind: And I have heard several others affirm, that they have seen the Indians use a Plant to guard themselves against the venom of this sort of Snake; but they were not Observers nice <note>here meaning "precise" or "detailed"</note> enough to inform me what kind it was of.

<column-break/>

### Little Sparrow.

THIS Bird is entirely brown. It is smaller than our Hedge-Sparrow; but otherwise it resembles it greatly. These Sparrows are not in great number. One almost always sees them alone, hopping under the bushes. They feed on Insects, & stay close to houses. They produce their young, & stay all year in Virginia & Carolina.

### Purple Bindweed of Carolina.

THE flower of this Bindweed is of a Purple pulling towards Red, of the size & the form of that of the ordinary white Bindweed. It flowers in the month of June. Its leaves are made like the point of an arrow. A very highly esteemed Gentleman in Carolina, named Colonel *Moore*, assured me that he had seen an Indian who, after having rubbed himself with the juice of this Plant, touched a rattlesnake with bare hands, without receiving any discomfort from it, although this snake is considered to be the most venomous of all. I have also heard several People say that the Indians use the juice of a Plant to protect themselves from the venom of this snake; but these People were not capable of specifying to me the one that had this virtue.

<vocab>Sparrow, Sparrow, Bind-weed, Bind-weed, Bind-weed, Carolina, Virginia, Colonel Moore, Indian, Rattle-Snake</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the physical characteristics and habits of a small brown sparrow (likely the Chipping Sparrow) and the Purple Bindweed. It includes a notable colonial anecdote regarding an Indigenous practice of using the plant's juice as a protective coating when handling rattlesnakes.</summary>

# THE SNOW SPARROW <note>original Latin: *Passer Nivalis*</note>

### The Snow-bird.
THE Bill of this Bird is white: The Breast and Belly white. All the rest of the Body black; but in some places dusky, inclining to Lead-Colour. In *Virginia* and *Carolina* they appear only in Winter: and in Snow they appear most. In Summer none are seen. Whether they retire and breed in the North (which is most probable) or where they go, when they leave these Countries in the Spring, is to me unknown.

<column-break/>

### The Snow Sparrow <note>original French: *Moineau de neige*</note>
ITS bill, its breast, and its belly are white: All the rest of its body is black, except for some places which are almost the color of lead. One only sees these birds in *Virginia* and *Carolina* during the Winter, and almost always upon the Snow. They disappear absolutely in Summer. I am ignorant if they then retire toward the North to have their young; which however seems most probable to me.

---

## *Virginian Broom-rape with a five-petaled nodding flower* <note>original Latin: *Orobanche Virginiana flore pentapetalo cernuo*</note> Pluk: Alma. <note>referring to Leonard Plukenet's *Almagestum Botanicum*, a significant 17th-century botanical catalog</note>

### BROOM-RAPE.
THIS Plant rises to the height of eight or ten inches; is of a Flesh-Colour; the stalks thinly beset with small narrow sharp pointed Leaves. The Flowers are <term>monopetalous<span>A flower made of a single petal, or where the petals are fused into a one-piece tube or shape.</span></term>, but deeply furrow'd from the stalk to the Top of the Flower, where it divides into several sections. Within the Flower is an oval <term>canulated<span>Grooved or tube-like in shape, similar to a fluted column.</span></term> <term>capsula<span>A dry fruit or seed pod that releases seeds when it matures.</span></term>, of the size of a Hazel-Nut, containing very small Seeds like Dust. This *Capsula* is surrounded with many yellow <term>Stamina<span>The male, pollen-producing parts of a flower.</span></term>.

<column-break/>

### Broom-rape of Virginia <note>original French: *Orobanche de la Virginie*</note>
THIS Plant rises to the height of eight or ten inches. It is of a flesh-color. Its stems are furnished here and there with small narrow leaves, ending in very sharp points. Its flowers are single-petaled, but deeply furrowed from the stem to the top of the flower, where it divides into several sections. There is inside the flower an oval, fluted capsule, of the size of a Hazelnut: It contains several seeds as fine as dust. This capsule is surrounded by yellow stamens.

<summary>This page describes the migration habits of the Snow-bird (Dark-eyed Junco) and the physical characteristics of a Virginian Broom-rape plant, noting its unique seed pod and flower structure.</summary>
<keywords>Snow-bird, Snow Sparrow, Virginia, Carolina, Broom-rape, Orobanche, botany, ornithology, migration, Leonard Plukenet</keywords>

P. 37
K

# THE TWO-COLORED LITTLE BAHAMA SPARROW. <note>original Latin: *Passerculus bicolor bahamiensis*</note>

## The Bahama Sparrow.

THIS is about the size of a Canary Bird. The Head, Neck, and Breast are black: All the other Parts of it of a dirty green Colour. It is the commonest little Bird I observed in the Woods of the Bahama Islands. It uses to perch on the top of a Bush and sing, repeating one set Tune in manner of our **Chaffinch**. <note>The Chaffinch (*Fringilla coelebs*) is a common European songbird known for its repetitive, melodic song.</note>

<column-break/>

## The Bahama Sparrow. <note>original French: *Moineau de Bahama*</note>

It is about the size of a **Canary**. <note>original: *Sérin de Canaries*</note> Its head, its neck, and its breast are black; all the rest of its body is of a dull green. It is the most common small bird of those I observed in the woods of the Bahama Islands. It usually perches on the top of a bush, where it sings by always repeating exactly the same tune, as does our **Chaffinch**. <note>original: *Pinçon*</note>

---

### The five-leaved Trumpet Tree with a larger rose-colored flower and flat pods. <note>original Latin: *Bignonia arbor pentaphylla flore roseo majore filiquis planis*</note> <note>Plumier's Catalog. This refers to the work of Charles Plumier, a French botanist who documented Caribbean flora.</note>

#### BIGNONIA. <note>The genus *Bignonia* was named in honor of Jean-Paul Bignon, librarian to Louis XIV.</note>

THIS Shrub usually rises to the height of about ten Feet. From the larger Branches shoots forth long slender Stalks, at the end of every of which are five leaves fixed on foot-stalks an inch long. Its flower is <term>monopetalous</term> <note>A flower consisting of a single petal or having all petals fused into a single continuous shape.</note>, of a Rose-colour, and somewhat Bell-shaped, though the margin is deeply divided into five or six Sections, to which succeed Pods of five Inches long, hanging in Clusters, and containing within them small brown Beans.

<column-break/>

#### Bignonia.

THIS Plant rises as a shrub to the height of about ten feet. The thick branches sprout long, slender stems, which carry at their extremity five leaves attached by <term>pedicles</term> <note>Small stalks that support a single leaf or flower.</note> an inch long. Its flower is <term>monopetalous</term>. It has roughly the shape of a bell; but its edges are deeply cut into five or six sections. When the flower has passed, it is succeeded by pods five inches long, attached in clusters: they contain small brown peas.

<summary>This page describes the appearance and habits of the Bahama Sparrow and provides a botanical description of the Five-leaved Trumpet Tree, noting its height, leaf structure, and bell-shaped flowers.</summary>

<keywords>Bahama Sparrow, Passerculus bicolor, Bignonia, Trumpet Tree, Caribbean flora, ornithology, 18th-century botany, Mark Catesby, Bahama Islands</keywords>

# THE RED GROSBEAK. <note>original Latin: *Coccothraustes ruber*</note>

## The red Bird.

<image-desc>Decorative ornamental drop cap 'I' depicting a landscape with a building and trees.</image-desc>

IN Bigness it equals if not exceeds the **Sky-Lark**. <note>The Sky-Lark (*Alauda arvensis*) was a common point of size comparison for European readers.</note> The Bill is of a pale red, very thick and strong. A black List <note>A "list" refers to a stripe or border.</note> encompasses the Basis of it. The Head is adorned with a towering Crest, which it raises and falls at Pleasure. Except the Black round the Basis of the Bill, the whole Bird is scarlet, though the Back and Tail have least Lustre, being darker and of a more cloudy red.

The Hen is brown; yet has a tincture of red on her Wings, Bill and other parts. They often sing in Cages as well as the Cocks. These Birds are common in all parts of America, from New-England to the Capes of Florida, and probably much more South. They are seldom seen above three or four together. They have a very great Strength with their Bill, with which they will break the hardest Grain of **Maiz** <note>Maize, or Indian corn.</note> with much facility. It is a hardy and familiar Bird. They are frequently brought from Virginia and other parts of North America for their Beauty and agreeable Singing, they having some Notes not unlike our Nightingale, which in England seems to have caused its Name of the **Virginia-Nightingale**, though in those Countries they call it the Red-Bird.

---

# THE WHITE VIRGINIAN WALNUT. <note>original Latin: *Nux Juglans alba Virginiensis*. This entry cites "Park. Theat. 1414," referring to John Parkinson’s botanical encyclopedia *Theatrum Botanicum*, published in 1640.</note>

## THE HICCORY TREE.

<image-desc>Decorative ornamental drop cap 'T' containing a scene with a tree.</image-desc>

THIS is usually a tall Tree, and often grows to a large Bulk, the Body being from two to three Feet Diameter. The Leaves are serrated <note>Saw-toothed along the edges.</note>, narrower and sharper pointed than the Walnut, but in Manner of growing on foot-stalks, like it. The Nuts are inclosed in like manner with the Walnut, with an outer and inner Shell. In October, at which time they are ripe, the outer Shell opens and divides in Quarters, disclosing the Nut, the shell of which is thick, not easily broke but with a Hammer. The Kernel is sweet and well tasted, from which the **Indians** <note>Referring to the Indigenous peoples of the region.</note> draw a wholesome and pleasant Oil, storing them up for their Winter-Provision. The Hogs and many wild Animals receive great Benefit from them. The Wood is coarse-grained; yet of much use for many things belonging to Agriculture. Of the **Saplings** <note>Young trees.</note> or young Trees are made the best Hoops for Tobacco, Rice and Tar-Barrels: And for the Fire no Wood in the Northern parts of America is in so much Request. The Bark is deeply furrowed.

---

# THE CAROLINA WALNUT WITH VERY SMALL FRUIT AND A SMOOTH SHELL. <note>original Latin: *Nux Juglans Carolinensis fructu minimo putamine levi*</note>

## THE PIGNUT.

<image-desc>Decorative ornamental drop cap 'T' with scrollwork designs.</image-desc>

THE Branches of this Tree spread more, are smaller, and the Leaves not so broad as the Hiccory; nor is the Bark so wrinkled. The Nuts are not above one fourth part so big as those of the Hiccory, having both the inner and outer shell very thin; so that they may easily be broke with one’s Fingers. The kernels are sweet; but being small, and covered with a very bitter skin, makes them useless, except for Squirrels and other Wild Creatures.

Another Walnut remains to be observed, which I never saw but in Virginia and is there called the **white Walnut**. <note>Likely referring to the Butternut (*Juglans cinerea*).</note> The Tree is usually small; the Bark and Grain of the Wood very White: The Nut is about the size or rather less than the black Walnut, of an oval form, the outermost shell being rough.

<column-break/>

## The Cardinal. <note>original French: *Le Cardinal*</note>

<image-desc>Decorative ornamental drop cap 'C' with a figure inside.</image-desc>

THIS Bird equals or even surpasses in size the common **Lark**. <note>original French: *l’Alloüette commune*</note> Its beak is of a pale red, very thick and very strong; a black stripe surrounds the base. Its head is adorned with a large crest that it raises and lowers as it wishes. Except for the black stripe at the base of its beak, its entire body is scarlet; although the back and the tail have less brilliance than the rest, because they are of a deeper and darker red.

The Female is brown; however, she has some shades of red in the color of her wings, her beak, and the rest of her body. In a cage she often sings as well as the Male. These birds are common in all parts of America, from New England to the Cape of Florida, and likely even further towards the South. One hardly ever sees more than three or four together. They have great strength in the beak, with which they easily break the hardest grains of **Maize**. <note>original French: *Maiz*</note> They are bold and familiar. A great number are brought from Virginia and other places in the North of America because of their beauty and their pleasant warbling; they have in their song some tones approaching those of the Nightingale. That is why they have been named in England, **Virginia Nightingales**, <note>original French: *Rossignols de la Virginie*</note> though in that country they are called **Red Birds**. <note>original French: *Oiseaux rouges*</note>

---

## White Walnut, called Hickory in Virginia. <note>original French: *Noyer blanc, nommé Hiccori à la Virginie*</note>

<image-desc>Decorative ornamental drop cap 'C' similar to previous.</image-desc>

THIS tree is usually quite tall; and often it becomes very large, and pushes out many very extended branches. Its trunk is sometimes up to two or three feet in diameter. Its leaves are indented, narrower and more pointed than those of the European Walnut; but the fruits grow and are attached in the same manner as common Walnuts. They are likewise enveloped in a double shell. In the month of October, which is the time when its fruits are ripe, the outer bark opens, divides into quarters, and reveals the Nut, whose shell is thick, and which can hardly be broken except with a hammer. The inside is sweet and of good taste. The **Indians** <note>original French: *Les Indiens*</note> draw a very healthy and very pleasant oil from them, and make a provision of them for their winter. These fruits are of great help to the Pigs and several species of wild beasts. The wood of this tree has a coarse grain; however, it is of very great use for several things in agriculture. From the young trees they make excellent hoops for the barrels where they put Tobacco, Rice, and Tar; and for burning there is no better wood in all the North of America. Its bark is extremely wrinkled.

---

## Carolina Walnut. <note>original French: *Noyer de la Caroline*</note>

<image-desc>Decorative ornamental drop cap 'L' with landscape or scrollwork.</image-desc>

THE branches of this tree spread more and are more slender; its leaves are narrower than those of the White Walnut of Virginia, nor is its bark so wrinkled. Its fruits are only a quarter of the size of the others, and their shells are very thin, so that they can easily be broken with the fingers. The flesh is sweet; but as there is little of it, and it is covered with a very bitter skin, there are only Squirrels and some other wild animals that make use of them.

I must also observe another species of Walnut, which I have never seen except in Virginia, which is called in that country the **White Walnut**. <note>original French: *Noyer blanc*</note> This tree is usually small; the bark and the wood are very white. The fruit is roughly the size of that of the Black Walnut, of an oval shape; its outer envelope is rough.

# THE RED GROSBEAK. <note>original Latin: *Coccothraustes ruber*</note>

## The Red Bird.

<image-desc>Decorative ornamental drop cap 'I' depicting a landscape with a building and trees.</image-desc>

IN size it equals, if not exceeds, the **Sky-Lark**. <note>The Sky-Lark (*Alauda arvensis*) was a standard size reference for European readers of the 18th century.</note> The beak is of a pale red, very thick and strong. A black band <note>original: "list," an archaic term for a stripe or border.</note> encompasses the base of it. The head is adorned with a towering crest, which it raises and falls at pleasure. Except for the black around the base of the beak, the whole bird is scarlet, though the back and tail have the least luster, being darker and of a more cloudy red.

The female <note>original: "hen"</note> is brown, yet has a tint of red on her wings, beak, and other parts. They often sing in cages as well as the males <note>original: "cocks"</note>. These birds are common in all parts of America, from New England to the Capes of Florida, and probably much further south. They are seldom seen in groups of more than three or four together. They possess very great strength in their beaks, with which they can break the hardest grain of **Maize** <note>Indian corn.</note> with great ease. It is a hardy and familiar bird. They are frequently brought from Virginia and other parts of North America for their beauty and agreeable singing; they have some notes not unlike our Nightingale, which in England seems to have caused it to be named the **Virginia-Nightingale**, though in those countries they call it the Red-Bird.

---

# THE WHITE VIRGINIAN WALNUT. <note>original Latin: *Nux Juglans alba Virginiensis*. This species is part of the Hickory family. The text cites "Park. Theat." referring to John Parkinson’s *Theatrum Botanicum* (1640), a massive herbal encyclopedia.</note>

<meta>This page continues the catalog of North American flora and fauna by Mark Catesby, following the previous entry on the Red Grosbeak (Northern Cardinal). Plate 39 documents the Blue Grosbeak and the Sweetbay Magnolia, illustrating the symbiotic beauty of these Carolinian species.</meta>

# BLUE GROSBEAK.
<note>original Latin: *COCCOTHRAUSTES CÆRULEA*. In modern taxonomy, this bird is known as *Passerina caerulea*.</note>

## The blue Grosbeak.
<note>original: "blew," an archaic spelling of blue.</note>

A narrow black strip surrounds the base of the beak and joins to the eyes: The head and whole body, except the tail and part of the wings, are a deep blue. Below the shoulder of the wing are a few red feathers: The lower part of the wing and tail are brown, mixed with green: The legs and feet are a dusky black.

The female is dark brown all over, with a very small mixture of blue. It is a very uncommon and solitary bird, seen only in pairs. They have only one single note, and do not appear in winter. I have not seen any of these birds in any part of *America* except *Carolina*.

<column-break/>

## Blue Grosbeak.
<note>original French: *Gros-Bec bleu*.</note>

A very narrow black line surrounds the base of its beak and joins its eyes. Its head and its whole body, except its tail and a part of its wings, are of a dark blue. Below the shoulder of the wing there are some red feathers. All the bottom of its wings and its tail are brown, with a shade of green. Its legs and its feet are of an obscure black.

The *female* is entirely of a dark brown, mixed with a little blue. This bird is very rare and very solitary. One never sees more than two together, namely the male and the female. Its song consists of only a single note. It does not appear at all in winter. I have never seen it in any part of *America* except Carolina.

---

->*Magnolia with laurel-like leaves, white underneath.*<-
<note>original Latin: *Magnolia Lauri folio, ſubtus albicante*. This refers to the Sweetbay Magnolia, now classified as *Magnolia virginiana*.</note>

---

### THE SWEET FLOWERING BAY.

This is a small tree, usually growing sixteen feet high; the wood is white and spongy, covered with a white bark. The leaves are in shape like those of the common bay, but of a pale green, having their back-sides white: In May they begin to blossom, continuing most part of the summer to perfume the woods with their fragrant flowers, which are white, made up of six <term>petals</term><note>The original uses the Latin "Petala." These are the individual leaves of the flower's corolla.</note>, having a rough conical <term>style</term><note>The original uses the Latin "Stylus," referring to the pollen-receiving part of the flower.</note>, or the beginning of the fruit: which, when the petals fall, increases to the size and shape of a large walnut, thick set with knots or ridges; from each of which, when the fruit is ripe, flat seeds are discharged the size of kidney beans, having a kernel within a thin shell, covered with a red skin. These red seeds, when discharged from their cells, do not fall to the ground, but are supported by small white threads about two inches long. The fruit at first is green; when ripe, red; and when declining, it turns brown: They grow naturally in moist places, and often in shallow water; and what is extraordinary, when they are moved to high dry ground, they become more regular and handsomer trees, and are more prolific of flowers and fruit: They usually lose their leaves in winter, unless it be moderate.

<column-break/>

### Laurel with fragrant flowers.
<note>original French: *Laurier au Fleurs odorantes*.</note>

This tree hardly rises higher than sixteen feet. Its wood is white and spongy; its bark white: Its leaves have the shape of those of the common laurel, but they are of a pale green on top, and white underneath. It begins to flower in the month of May, and continues during almost all the summer to perfume the woods with the pleasant odor of its flowers. They are white, composed of six leaves [petals], in the middle of which is a conical pistil which is the beginning of the fruit. When its petals have fallen, the pistil increases to the size of a large nut. It is all covered with knots, or small eminences, which open when the fruit is ripe, and let fall flat seeds of the size of a small bean. They contain a kernel enclosed in a very thin shell, covered with a red skin. When these seeds come out of their cells, they do not fall to the ground, but they remain suspended by white threads about two inches long. The fruits are first green; then red when they are ripe; and finally they become brown in their decline. This tree grows on its own in a humid soil, and often in shallow waters: And what is surprising, is that if one transplants it into a dry and elevated terrain, the tree becomes more beautiful and better formed, and produces more flowers and fruits. It loses its leaves in winter unless the cold is very moderate.

---

This beautiful flowering tree is a native both of *Virginia* and *Carolina*, and is growing at Mr. *Fairchild’s* in *Hoxton*, and at Mr. *Collinson’s* at *Peckham*, where it has for some years past produced its fragrant blossoms, requiring no protection from the cold of our severest winters.
<note>Thomas Fairchild and Peter Collinson were famous English botanists and gardeners of the 18th century who helped introduce North American plants to Europe.</note>

This beautiful tree, which produces such pleasant flowers, is originally from *Carolina* and *Virginia*. One sees them in the garden of Mr. *Fairchild* at *Hoxton*, and in that of Mr. *Collinson* at *Peckham*, where these trees have flowered regularly for several years, without being obliged to defend them against the most rigorous winters.

<meta>This page continues Mark Catesby’s documentation of the flora and fauna of the Bahama Islands. Having previously described the birds and plants of Carolina, Catesby here focuses on the biodiversity of islands like Providence and Eleuthera, specifically the relationship between the Purple Grosbeak and the toxic Poison-wood tree.</meta>

## PURPLE GROSBEAK.
<note>original Latin: *COCCOTHRAUSTES PURPUREA*. This bird is now identified as the Greater Antillean Bullfinch (*Loxigilla violacea*), a species common to the Bahamas.</note>

### The Purple Grosbeak.

THIS Bird is of the Size of a Sparrow. Over the Eyes, the Throat, and at the vent <note>original: "Vent"; the opening for digestive and reproductive tracts in birds</note> under the Tail, are Spots of red: All the rest of the Body is entirely of a deep purple-colour. The Hen <note>The female bird.</note> is all over brown, but has the like red Spots as the Cock. These Birds are Natives of many of the Bahama Islands.

---

## *Poison-wood with winged leaves and scattered purple pear-shaped fruit.*
<note>original Latin: *Toxicodendron folijs alatis fructu purpureo Pyri formi ſparso*. This is the *Metopium toxiferum*, commonly known as the Florida Poisonwood.</note>

### THE POISON-WOOD.

THIS is generally but a small Tree; has a light coloured smooth bark. Its leaves are winged, the middle rib seven or eight inches long, with pairs of leaflets <term>Pinnæ|The individual leaflets of a compound wing-like leaf</term> one against another on inch-long foot stalks. The fruit hang in bunches; are shaped like a Pear, of a purple-colour, covering an oblong hard Stone.

From the trunk of this Tree distills a Liquid black as Ink, which the Inhabitants say is Poison. <note>The sap contains urushiol, the same irritant found in poison ivy, which causes severe skin blistering.</note> Birds feed on the Berries, particularly this Grosbeak on the mucilage that covers the Stone. It grows usually on Rocks in Providence, Eleuthera <note>original: "Ilathera," an archaic spelling of the Bahamian island.</note> and other of the Bahama Islands.

<column-break/>

### Purple Grosbeak.
<note>original French: *Gros bec violet*.</note>

THIS Bird is the size of a Sparrow. Above the eyes, on the throat, and toward the vent under the tail, it has red spots. All the rest of its body is of a dark Violet. The Female is brown; except for the red spots she has in the same places as the Male. These Birds are found in several of the Bahama Islands.

---

### Poison-wood.
<note>original French: *Bois Empoisonné*.</note>

THIS tree is usually quite small. Its bark is smooth, and of a light color. Its leaves are arranged in pairs, and attached by stalks of one inch, upon ribs of seven or eight inches in length. Its fruits form Clusters: They have the shape of a purple Pear, which encloses a very hard stone. From the Trunk of this tree there distills a liquor black as ink: The Inhabitants say that it is poisonous. The Birds, and especially the Grosbeak, feed on its fruits, that is to say, on the Pulp that covers the stone. This tree grows usually on Rocks on the Island of Providence, on that of Eleuthera, and several others of those in the Bahamas.

<summary>This page describes the Purple Grosbeak (Greater Antillean Bullfinch) and the Poison-wood tree, noting the bird's reliance on the tree's fruit despite the tree's toxic sap.</summary>
<keywords>Purple Grosbeak, Poison-wood, Metopium toxiferum, Bahama Islands, Eleuthera, Providence, ornithology, botany, toxicology</keywords>

# PURPLE GROSBEAK
<note>original Latin: *Coccothraustes purpurea*. This bird is now identified by modern naturalists as the Greater Antillean Bullfinch (*Loxigilla violacea*), a species common to the Bahamas and surrounding islands.</note>

## The Purple Grosbeak.

THIS Bird is of the Size of a Sparrow. Over the Eyes, the Throat, and at the vent <note>The vent is the opening for the bird's digestive and reproductive tracts; in this species, these feathers are a distinct rufous or red color.</note> under the Tail, are Spots of red: All the rest of the Body is entirely of a deep purple-colour. The Hen <note>The female bird.</note> is all over brown, but has the like red Spots as the Cock <note>The male bird.</note>. These Birds are Natives of many of the Bahama Islands.

---

# POISON-WOOD WITH WINGED LEAVES
<note>original Latin: *Toxicodendron folijs alatis &c.* Literally: "Poison-tree with winged leaves, etc." This is the *Metopium toxiferum*, commonly known today as the Florida Poisonwood.</note>

## THE POISON-WOOD.

THIS is generally but a small Tree; has a light coloured smooth bark. Its leaves are winged, the middle rib seven or eight inches long, with pairs of leaflets <term>Pinnæ|The individual leaflets of a compound leaf, arranged like the feathers of a wing.</term> one against another on inch-long foot stalks. The fruit hang in bunches; are shaped like a Pear, of a purple-colour, covering an oblong hard Stone.

From the trunk of this Tree distills a Liquid black as Ink, which the Inhabitants say is Poison. <note>The tree's sap contains urushiol, the same powerful irritant found in poison ivy, which causes severe skin blistering and rashes upon contact.</note> Birds feed on the Berries, particularly this Grosbeak on the mucilage <note>A thick, gluey substance produced by the plant, often surrounding the seed.</note> that covers the Stone. It grows usually on Rocks in Providence, Eleuthera, and other Bahama Islands.

<summary>This page illustrates the Purple Grosbeak (Greater Antillean Bullfinch) and the Poison-wood tree, highlighting how the bird safely consumes the fruit of a plant that is highly toxic to humans.</summary>
<keywords>Purple Grosbeak, Greater Antillean Bullfinch, Poison-wood, Metopium toxiferum, Bahama Islands, Mark Catesby, natural history, botany, ornithology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Mark Catesby’s survey of North American wildlife, shifting focus from the tropical species of the Bahamas (such as the Purple Grosbeak and Poison-wood seen on the previous page) back to the temperate flora and fauna of the American Southeast, specifically documenting the Purple Finch and the Tupelo tree.</meta>

# THE PURPLE FINCH
<note>original Latin: *Fringilla purpurea*. This bird is now scientifically classified as *Haemorhous purpureus*. It is known for the male's vibrant "raspberry-red" plumage, which Catesby describes as purple.</note>

## The Purple Finch.

THIS Bird is about the size of a Linnet <note>A small European songbird in the finch family, used here as a familiar point of comparison for 18th-century readers.</note>. The Cock <note>The male bird.</note> is of a purple-colour, appearing as if the feathers were stained with wine; this colour is most vivid on the Head and Breast, while the wings and tail are brown, edged with a purple hue. The Hen <note>The female bird.</note> is entirely brown, with some light spots on the breast. They are found in **Carolina** and **Virginia**, and are observed to feed on the buds of trees in the spring.

---

# THE TUPELO TREE
<note>original Latin: *Tupelo Arbor &c.* Literally: "The Tupelo Tree, etc." The name "Tupelo" is derived from the Muscogee (Creek) words *ito* (tree) and *opilwa* (swamp). This species is likely the Water Tupelo (*Nyssa aquatica*).</note>

## THE TUPELO TREE.

THIS Tree generally grows in very wet and swampy places. It rises to a great height with a large trunk, having a thick, deeply-furrowed bark. The leaves are large, shaped like an egg, and of a dark green colour. The fruit is an oval berry, which, when ripe, is of a dark purple or blackish colour, containing a hard, furrowed Stone <term>Stone|The hard, woody inner layer (endocarp) of a fruit like a peach or olive, which protects the seed.</term>. The wood is soft and light, yet very tough and difficult to split, making it useful for the manufacturing of bowls and trays.

<keywords>Purple Finch, Haemorhous purpureus, Tupelo Tree, Nyssa aquatica, Carolina, Virginia, ornithology, botany, swamp flora</keywords>

--- Page 141 ---
<meta>This page continues Mark Catesby’s documentation of North American species, specifically focusing on the Purple Finch and the Tupelo Tree. It follows his previous descriptions of birds and trees found in the coastal regions of the mid-Atlantic and Southern colonies.</meta>

# THE PURPLE FINCH <note>original Latin: *FRINGILLA PURPUREA*. This bird is now scientifically known as *Haemorhous purpureus*.</note>

## The Purple Finch.

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'I' featuring floral patterns within a square frame.</image-desc>

IN Size and Shape this Bird differs but little from our Chaffinch <note>A common European songbird.</note>. The Belly is White: The rest of the Body is of a dusky purple Colour; but with a Mixture of Brown in some Parts; particularly, the interior Vanes <term>Vanes|The flat, spreading part of a feather on either side of the central shaft.</term> of the Wing-Feathers are brown, as are the Tail-Feathers towards the End. The Hen is brown, having her Breast spotted like our Mavis <note>An old English name for the Song Thrush (*Turdus philomelos*).</note>. When they first appear in Carolina (which is usually in November) they feed on the Berries of Juniper; and in February they destroy the swelling Buds of Fruit-Trees, in like manner as our Bull-finches <note>The Eurasian Bullfinch, known for its habit of eating the buds of orchards.</note>. They associate in small Flights, and retire at the Approach of Winter.

---

# THE TREE GROWING IN WATER <note>original Latin: *Arbor in aqua nascens, folijs latis acuminatis & non dentatis fructu Eleagni minore*. Translated: "A tree growing in water, with broad pointed and non-toothed leaves, and a fruit smaller than the Oleaster (Wild Olive)."</note>

## The TUPELO TREE.

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'T' featuring floral patterns within a square frame.</image-desc>

THIS Tree usually grows large and spreading, with an erect Trunc <note>Trunk.</note> and regular Head. The Leaves are shaped like those of the Bay-Tree. In Autumn its Branches are thick set with oval black Berries on long Foot-stalks, each Berry having a hard channell'd flattish Stone. These Berries have a very sharp and bitter Tast, yet are Food for many Wild Animals, particularly Raccoons, Opossums, Bears, &c. The Grain of the Wood is curled and very tough, and therefore very proper for Naves <term>Naves|The central hubs of a wheel through which the axle passes.</term> of Cart-wheels and other Country-Uses. They grow usually in moist Places, in Virginia, Maryland and Carolina.

<column-break/>

## The Purple Finch. <note>original French: *Pinçon violet*.</note>

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'C' featuring a bird motif within a square frame.</image-desc>

THIS bird is roughly the same size and shape as our Finch. It has a white belly, and the rest of the body is a deep violet, mixed with brown in some places; above all, the interior fringes of the wing feathers are very brown. The tail feathers are of the same color at their tips. The female is brown, and has a spotted breast like our Song Thrush <note>original: *Mauvis*.</note>. When these birds appear in Carolina (which usually happens in the month of November) they feed on Juniper berries, and in the month of February they destroy the buds of fruit trees, just like our Bullfinches <note>original: *Pivoines*. In 18th-century French, *pivoine* was a common name for the bullfinch because of its peony-red breast.</note>. They join together in small flocks and depart at the beginning of winter.

---

## The Tree Named Tupelo. <note>original French: *Arbre nommé Tupelo*.</note>

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'C' featuring floral patterns within a square frame.</image-desc>

THIS tree usually becomes very tall and very spread out. Its trunk is straight; and its branches form a regular crown. Its leaves resemble those of the female Olive. In Autumn its branches are entirely covered with black and oval fruits, attached to long stalks. These fruits have hard, flattened, and grooved pits. They have a sharp and bitter taste; and yet several wild animals feed on them, especially Raccoons, Opossums, Bears, etc. The grain of this wood is curled and very rough; that is why it is very well-suited for the hubs of cartwheels and other utensils used in agriculture. This tree grows almost everywhere in Virginia, Maryland, and Carolina.

<summary>This page describes the appearance and behavior of the Purple Finch and the physical characteristics and uses of the Tupelo Tree in the American colonies.</summary>
<keywords>Purple Finch, Tupelo Tree, Nyssa aquatica, Carolina, Virginia, Maryland, 18th-century natural history, bird migration, timber uses</keywords>

# THE BAHAMA FINCH <note>original Latin: *FRINGILLA BAHAMENSIS*. This bird is now identified as the Bananaquit (*Coereba flaveola*).</note>

### The Bahama Finch.

It weighs fourteen Penny Weight <note>A traditional unit of weight; 14 pennyweights is approximately 21.7 grams or about three-quarters of an ounce.</note>. The Head is black; except a white Line which runs from the Bill over the Eye, and another under the Eye. The Throat is black, except a yellow Spot, close under the Bill. The Breast is Orange-colour'd; the Belly white; the Upper-part of the Neck and the Rump, of a dusky Red; the Back black; the Wings and Tail brown, with a Mixture of white; the Legs and Feet, Lead-Colour. These Birds are frequent on many of the *Bahama* Islands.

<column-break/>

### Bahama Finch <note>original French: *Pinçon de Bahama*</note>

*It weighs six drachms <note>A drachm, or dram, is an old measurement of weight. Six drachms is roughly equivalent to the fourteen pennyweights mentioned in the English text.</note>. Its head is black, except for a white stripe that extends from the beak to above the eye, and another below it. Its throat is entirely black, except for a yellow spot located immediately under the beak. Its breast is orange; its belly white; the top of the neck and the rump are a dark red. Its back is black; its wings and its tail are brown, mixed with white; its legs and its feet are the color of lead. These birds are common on several of the* Bahama *islands.*

---

## THE BROAD-LEAVED GUAIACUM WITH BLUE FLOWERS <note>original Latin: *Arbor Guajaci latiore folio, Bignoniæ flore cæruleo, fructu duro in duas partes dissiliente, seminibus alatis imbricatim positis*. Translated: "The Guaiacum tree with a broader leaf, a blue Bignonia-like flower, a hard fruit splitting into two parts, and winged seeds overlapping like roof-tiles." This is likely the Lignum Vitae (*Guaiacum sanctum*).</note>

### The broad leaf'd Guaicum, with blew Flowers.

This is a Tree of a Middle-Size. The Leaves are winged, with many small pointed alternate Lobes. In *May* there proceeds from the Ends of its Branches several spreading Foot-stalks bearing blew Flowers, in Form not unlike those of the Fox-glove <note>A common garden plant with bell-shaped flowers.</note>, which are succeeded by large flat roundish Seed-Vessels, or Pods, commonly two Inches over, containing many small flat winged Seeds. This Tree grows on several of the *Bahama* Islands, particularly near the Town of *Nassau* on the Island of *Providence*.

<column-break/>

### Guaiacum Tree, with blue flowers <note>original French: *Arbre de Guaiac, aux fleurs bluës*</note>

*This tree is of a medium size. Its leaves are pointed and arranged alternately along the stems. In the month of* May*, several stems emerge from the ends of its branches, spreading away from one another and bearing flowers quite similar to those of the Foxglove. They are followed by large pods, almost round, and usually two inches in diameter; within these are contained several small, flat, winged seeds. This tree grows on several of the* Bahama *islands, especially in the vicinity of the town of* Nassau *on the Island of Providence.*

# THE AMERICAN GOLDFINCH <note>original Latin: *CARDUELIS AMERICANUS*. The genus name *Carduelis* is derived from the Latin *carduus*, meaning "thistle," a favorite food source for these birds.</note>

### The American Goldfinch.

# THE WILD LICORICE-LEAVED ACACIA <note>original Latin: *ACACIA ABRUÆ FOLIJS &c.* The plant is identified by its resemblance to the *Abrus* plant (Wild Licorice or Rosary Pea), specifically noting its pinnate or "feather-like" compound leaves.</note>

# THE AMERICAN GOLDFINCH <note>original Latin: *CARDUELIS AMERICANUS*</note>

### The American Goldfinch.

THIS agrees, in Size and Shape, with our Gold-finch. The Bill is of a dusky white; the Fore-part of the Head black; the Back-part of a dirty Green. All the Under-part of the Body, from the Bill to the Vent, and likewise the Back, of a bright Yellow; the Wings black, having some of the smaller Feathers edged with dusky White; the Legs and Feet brown. They feed on Lettice and Thistle Seed. These Birds are not common in Carolina. In Virginia they are more frequent; and at New-York they are most numerous; and are there commonly kept in Cages.

<column-break/>

### The American Goldfinch <note>original French: *Chardonneret de L'Amerique*</note>

IT is of the same size and the same shape as our Goldfinch. Its bill is of a dusky white. The front of its head is black, and the back of a dull green. All the underside of its body, as well as its back, is a bright yellow. Its wings are black, and some of their small feathers are bordered with white. Its legs and its feet are brown. It feeds on lettuce and thistle seed. This bird is rare in Carolina; more common in Virginia; but one sees a greater number in New York, where they are kept in cages.

---

### *The Wild Licorice-leaved Acacia, with three-pronged thorns and an oval pod enclosing a single seed.* <note>original Latin: *Acacia Abruæ folijs, triacanthos, capsulâ ovali unicum semen claudente*. The term *triacanthos* refers to the three-pointed thorns characteristic of the Honey Locust family.</note>

**ACACIA.**

THIS Tree grows to a large Size and spreading. The Leaves are winged <note>term: winged (pinnate). This refers to a leaf structure where small leaflets are arranged on either side of a stem, resembling a feather.</note>, composed of many small pointed Lobes, like most others of its Tribe. The Fruit is somewhat like a Bean, contained in an oval Capsula <note>original Latin: *capsulâ*, meaning a seed pod</note>, and grows commonly five or six together in a Bunch. Many very large sharp Thorns are set on its Branches and larger Limbs. This Tree I never saw but at the Plantation of Mr *Waring* on *Ashley* River, growing in shallow Water.

<column-break/>

**Acacia.**

THIS tree becomes very tall and very wide-spreading. Its leaves are small, pointed, and arranged oppositely along the stems, like those of most other trees of its class. Its fruit resembles a bean a little, enclosed in an oval capsule. It is usually found in clusters of five or six. Its branches have several large and very sharp spines. I have never seen this tree except at the plantation of Mr. *Waring*, on the *Ashley* River, in shallow water.

<keywords>American Goldfinch, Carduelis Americanus, Acacia, Honey Locust, Carolina, Virginia, New York, Ashley River, Mark Catesby, ornithology, botany</keywords>

<meta>This page follows the description of the American Goldfinch and the Acacia from page 43. It continues Mark Catesby’s documentation of North American flora and fauna, specifically focusing on species found in the coastal regions of South Carolina.</meta>

<margin>p. 44.</margin>

<header>THE THREE-COLORED FINCH <note>original Latin: *FRINGILLA TRICOLOR*</note></header>

<note>Library bookplate in top left margin: FROM THE LIBRARY OF MILDRED [...] with a heraldic crest.</note>

## The Painted Finch.

THIS weighs nine Penny weight <note>A pennyweight is an old unit of mass, roughly 1.5 grams; nine pennyweights is about 14 grams.</note>, and is about the bigness of a Canary Bird. The Head and upper Part of the Neck, of an Ultramarine blew. The Throat, Breast and Belly of a bright Red: The Back green, inclining to yellow. The Wings are composed of green, purple and dusky red Feathers: The Rump red: The Tail dusky red, with Mixture of Purple. Tho' a particular Description may be requisite, in order to give the more perfect Idea of this Bird, yet its Colours may be comprised in Three: The Head and Neck are blew; the Belly red, and the Back green. Its Notes are soft; but have not much Variety. They breed in Carolina, and affect much to make their Nests in Orange-Trees. They do not continue there in Winter; nor do they frequent the Upper-parts of the Country. I never saw one fifty Miles from the Sea. Tho' the Cock <note>Male bird.</note> is so elegant, the Hen <note>Female bird.</note> is as remarkable for her plain Colour, which is not unlike that of a Hen-Sparrow, but with a faint Tincture of Green.

His Excellency, Mr *Johnson* <note>Robert Johnson (1677–1735), twice governor of South Carolina.</note>, the present Governour of *South-Carolina*, kept four or five of these Birds, (taken from the Nest) in Cages two Years; in all which Time the Cocks and Hens varied so little in Colour, that it was not easie to distinguish them. I have likewise caught the Cock and Hen from their Nest, and could see little Difference, they being both alike brown. How many Years it is before they come to their full Colour, is uncertain. When they are brought into this cold Climate, they lose much of their Lustre, as appear'd by some I brought along with me. The *Spaniards* call this Bird *Mariposa pintada*, or the Painted Butterfly.

---
->The Florida Hollyhock with five capsules, Laurel-like leaves, slightly notched, and seeds winged like those of conifers <note>original Latin: *Alcea Floridana quinque capsularis Laurinis folijs, leviter crenatis, seminibus coniferarum instar alatis*</note>, Plukenet's *Amaltheum Botanicum*, page 7, Plate 352.<-
---

## The LOBLOLLY BAY.

THIS is a tall and very streight Tree, with a regular Piramidal shaped Head. Its Leaves are shap'd like those of the common Bay, but serrated <note>Having a jagged, saw-like edge.</note>. It begins to blossom in May, and continues bringing forth its Flowers the greatest part of the Summer. The Flowers are fixed to Foot-stalks, four or five Inches long; are monopetalous <note>Having a corolla consisting of a single petal.</note>, divided into five Segments, encompassing a Tuft of Stamina, headed with yellow Apices; which Flowers in November are succeeded by a conic capsula <note>A dry fruit that releases seeds when ripe.</note> having a divided Calix. The Capsula when ripe opens and divides into five Sections, disclosing many small half Winged Seeds. This Tree retains its Leaves all the Year, and grows only in wet Places, and usually in Water. The Wood is somewhat soft; yet I have seen some Beautiful Tables made of it. It grows in Carolina; but not in any of the more Northern Colonies.

<column-break/>

## The Three-Colored Finch <note>original French: *Pinçon de trois Couleurs*</note>

THIS Bird weighs about four drachms <note>A drachm, or dram, is an old unit of weight; four drachms is approximately 15 grams.</note>, and is nearly the size of a Canary. Its head and the top of its neck are of an ultramarine blue. Its throat, its breast, and its belly are a brilliant red. Its back is a green inclining toward yellow. Its wings are composed of purple and dark red feathers. The lower back and the tail are a dark red, mixed with purple. Although to give a more exact idea of this bird one must make a detailed description, nevertheless one can reduce its different colors to three. The head and the neck are blue; the belly red, and the back green. Its song is sweet, but has little variety. They raise their young in Carolina, and principally choose orange trees to make their nests. They do not remain in this country during the winter, and do not enter very far inland. I have never seen one fifty miles from the sea. Although the male is so beautiful, the female is no less remarkable for her simple color, very close to that of a female sparrow, but with a small nuance of green.

His Excellency Mr. *Johnson*, today Governor of South Carolina, for two years kept in cages four or five of these birds, which had been taken from the nest. During all this time the males and the females differed so little in color that it was very difficult to distinguish them. I myself took some from the nest, and could find no difference between the male and the female, both being equally brown. It is unknown how many years pass before their colors reach their perfection. They lose much of their luster when they are brought into this cold climate, as I have experienced with some that I brought with me. The Spaniards call this bird *Mariposa pintada*, or the butterfly of diverse colors.

## Florida Hollyhock <note>original French: *Alcée de la Floride*</note>

THIS tree is tall and very straight. Its branches form a regular pyramid. Its leaves are of the same shape as those of the common Laurel; but they are notched. It begins to flower in the month of May, and continues to produce flowers during almost all the summer. These flowers are attached to stalks four or five inches long. They are of a single petal, and divided into five segments, which surround a tuft of stamens, whose heads are yellow. These flowers are followed in the month of November by conical capsules, whose calyx is divided. When they are ripe they open, and divide into five sections, and reveal small seeds. This tree keeps its leaves all year; it grows only in humid places, and often even in water. Its wood is a little soft; however, I have seen very beautiful tables made of it. It grows in Carolina, but not in the more northern colonies.

<summary>This page describes the Painted Bunting (referred to as the Painted Finch), noting its vibrant plumage and coastal habitat, alongside the Loblolly Bay tree, a wetland species known for its pyramidal shape and white flowers.</summary>

## The Painted Finch.

->**Florida Rose-Mallow** <note>original: *Althea Floridana*</note><-

# THE BLUE LINNET <note>original Latin: *Linaria caerulea*</note>

## The Blue Linnet.

->Mark Catesby <note>original: MC</note><-

**Nightshade etc. with flesh-colored flowers** <note>original Latin: *Solanum &c flore carneo*. While Catesby identifies this as a "Solanum" (the nightshade family), the plant depicted with its tubular flowers and bulbous root is more characteristic of species found in the American South, such as a type of False Foxglove.</note>

<meta>This page continues Mark Catesby’s documentation of North American species, following page 44 which described the Painted Bunting and the Florida Rose-Mallow. Here, on Plate 45, Catesby illustrates the Indigo Bunting (which he calls the Blue Linnet) and a plant that modern botanists identify as a species of Trillium, though Catesby categorized it with the nightshades.</meta>

# THE BLUE LINNET <note>original Latin: *Linaria caerulea*</note>

## The Blue Linnet.

THIS Bird is rather smaller than a Goldfinch; weighing eight pennyweights <note>A pennyweight is an old unit of mass; eight pennyweights is approximately 12.4 grams.</note>. The whole Bird appears, from a short distance, to be an entirely blue color: But, upon a nearer view, it is as follows: The bill is black and lead-colored. On the crown of the head, the blue is most resplendent and deeper than in any other part. The neck, back, and belly are a lighter blue. The large wing feathers are brown, edged with blue. The tail is brown, with a tint of blue. None of these birds are found within the settled parts of Carolina; I have never seen any closer than 150 miles from the sea, as they live only in the hilly parts of the country. Their song is somewhat like those of our English linnets.

The Spaniards in Mexico call this bird *Azul lejos* <note>original: "Azul lexos." In 18th-century Spanish, 'x' was often used where 'j' is used today; *Azul lejos* translates literally as "Far Blue."</note>, or the Far-fetched Blue-bird.

## THREE-LEAVED NIGHTSHADE WITH SIX-PETALED FLESH-COLORED FLOWERS <note>original Latin: *Solanum triphylion flore hexapetalo carneo*. Although Catesby classifies this as a *Solanum* (nightshade), his description and illustration of three leaves and three petals clearly identify this as a *Trillium*, likely *Trillium catesbaei*, named in his honor.</note>

THIS has a tuberous root from which shoot forth two or three straight stalks, about eight inches high. On these are set three ribbed leaves in a triangle; from between them proceeds its flower, of a pale red, composed of six spreading leaves <note>In modern botany, these "six leaves" are understood as three petals and three sepals.</note>—three large and three smaller—with stamens of unequal lengths. The flower is succeeded by its seed-vessel, in the form and size of a small hazelnut, but somewhat channeled and covered by a perianth <note>The outer part of a flower.</note>, which divides into three and turns back. The capsule contains innumerable small seeds, like dust. I found this plant at the sources of great rivers; I have not seen any in the inhabited parts of Carolina.

<column-break/>

## THE BLUE LINNET <note>original French: *Linotte bleue*</note>

THIS bird is smaller than a goldfinch. It weighs two and a half drachms <note>A drachm, or dram, is an old unit of weight; Catesby's English text uses pennyweights, while the French uses drachms to provide a familiar measurement for French readers.</note>. From a distance, it appears entirely blue: but on examining it closely, one notices the following. Its beak is black and lead-colored. The top of its head is a darker blue than any other part of its body. Its neck, back, and belly are a paler blue. The large feathers of its wings are brown, edged with blue. Its tail is brown, with a shade of blue. None of these birds are found in the settlements of Carolina: and I have never seen one closer to the sea than one hundred and fifty miles, for they keep only to the mountains of the country. Their warbling resembles that of our linnets. The Spaniards of Mexico call this bird *Azul lejos* <note>original: Azul lexos</note>, or the blue bird that comes from afar.

---

THIS plant has a tuberous root, from which emerge two or three straight stems, about eight inches long, each supporting three leaves arranged in a triangle and divided by ribs along their entire length. The flower grows from between these leaves. It is a pale red and composed of six leaves—three large and three small—which spread far apart from one another, and stamens of unequal length. The flower is followed by the seed enclosed in a capsule the size of a hazelnut, though slightly grooved. It is covered by a membrane which separates into three and folds backward. This capsule contains an infinity of small seeds like dust. I found this plant at the sources of the great rivers; and I have seen none in the part of Carolina that is inhabited.

<sig>M</sig>

FROM THE LIBRARY OF MILDRED BLISS

# THE CAROLINA CHATTERER <note>original Latin: *Garrulus Carolinensis*. This bird is now known as the Cedar Waxwing (*Bombycilla cedrorum*).</note>

### The Chatterer.

IT weighs an ounce; and is rather less than a sparrow. The bill is black; the mouth and throat are large. From the nostrils runs a black stripe to the back of its head, like velvet, with a line of white on the lower edge, in which the eyes are situated. The rest of its head and neck is brown. On its crown is a pyramidal crest of the same color. The breast is brown; the back and the feathers covering the wings are somewhat darker; the belly is pale yellow. What distinguishes this bird from others are eight small red patches at the tips of eight of the smaller wing feathers, which have the color and consistency of red sealing wax. When the wing is closed, these patches join together and form a large red spot. The tail is black, except for the tip, which is yellow.

---

## *Shrub with dogwood-like leaves in pairs, flowers resembling the Star Anemone, with thick, rigid petals of a dull red color; aromatic bark.* <note>original Latin: *Frutex corni folijs conjugatis, floribus instar Anemones stellatæ, petalis crassis, rigidis, colore sordidè rubente; cortice aromatico*. This plant is *Calycanthus floridus*, commonly known as Carolina Allspice or Sweetshrub.</note>

THIS shrub usually grows about eight or ten feet high. The leaves are set opposite to each other. The flowers resemble, in form, those of the *Star-Anemone*, composed of many stiff copper-colored petals, enclosing a tuft of short yellow *stamens* <term>stamina: the pollen-producing part of a flower</term>. The flowers are followed by a roundish fruit, flat at the top. The bark is very aromatic and as fragrant as cinnamon. These trees grow in the remote and hilly parts of *Carolina*, but are not found anywhere among the settled inhabitants.

<column-break/>

### The Carolina Chatterer <note>original French: *Le Jaseur de la Caroline*</note>

IT weighs an ounce, and is a little smaller than a sparrow. It has a black bill: the opening is large, as is its throat. From its nostrils to the back of its head extends a black and velvety stripe, bordered with a little white. In the middle of this stripe are the eyes. The rest of its head and its neck are brown. It also has a pyramidal brown crest on its head. Its breast is brown. Its back and the feathers of its wings, which are hidden, are of a slightly darker brown. Its belly is a pale yellow. What distinguishes this bird from others are eight small red spots that it has at the extremities of the eight small feathers of the wing. These spots are precisely of the same color and the same consistency as red Spanish Wax <note>original French: *Cire d'Espagne*; a high-quality sealing wax</note>. When the wing is closed, these spots, by gathering together, form a single very large one. Its tail is black, except for a small yellow band that finishes it.

---

THIS shrub ordinarily rises to eight or ten feet in height. Its leaves are opposite to one another. Its flowers resemble in their shape those of the *Star Anemone*. They are composed of several stiff, copper-colored leaves <note>In 18th-century botany, "leaves" often referred to petals</note>, which enclose a tuft of small yellow stamens. They are succeeded by round fruits, flattened at their ends. The bark of this shrub is very aromatic, and as fragrant as cinnamon. It grows in the remote and mountainous places of *Carolina*. None are found among the plantations.

<summary>This page describes the physical characteristics of the Cedar Waxwing and the aromatic Carolina Allspice, noting the bird's unique wax-like wing tips and the plant's cinnamon-like fragrance.</summary>
<keywords>Cedar Waxwing, Carolina Allspice, Sweetshrub, Mark Catesby, North American birds, aromatic plants, Carolina natural history</keywords>

# THE CAROLINA CHATTERER <note>original Latin: *Garrulus Carolinensis*. This bird is now known as the Cedar Waxwing (*Bombycilla cedrorum*).</note>

### The Chatterer.

IT weighs an ounce; and is rather less than a sparrow. The bill is black; the mouth and throat are large. From the nostrils runs a black stripe to the back of its head, like velvet, with a line of white on the lower edge, in which the eyes are situated. The rest of its head and neck is brown. On its crown is a pyramidal crest of the same color. The breast is brown; the back and the feathers covering the wings are somewhat darker; the belly is pale yellow. What distinguishes this bird from others are eight small red patches at the tips of eight of the smaller wing feathers, which have the color and consistency of red sealing wax. When the wing is closed, these patches join together and form a large red spot. The tail is black, except for the tip, which is yellow.

---

## Shrub with dogwood-like leaves in pairs, flowers resembling the Star Anemone, with thick, rigid petals of a dull red color; aromatic bark. <note>original Latin: *Frutex corni folijs conjugatis, floribus instar Anemones stellatæ, petalis crassis, rigidis, colore sordidè rubente; cortice aromatico*. This plant is *Calycanthus floridus*, commonly known as Carolina Allspice or Sweetshrub.</note>

THIS shrub usually grows about eight or ten feet high. The leaves are set opposite to each other. The flowers resemble, in form, those of the *Star-Anemone*, composed of many stiff copper-colored petals, enclosing a tuft of short yellow *stamens* <term>stamina: the pollen-producing part of a flower</term>. The flowers are followed by a rou... <note>The text ends mid-word, likely referring to a "roundish" seed pod.</note>

## THE BLUE AMERICAN ROBIN. <note>original Latin: *Rubicula Americana Cærulea*</note>

->The Blue Bird.<-
THIS Bird weighs nineteen Penny-weight <note>A pennyweight is a historical unit of mass; 19 pennyweights is approximately 29.5 grams or roughly 1 ounce.</note>, and is about the size of a Sparrow. The Eyes are large. The Head and Upper-part of the Body, Tail and Wings are of a bright Blue, except that the Ends of the Wing-Feathers are brown. The Throat and Breast are of a dull Red; the Belly white. It is a Bird of a very swift Flight, its Wings being very long; so that the Hawk generally pursues it in vain. They make their Nests in Holes of Trees; are harmless Birds, and resemble our Robin-red-breast. They feed on Insects only.

These Birds are common in most Parts of North America, I having seen them in *Carolina*, *Virginia*, *Maryland*, and the *Bermuda* Islands.

<column-break/>

->The Carolina Red-breast.<- <note>original French: *Rouge Gorge de la Caroline*</note>
THIS bird weighs one ounce. It is about the size of a sparrow. Its eyes are large. Its head, the top of its body, its tail, and its wings are of a very bright blue, except that the tips of the wing feathers are brown. Its throat and its breast are of a dull red. Its belly is white. This bird flies very fast, its wings being very long, such that the hawk pursues it in vain. It makes its nest in the holes of trees. It is a very gentle bird: it resembles our robin-redbreast. It feeds only on insects. It is very common throughout northern America; for I have seen them in Carolina, Virginia, Maryland, and the Bermuda islands.

---

->*Low Smilax without thorns, with Birth-wort-like leaves and red berries.*<- <note>original Latin: *Smilax non spinosa, humilis, folio Aristolochiæ, baccis rubris*</note>

THIS Plant sometimes trails on the Ground, the Leaves resembling those of the *Birth-wort* <term>Birth-wort: a name for plants in the genus *Aristolochia*, historically used in childbirth but here referring to the leaf shape</term>, set alternately on its slender Stalks; from which hang Clusters of small red Berries of an oval Form but pointed, each containing a very hard round Seed.

<column-break/>

THIS plant sometimes creeps upon the earth. Its leaves resemble those of the *Birth-wort*. They are arranged alternately on very thin stems; from which hang in clusters small red berries, oval and pointed. Each berry contains a very hard, round seed.

--- Page 160 ---
<meta>This page continues Mark Catesby’s Natural History of Carolina, Florida, and the Bahama Islands. It follows page 47, which described the Eastern Bluebird (the "Blue American Robin"). On this page (48), Catesby describes the Baltimore Oriole and the American Tulip Tree.</meta>

# YELLOW BIRD VARIED WITH GOLD AND BLACK <note>original Latin: *Icterus ex aureo nigroque varius*. *Icterus* was a classical name for a yellow bird, and here Catesby uses it to classify the oriole.</note>

## The Baltimore-Bird
Is about the Size of a Sparrow; weighing a little above an Ounce. The Bill is sharp and tapering; the Head and half-way down the Back, of a shining Black. The Wings, except the Upper-parts (which are Yellow) are black, with most of the Feathers edged on both Sides with White. The Rest of the Body is of a bright Colour, between Red and Yellow. The two uppermost Feathers of the Tail are black; the Rest yellow: The Legs and Feet of a Lead-Colour. It disappears in Winter. This Gold-colour’d Bird I have only seen in *Virginia* and *Maryland*; there being none of them in *Carolina*. It is said to have its Name from the Lord *Baltimore’s* Coat of Arms, which are *Paly* of six *Topaz* and *Diamond*, a Bend, counterchang’d <note>Heraldic terminology describing a shield with six vertical stripes of gold (Topaz) and black (Diamond). Because the bird shared these striking colors, it was named after the proprietor of the Maryland colony.</note>; his Lordship being a Proprietor in those Countries. It breeds on the Branches of tall Trees, and usually on the Poplar or Tulip-Tree. Its Nest is built in a particular manner, supported only by two Twigs fix’d to the Verge of the Nest, and hanging most commonly at the Extremity of a Bough.

---

# Virginian Tulip-bearing tree with a three-parted maple leaf, the middle segment as if cut off <note>original Latin: *Arbor Tulipifera Virginiana tripartito aceris folio, media lacinia velut abscissa*. This is the American Tulip Tree, *Liriodendron tulipifera*.</note>
->Pluk. Phytog. Tab: 117. & Tab. 248.<- <note>A reference to Leonard Plukenet’s *Phytographia*, an illustrated botanical work published in the late 17th century.</note>

This Tree grows to a very large Size; some of them being Thirty foot in Circumference. Its Boughs are very unequal and irregular, not straight, but making several Bends or Elbows; which peculiarly makes this Tree distinguishable, at a great Distance, from all other Trees even when it has lost its Leaves. The Leaves stand on Foot-stalks, about a Finger in Length: They somewhat resemble the smaller Maple in Shape, but are usually five or six Inches over, and, instead of being pointed at the End, seem to be cut off with a Notch.
The Flowers have been always compared to Tulips; whence the Tree has received its Name; tho’, I think, in Shape they resemble more the *Fritillaria* <note>A genus of spring-flowering plants in the lily family, often having nodding, bell-shaped flowers.</note>. They are composed of seven or eight Petala <note>Petals.</note>; the Upper-part being of a pale-green, and the Lower-part shaded with Red and a little yellow intermix’d. They are at first enclosed by a *Perianthium* <note>The outer envelope of a flower, consisting of the calyx or corolla.</note>, which opens and falls back when the Flower blows. These Trees are found in most parts of the Northern Continent of *America*, from the *Cape* of *Florida* to *New-England*. The Timber is of great Use.

<column-break/>

## The Baltimore Bird <note>original French: *L’Oiseau Baltimore*.</note>
This bird is roughly the size of a sparrow. It weighs a little more than an ounce. Its beak is conical and very sharp. From the head to the middle of the back it is a glossy black. Its wings are black, except for their upper part, which is yellow. Most of the feathers are bordered with white on both sides. All the rest of its body is of a brilliant color, between red and yellow. The two upper feathers of its tail are black, and the others yellow. Its legs and its feet are lead-colored. It disappears in winter. I have only seen this gold-colored bird in *Virginia* and *Maryland*. There are none in *Carolina*. It is said that it took its name from the arms of Lord *Baltimore* who bears "paly of six gold and black," because this Lord is one of the proprietors of this country. It makes its nest on the branches of the tallest trees and usually on those of the poplar, or the tulip tree. It attaches it in a particular manner, usually at the extremity of a large branch; so that it is supported only by two small shoots that enter into its edges.

---

This tree becomes very large; some have up to thirty feet in circumference. Its branches are very unequal and very irregular. They do not extend in a straight line; but they are very bent in several places; which makes this tree recognizable from very far away, even when it has lost all its leaves. They have stalks as long as a finger and somewhat resemble the Maple in their shape; but they are five or six inches across; and instead of ending in a point, it seems that they are cut with a notch. Its flowers have always been compared to tulips; and it is from this resemblance that the tree took its name. I believe, however, that their shape approaches more closely to that of the flowers of the *fritillaria*. They are composed of seven or eight leaves <note>In 18th-century botanical French, *feuilles* (leaves) often referred to petals.</note>, of which the upper part is a pale green, and the rest tinted with red, with a little yellow mixed in. At the beginning they are enclosed by a *perianthium* which opens backward when they bloom. These trees are found almost throughout the continent of *North America*, from *Cape Florida* to *New England*; Their wood is of great use for buildings.

# THE YELLOW BIRD VARIED WITH GOLD AND BLACK <note>original Latin: *Icterus ex aureo nigroque varius*. In Catesby’s time, *Icterus* (from the Greek word for jaundice or a yellow bird) was the standard name for birds of this brilliant color.</note>

## The Baltimore-Bird
Is about the Size of a Sparrow; weighing a little above an Ounce. The Bill is sharp and tapering; the Head and half-way down the Back, of a shining Black. The Wings, except the Upper-parts (which are Yellow) are black, with most of the Feathers edged on both Sides with White. The Rest of the Body is of a bright Colour, between Red and Yellow. The two uppermost Feathers of the Tail are black; the Rest yellow: The Legs and Feet of a Lead-Colour. It disappears in Winter. 

This Gold-colour’d Bird I have only seen in *Virginia* and *Maryland*; there being none of them in *Carolina*. It is said to have its Name from the Lord *Baltimore’s* Coat of Arms, which are *Paly* of six *Topaz* and *Diamond*, a Bend, counterchang’d <note>Catesby uses heraldic terminology here. *Paly of six* refers to six vertical stripes; *Topaz* represents gold (yellow) and *Diamond* represents black (sable). This explains why the bird, with its black and orange-gold plumage, was named after the proprietor of the Maryland colony.</note>; his Lordship being a Proprietor in those Countries. It breeds on the Branches of tall Trees, and usually on the Poplar or Tulip-Tree. Its Nest is built in a particular manner, supported only by two Twigs fix’d to the Verge of the Nest, and hanging most commonly at the Extremity of a Bough.

# THE VIRGINIAN TULIP-BEARING TREE WITH A THREE-PARTED MAPLE LEAF, THE MIDDLE SEGMENT AS IF CUT OFF <note>original Latin: *Arbor Tulipifera Virginiana tripartito aceris folio, media lacinia velut abscissa*. This is the *Liriodendron tulipifera*, commonly known as the Tulip Poplar or Tulip Tree.</note>

This Tree rises to a very great Height and Stature; the Trunk is often very large; the Bark is smooth and of a greyish Colour. The Leaves are of a very singular Shape, appearing as if the end of the Leaf had been cut off with a pair of Scissors. The Flowers are produced at the ends of the Branches, in Shape and Size resembling a Tulip <note>Though called a "Tulip Tree" for its flowers, this species is actually a member of the Magnolia family.</note>; they are composed of six Petals, of a pale Green, with a large Spot of Red or Orange near the Bottom of each Petal. In the Center is a large, upright Cone-like seed vessel. These Trees grow in great abundance in most of the Northern Parts of *America*, and are highly valued for their Size and the Usefulness of their Timber.

<summary>This page describes the Baltimore Oriole, noting its naming after Lord Baltimore's heraldic colors, and the North American Tulip Tree, known for its distinctively "clipped" leaves and tulip-like flowers.</summary>

P. 49.

# THE SMALLER YELLOW BIRD <note>original Latin: *Icterus Minor*. While Catesby uses the classical term for a yellow bird, this species is now known as the Orchard Oriole (*Icterus spurius*).</note>

## The Bastard Baltimore <note>In 18th-century natural history, "bastard" was often used to describe a species that resembled another—in this case, the Baltimore Oriole—but was smaller or slightly different in color.</note>

WEIGHS thirteen Pennyweight <note>Approximately 20 grams.</note>. The Bill is sharp-pointed; the Throat black; the Tail brown; as are its Wings, having most of the Feathers edged with White. All the rest of the Bird is yellow, the Breast being brightest.

The Hen being as handsomely clothed (though with very different colored Feathers) induced me to give the Figures of Both. Her Head and Upper-part of the Back are of a shining Black; the Breast and Belly of a dirty Red; as is the lower Part of the Back and Rump. The Upper-part of the Wing is red; the lower part dusky black; the Tail black; the Legs and Feet blue in both Sexes.

---

->Bignonia with Achiote-like leaves, with a dull white flower, sprinkled on the inside with purple and yellow spots, and a very long and very narrow pod. <note>original Latin: *Bignonia Urucu folijs flore ſordidè albo, intus maculis purpureijs & luteis aſperſo, ſiliqua longiſſima & anguſtiſſima.* Catesby compares the leaves to the *Urucu* or Achiote tree, known for its heart-shaped leaves.</note><-

---

## THE CATALPA TREE.

THIS is usually a small Tree, seldom rising above 20 Foot in Height: The Bark smooth; the Wood soft and spongy; the Leaves shaped like those of the *Lilac* <note>original: *Lilax*.</note>, but much larger, some being ten inches over. In May it produces spreading Bunches of tubulous Flowers, like the common Fox-glove, white, only variegated with a few reddish purple Spots and yellow Streaks on the Inside. The Calix <note>The outer protective part of the flower bud.</note> is of a Copper-Color. These Flowers are succeeded by round Pods, about the thickness of one’s Finger, fourteen Inches in Length; which, when ripe, opens and displays its Seeds, which are winged, and lie over each other like the Scales of Fish. This Tree was unknown to the inhabited Parts of *Carolina* till I brought the Seeds from the remoter Parts of the Country. And though the Inhabitants are little curious in Gardening, yet the uncommon Beauty of the Tree has induced them to propagate it; and it has become an Ornament to many of their Gardens, and probably will be the same to ours in *England*, it being as hardy as most of our American Plants; many of them now at Mr. *Bacon’s* at *Hoxton* <note>Hoxton was then a village on the outskirts of London, famous for its commercial nurseries and botanical gardens.</note>, having stood out several Winters without any Protection except the first Year.

<column-break/>

## The Bastard Baltimore. <note>This column translates Catesby’s French text into English.</note>

IT weighs about five drams <note>Catesby uses "drachmes" in the French text, which is roughly equivalent to his "pennyweight" in the English column.</note>. Its beak is very sharp; its throat black; its tail brown; and its wings also, of which most of the feathers have white tips. All the rest of the bird is yellow; but the yellow of the breast is the most vivid. The extreme beauty of the female, although very different from the male in colors, engaged me to describe them both. Her head and the upper part of her back are of a shining black; her breast and her belly of a dull red; as is the rest of the back. The top of her wings is red, and the bottom a brownish black. Her tail is black. The male and the female have blue legs and feet.

---

## Bignonia with Achiote-like leaves. <note>original French: *Bignonia aux feuilles de Rocou*. *Rocou* is the French term for Achiote or *Urucu*.</note>

THIS tree is usually small, and hardly rises to more than twenty feet in height. Its bark is smooth: Its wood is soft and spongy. Its leaves have the shape of those of the Lilac, but much larger, some having a length up to ten inches. In the month of May it produces bunches of tubular flowers, like those of the common Fox-glove <note>original French: *Gantelée*.</note>. These flowers are white, only variegated inside with some purple spots and some yellow stripes. Their Calyx is copper-colored. When they have passed, they are succeeded by round pods, as thick as a finger, and fourteen inches long, which open when they are ripe, and show the seeds. They are lying one upon the other like the scales of a fish. These trees were not known at all in the inhabited part of *Carolina*, until I had brought the seed from places deeper within the country; and although the inhabitants are very little interested in gardening, nevertheless the singular beauty of this tree has engaged them to sow it; and today it makes the ornament of several of their gardens; and probably the same thing will happen in *England*, since this tree is no more delicate than most of our plants from America. There are several of them today at *Hoxton* at Mr. *Bacon’s*, which have resisted several winters, without requiring any particular care, except for the first year.

<sig>N</sig>
