# AMERICAN WATER-DROPPING BIRD WITH A YELLOW BREAST. <note>original Latin: *Oenanthe Americana Pectore Luteo*. While "Oenanthe" usually refers to wheatears, Catesby is describing the Yellow-breasted Chat (*Icteria virens*).</note>

->The Yellow-breasted Chat.<-

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap initial 'T' containing a woodcut illustration of a bird perched on a leafy branch.</image-desc>
THIS is about the size of our Sky-lark: The bill is black; the head and all the upper part of the back and wings are of a brownish green; the neck and breast are yellow. A white streak reaches from the nostrils over the eye, under which is also a white spot. From the lower mandible <term>mandible: the lower part of a bird's beak</term> of the bill runs a narrow white line. The belly is a dusky white; the tail is brown; the legs and feet are black. I never saw this bird in the inhabited parts. They frequent the upper parts of the country, two hundred and three hundred miles distant from the sea. They are very shy birds, and hide themselves so obscurely that after many hours attempting to shoot one, I was at last necessitated to employ an Indian <note>Catesby often relied on the superior tracking and hunting skills of Indigenous people to collect specimens that were too elusive for European collectors.</note>, who did it not without the utmost of his skill. They frequent the banks of great rivers, and their loud chattering noise reverberates from the hollow rocks and deep cane-swamps. The figure represents the singular manner of their flying with their legs extended.

<column-break/>

->Yellow-breasted White-rump.<- <note>original French: *Cul-blanc à la poitrine jaune*.</note>

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap initial 'C' containing a woodcut illustration of a landscape with a house and trees.</image-desc>
THIS bird is in its form nearly like our Lark. It has a black bill; its head and the whole upper part of the back and the wings are of a brownish green. Its neck and its breast are yellow. A white stripe extends from the nostrils to above the eyes, under which there is also a white spot. A very narrow white stripe starts from the lower mandible of the bill. Its belly is a dirty white; its tail is brown; its legs and feet are black. I have never seen this bird in inhabited places. It stays further inland, two or three hundred miles from the sea. It is very wild. It hides itself so well that after having spent several hours trying to shoot one, I was finally forced to use an Indian, who used all his skill to succeed. This bird frequents the banks of large rivers, and its bright song is echoed with force by the caves of the rocks and the surrounding cane marshes. The figure represents the singular way in which this bird flies with its legs extended.

---

# Three-leaved nightshade with a six-petaled flower: three purple petals upright, the others green and bent back. <note>original Latin: *Solanum triphyllon flore hexapetalo tribus petalis purpurejs erectis cæteris viridibus reflexis*. Despite the name "Solanum" (nightshade), this is a Trillium, likely *Trillium cuneatum*.</note> Plukenet, *Phytographia*, Plate 111. <note>original: *Pluk. Phytog. Tab. CXI*. Referring to Leonard Plukenet, a royal professor of botany whose illustrated works were standard references for Catesby.</note>

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap initial 'T' containing a woodcut illustration of a bird.</image-desc>
THIS plant rises with a single straight stalk, five or six inches high. From the top of it spread forth three broad pointed leaves, placed triangularly and hanging down. These leaves have each three ribs and are variegated with dark and lighter green. From between these leaves shoots forth the flower, consisting of three purple petals growing erect, having its <term>perianthium: the outer envelope of a flower, including the calyx and corolla</term> divided into three.

They grow in shady thickets in most parts of Carolina.

<column-break/>

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap initial 'C' containing a woodcut illustration of a landscape with rolling hills.</image-desc>
THIS plant rises with a single, quite straight stem, five or six inches high. From the top of it come three large pointed leaves, placed in a triangle and hanging down. They each have three ribs and are variegated with green, both lighter and darker. The flower is born from between the leaves. It consists of three violet petals which rise straight up. Its calyx is divided into three. This plant is found almost throughout Carolina and in very dense woods.

<summary>This page provides the first scientific descriptions and illustrations of the Yellow-breasted Chat and a species of Trillium (then categorized as a nightshade), noting the bird's elusive nature and the plant's distinctive three-leaf structure.</summary>

<header>THE PURPLE SWALLOW <note>original Latin: "HIRUNDO PURPUREA"</note></header>

## The Purple Martin

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'I' depicting a classical scene with a pedestal, architectural elements, and foliage.</image-desc>
This bird is larger than our common Martin. The whole bird is of a dark shining purple; the wings and tail being more dusky and inclining to brown. They breed like pigeons in lockers prepared for them against houses, and in gourds hung on poles for them to build in. They are of great use about houses and yards for pursuing and chasing away crows, hawks, and other <term>vermin</term> <note>In this historical context, "vermin" refers to any predatory animal that might threaten livestock or poultry.</note> from the poultry. They retire at the approach of winter, and return in the spring to Virginia and Carolina.

### Smilax (perhaps) smooth, with an angular ivy-like leaf <note>original Latin: "Smilax (forte) lenis, folio anguloſo hederaceo"</note>

**THE** stalks of this plant are slender, running up the walls of old houses and twining about posts and trees. The leaves resemble our common ivy. I never saw it in flower; but it bears red berries, about the bigness of small peas, which grow in clusters.

<column-break/>

## Purple-colored Martin <note>original French: "Martinet couleur de pourpre"</note>

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'C' featuring ornate scrollwork and floral designs.</image-desc>
*This bird is larger than our ordinary martin. It is entirely of a deep and brilliant violet. Its wings and its tail are darker than the rest, and almost brown. They raise their young like pigeons, in holes made expressly for them around houses, and in gourds attached to large poles; for they are very useful around houses and courtyards, from which they drive away crows, birds of prey, and the beasts that would destroy the poultry. They withdraw at the approach of winter from* Virginia and Carolina, *and return there in the spring.* <note>original French: "CET oiſeau est plus gros que nôtre martinet ordinaire... & y retournent au printems."</note>

---

*The stems of this plant are very slender. They climb against the walls of old houses and twist themselves around trees and posts. Its leaves resemble those of the common ivy. I have never seen its flowers. It bears clusters of red berries, sized approximately like small peas.* <note>original French: "LES tiges de cette plante font fort menües... à peu prés comme de petits pois."</note>

# THE CRESTED FLY-CATCHER WITH A YELLOW BELLY <note>original Latin: "MUSCICAPA CRISTATA VENTRE LUTEO"</note>

### The Crested Fly-Catcher

WEIGHS one Ounce. The Bill is black and broad; the Upper-part of the Body of a muddy Green; the Neck and Breast of a Lead-Colour; the Belly yellow; the Wings brown, having most of the <term>Vanes</term> <note>The flat, web-like part of a feather on either side of the central shaft.</note> of the Quill-Feathers edg'd with Red. The two middle Feathers of the Tail are all brown; the interior Vanes of the Rest are red. The Legs and Feet are black. It breeds in *Carolina* and *Virginia*, but retires in Winter. This Bird, by its ungrateful brawling Noise, seems at Variance and displeased with all others.

<column-break/>

### The Crested Fly-Catcher <note>original French: "Le preneur de Mouches huppé"</note>

IT weighs one ounce. Its beak is black and wide. The upper part of its body is a dark green; its neck and breast are lead-colored; its belly yellow; its wings are brown, and have most of the large feathers edged with red. The two feathers in the middle of the tail are all brown; and the interior fringes of the other feathers of its tail are red. Its legs and feet are black. It produces its young in *Carolina* and *Virginia*; but it retreats in winter. It seems by the disagreeable cries of this bird that it is always in a quarrel and is not pleased with any other.

---

## *Smilax with black Bryony leaves, a thorny stem, and black berries* <note>original Latin: "Smilax Bryoniæ nigræ folijs caule spinoso, baccis nigris"</note>

THIS Plant shoots forth with many pliant thorny Stems; which, when at full Bigness, are as big as a Walking Cane, and jointed; and rises to the Height usually of twenty Feet, climbing upon and spreading over the adjacent Trees and Shrubs, by the Assistance of its Tendrels. In Autumn it produces Clusters of black round Berries, hanging pendent to a Foot Stalk, above three Inches long, each Berry containing a very hard roundish Seed. The Roots of this Plant are tuberous divided into many Knots and Joints; and, when first dug out of the Ground, are soft and juicy, but harden in the Air to the Consistence of Wood. Of these Roots the Inhabitants of *Carolina* make a <term>Diet-drink</term> <note>A medicinal herbal infusion or "tea" used in the 18th century to treat various ailments or "purify" the blood.</note>, attributing great Virtues to it in cleansing the Blood &c. They likewise in the Spring boil the tender Shoots and eat them prepared like Asparagus. 'Tis call'd there *China Root*.

<column-break/>

THIS Plant pushes out several pliant, thorny, and knotty stems. When they have reached their full growth they are the size of a cane, and usually rise to a height of twenty feet, climbing and attaching themselves with their <term>hands</term> <note>original French: "mains," a historical term for tendrils.</note> onto nearby trees and bushes. In autumn it produces clusters of round black berries, which are attached to a stem about three inches long. Each berry contains a very hard round seed. The roots of this plant are tuberous, divided into several nodes. When one pulls it from the ground, they are tender and full of juice; but they become as hard as wood in the air. The inhabitants of *Carolina* make a drink from these roots to which they attribute great virtues, such as purifying the blood etc. In the spring they also boil the shoots of this plant, and eat them like asparagus. In this country it is called the *Chinese* root.

<summary>This page describes the physical appearance and temperament of the Crested Fly-catcher alongside the medicinal and culinary uses of the China Root (Smilax) vine in colonial America.</summary>
<keywords>Crested Fly-catcher, Great Crested Flycatcher, China Root, Smilax, natural history, Carolina, Virginia, medicinal plants, colonial diet, ornithology</keywords>

# THE CRESTED FLYCATCHER WITH A YELLOW BELLY <note>original Latin: "MUSCICAPA CRISTATA VENTRE LUTEO"</note>

### The Crested Flycatcher

WEIGHS one ounce. The bill is black and broad; the upper part of the body is of a muddy green; the neck and breast are of a lead-color; the belly is yellow; the wings are brown, having most of the <term>vanes</term> <note>The flat, web-like part of a feather on either side of the central shaft.</note> of the quill feathers edged with red. The two middle feathers of the tail are entirely brown; the interior vanes of the rest are red. The legs and feet are black. It breeds in *Carolina* and *Virginia*, but retires in winter. This bird, by its ungrateful brawling noise, seems at variance and displeased with all others. <note>Catesby often attributed human temperaments to birds; the "brawling noise" refers to the loud, harsh "wheep" call of the Great Crested Flycatcher.</note>

The two feathers in the middle of the tail are all brown; and the interior fringes of the other feathers of its tail are red. Its legs and feet are black. It produces its young in *Carolina* and *Virginia*; but it retreats in winter. It seems by the disagreeable cries of this bird that it is always in a quarrel and is not pleased with any other.

# THE YELLOW JASMINE AND THE BLACKISH FLYCATCHER

### Yellow Jasmine <note>original Latin: "Jasminum luteum"</note>

The yellow Jessamy. <note>This plant is now scientifically known as *Gelsemium sempervirens*, commonly called the Carolina Jessamine. It is a climbing evergreen vine famous for its fragrant, trumpet-shaped flowers, though all parts of the plant are poisonous.</note>

<column-break/>

### Blackish Flycatcher <note>original Latin: "Muscicapa nigrescens"</note>

The blackcap Flycatcher. <note>In modern ornithology, this bird is identified as the Gray Catbird (*Dumetella carolinensis*). Catesby’s Latin name *nigrescens* refers to its dark, "blackish" grey plumage, and "blackcap" refers to the distinct patch of black feathers on its crown.</note>

# THE BLACKISH FLYCATCHER. <note>original Latin: "MUSCICAPA NIGRESCENS"</note>

## The Blackcap Fly-catcher. <note>Now known as the Gray Catbird (*Dumetella carolinensis*). While Catesby identifies it as a fly-catcher due to its diet, it is actually a member of the mavis or mockingbird family.</note>

THE Bill is broad and black; the Upper-part of the Head, of a dusky black; the Back, Wings and Tail are brown; the Breast and Belly white, with a Tincture of yellowish Green. The Legs and Feet are black. The Head of the Cock <note>A term for the male bird.</note> is of a deeper Black than that of the Hen, which is all the Difference between them. I don't remember to have seen any of them in Winter. They feed on Flies and other Insects.

They breed in Carolina.

---

# Climbing Yellow Fragrant Virginian Jasmine, Evergreen <note>original Latin: "Gelseminum sive Jasminum luteum ordoratum Virginianum scandens, semper virens"</note>
### Parkinson, *Theatre of Plants*, page 1465. <note>John Parkinson was a 17th-century herbalist to King Charles I; his "Theatrum Botanicum" (1640) was a foundational botanical text.</note>

THIS Plant grows usually in moist Places, its Branches being supported by other Trees and Shrubs on which it climbs. The Leaves grow opposite to each other from the Joynts of the Stalks; from whence likewise shoot forth yellow tubulous <note>tube-shaped</note> Flowers; the Verges <note>edges</note> of which are notched or divided into five Sections. The Seeds are flat and half winged, contained in an oblong pointed Capsule, which, when the Seeds are ripe, splits to the Stalk and discharges them. The Smell of the Flowers is like that of the Wall-Flowers. These Plants are scarce in Virginia, but are every where in Carolina. They are likewise at Mr. Bacon's at Hoxton <note>Hoxton was a village just outside London known for its commercial nurseries and botanical gardens in the 18th century.</note>; where, by their thriving State, they seem to like our Soil and Climate. Tho' Mr. Parkinson calls it *evergreen* <note>original Latin: "semper virens"</note>, I have always found it lose its Leaves in Winter.

<sig>O</sig>

<column-break/>

## Blackish Fly-catcher. <note>original French: "Preneur de Mouches noirâtre"</note>

*THE beak is wide and black. The top of its head is a deep black. Its back, wings, and tail are brown; its breast and belly white with a shade of yellowish green. Its legs and feet are black. The head of the male is a darker black than that of the female; and they differ only in this. I do not remember seeing any of these birds during the winter. They feed on flies and other insects, and raise their young in Carolina.*

---

<br/>
<br/>
<br/>

*THIS plant grows ordinarily in moist soil. Its branches are supported by neighboring trees and bushes, upon which it climbs. Its leaves are arranged opposite one another from the junctions of the stems to their ends. The flowers are born between the stem and the branch. They are yellow and tube-shaped. Their extremities are cut into five parts. The seeds are flat, and winged on one side, enclosed in an oblong capsule ending in a point. When the seeds are ripe, this capsule opens by pulling back toward the stem, and lets them fall. The odor of these flowers is the same as that of yellow violets <note>original French: "violettes jaunes," Catesby uses this to mean Wall-flowers.</note>. This plant is rare in Virginia, but is found everywhere in Carolina. There are also some at Monsieur Bacon's in Hoxton, where they are in such good condition that it clearly appears our soil and our climate are not contrary to them. Although Mr. Parkinson calls this plant evergreen <note>original Latin: "semper virens"</note>, I have always found that it lost its leaves in winter.*

# THE BROWN FLY-CATCHER. <note>original Latin: "MUSCICAPA FUSCA"</note>

## The little brown Fly-catcher <note>This bird is now identified as the Eastern Phoebe (*Sayornis phoebe*), a migratory songbird known for its "fee-bee" call.</note>

<image-desc>Ornate decorative drop cap 'W' depicting a bird in a landscape with hills and water.</image-desc>Weighs nine Penny-weight <note>A pennyweight is an old unit of mass; nine pennyweights is approximately 14 grams.</note>. The Bill is very broad and flat ; the upper Mandible <note>The upper part of the beak.</note> black ; the lower yellow. All the Upper-part of the Body of a dark Ash-Colour. The Wings are brown, with some of the smaller Feathers edged with White : All the Under-part of the Body dusky white, with a Tincture of yellow : The Legs and Feet black.

# THE RED-EYED FLY-CATCHER. <note>original Latin: "MUSCICAPA OCULIS RUBRIS"</note>

## The red-Ey’d Fly-catcher <note>Now known as the Red-eyed Vireo (*Vireo olivaceus*). Although Catesby classifies it as a fly-catcher, modern ornithology places it in the vireo family.</note>

<image-desc>Ornate decorative drop cap 'W' depicting a man in a landscape standing near a large tree.</image-desc>Weighs ten Penny-weight and an half. The Bill Lead-Colour : The Iris of the Eyes red. From the Bill, over the Eyes, runs a dusky white Line, border’d above with a black Line. The Crown of the Head is gray. The Rest of the Upper-Part of the Body is green. The Neck, Breast and Belly white ; the Legs and Feet black. Both these breed in *Carolina*, and retire Southward in Winter.

# Tree with laurel-like leaves, flowers from the leaf-axils, five-petaled, and provided with many stamens. <note>original Latin: "Arbor lauri folio, floribus ex foliorum, alis pentapetalis, pluribus staminibus donatis." This plant is identified as the Leatherwood or Titi (*Cyrilla racemiflora*).</note>

<image-desc>Ornate decorative drop cap 'T' depicting a landscape under a cloudy sky.</image-desc>This Shrub has a slender Stem, and grows usually about 8 or 10 Feet high. Its Leaves are in Shape like those of a Pear, growing alternately on Foot-stalks of an Inch long ; from between which proceeds small whitish Flowers, consisting of five Petals ; in the Middle of which shoot forth many tall Stamina, headed with yellow Apices <note>Anthers, the pollen-bearing parts of the flower.</note>. The Roots of this Plant are made Use of in Decoctions <note>A concentrated liquor resulting from heating or boiling a substance.</note>, and are esteemed a good Stomachic <note>A medicine that promotes appetite or assists digestion.</note> and Cleanser of the Blood. The Fruit I have not seen. This Plant grows in moist and shady Woods, in the lower Parts of *Carolina*.

<column-break/>

## Little brown Fly-catcher. <note>original French: "Petit Preneur de Mouches brun."</note>

<image-desc>Ornate decorative drop cap 'E' depicting a bird perched in a landscape with a building in the distance.</image-desc>This bird weighs three drachms <note>original French: "dragmes." A drachm is roughly 3.9 grams; three drachms is approximately 11.7 grams, slightly different from the nine pennyweights listed in the English column.</note>. Its beak is very wide and flat. The upper mandible is black, the lower yellow. The whole upper part of its body is a dark ash color. Its wings are brown, except that some of the smallest feathers are bordered with white. The whole underside of its body is a dull white, with a shade of yellow: its legs and its feet are black.

## Red-eyed Fly-catcher. <note>original French: "Preneur de Mouches aux yeux rouges."</note>

<image-desc>Ornate decorative drop cap 'C' depicting a landscape with trees and a low horizon.</image-desc>This bird weighs a little more than three drachms. The iris of its eyes is red: from the beak to above the eyes extends a dull white stripe, bordered by a black line above. The top of its head is gray, and all the rest, down to the tail, is green. Its neck, its breast, and its belly are white; its legs and its feet are red <note>Note: The French text describes the feet as red ("rouges"), whereas the English column describes them as black.</note>. Both of these last species of fly-catchers raise their young in *Carolina*, and retreat toward the South in winter.

<image-desc>Ornate decorative drop cap 'C' depicting a landscape with rolling hills.</image-desc>This shrub has a very slender trunk. It ordinarily rises to a height of eight or ten feet. Its leaves resemble those of a pear tree; and are arranged alternately, on stems an inch long. From between the leaves emerge small whitish flowers, composed of five petals; from the middle of which emerge several long stamens, which have small yellow heads. One makes use of the root of this plant in decoction; and it is attributed with the virtue of purifying the blood and strengthening the stomach. I have not seen the fruit at all. This plant grows in marshy and covered woods, in the lowest places of *Carolina*.

<summary>This page provides physical descriptions and seasonal behaviors of the Eastern Phoebe and the Red-eyed Vireo, alongside a botanical profile of the Leatherwood shrub and its medicinal uses.</summary>

# THE BROWN FLY-CATCHER. <note>original Latin: "MUSCICAPA FUSCA"</note>

## The little brown Fly-catcher <note>This bird is now identified as the Eastern Phoebe (*Sayornis phoebe*), a migratory songbird known for its "fee-bee" call and habit of wagging its tail.</note>

<image-desc>Ornate decorative drop cap 'W' depicting a bird in a landscape with hills and water.</image-desc>Weighs nine Penny-weight <note>A pennyweight is an old unit of mass; nine pennyweights is approximately 14 grams.</note>. The Bill is very broad and flat; the upper Mandible <note>The upper part of the beak.</note> black; the lower yellow. All the Upper-part of the Body of a dark Ash-Colour. The Wings are brown, with some of the smaller Feathers edged with White: All the Under-part of the Body dusky white, with a Tincture of yellow: The Legs and Feet black.

# THE RED-EYED FLY-CATCHER. <note>original Latin: "MUSCICAPA OCULIS RUBRIS"</note>

## The red-Ey’d Fly-catcher <note>Now known as the Red-eyed Vireo (*Vireo olivaceus*). Although Catesby classifies it as a fly-catcher due to its diet, modern ornithology places it in the vireo family.</note>

<image-desc>Ornate decorative drop cap 'W' depicting a man in a landscape standing near a large tree.</image-desc>Weighs ten Penny-weight and an half <note>Approximately 16 grams.</note>. The Bill Lead-Colour: The Iris <note>The colored part of the eye surrounding the pupil.</note> of the Eyes red. From the Bill, over the Eyes, runs a dusky white Line, border’d above with a black Line. The Crown of the Head is gray. The Rest of the Upper-Part of the Body is green. The Neck, Breast and Belly white; the Legs and Feet black. Both these breed in *Carolina*, and retire Southward in Winter.

# THE LAUREL-LEAVED TREE. <note>original Latin: "ARBOR LAURI FOLIO"</note>

## Tree with laurel-like leaves, flowers from the leaf-axils, five-petaled, and provided with small fruits. <note>This refers to the Gum Bully, noted for its sturdy wood and the medicinal "gum" it produces.</note>

The Leaves are of a dark green, thick and stiff, and of the shape of those of the Laurel. The Flowers grow in clusters from the joints of the stalks, appearing in *July*. Each Flower is small and white, consisting of five petals. These are succeeded by small, round, black Berries, containing a single hard seed. This tree is found in the more southern parts of *Carolina*, near the sea-coast.

A hand-colored natural history engraving from Mark Catesby's "Natural History of Carolina, Florida and the Bahama Islands." ->*Scented male Cornel,*<- <note>original Latin: "Cornus mas Odorata"</note>

->*Red-crowned Flycatcher.*<- <note>original Latin: "Muscicapa Corona rubra"</note>
->*The Tyrant.*<-

# THE SASSAFRAS TREE <note>The Sassafras (*Sassafras albidum*) was a plant of immense interest to early European explorers due to its aromatic fragrance and supposed medicinal virtues; it was one of the first major exports from the New World to Europe.</note>

## *Cornus mas Odorata*

The Sassafras tree is distinguished by its varied leaf shapes—some simple ovals, others shaped like mittens, and some with three lobes—all appearing on the same branch. In the spring, it produces small clusters of yellow flowers, which are followed by dark blue berries held in striking red, cup-shaped bases. <note>These red "cups" are the pedicels, which provide a vivid contrast to the dark fruit, attracting birds.</note>

# THE TYRANT <note>Now known as the Eastern Kingbird (*Tyrannus tyrannus*).</note>

## *Muscicapa Corona rubra*

This bird is named **The Tyrant** for its exceptionally bold and aggressive nature. It famously defends its nesting territory against much larger birds, including crows and even hawks, diving at them until they are driven away. The name *Corona rubra* <note>Latin: "Red Crown"</note> refers to the hidden patch of bright orange-red feathers on its head, which it typically reveals only when excited or during a territorial display. 

The Tyrant belongs to the family of **Flycatchers**; it perches prominently on the outer branches of trees, such as this Sassafras, darting out with great agility to snatch insects from the air before returning to its post.

<summary>This page illustrates the Eastern Kingbird, known to 18th-century naturalists as "The Tyrant" for its aggressive behavior, alongside the aromatic Sassafras tree.</summary>
<keywords>Eastern Kingbird, Sassafras, Mark Catesby, Flycatchers, North American botany, colonial medicine, ornithology</keywords>

--- Page 183 ---
<meta>This page continues Mark Catesby’s "Natural History of Carolina, Florida and the Bahama Islands," following Plate 54 which described the Eastern Phoebe and the Red-eyed Vireo. Here, on Plate 55, Catesby illustrates the Eastern Kingbird and the Sassafras tree, continuing his study of North American birds and the flora they inhabit.</meta>

# RED-CROWNED FLYCATCHER. <note>original Latin: "Muscicapa Corona Rubra." This bird is now commonly known as the Eastern Kingbird (*Tyrannus tyrannus*).</note>

### The Tyrant.

The Bill is broad, flat and tapering. The Crown of the Head has a bright red Spot, environ'd with black Feathers; which, by contracting, conceals the Red; but, when they are spread, it appears with much Lustre, after the Manner of the *Goldcrest*. <note>original Latin: "Regulus cristatus," referring to the small Kinglet or Goldcrest bird of Europe which also has a hidden colorful crown.</note> The Back, Wings and Tail are brown; the Neck, Breast and Belly white; the Legs and Feet black. There appears little or no Difference between the Cock and Hen. They appear in *Virginia* and *Carolina* about *April*; where they breed, and retire at the Approach of Winter. The Courage of this little Bird is singular. He pursues and puts to Flight all kinds of Birds that come near his Station, from the smallest to the largest, none escaping his Fury; nor did I ever see any that dar'd to oppose him while flying; for he does not offer to attack them when sitting. I have seen one of them fix on the Back of an Eagle, and persecute him so, that he has turned on his Back into various Postures in the Air, in order to get rid of him, and at last was forc'd to alight on the Top of the next Tree, from whence he dared not move, till the little Tyrant was tired, or thought fit to leave him. This is the constant Practice of the Cock while the Hen is brooding: He sits on the Top of a Bush, or small Tree, not far from her Nest; near which if any small Birds approach, he drives them away; but the great Ones, as Crows, Hawks, and Eagles, he won't suffer to come within a Quarter of a Mile of him without attacking them. They have only a chattering Note, which they utter with great Vehemence all the Time they are fighting.

When their Young are flown, they are as peaceable as other Birds. It has a tender Bill, and feeds on Insects only. They are tame and harmless Birds. They build their Nest in an open manner on low Trees and Shrubs, and usually on the Sassafras-Tree.

<column-break/>

### The Tyrant. <note>original French: "Le Tyran." This column provides a translation of the French text, which mirrored Catesby's English description for his international audience.</note>

His beak is wide, flat, and tapers to a point. He has on his head a very brilliant red spot, surrounded by black feathers, which, by closing together, hide this spot; it reappears with radiance when these feathers are extended, like the Goldcrest. His back, his wings and his tail are brown; his neck, his breast and his belly white; his legs and his feet black. One sees little or no difference between the male and the female. He appears in Virginia and Carolina toward the month of April. He raises his young there, and retreats at the beginning of winter. The courage of this little bird is remarkable. He pursues and puts to flight all birds, small or large, who approach the place he has chosen. None escape his fury; and I have not even seen that other birds dared to resist him when he flies; for he does not attack them otherwise. I saw one who attached himself to the back of an eagle, and persecuted him in such a way that the eagle turned over on his back, and tried to deliver himself by the different postures he took in the air; but finally he was obliged to stop on the top of a neighboring tree, until this little tyrant was tired, or judged it appropriate to leave him. This is the ordinary maneuver of the male while the female broods. He perches on the summit of a bush or shrub near his nest; and if some small bird approaches it, he gives chase: but for the large ones, like crows, hawks, and eagles, he does not permit them to approach within a quarter of a mile of him without attacking them. His song is only a kind of cry that he pushes out with much force during all the time that he fights. When his young have taken flight, he becomes as sociable as other birds. As he has a tender beak, he feeds only on insects. He is gentle and without malice. He makes his nest completely in the open on shrubs and in bushes, and ordinarily on the Sassafras.

---

## Scented male Cornel, with a three-lobed leaf and smooth margin, called Sassafras. <note>original Latin: "Cornus mas odorata, folio trifido margine plano, Sassafras dicta." Catesby references Leonard Plukenet’s "Almagestum Botanicum," an influential botanical catalog of the time.</note>

This is generally a small Tree; the Trunc usually not a Foot thick. The Leaves are divided into three Lobes by very deep Incisures. <note>The Sassafras is famous for having three distinct leaf shapes on the same plant: oval, mitten-shaped, and three-lobed.</note> In *March* comes forth Bunches of small yellow Flowers with five Petals each; which are succeeded by Berries, in Size and Shape not unlike those of the Bay-Tree, hanging on red Foot-stalks, with a Calix <note>term: Calix (Calyx): The protective outer layer of a flower, which in the Sassafras forms a bright red cup holding the blue berry.</note> like that of an Acorn; which Calix is also red. The Berries are at first green, and, when ripe, blue. These Trees grow in most Parts of the Northern Continent of *America*, and generally on very good Land. The Virtue of this Tree is well known, as a great Sweetner of the Blood: <note>In the 18th century, Sassafras was highly valued in Europe as a "blood purifier" and a treatment for various ailments, including syphilis and skin diseases.</note> I shall therefore only add, that in *Virginia* a strong Decoction of the Root has been sometimes given with good Success for an intermitting Feaver. <note>term: intermitting Feaver: A fever that comes and goes at intervals, often associated with malaria in the colonial South.</note> This Tree will bear our Climate, as appears by Several now at Mr. *Collinson's* at *Peckham*, and at Mr. *Bacon's* in *Hoxton*; where they have withstood the Cold of several Winters. <note>Peter Collinson was a famous Quaker botanist and merchant in London who served as a central hub for the exchange of plants between the American colonies and England.</note>

<column-break/>

This tree is ordinarily small. Its trunk is hardly more than a foot in diameter. Its leaves are divided into three lobes by very deep notches. It pushes out in the month of March clusters of small yellow flowers, composed of five petals. They are followed by berries, which closely resemble, by their size and by their shape, those of the laurel. These berries are attached to red stalks, and have a cup like that of the acorn, which is red also. At first they are green; then blue when they are ripe. This tree grows in almost all the northern continent of America, and ordinarily in the best soil. It is well enough known how much this tree is fit to sweeten the Blood. I will only remark that sometimes in Virginia a strong decoction of its root has been employed with success in intermittent fevers. This tree accommodates itself to our climate; as it appears by several which are at Peckham at Mr. Collinson’s, and at Hoxton at Mr. Bacon’s, where they have sustained several winters.

<summary>Catesby describes the aggressive territorial behavior of the Eastern Kingbird (The Tyrant) and provides a botanical profile of the Sassafras tree, noting its medicinal uses and successful cultivation in English gardens.</summary>
<keywords>Eastern Kingbird, Tyrannus tyrannus, Sassafras albidum, medicinal plants, Virginia, Peter Collinson, ornithology, botany, colonial medicine, bird behavior</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Mark Catesby’s "Natural History of Carolina, Florida and the Bahama Islands." Page 55 concluded the description of the Eastern Kingbird (the "Tyrant"); here on page 56, Catesby introduces the Summer Red-Bird and the Western Plane Tree.</meta>

# RED FLYCATCHER. <note>original Latin: "Muscicapa Rubra." This bird is now known as the Summer Tanager (*Piranga rubra*).</note>

## The Summer Red-Bird.

THIS is about the Size of a Sparrow. It has large black Eyes. The Bill is thick and clumsy, and of a yellowish Cast. The whole Bird is of a bright Red, except the interior Vanes <term>the flat, web-like part of the feather</term> of the Wing-Feathers, which are brown, but appear not, unless the Wings are spread. They are Birds of Passage <note>A phrase used in the 18th century to describe migratory birds.</note>, leaving *Virginia* and *Carolina* in Winter. The Hen is brown, with a Tincture of yellow.

<column-break/>

## Red Flycatcher. <note>Translated from the French: "Preneur de Mouches Rouge."</note>

IT is about the size of a sparrow. It has large black eyes. Its beak is thick, coarse, and yellowish. The entire bird is a beautiful red, except for the interior fringes of the wing feathers which are brown; but they only appear when the wings are extended. It is a migratory bird, which leaves Carolina and Virginia in winter. The female is brown with a shade of yellow.

---

# *WESTERN PLANE TREE.* <note>original Latin: "Platanus Occidentalis." This is the American Sycamore.</note>

## THE WESTERN PLANE TREE.

THIS Tree usually grows very large and tall. Its Leaves are broad, of a light Green, and somewhat downy on the Back-side. Its Seed-Vessels are globular, hanging single and pendent on Foot-stalks of about four or five Inches long; The Fruit in the Texture of it, resembling that of the *Oriental Plane Tree*. <note>original Latin: "Platanus Orientalis," the species found in Eurasia.</note> The Bark is smooth and usually so variegated with White and Green, that they have a fine Effect amongst the other Trees. In *Virginia* they are plentifully found in all the lower Parts of the Country; but in *Carolina* there are but few, except on the hilly Parts, particularly on the Banks of *Savanna* River.

<column-break/>

## Western Plane Tree. <note>Translated from the French: "Platane Occidental."</note>

THIS tree is usually very high and very wide-spreading. Its leaves are broad, of a light green, and a little hairy underneath. The capsules, which contain its seed, are round. Each is attached and hanging to a pedicule <term>a small stalk</term> of about four or five inches long. Its fruit resembles that of the oriental plane tree. Its bark is smooth, and usually mixed with green and white, so that they produce a very beautiful effect among the other trees. In *Virginia* one finds a great number of these trees in all the low places; but in *Carolina* there are but few, except on the heights, especially on the banks of the Savannah River.

<summary>This page describes the Summer Tanager (referred to as the Summer Red-Bird) and the American Sycamore (the Western Plane Tree), detailing their physical appearances and geographic distribution across Virginia and Carolina.</summary>

## The Summer Red-Bird

THIS bird is about the size of a sparrow. It has large black eyes. The bill is thick and stout, and of a yellowish cast. The entire bird is a bright red, except for the interior vanes <term>the flat, web-like part of the feather</term> of the wing-feathers, which are brown, but do not appear unless the wings are spread. They are migratory birds <note>original: "Birds of Passage," a common 18th-century term for species that travel seasonally.</note>, leaving *Virginia* and *Carolina* in winter. The female <note>original: "Hen."</note> is brown, with a tint of yellow.

## Red Flycatcher <note>This section translates the French text often found in Catesby's original volumes: "Preneur de Mouches Rouge."</note>

IT is about the size of a sparrow. It has large black eyes. Its beak is thick, coarse, and yellowish. The entire bird is a beautiful red, except for the interior fringes of the wing feathers which are brown; but they only appear when the wings are extended. It is a migratory bird, which leaves Carolina and Virginia in winter. The female is brown with a shade of yellow.

# *WESTERN PLANE TREE* <note>original Latin: "Platanus Occidentalis." The American Sycamore is famous for its peeling, multicolored bark.</note>

## THE WESTERN PLANE TREE

THIS tree usually grows very large and tall. Its leaves are broad, of a light green, and somewhat downy on the back-side. Its seed-vessels are globular, hanging single and drooping on stalks of about four or five inches long. The fruit, in its texture, resembles that of the *Oriental Plane Tree* <note>original Latin: "Platanus Orientalis," the species found in Eurasia and often mentioned in classical literature.</note>. The bark is smooth and usually so variegated with white... <note>The text ends abruptly here, referring to the characteristic "camouflage" appearance of sycamore bark.</note>

<meta>This page continues Mark Catesby's "Natural History of Carolina, Florida and the Bahama Islands," following page 56's discussion of the Summer Red-Bird and the Western Plane Tree. Catesby continues his method of pairing a North American bird with a specific botanical specimen.</meta>

# CRESTED TITMOUSE. <note>original Latin: "PARUS CRISTATUS." Although Catesby uses the name of the European species, he is describing the Tufted Titmouse (*Baeolophus bicolor*).</note>

->The crested Titmouse.<-

IT weighs thirteen <term>Penny-weight</term> <note>A unit of mass in the troy weight system, roughly 1.55 grams.</note>. The Bill is black, having a Spot a little above it of the same Colour; except which, and all the Upper-part of the Body is gray. The Neck and all the Under-part of the Body is white, with a faint Tincture of Red, which just below the Wings is deepest. The Legs and Feet are of a Lead-Colour. It erects its Crown-Feathers into a pointed Crest. No Difference appears between the Cock and Hen. They breed in and inhabit *Virginia* and *Carolina* all the Year. They do not frequent near Houses, their Aboad being only amongst the Forest-Trees from which they get their Food; which is Insects.

---

# Virginian Cistus, with the flower and scent of Honeysuckle. Master Banister. <note>original Latin: "Cistus Virginiana, flore & odore Periclymeni. D. Banister." This plant is now known as the Swamp Azalea (*Rhododendron viscosum*). Catesby cites John Banister, an early English clergyman and naturalist in Virginia.</note>

->THE UPRIGHT HONEYSUCKLE.<-

THIS Plant rises usually with two or three stiff streight Stems, which are small, except where the Soil is very moist and rich; where they grow to the Size of a Walking Cane, twelve or sixteen Feet high, branching into many smaller Stalks, with Leaves alternately placed. At the Ends of the Stalks are produc'd Bunches of Flowers, resembling our common Hony-suckle; not all of a Colour, some Plants producing White, some red, and others purplish, of a very pleasant Scent, tho' different from ours. The Flowers are succeeded by long pointed <term>Capsulas</term> <note>Seed pods or capsules.</note>, containing innumerable very small Seeds. 'Tis a Native of *Virginia* and *Carolina*, but will endure our Climate in the open Air, having for some years past produc'd its beautiful and fragrant Blossoms at Mr. *Bacon*'s at *Hoxton*, and at Mr. *Collinson*'s at *Peckham* <note>Hoxton and Peckham were then villages near London famous for the gardens of botanical enthusiasts like Peter Collinson.</note>;

<sig>P</sig>

<column-break/>

->Crested Titmouse.<- <note>original French: "Mesange huppée."</note>

THIS bird weighs four <term>drams</term> <note>original French: "dragmes." An apothecary weight roughly equivalent to 3.9 grams.</note>. Its beak is black. A little above it there is a spot of the same color. Apart from that, all the upper part of the bird is gray. Its neck and all the underpart of its body is white, with a slight nuance of Red, which is stronger under the wings. Its legs and its feet are lead-colored. When it raises its crest, it ends in a point. There appears to be no difference between the male and the female. They produce their young in *Carolina* and *Virginia* and remain there all year. They hardly approach houses, and stay only in the forests, where they find the insects on which they feed.

---

->Upright Honeysuckle.<- <note>original French: "Chevre-feuille droit."</note>

THIS plant usually rises with two or three stems that are quite straight and stiff. They are slender, except when the soil is very rich and very moist; for then they reach the thickness of a Cane, and a height of twelve to sixteen feet, furnished with several small branches on which the leaves are arranged alternately. From the tips of these branches come clusters of flowers which resemble our common honeysuckle. These flowers are not all of the same color. Some plants produce white ones, others red, and others purplish. When the flowers have passed, they are succeeded by long and pointed *Capsules*, which contain an infinity of very small seeds. This plant is native to *Virginia* and *Carolina*, but it withstands our climate even in the open air. For several years it has produced its beautiful and fragrant flowers at the home of Mr. *Bacon* in *Hoxton*, and at the home of Mr. *Collinson* in *Peckham*.

<summary>This page provides descriptions of the Tufted Titmouse and the Swamp Azalea, emphasizing their physical traits, habitats in the American colonies, and the plant's successful introduction to English gardens. Mark Catesby highlights the bird's year-round residency in the forest and the plant's fragrant, varied blossoms.</summary>

<keywords>Tufted Titmouse, Swamp Azalea, Mark Catesby, Virginia flora, Carolina fauna, 18th-century botany, Peter Collinson, ornithology</keywords>

# TIT WITH A YELLOW RUMP <note>original Latin: "PARUS UROPYGEO LUTEO." Now classified as the Yellow-rumped Warbler (*Setophaga coronata*).</note>

### The Yellow-rump.
<image-desc>Ornamental drop cap 'T' containing a bird perched on a branch within a square floral-patterned border.</image-desc>
THIS is a Creeper, and seems to be of the Tit-kind. The most distinguish'd Part of this Bird is its Rump, which is yellow. All the Rest of the Feathers are brown, having a faint Tincture of Green. It runs about the Bodies of Trees, and feeds on Insects, which it pecks from the Crevisses of the Bark. The Hen differs little from the Cock in the Colour of its Feathers. They are found in *Virginia*.

## Helleborine with lily-like leaves surrounding the stem, with a single six-petaled flower; three petals long and narrow of a dark purple, the others shorter and rose-colored. <note>original Latin: "Helleborine Lilijfolio caulem ambiente, flore unico hexapetalo, tribus petalis longis angustis obscurè purpureis, cateris brevioribus roseis." This plant is likely the Rosebud Orchid (*Cleistesiopsis divaricata*).</note>

### The LILLY-LEAF'D HELLEBORE.
THIS Plant has a bulbous Root; from which arises a single Stem of about a Foot high, encompass'd by the Bottom-part of one Leaf as by a Sheath. At the Top grows the Flower, composed of six <term>Petala</term> <note>The individual leaves or segments of the corolla of a flower; petals.</note>; three of them long, and of a dark purple Colour; the other three shorter, of a pale Rose-Colour, and commonly turning back, with a <term>Pistillum</term> <note>The female organs of a flower, comprising the stigma, style, and ovary.</note> in the Middle. It grows in wet Places.

## Climbing Dog's-bane with heart-shaped leaves and white flowers. <note>original Latin: "Apocynum Scandens folio, cordato flore albo."</note>

### DOGS-BANE.
THIS Plant climbs upon and is supported by Shrubs and Trees near it. Its Leaves grow opposite to each other, on Foot-stalks less than an Inch long. The Flowers grow usually four or five in a Cluster, are white, and consist of five <term>Petala</term>, succeeded by long cylindrical Pods, growing by Pairs, containing many flat Seeds not unlike the Rest of the *Apocynums*. It grows on most of the *Bahama* Islands.

<column-break/>

### Titmouse with the yellow rump. <note>original French: "Mesange au croupion jaune."</note>
<image-desc>Ornamental drop cap 'C' depicting a bird in a landscape with a tree and foliage, within a square border.</image-desc>
THIS bird runs upon trees like the <note>original French: "piverd"</note> woodpecker, and appears to be a species of titmouse. What is most particular about it is its rump, which is yellow. All the rest of its feathers are brown, with a light tincture of green. It runs upon the trunks of trees, and feeds on insects that it pulls from the crevices of their bark. The female differs very little from the male in its color. It is found in *Virginia*.

### Hellebore. <note>original French: "Elleborine."</note>
THIS plant has a bulbous root, from which comes a single stem about one foot high. It is surrounded at the bottom by a single leaf which serves it as a sheath. The flower comes from the top of the stem. It is composed of six leaves <note>petals</note>; of which three are long and of a deep violet, the three others shorter, of a pale rose color, and usually turned back, with a pistil in the middle. This plant grows in humid places.

### Dog's-bane. <note>original French: "Apocyn."</note>
THIS plant climbs and is supported by the trees and bushes that are found near it. Its leaves are arranged opposite one another, on stalks that are less than an inch long. Its flowers are usually in clusters of four or five. They are white, and composed of five leaves <note>petals</note>. They are followed by long cylindrical pods, which come in pairs. They contain several flat seeds quite similar to those of other Dog's-banes. This plant is found in most of the *Bahama* islands.

# TIT WITH A YELLOW RUMP <note>original Latin: "PARUS UROPYGEO LUTEO." This bird is now known to modern science as the Yellow-rumped Warbler (*Setophaga coronata*).</note>

### The Yellow-rump.
<image-desc>Ornamental drop cap 'T' containing a bird perched on a branch within a square floral-patterned border.</image-desc>
THIS is a Creeper <note>Catesby uses "Creeper" to describe birds that forage by climbing or scurrying along tree trunks.</note>, and seems to be of the Tit-kind. The most distinguished part of this bird is its rump, which is yellow. All the rest of the feathers are brown, having a faint tincture of green. It runs about the bodies of trees and feeds on insects, which it pecks from the crevices of the bark. The hen <note>The female bird.</note> differs little from the cock in the color of its feathers. They are found in Virginia.

# THE BAHAMA TITMOUSE. <note>original Latin: "Parus Bahamiensis." This bird is now known as the Bahama Bananaquit (*Coereba flaveola bahamensis*).</note>

## The Bahama Titmouse.

The Bill of this Bird is black, and a little bending; the Upper-part of the Head, Back and Wings brown. A white Line runs from the Bill over the Eyes to the Back of the Head. The Breast is yellow, as are the Shoulders of the Wings. The Tail is somewhat long, having the Upper-part brown, and the Under dusky white.

---

->Jasmine Tree, with white flowers, leaves like the *Cenchramidea* <note>A term used by early botanists like Plukenet to describe plants with box-like leaves; likely referring to the Sea Ox-eye or similar coastal foliage</note>, oval fruit, and small black seeds wrapped in mucilage.<- <note>original Latin: "Arbor Jasmini, floribus albis, foliis Cenchranmideæ, fructu ovali, seminibus parvis nigris mucilagine involutis."</note>

## The Seven Years Apple. <note>Modern identification: *Casasia clusiifolia*, a hardy evergreen shrub native to the Caribbean and Florida.</note>

This Shrub grows from six to ten Feet high, with a Stem seldom bigger than ones Wrist, having a wrinkled light colour'd Bark. The Leaves grow in Clusters, and are about the Bigness of those of our common Laurel, having a wide Notch or Indenture at the End, which is broadest. These Leaves are very thick and stiff, and usually curl up as the Figure represents. The Flowers grow in Bunches; are monopetalous; and in Form and Size resemble our common Jessamin; white in Colour with a faint Tincture of Red. The Fruit hangs by a Foot-stalk of an Inch long, of an Oval Form, the Outside being shaded with Green, Red and Yellow. When ripe, it is of the Consistence of a mellow Pear, containing a pulpy Matter, in Colour, Substance and Tast not unlike the <term>Cassia fistula</term> <note>Also known as the Golden Shower tree; its seed pods contain a dark, sweet, medicinal pulp used historically as a laxative.</note>. For nine Months I observ'd a continual Succession of Flowers and Fruit, which ripens in seven or eight Months. I know not for what Reason the Inhabitants of the *Bahama*-Island (where it grows) call it the Seven Years Apple.

<column-break/>

## The Bahama Titmouse. <note>Translated from the original French: "Mesange de Bahama."</note>

The beak of this bird is black and a little curved. The top of its head, its back, and its wings are brown. A white stripe extends from its beak to the back of its head. Its breast is yellow, as is the top of its wings. Its tail is quite long, brown on top and a dull white underneath.

---

## Seven Years Apple. <note>Translated from the original French: "Pomme de sept ans."</note>

This shrub rises to six or seven feet. Its trunk is hardly thicker than a wrist. Its bark is wrinkled and light in color. Its leaves come in clusters, and are about the size of those of the common laurel. They have a large notch at their tip, which is wider than the rest of the leaf. These leaves are strong, stiff, and very thick; and usually they fold back, as is indicated in the figure. Its flowers come in clusters. They are monopetalous <note>Having the petals fused into a single tube or shape</note> and resemble our common jasmine in their form and size. Their color is white, mixed with a little red. Its fruit hangs from a pedicle an inch long. Its shape is oval; and its color is mixed with green and yellow. When it is ripe, it has the consistency of a soft pear. It contains a pulp, which in color, substance, and taste, is quite similar to the <term>Cassia</term>. I have observed for nine months, in this plant, a continual succession of flowers and fruits, which ripen in the space of seven or eight months. Thus I do not know why the inhabitants of the Bahama Islands, where this plant grows, name it the seven years apple.

<vocab>Parus Bahamiensis, Bahama Titmouse, Mesange de Bahama, Seven Years Apple, Pomme de sept ans, Arbor Jasmini, Cassia fistula, Bahama-Island</vocab>

<meta>Following the description of the Bahama Titmouse and the Seven Years Apple on page 59, this page continues the catalog of North American flora and fauna, shifting focus back to the mainland of Carolina. # THE TITMOUSE WITH A BLACK HOOD. <note>original Latin: "Parus Cucullo Nigro." This bird is known today as the Hooded Warbler (*Setophaga citrina*).</note>

## The Hooded Titmouse.

This bird is approximately the size of a Goldfinch, with a black bill. A broad black band encircles the neck and the back of the head, resembling a hood; except for this, the front of the head and the entire underside of the body are yellow. The back, wings, and tail are a dull olive-green. These birds inhabit thickets and shaded areas in the unsettled regions of Carolina.

# THE TREE GROWING IN THE WATER, WITH BROAD POINTED AND TOOTHED LEAVES, AND FRUIT LARGER THAN THE WILD OLIVE. <note>original Latin: "Arbor in aqua naſcens, folijs latis acuminatis & dentatis, fructu Eleagni majore." The term *Eleagni* refers to the Oleaster or Wild Olive.</note>

## The Water-Tupelo. <note>Scientific name: *Nyssa aquatica*.</note>

This tree features a massive trunk, particularly near the ground, and reaches a great height. The leaves are broad and marked with irregular notches or teeth. Its flowers emerge from the sides of the branches on stalks about three inches long. These consist of several small, narrow, greenish <term>petals<note>the individual segments of a flower’s corolla</note></term> situated at the top of an oval base, which is the developing fruit; at the bottom of this base, the <term>perianth<note>the outer envelope of a flower, including the calyx</note></term> divides into four parts. When fully mature, the fruit matches a small Spanish olive in size, shape, and color, and contains a single hard, grooved pit. The grain of the wood is white, soft, and porous. The roots are even more so, possessing a consistency similar to cork; for this reason, they are used in Carolina as cork substitutes for plugging gourds and bottles. These trees are always found in wet environments, typically in the shallow reaches of rivers and in swamps.

<column-break/>

## The Titmouse with a Black Hood. <note>translated from original French: "Meſange au Capuchon noir"</note>

This bird is roughly the size of a goldfinch. Its beak is black. A broad black stripe surrounds its neck and the back of its head, looking like a hood. Aside from that, the front of its head and the entire upper <note>The French text uses "dessus" (upper), whereas the English column specifies the "under-part" of the body is yellow. Observation of the species confirms the underside is yellow.</note> part of its body is yellow. Its back, wings, and tail are a dingy green. It frequents small woods and shaded places in the uninhabited parts of Carolina.

## The Tupelo, which grows in the water. <note>translated from original French: "Tupelo, qui croît dans l'eau"</note>

This tree has a very large trunk, especially near the ground. It becomes very tall. Its leaves are broad, with irregular notches. Its flowers are born from the sides of its branches. They are attached to <term>pedicels<note>small stalks bearing an individual flower</note></term> about three inches long and consist of several small, narrow, greenish leaves placed on the top of an oval body, which is the beginning of the fruit; at the bottom of which is the calyx, which is divided into four. When this fruit has reached its maturity, it resembles a small Spanish olive in its size, shape, and color, and contains a hard and grooved stone. The wood of this tree has a white, soft, and spongy grain. Its roots are much more so, approaching the consistency of cork; they are also used in Carolina for the same purposes, such as corking bottles and gourds. These trees always grow in damp places, and usually in the shallower parts of rivers and in marshes.

<summary>This page describes the Hooded Warbler (identified as a Titmouse) and the Water-Tupelo tree, detailing their appearance and the tree's utility as a cork substitute in colonial Carolina.</summary>
<keywords>Hooded Warbler, Water-Tupelo, Carolina flora and fauna, colonial botany, Nyssa aquatica, Setophaga citrina, cork substitutes</keywords>

# THE TREE GROWING IN THE WATER, WITH BROAD POINTED AND TOOTHED LEAVES, AND FRUIT LARGER THAN THE WILD OLIVE. <note>original Latin: "Arbor in aqua nascens, folijs latis acuminatis & dentatis, fructu Eleagni majore." The term *Eleagni* refers to the Oleaster or Wild Olive.</note>

<meta>This page continues Mark Catesby’s documentation of the flora and fauna of North America. # THE YELLOWISH AMERICAN TITMOUSE. <note>original Latin: "Parus Americanus Lutescens." This bird is known today as the Pine Warbler (*Setophaga pinus*).</note>

## The Pine-creeper

It weighs eight penny-weight and five grains <note>About 12.7 grams; a "penny-weight" is a jeweler's measurement used here for precision.</note>. The bill is black. The upper part of the body, from the bill to the tail, is of a yellowish green. The neck and breast are yellow. The belly near the tail is white. The wings are brown, with some spots of white. The tail is brown; except for the two outermost feathers, which are half white. The legs are a dusky black. The hen <note>The female bird.</note> is brown all over. They creep about trees, particularly pine and fir trees, from which they peck insects and feed on them. These, along with most of the other creepers and titmice, associate together in small flights <note>Small flocks.</note>, and are mostly seen on leafless trees in winter.

---

# PRIVET WITH BAY LEAVES AND VIOLET FRUIT. <note>original Latin: "Ligustrum Lauri folio, fructu violaceo." This is likely the Carolina Laurel Cherry (*Prunus caroliniana*).</note>

## The PURPLE-BERRIED BAY.

This tree usually grows sixteen feet high, and the trunk is from six to eight inches in diameter. The leaves are very smooth and of a brighter green than the common bay tree; otherwise, in shape and manner of growing, it resembles it. In March, from between the leaves, spikes shoot forth that are two or three inches in length, consisting of small white flowers with four petals <note>original: "tetrapetalous."</note>, growing opposite to each other on foot-stalks half an inch long. The fruit that follows are globular berries, about the size of those of the bay tree, and covered with a purple-colored skin, enclosing a kernel which divides in the middle.

<column-break/>

## The Brown American Titmouse. <note>This column is a translation of the French text, which serves as a parallel description to the English on the left.</note>

This bird weighs about three drachms <note>An old measurement of weight; Catesby uses "penny-weight" in the English and "drachmes" in the French.</note>. Its beak is black. The upper part of its body, from the beak to the tail, is a yellowish green. Its neck and its breast are yellow; and the underside of its belly, toward the tail, is white. Its wings are brown, with some white spots. Its tail is brown, except that the two feathers that terminate it are half white. Its legs are a dirty white. The female is entirely brown. This bird climbs on the trunks of trees, particularly pines and firs, from which it pulls insects, on which it feeds. It associates in small flocks; and it is seen, especially during the winter, on trees stripped of their leaves, just like the other species of titmice and creepers.

## Privet with violet berries.

This tree usually grows to the height of sixteen feet, and its trunk is from six to eight inches in diameter. Its leaves are very smooth and of a more vivid green than those of the common laurel. Otherwise, in its manner of growth and its form, it entirely resembles it. In the month of March, there emerge from between its leaves spikes of two or three inches in length, covered with very small white flowers, composed of four leaves <note>In 18th-century botanical English and French, "leaves" or "feuilles" was often used to mean "petals."</note>, and which are attached, one opposite the other, by stalks half an inch long. The fruits which succeed them are round berries, about the same size as those of the laurel. They are covered with a violet skin and contain a kernel which separates through the middle.

<summary>This page provides a detailed natural history of the Pine-creeper (Pine Warbler) and the Purple-Berried Bay (Carolina Laurel Cherry), noting their physical characteristics, habits, and seasonal cycles.</summary>
<keywords>Pine-creeper, Pine Warbler, Purple-Berried Bay, Carolina Laurel Cherry, American birds, colonial botany, Mark Catesby</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the catalog of North American species by Mark Catesby. # THE YELLOW-THROATED AMERICAN TITMOUSE. <note>original Latin: "Parus Americanus Gutture Luteo." This bird is known today as the Yellow-throated Warbler (*Setophaga dominica*).</note>

### The Yellow-throated Creeper

<image-desc>Decorative initial W containing a landscape with a house, a bridge over a stream, and trees.</image-desc>
WEIGHS seven penny-weight <note>Approximately 10.9 grams; a "penny-weight" was a common unit for weighing small objects like jewelry or birds.</note>. The bill is black. The fore-part of the head is black, having two yellow spots on each side, next the upper mandible <note>The upper part of the bird's beak.</note>. The throat is of a bright yellow, bordered on each side with a black list <note>A stripe or band of color.</note>. The back and hind-part of the head are grey. The wings are of a darker grey, inclining to brown, with some of their covert feathers edged with white. The under-part of the body is white, with black spots on each side, next the wings. The tail is black and white. The feet are brown; and, like those of the *Certhia* <note>The genus name for Treecreepers, birds known for climbing bark.</note>, have very long claws, which assist them in creeping about trees in search of insects, on which they feed. There is neither black nor yellow upon the **hen** <note>The female bird.</note>. They are frequent in *Carolina*.

---

# VIRGINIA MAPLE WITH LARGE LEAVES, SILVERY BENEATH AND SHINING GREEN ABOVE. <note>original Latin: "Acer Virginianum, folio majore, subtus argenteo, suprà viridi splendente." This refers to Leonard Plukenet’s description in his *Almagestum Botanicum*, a famous 17th-century botanical catalog.</note>

### The RED FLOWERING MAPLE. <note>Now known as the Red Maple (*Acer rubrum*).</note>

THESE trees grow to a considerable height; but their trunks are not often very large. In *February*, before the leaves appear, the little red blossoms open, and continue in flower about three weeks; and are then succeeded by the keys <note>The winged seeds of the maple tree, formally called samaras.</note>, which are also red, and, with the flowers, continue about six weeks, adorning the woods earlier than any other forest-trees in *Carolina*. They endure our *English* climate as well as they do their native one; as appears by many large ones in the garden of Mr. *Bacon* at *Hoxton* <note>Hoxton was then a village outside London famous for its commercial nurseries and the private gardens of wealthy botanical collectors.</note>.

<column-break/>

### The American Titmouse with a yellow throat. <note>Translation of the French title: "Mesange de l'Amerique à la gorge jaune."</note>

<image-desc>Decorative initial E containing a rural scene with a shepherd holding a crook, standing with sheep.</image-desc>
IT weighs about two and a half drams <note>original: "deux drachmes & demie." A "drachme" or dram is roughly 3.9 grams, making this weight about 9.75 grams, slightly different from the English column's measurement.</note>. Its bill is black. The front of its head is black. It has two yellow spots on each side, just below the upper mandible. Its throat is a brilliant yellow, ended on each side by a black stripe. Its back and the back of its head are grey. Its wings are of a darker grey and almost brown; some of their large feathers are edged with white. The underside of its body is white, with some black spots on each side, near the wings. Its tail is black and white. Its feet are brown; and, just like those of the little Treecreeper <note>original: "Grimpereau."</note>, they are armed with very long claws; which serves it well in climbing trees to search for the insects on which it feeds. The female has neither yellow nor black. This titmouse is very common in Carolina.

---

### Red-flowered Maple. <note>Translation of the French title: "Erable aux fleurs rouges."</note>

THESE trees grow to a considerable height, but their trunks are rarely very thick. In the month of *February*, before the leaves appear, their small red flowers begin to open, and last about three weeks; after which they are followed by the fruits <note>Referring to the winged seeds.</note>, which are also red, and last with the flowers about six weeks. These trees embellish the woods of *Carolina* earlier than any other that grows in the forests. They can suffer the climate of *England* as well as their own; as it appears by several beautiful trees of this species which are in the garden of Mr. *Bacon* at *Hoxton*.

->*The Yellow Titmouse*<- <note>original Latin: "Parus luteus." This bird is known today as the Prothonotary Warbler (*Protonotaria citrea*).</note>

->*The Red Bay*<- <note>original Latin: "Laurus Carolinensis." This tree is known today as the Red Bay (*Persea borbonia*), a member of the laurel family.</note>

# THE YELLOW TITMOUSE.

THIS bird is about the same size as a common Titmouse. The bill is black; the head, neck, and breast are of a very beautiful, vibrant yellow; the wings, back, and tail are of a dark copper-color, or perhaps more accurately a greenish-brown; the belly is white. These birds are seen in *Carolina* during the summer months. <note>Catesby often grouped small, active songbirds under the general heading of "titmice" or "creepers" before modern bird families were fully established.</note>

---

# THE RED BAY.

THIS tree rises usually to a great height, and the trunk grows to a large size, making it one of the more prominent trees in the forests of *Carolina*. The leaves are of a long, oval shape, similar to those of the common European Laurel, and are a dark, shining green. The flowers are small and white, and are succeeded by berries of a dark blue, which are held in bright red cups supported by long red stalks <note>These "red cups" are the pedicels, which become strikingly colorful as the fruit ripens.</note>. The wood is very fine-grained and is highly valued for making cabinets and other furniture, as well as for ship-building, due to its durability.

# THE YELLOW CAROLINA TITMOUSE. <note>original Latin: *Parus Carolinensis Luteus*</note>

->The Yellow Titmouse.<-
<column-break/>
->The Yellow Titmouse.<- <note>original French: *Mésange jaune*</note>

IT is smaller than a Wren. It appears entirely yellow; but, upon closer inspection, is as follows. The bill is slender. The head, breast, and belly are a bright yellow. The back is a greenish-yellow. The tail is brown, mixed with yellow. The female is not as bright a yellow as the male. This bird breeds in Carolina, but migrates away at the approach of winter. <note>This bird is now known as the Prothonotary Warbler (*Protonotaria citrea*), famous for its brilliant golden-yellow plumage.</note>
<column-break/>
IT is smaller than a Wren. At first glance it appears all yellow; but by examining it closely, one finds it to be as follows. Its bill is thin. Its head, its breast, and its belly are a vivid yellow. Its back is a greenish-yellow; its tail brown, with a shade of yellow. The female is not of such a brilliant yellow as the male. They raise their young in Carolina and depart at the beginning of winter.

---

### Carolina Laurel, with pointed leaves and blue berries sitting on long red stalks. <note>original Latin: *Laurus Carolinensis, folijs acuminatis, baccis cæruleis, pediculis longis rubris insidentibus*</note>

->The RED BAY.<-
<column-break/>
->The RED BAY.<- <note>original French: *Laurier rouge*</note>

THE leaves of this tree are shaped like those of the common Bay <note>referring to the Mediterranean Bay Laurel, *Laurus nobilis*</note>, and have an aromatic scent. The berries, when ripe, are blue, growing two, and sometimes three together, on foot-stalks two or three inches long. These stalks are red, as is the <term>calyx</term> <note>the cup-like base of the fruit</note>, which is notched around the edges. These trees are not common in Virginia, except in some places near the sea. In Carolina they are seen everywhere, particularly in low, swampy lands. In general, they only reach the size of small trees and shrubs; though on some islands and specific places near the sea, they grow into large trees with straight trunks. The wood is fine-grained and excellent for making cabinets and other furniture. I have seen some of the best of this wood selected that resembled <term>watered satin</term> <note>original: "Water'd Sattin" / "Satin ondé" — wood with a wavy, shimmering grain similar to moiré silk</note>; it exceeded in beauty any other kind of wood I have ever seen.
<column-break/>
THE leaves of this tree have the same shape as those of the common Laurel and have an aromatic odor. Its berries, when they are ripe, are blue: they come in pairs, and sometimes three by three, attached to pedicles two or three inches long, and red; the same as the calyx of the fruit, whose edges are indented. These trees are not common in Virginia, except in some places near the sea. One sees them everywhere in Carolina, mainly in the low and marshy lands. In general they hardly become more than small trees, though on some islands and in some particular places near the sea, one sees very large and very straight ones. Their wood is of a fine grain, and of excellent use for cabinets, etc. I have seen some chosen pieces of this wood which resembled waved satin, and whose beauty was above that of any other wood I have ever seen.

# THE FINCH-CREEPER <note>original Latin: *Parus Fringillaris*</note>

## The Finch-Creeper.

IT weighs five Penny-weight. <note>A pennyweight is a historical unit of mass equal to 24 grains or 1/20 of a troy ounce, approximately 1.55 grams.</note> The upper Mandible of the Bill is brown. The Under, yellow. The Head blue. It hath a white Spot over, and another under each Eye. The Upper-part of the Back is of a yellowish Green. The Rest of the Upper-part of the Body, Wings, and Tail, of a dusky Blue; the scapular Feathers <note>The feathers covering the "shoulders" of a bird.</note> having some white Spots. The Throat is yellow. The Breast, of a deeper yellow, divided by a dark blue List. <note>In this context, a "list" refers to a stripe or band of color.</note> The Belly white. Near the Breast some Feathers are stain'd with Red. The Feet are dusky yellow. These Birds creep about the Trunks of large Trees; and feed on Insects, which they gather from the Crevices of the Bark. They remain the Winter in Carolina. <note>This bird is the Northern Parula (*Setophaga americana*), a small warbler known for its "creeping" behavior while foraging for insects.</note>

---

## Shrub with Bird-Cherry leaves, not serrated; with white, single-petaled, bell-shaped flowers; and a thick four-angled fruit. <note>original Latin: *Frutex, Padi foliis non serratis, floribus monopetalis albis, campani-formibus, fructu crasso tetragono*</note>

THE Trunc of this Shrub is slender. Sometimes two or three Stems rise from the same Root to the Height usually of ten Feet. The Leaves are in Shape like Those of a Pear. In February and March, come white Flowers, in Form of a Bell, hanging usually two and three together, on Inch-long Foot-Stalks, from the Sides of the Branches. From the Middle of the Flower shoot forth four Stamina, with a Stylus extending half an Inch beyond them, of a reddish Colour. These Flowers are succeeded by oblong quadrangular Seed-Vessels, pointed at the Ends. <note>This plant is the Carolina Silverbell (*Halesia carolina*), prized for its drooping, bell-like blossoms.</note>

<column-break/>

## The Finch-Titmouse <note>original French: *Mesange-Pinson*</note>

THIS bird weighs a little less than two drams. <note>original French: *drachmes*. This refers to the apothecary dram, roughly equivalent in weight to the English pennyweight mentioned in the first column.</note> The upper mandible of its beak is brown; the lower is yellow. Its head is blue. It has a white spot above and another below each eye. The top of its back is of a yellowish green. All the lower part of its back, its wings, and its tail are of a dark blue. The feathers that cover the upper part of its wings have some white spots. Its throat is yellow. Its breast is of a darker yellow, divided by a dark blue stripe. Its belly is white. Near the breast it has some feathers stained with red. Its feet are a dark yellow. These birds climb on the trunks of large trees and feed on insects which they pull from between the crevices of their bark. They remain during the whole winter in Carolina.

THE trunk of this shrub is thin. Sometimes there rise from the same root two or three stems at once, usually to the height of ten feet. Its leaves have the shape of those of the pear tree. In February and March it puts forth white flowers in the shape of a bell: They hang from the sides of the branches by stalks an inch long, to which they are attached two or three together. There comes out from the middle of the flower four stamens with a red piston <note>An early term for the pistil, the female reproductive part of the flower.</note> which passes them by half an inch. To these flowers succeed seeds enclosed in oblong capsules with four angles, and ending in a point.
