## CAROLINA HONEY-EATING BIRD <note>original Latin: *Mellivora Avis Carolinensis*</note>
### The Humming-Bird.

THERE is but one kind of this bird in *Carolina*, which in the summer frequents the northern continent as far as *New England*. The body is about the size of a bumblebee. The bill is straight, black, and three-quarters of an inch long. The eyes are black; the upper part of the body and head are of a shining green; the whole throat is adorned with feathers placed like the scales of fish, of a crimson metallic resplendency; the belly is a dusky white; the wings are of a singular shape, not unlike the blade of a <term>Turkish scimitar</term> <note>A curved, single-edged oriental sword.</note>; the tail is of a <term>copper-color</term>, except the uppermost feather, which is green. The legs are very short and black. It receives its food from flowers, after the manner of bees; its tongue being a tube, through which it sucks the honey from them. It so balances itself by the quick hovering of its wings, that it seems to be without motion in the air. They rove from flower to flower, on which they wholly subsist. I never observed nor heard that they feed on any other thing than flowers. They breed in *Carolina*, and retire at the approach of winter.

What *Léry* and *Thevet* <note>Jean de Léry (1534–1613) and André Thevet (1516–1590) were French explorers who wrote early accounts of Brazil.</note> say of their singing is just as true as what is said of the harmonious song of the swan, as *Marcgraf* <note>Georg Marcgraf (1610–1644), a German naturalist who studied the biology of Brazil.</note> truly observes.

*Hernández* <note>Francisco Hernández de Toledo (1514–1587), a physician and naturalist who cataloged the plants and animals of Mexico.</note> bespeaks the credit of his reader, saying it is no idle tale when he affirms the manner of their lying torpid, or sleeping, all winter in *Hispaniola* and many other places between the tropics. I have seen these birds all the year round there, there being a perpetual succession of flowers for them to subsist on.

<column-break/>

### The Hummingbird <note>original French: *Le Colibri*</note>

There is only one species of this bird in *Carolina*, which moves toward the north during the summer, as far as *New England*. Its body is about the size of a *bumblebee*. Its bill is straight, black, and three-quarters of an inch long. Its eyes are black: its head and the upper part of its body are a very vivid green. Its entire throat is adorned with feathers placed like the scales of a fish, as brilliant as crimson enamel. Its belly is a dull white. Its wings are of a peculiar shape, quite similar to the blade of a <term>Turkish scimitar</term>. Its tail is the color of <term>copper</term>, except the middle feather, which is green. Its legs are short and black. It draws its nourishment from flowers, from which it sucks the honey. It balances itself in such a manner by the rapid movement of its wings that it seems to support itself without motion. It flies from flower to flower, for it is only from them that it draws its nourishment, and I have never observed, nor even heard it said, that it feeds on any insect, or anything other than flowers. They raise their young in *Carolina* and retreat at the beginning of winter. What *Léry* and *Thevet* attribute to their song is as true as what is said of the harmonious song of swans; *Marcgraf* has noted this very well.

*Hernández* tries to gain the confidence of his readers by telling them that it is not a tall tale when he assures them that it remains numb, or sleeping, throughout the winter in *Saint-Domingue* <note>The French name for the island of Hispaniola.</note> and several other places between the *Tropics*. I have seen these birds there throughout the entire year, because they find a continuous succession of flowers from which they feed.

---

## BIGNONIA WITH ASH-TREE LEAVES AND SMALLER SCARLET FLOWER <note>original Latin: *Bignonia, Fraxini folijs, coccineo flore minore*</note>
### The Trumpet-Flower.

THESE plants climb upon trees, on which they run to a great height; and are frequently seen to cover the trunks of tall trees. The leaves are winged, consisting of many serrated <note>leaf edges that are notched or saw-toothed</note> lobes, standing by couples, opposite to each other on one rib. In *May, June, July* and *August*, it produces bunches of red flowers, somewhat like the common foxglove. Each flower shoots from a long reddish-colored <term>calyx</term> <note>The outer leafy part of a flower that holds the petals.</note>; is monopetalous <note>composed of a single petal or having petals fused into a tube</note>, swelling in the middle, and opens at the top into five lips, with one pointal <note>an archaic term for the pistil, the seed-bearing organ of a flower</note> arising from the <term>calyx</term>, through the middle of the flower. In *August*, the pods or seed-vessels appear. They are, when full grown, eight inches long, narrow at both ends, and divide into two equal parts, from top to bottom, discovering many flat winged seeds. The hummingbirds delight to feed on these flowers; and, by thrusting themselves too far into the flower, are sometimes caught.

<column-break/>

### Bignonia, etc.

THESE plants climb up trees, upon which they rise to a great height. One often sees them covering the dead trunks of large trees. Their leaves are winged and are formed of several serrated lobes, attached in pairs, one across from the other, on the same rib. In *May, June, July* and *August*, they produce clusters of red flowers, quite similar to those of the common foxglove. Each flower comes out of a long red <term>calyx</term>. It is monopetalous, swollen in its middle. Upon opening, it is divided into five parts, with a pistil that originates in the <term>calyx</term> and passes through the middle of the flower. In the month of *August*, the pods, or the vessels that contain the seed, begin to appear. When they have reached their maturity, they are eight inches long, narrow at both ends; they divide into two equal parts and reveal a great number of flat, winged seeds.

The Hummingbird loves to feed on these flowers; and often, by plunging too far into them, they allow themselves to be caught.

<summary>Mark Catesby describes the Ruby-throated Hummingbird’s unique physical traits and feeding habits, specifically its interaction with the Trumpet-Flower (Campsis radicans). He also corrects contemporary myths regarding the bird's song and winter hibernation.</summary>
<keywords>Ruby-throated Hummingbird, Trumpet Creeper, Mark Catesby, nectar, pollination, bird migration, botanical illustration, Carolina, natural history</keywords>

# BLACK-CROWNED FLYCATCHER <note>original Latin: *Muscicapa vertice nigro*</note>

## The Cat-Bird.

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial letter T containing a bird perched on a branch amidst foliage.</image-desc>THIS Bird is about the Size of or somewhat bigger than a Lark. The Crown of the Head is black; the Upper-part of the Body, Wings and Tail, dark brown; particularly the Tail approaches nearest to Black. The Neck, Breast, and Belly, are of a lighter Brown. From the <term>Vent</term> <note>The area around the cloaca or under-tail coverts.</note>, under the Tail, shoot forth some Feathers, of a dirty Red <note>This refers to the "rusty" undertail coverts characteristic of the Gray Catbird (*Dumetella carolinensis*).</note>. This Bird is not seen on lofty Trees; but frequents Bushes and Thickets; and feeds on Insects. It has but one Note; which resembles the Mewing of a Cat, and which has given it its Name. It lays a blue Egg; and retires from *Virginia* in Winter.

## AMERICAN ALDER-LEAVED SAW-TOOTHED SHRUB, WITH FIVE-PETALED WHITE FLOWERS ARRANGED IN A SPIKE <note>original Latin: *Alni folia Americana serrata, floribus pentapetalis albis, in spicam dispositis*</note>

### Plukenet, Phytographia, Plate 115, Figure 1. <note>A reference to Leonard Plukenet (1642–1706), a prominent English botanist.</note>

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial letter T with decorative floral and scroll patterns.</image-desc>THIS Shrub <note>Commonly known today as Sweet Pepperbush or Summer-sweet (*Clethra alnifolia*).</note> grows in moist Places, and sometimes in Water, from which it rises, with many slender Stems, to the Height of ten or fourteen Feet. The Leaves are somewhat rough, placed alternately, <term>serrated</term> <note>Having a saw-toothed edge.</note>, and in Shape not unlike Those of the White Thorn <note>The Hawthorn (*Crataegus*).</note>. In *July* there shoots from the Ends of the Branches, Spikes of white Flowers, four or five Inches long. Each Flower consists of five Petals and a Tuft of small <term>Stamina</term> <note>The pollen-producing parts of the flower.</note>. These Flowers are thick set on Foot-stalks a Quarter of an Inch long; and are succeeded by small oval pointed <term>Capsula’s</term> <note>Seed pods.</note>, containing many chaffy Seeds. This Plant endures our Climate in the open Air, and flourishes at Mr. *Bacon’s* in *Hoxton* <note>Hoxton was an area in London famous for its nurseries and botanical gardens in the 18th century.</note>.

<column-break/>

## The Cat-Bird <note>original French: *Le Chat-oiseau*</note>

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial letter C containing a bird in flight or among floral scrolls.</image-desc>THIS bird is as large as, and even slightly larger than, a Lark. The top of its head is black; the top of its body, its wings, and its tail are a dark brown: Above all its tail comes closest to black. Its neck, its breast, and its belly are of a lighter brown. From its <term>anus</term> <note>Translated from the French 'anus', referring again to the vent or undertail area.</note>, under its tail, there emerge some feathers of a dull red. One does not see this bird on tall trees. It frequents only shrubs and bushes, and feeds on insects. It has only one tone in its voice, which resembles the mewing of a cat; and it is from this that it has taken its name. It lays a blue egg; and leaves Virginia in winter.

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial letter C with decorative floral and scroll patterns.</image-desc>THIS shrub grows in humid places, and sometimes in water, from which it rises with several slender stems to the height of ten or fourteen feet. Its leaves are a little rough, placed alternately, indented, and nearly the shape of those of the white thorn. In the month of July, it pushes from the tops of these branches clusters of white flowers, five or six inches long. Each flower is composed of five leaves <note>In 18th-century botany, petals were often referred to as "leaves" of the flower.</note>, and a tuft of small stamens. These flowers are strongly attached by pedicles a quarter of an inch long. They are followed by small oval and pointed capsules, which contain several light seeds. This plant tolerates our climate, even in the open air; and flowers at Mr. Bacon's in Hoxton.

# THE AMERICAN REDSTART AND THE BLACK WALNUT

MC <note>The etched monogram of the author and artist, Mark Catesby.</note>

*The American Redstart* <note>original Latin: *Ruticilla Americana*. The name "Ruticilla" is derived from the Latin *rutilus* (red or golden-red) and the scientific suffix *cilla* (tail), referring to the bird's distinctive orange-red tail patches.</note>                                                                     *The Black Walnut* <note>original Latin: *Juglans Nigra*. The genus name *Juglans* is a contraction of the Latin *Jovis glans*, meaning "Jupiter's acorn" or "nut of the gods."</note>

<meta>This page continues Mark Catesby’s documentation of North American species, pairing the American Redstart with the Black Walnut tree. # THE AMERICAN REDSTART
<note>original Latin: *Ruticilla Americana*. The name "Redstart" comes from the Old English "stert," meaning tail—referring to the bird's distinctive orange-red tail feathers.</note>

## The Red-Start,
<image-desc>Decorative initial T featuring a bird perched among scrolls and foliage.</image-desc>
THIS Bird is about the Size of, or rather less than, our Redstart <note>Referring to the Common Redstart (*Phoenicurus phoenicurus*) found in Europe.</note>; and has a slender black Bill: The Head, Neck, Back, and Wings, are black; except, that Five or Six of the exterior Vanes <note>The flat part of a feather.</note> of the larger Wing-Feathers are partly red. The Breast is red; but divided by a grey List <note>A "list" refers to a stripe or band of color.</note>; of which Colour is the Belly. The Tail is red, except the End, which is black. The Legs and Feet are black. The Hens are brown.

These Birds frequent the Shady Woods of *Virginia*; and are seen only in Summer.

---

### Virginian Black Walnut <note>original Latin: *Nux juglans nigra Virginienfis*. The reference "Park: 1414" points to John Parkinson’s famous 1640 botanical work, *Theatrum Botanicum*, page 1414.</note>

## The Black Walnut.
<image-desc>Decorative initial M with intricate foliate patterns.</image-desc>
MOST Parts of the Northern Continent of *America* abound with these Trees, particularly *Virginia* and *Maryland*, towards the Heads of the Rivers, where, in low rich Land, they grow in great Plenty, and to a vast Size. The Leaves are much narrower and sharper pointed than those of our Walnut <note>Referring to the English or Persian Walnut (*Juglans regia*).</note>, and not so smooth. The Thickness of the inner Shell requires a Hammer to break it. The outer Shell is very thick and rough on the Outside. The Kernels are very oily and rank <note>In this context, "rank" means having a strong, pungent, or overly intense flavor.</note> tasted; yet, when laid by some Months, are eat by *Indians*, Squirrels &c. It seems to have taken its Name from the Colour of the Wood, which approaches nearer to Black than any other Wood that affords so large Timber. Wherefore it is esteemed for making Cabinets, Tables, &c.

<column-break/>

## The American Nightingale of the Wall <note>original French: *Le Rossignol de muraille de l'Amerique*. This was the common 18th-century French name for the Redstart.</note>
<image-desc>Decorative initial C with floral and foliate scrollwork.</image-desc>
*THIS bird is of nearly the same shape, or even smaller than our Nightingale of the Wall <note>Redstart</note>. It has a thin, black beak. Its head, its neck, its back, and its wings are black; except five or six of the exterior fringes of the large wing feathers, which are partly red. Its breast is red, but divided by a gray stripe. Its belly is gray. Its tail is red, except that its end is black. Its legs and its feet are black. The female is entirely brown. These birds frequent the most covered woods of Virginia; and one sees them only in summer.*

## Black Walnut <note>original French: *Noyer noir*.</note>
<image-desc>Decorative initial L with scroll and floral motifs.</image-desc>
*THE largest part of the southern continent of America <note>The French text curiously says "meridional" (southern), while the English says "northern."</note> has many of these trees; above all Virginia and Maryland toward the source of the rivers, there, where they come in great abundance in low and rich soils, and grow extraordinarily. Their leaves are much narrower, more pointed, and less smooth than those of our common walnut. The thickness of the internal shell is such that one can only break it with a hammer. The external shell is very thick and very rough on the outside. The kernels are very oily, and of a very strong taste. However, the Indians as well as the Squirrels etc. eat them, after having kept them some time. It seems that this tree has taken its name from the color of its wood, which approaches closer to black than any other wood that provides such large timber. This is why it is esteemed for making Cabinets, Tables, etc.*

<summary>A descriptive entry for the American Redstart and the Black Walnut tree, detailing their physical characteristics, habitats in Virginia and Maryland, and the practical uses of the walnut wood.</summary>
<keywords>American Redstart, Black Walnut, Virginia, Maryland, 18th-century natural history, ornithology, timber, John Parkinson</keywords>

# THE LITTLE BLACK BULLFINCH <note>original Latin: *Rubicilla minor nigra*</note>

## The little black Bulfinch
THIS is about the Size of a Canary-Bird. The whole Bird is black, except the Shoulders of the Wings, and Part of the Vanes <note>The flat, webbed part of a feather.</note> of two of the largest Wing-Feathers, which are white. The Bill is thick and short, having a Notch in the upper Mandible like that of a Hawk. This Bird is an Inhabitant of Mexico; and is called by the Spaniards, *Mariposa nigra*, i. e. Black Butterfly. Whether this be a Cock or Hen, I know not.

<column-break/>

## Little Black Redstart <note>original French: *Petit Rouge-queue noir*. Interestingly, while the English text identifies it as a Bullfinch, the French identifies it as a "Red-tail" or Redstart.</note>

It is nearly the size of a Canary-bird. This bird is entirely black, except the top of the wings, and a part of the fringes of the two largest feathers of the wing, which are white. Its beak is thick and short, having a notch in the upper mandible, like that of a Falcon. This bird lives in Mexico, and the Spaniards call it *Mariposa-Nigra*, which is to say, Black Butterfly. I do not know if this one is male or female.

Citing Leonard Plukenet’s *Almagestum Botanicum*.</note><-

## The FRINGE TREE <note>Today known as *Chionanthus virginicus*, prized for its delicate, thread-like white flowers.</note>

ON the Banks of Rivulets and running Streams this Shrub is most commonly found. It mounts from six to ten Feet high, usually with a crooked irregular small Stem. Its Leaves are of a light Green, and shaped like those of the Orange. In *May*, it produces Bunches of White Flowers hanging on branched Footstalks, of half an Inch long. Each Flower has four narrow thin Petals about two Inches long. To these succeed round dark blue Berries; of the Size of Sloes <note>The small, sour fruit of the blackthorn bush.</note>.

<column-break/>

## Tree with Fringed Flowers <note>original French: *Arbre aux Fleurs frangées*.</note>

One commonly finds this shrub on the banks of small brooks and running waters. It rises from six to ten feet. Its stem is commonly small, twisted, and irregular. Its leaves are of a light green, and shaped like those of the Orange tree. In the month of May it produces clusters of white flowers, which hang from branched stalks, half an inch long. Each flower has four narrow, thick, and long petals of about two inches. These are followed by round berries, of a dark blue, the size of wild sloes.

## The Little Black Redstart <note>original French: *Petit Rouge-queue noir*. While the English text identifies it as a Bullfinch, the French identifies it as a "Red-tail" or Redstart, showing the fluidity of naming in early natural history.</note>

This bird is nearly the size of a Canary. It is entirely black, except for the top of the wings and part of the edges of the two largest wing feathers, which are white. # THE BELTED KINGFISHER <note>original Latin: *Ispida*</note>

## The Wax Myrtle <note>original Latin: *Myrtus*</note>

This bird is notable for its large, powerful head and a distinctive shaggy crest of feathers that it can raise at will. Its plumage is primarily a leaden blue-gray, accented by a bright white collar around the neck. A unique feature of this species is the reddish-brown band across its breast, which identifies it as a female. In this illustration, the bird holds a small, silvery fish in its long, sharp, black beak, demonstrating its skill as a tireless hunter of the waterways.

<column-break/>

The bird sits upon a branch of the **Wax Myrtle** <note>original Latin: *Myrtus*. While Catesby uses the general Latin term for Myrtle, he is depicting *Morella cerifera*, also known as the Southern Bayberry.</note>. This shrub is highly valued in the American colonies for its small, round berries, which are seen here in dark blue clusters. These berries are coated in a natural white wax; when boiled in large quantities, the wax is skimmed off and used to create candles that are prized for their firm texture and pleasant, balsamic scent. The leaves are long and lance-shaped, remaining a vibrant green.

-># THE KINGFISHER <note>original Latin: *Ispida*</note><-

### The King-Fisher.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial T featuring a bird perched in a landscape with a river and mountains in the background.</image-desc>
This kind of King-fisher is somewhat larger than a Blackbird. The bill is two inches and a half long, and black. The eyes are large. His head is covered with long bluish feathers <note>The "shaggy crest" typical of the Belted Kingfisher</note>. Under the eye there is a white spot, and another at the base of the upper mandible of the bill. All the upper part of the body is of a dusky blue. The neck is white, with a broad lift <note>A horizontal band or stripe</note> of dusky blue across it; under which the breast is muddy red. The belly is white. The quill-feathers of the wing are black, having some white on their interior vanes, edged with blue and black, with transverse white spots, not appearing but when the wing is spread open. The tail is dusky blue, with the end white, as are most of the quill-feathers. It has four toes, one only being behind. Its cry, its solitary abode about rivers, and its manner of feeding are much the same as of those in England. It preys not only on fish but likewise on lizards.

<column-break/>

### The King-Fisher <note>original French: *L’Alcyon*</note>.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial C containing leafy foliage and floral patterns.</image-desc>
This species of King-fisher is a little larger than a blackbird. Its beak is two and a half inches long, and black. Its eyes are large. Its head is covered with long bluish feathers. It has a white spot under the eye, and another at the base of the upper mandible. All the top of its body is a dark blue. Its neck is white, with a wide band of dark blue across it; below which the breast is a dull red. Its belly is white. The large feathers of the wing are black, having a little white on their interior fringes, which are bordered with blue and black, with some white spots across them, which appear only when the wing is open. Its tail is a deep blue, white at the end, like most of the large wing feathers. It has four toes, one of which is at the back. Its cry, its way of feeding and of frequenting secluded places on rivers, strongly resemble what one notices in the same bird in England. Lizards are its prey as well as fish.

---

->*Myrtle, similar to the Brabant species, from Carolina, bearing berries, with fruit in stalkless clusters containing a single stone.* <note>original Latin: "Myrtus, Brabanticæ similis, Carolinensis, baccata, fructu racemoso sessili monopyreno." This refers to a citation from Leonard Plukenet’s *Almagestum Botanicum*.</note><-

---

### The narrow-leaved Candle-berry MYRTLE.

These are usually but small trees or shrubs, about twelve feet high, with crooked stems, branching forth near the ground irregularly. The leaves are long, narrow, and sharp pointed. Some trees have most of their leaves serrated <note>having a saw-toothed edge</note>; others not. In May, the small branches are alternately and thick set with oblong tufts of very small flowers resembling, in form and size, the catkins of the hazel-tree, colored with red and green. These are succeeded by small clusters of blue berries, closely connected, like bunches of grapes. The kernel is enclosed in an oblong hard stone, incrustated over with an unctuous mealy consistence <note>an oily, powdery coating</note>; which is what yields the wax; of which candles are made in the following manner.

In November and December, at which time the berries are mature, a man with his family will remove from his home to some island or sandbanks near the sea, where these trees most abound, taking with him kettles to boil the berries in. He builds a hut with Palmetto-leaves, for the shelter of himself and family while they stay, which is commonly three or four weeks.

The man cuts down the trees, while the children strip off the berries into a porridge-pot; and having put water to them, they boil them until the oil floats; which is skimmed off into another vessel. This is repeated till there remains no more oil. This, when cold, hardens to the consistence of wax, and is of a dirty green color. Then they boil it again, and clarify it in brass kettles; which gives it a transparent greenness.

These candles burn a long time, and yield a grateful smell. They usually add a fourth part of tallow <note>rendered animal fat</note>; which makes them burn clearer.

<column-break/>

### The Candle-Myrtle <note>original French: *La Myrte à Chandelle*</note>.

These trees are usually small; or rather they are only shrubs twelve feet high, whose stem is twisted, and pushes its branches very close to the ground, and in an irregular manner. Its leaves are long, narrow and very pointed: most of these trees have serrated leaves; others, not. In the month of May, the small branches have oblong tufts of very small flowers, which resemble, by their shape and size, hazel catkins. These tufts are placed alternately very close to each other, and mixed with red and green. They are followed by small clusters of blue berries, very tight, like bunches of grapes. The seeds are enclosed in a hard, oblong stone covered with an oily and mealy substance; from which one extracts the wax from which candles are made in the following way. In the month of November and December, at which time the berries are ripe, a man with his family will leave his house to go to some island, or on some bank near the sea, where there are many of these trees. He will carry with him kettles to boil the berries, and will build a hut with palm leaves, to retreat there while he stays in that place; and it is usually for three or four weeks.

The man fells the trees, while the children gather the berries, which they put in a bowl with water, which they boil until the oil floats. It is removed with a skimmer. This is continued until no more appears. This oil hardens like wax upon cooling. It is of a dull green. Afterwards it is boiled once more; and it is clarified in copper kettles; which makes it a transparent green. These candles last a long time, and give off a pleasant odor. One usually adds a quarter of tallow; which makes them light better.

<s>

<summary>This page describes the Belted Kingfisher and the Wax Myrtle, emphasizing the practical method used by American colonists to extract wax from berries to create fragrant green candles.</summary>
<keywords>Belted Kingfisher, Wax Myrtle, Candleberry, wax making, natural history, Carolina, colonial industry, morella cerifera</keywords>

# AMERICAN GALLINULE <note>original Latin: *Gallinula Americana*; this bird is now known as the Sora (*Porzana carolina*)</note>

<columns>2</columns>

## The Sora <note>original: *Soree*</note>

<image-desc>Ornamental drop cap 'T' depicting a landscape with trees and foliage.</image-desc>
THIS Bird, in Size and Form, resembles our Water-Rail. The whole Body is covered with brown Feathers; the Under-part of the Body being lighter than the upper. The Bill and Legs are brown. These Birds become so very fat in Autumn, by feeding on Wild Oats <note>likely *Zizania aquatica*, or Wild Rice</note>, that they cannot escape the *Indians*, who catch Abundance by running them down. In *Virginia* (where only I have seen them) they are as much in Request, for the Delicacy of their Flesh, as the Rice-Bird <note>The Bobolink</note> is in *Carolina*, or the Ortolan in *Europe*.

<column-break/>

## The American Rail <note>original French: *Le Rale de l'Amerique*</note>

<image-desc>Ornamental drop cap 'C' with scrollwork and floral designs.</image-desc>
THIS Bird, by its form and size, resembles our Black Rail <note>original French: *Râle-noir*</note>. Its entire body is covered in brown feathers; but the underside is less dark than the top. Its beak and its legs are brown. These birds become so fat in autumn, by dint of eating wild oats, that they cannot escape the Indians, who take a great number of them by tiring them out in a chase. In Virginia (and this is the only place where I have seen them) they are as much sought after for their delicacy as the Rice-birds in Carolina, or the *Ortolans in Europe*.

---

### Virginia Gentian, with a soapwort leaf and a longer blue flower <note>original Latin: *Gentiana Virginiana, Saponariæ folio, flore cæruleo longiore*</note>.
->History of Oxford <note>original: *Hist. Oxon.*, referring to Robert Morison's 1699 botanical work</note>, Vol 3. Page 184. Illustration Table 5. Section 12.<-

<columns>2</columns>

THIS Plant grows in Ditches and shady moist Places, rising usually sixteen Inches high, with upright strait Stems, having long sharp pointed Leaves, set opposite to each other, spreading horizontally. From the Joints of the Leaves come forth four or five monopetalous <note>A flower made of a single petal, or petals fused into a tube</note> blue Flowers; which, before they open, are in Form of a Rolling-Pin; but, when blown, are in Shape of a Cup with the Verge divided into five Sections.

<column-break/>

THIS plant grows in ditches and in shaded and humid places. It ordinarily rises to a height of sixteen inches. Its stems are straight, furnished with long and very pointed leaves, placed opposite one another, and extending horizontally. From the armpits <note>original French: *aisselles*; the botanical term is "axil," the angle between the leaf and stem</note> of these leaves emerge four or five blue monopetalous flowers; which, before opening, have the shape of a roller; and when they are open, they resemble a cup whose edges are divided into five sections.

# AMERICAN GALLINULE <note>original Latin: *Gallinula Americana*; this bird is now known by the common name Sora or Sora Rail (*Porzana carolina*)</note>

## The Sora <note>original: *Soree*</note>

<image-desc>Ornamental drop cap 'T' depicting a landscape with trees and foliage.</image-desc>
THIS Bird, in Size and Form, resembles our Water-Rail <note>The European Water Rail (*Rallus aquaticus*), which Catesby used as a familiar reference for his readers</note>. The whole Body is covered with brown Feathers; the Under-part of the Body being lighter than the upper. The Bill and Legs are brown. These Birds become so very fat in Autumn, by feeding on Wild Oats <note>likely *Zizania aquatica*, also known as Wild Rice, which grows abundantly in North American marshes</note>, that they cannot escape the *Indians*, who catch Abundance by running them down. In *Virginia* (where only I have seen them) they are as much in Request, for the Delicacy of their Flesh, as the Rice-Bird <note>The Bobolink (*Dolichonyx oryzivorus*)</note> is in *Carolina*, or the Ortolan <note>A small bunting considered a culinary delicacy in 18th-century Europe</note> in *Europe*.

<column-break/>

## The American Rail <note>original French: *Le Rale de l'Amerique*</note>

<image-desc>Ornamental drop cap 'C' with scrollwork and floral designs.</image-desc>
THIS Bird, by its form and size, resembles our Black Rail <note>original French: *Râle-noir*; Catesby is likely referring to the European Water Rail or a similar dark rail</note>. Its entire body is covered in brown feathers; but the underside is less dark than the top. Its beak and its legs are brown. These birds become so fat in autumn, by dint of eating wild oats, that they cannot escape the Indians, who take a great number of them by tiring them out in a chase. In Virginia (and this is the only place where I have seen them) they are as much sought after for their delicacy as the Rice-birds in Carolina, or the *Ortolans in Europe*.

### VIRGINIA GENTIAN <note>original Latin: *Gentiana Virginiana, Saponariæ folio, flore cæruleo longiori*; literally, 'Virginia Gentian, with a soapwort leaf and a longer blue flower'</note>

<image-desc>A tall botanical specimen grows from the water's edge, featuring vibrant blue, tubular flowers and lance-shaped leaves.</image-desc>

This Gentian <note>Now identified as the Soapwort Gentian (*Gentiana saponaria*) or Harvestbells</note> rises with a single stem to the height of about eighteen inches. The leaves are smooth, pointed, and grow in pairs along the stem, closely resembling the leaves of the Soapwort <note>A common plant of the pink family (*Saponaria officinalis*)</note>. The flowers appear at the joints where the leaves meet the stem; they are of a beautiful, deep blue color and have a long, tubular shape. This plant is usually found growing in moist, shady places near the banks of rivers and small streams in the woods of Virginia.

## THE CHATTERING PLOVER <note>original Latin: *Pluvialis Vociferus*; now commonly known as the Killdeer</note>

### The chattering Plover

<image-desc>Decorative initial T containing an etching of a bird (a plover) standing in a landscape.</image-desc>
THIS is about the Size of the larger Snipe. The Eyes are large, with a scarlet Circle. A black List <note>a stripe or band</note> runs from the Bill under the Eyes. The Forehead is white; above which it is black. The Rest of the Head is brown. The Throat and round the Neck is white; under which there is a broad black List encompassing the Neck. Another List of Black crosses the Breast, from the Shoulder of one Wing to that of the other. Except which, the Breast and Belly are white. The Back and Wings are brown; the larger Quill-Feathers being of a darker Brown. The small Rump-Feathers, which cover three Quarters of the Tail, are of a yellowish Red. The lower Part of the Tail is black. The Legs and Feet of a Straw-Colour. It hath no back Toes. These Birds are very frequent both in *Virginia* and *Carolina*; and are a great Hinderance to Fowlers by alarming the Game with their screaming Noise. In *Virginia* they are called *Killdeers*, from some Resemblance of their Noise to the Sound of that Word. They abide in *Carolina* and *Virginia* all the Year. The Feathers of the Cock and Hen differ not much.

<column-break/>

### The Chattering Plover <note>original French: *Pluvieur Criard*</note>

<image-desc>Decorative initial E containing an etching of a bird similar to the one in the first column.</image-desc>
THIS bird is nearly the size of the largest snipes. Its eyes are large, surrounded by a red circle. A black band extends from its beak to under its eyes. The front of its head is white; above it is black; all the rest is brown. Its throat and the area around its neck are white; and below there is a wide black band which encircles its neck. Another band of the same color crosses its breast, from the top of one wing to that of the other. Except for this, its breast and belly are entirely white. Its back and wings are brown. The large wing feathers are of a darker color. The small feathers of the rump, which cover three-quarters of its tail, are of a yellowish red. The rest of its tail is black. Its legs and feet are straw-colored. It has no back toe. These birds are very common in Virginia and Carolina, and do great harm to hunters; for they give the alarm to the game by their piercing cry. They are called *Kill-deers* in Virginia, because their cry has some resemblance to the sound of this word. They remain all year in Carolina and in Virginia. There is not much difference between the feathers of the male and those of the female.

---

### SHRUB WITH OBLONG POINTED LEAVES, AND FLOWERS ARRANGED IN SPIKES ON ONE SIDE <note>original Latin: *Frutex folijs oblongis acuminatis, floribus spicatis unoversu dispositis*</note>

### The SORREL-TREE.

<image-desc>Decorative initial T containing an etching of a landscape with a tree and clouds.</image-desc>
THE Trunc of this Tree is usually five or six Inches thick, and rises to the Height of about twenty Feet, with slender Branches thick set with Leaves, shaped like those of the Pear-Tree. From the Ends of the Branches proceed little white monopetalous <note>flowers consisting of a single petal, or petals fused into a tube</note> Flowers, like those of the Strawberry-Tree <note>referring to *Arbutus unedo*</note>, which are thick set on short Footstalks to one Side of many slender Stalks, which are pendant on one Side of the main Branch.

<column-break/>

### The Sorrel Tree <note>original French: *L’Ozeille Arbre*</note>

<image-desc>Decorative initial L containing an etching of a landscape with leafy plants.</image-desc>
THE trunk of this tree is ordinarily five or six inches in diameter, and rises to a height of about twenty feet, with very thin branches, furnished with many leaves, which resemble those of the pear tree. From the ends of these branches are born small, white, single-petaled flowers, like those of the Strawberry Tree. They are attached very close to one another by very short stalks, on one side only of several very thin stems, which hang to one side of the principal branch.

## THE SEA DOTTEREL <note>original Latin: *Morinellus Marinus*; a name used by Sir Thomas Browne, a 17th-century naturalist, to describe this shorebird</note>
## IS THIS TURNER’S SHOREBIRD? <note>original Latin: *An Cinclus Turneri?*; referring to a species described by William Turner and later by Francis Willughby in his *Ornithology* (Will. p. 311)</note>

---

->The Turnstone, or Sea-Dotterel.<-

<image-desc>A decorative drop cap 'T' containing a miniature landscape with a bird on a rocky shore.</image-desc>
THIS Bird has, in Proportion to its Body, a small Head, with a straight taper black Bill, an Inch long. All the Upper-part of the Body is brown, with a Mixture of White and black. The Quill-Feathers of the Wings are dark brown; the Neck and Breast black; the Legs and Feet light red. In a Voyage to America, *Anno* <note>Year</note> 1722, in 31 Degrees North Latitude and 40 Leagues from the Coast of *Florida*, the Bird from which this was figured flew on Board us and was taken. It was very active in turning up Stones, which we put into its Cage; but not finding under them the usual Food, it died. In this Action it moved only the upper Mandible; <note>The author likely observed the bird's specialized prying motion, though modern ornithology shows both parts of the beak are used.</note> yet would with great Dexterity and Quickness turn over Stones of above three Pounds Weight. This Property Nature seems to have given it for the finding of its Food; which is probably Worms and Insects on the Sea-Shore. By Comparing This with the Description of that in *Willughby’s Ornithology* <note>original Latin: *Will. Ornithog.*; a foundational book on birds by Francis Willughby, published in 1676</note> which I had then on board, I found this to be the same Kind with that he describes.

<column-break/>

->The Sea Lark <note>original French: *Alloüette de Mer*; though this term often refers to different shorebirds, Catesby uses it here as the French equivalent for the Turnstone</note><-

<image-desc>A decorative drop cap 'E' containing a miniature landscape with a bird and a body of water.</image-desc>
IT has a small head in proportion to its body. Its beak is straight, black, and conical, one inch in length. All the upper part of its body is brown, with a mixture of white and black. The large feathers of the wings are a dark brown. Its neck and breast are black; its legs and feet are a light red. This bird, which we have depicted, flew onto our vessel during a voyage to America, in the year <note>original Latin: *Anno*</note> 1722, at a latitude of 31 degrees, 40 leagues from the coast of Florida, and was captured there. It was very skillful at turning over stones that we had placed in its cage; but for lack of finding its usual food there, it died. In this action, it used only the upper part of its beak, turning stones of three pounds in weight with great skill and very quickly. It seems that Nature has given it this ability to find its food in this way, which probably consists of worms and other insects found on the sea coasts. By comparing this bird with the description that Mr. *Willughby* gives of the Sea Lark in his *Ornithology*, which I then had on board, I found that it was the same species.

---

## MARITIME TREE, with paired pear-shaped leaves ending in a point at the tip, and yellow flowers in clusters. <note>original Latin: *Arbor maritima, foliis conjugatis pyriformibus apice in summitate instructis, floribus racemosis luteis.* This is likely the Black Mangrove (*Avicennia germinans*).</note>

---

<image-desc>A decorative drop cap 'T' depicting a figure working in a garden or field with plants.</image-desc>
THIS Plant grows usually to the Height of four or five Feet, with many straight ligneous <note>woody</note> Stems; to which are set, opposite to each other at the Distance of five or six Inches, smaller single Stems. The Leaves grow opposite to one another on Footstalks half an Inch long, being narrow next the Stalk, and broad at the End; where they are a little pointed; in Shape like a Pear. The Flowers grow in Tufts, at the Ends of the Branches, on short Footstalks; each Flower being formed like a Cup, with yellow Apices <note>The tips of the stamens containing pollen</note>.

<column-break/>

<image-desc>A decorative drop cap 'C' depicting a landscape with tall plants and foliage.</image-desc>
THIS plant normally rises to the height of four or five feet. It pushes out several straight and woody stems, from which other smaller and solitary ones emerge at every distance of six inches, one opposite the other. The leaves are likewise arranged two by two, attached to stalks <note>original French: *pédicules*</note> half an inch long; being very narrow near the stalks, and wide toward the pointed end. They look much like a pear. The flowers grow in clusters toward the extremities of the branches, on short stalks. Each individual flower is in the shape of a bell, with yellow stamens <note>original French: *étamines*</note>.

P. 73.
T

# THE BAHAMIAN FLAMINGO <note>original Latin: *Phoenicopterus Bahamensis*. The name *Phoenicopterus* is derived from the Greek for "crimson-winged."</note>

## The Flamingo.

THIS Bird is two Years before it arrives at its perfect Colour; and then 'tis entirely red, except the Quill-Feathers, which are black. A full grown one is of equal Weight with a Wild-Duck; and, when it stands erect, is five Feet high. The Feet are webbed. The Flesh is delicate and nearest resembles that of a Partridge in Taste. The Tongue, above any other Part, was in the highest Esteem with the luxurious Romans <note>Ancient Roman gourmets, such as Apicius, considered flamingo tongues a supreme delicacy, often served at lavish imperial banquets.</note> for its exquisite Flavour.

These Birds make their Nests on Hillocks in shallow Water; on which they sit with their Legs extended down, like a Man sitting on a Stool. They breed on the Coasts of *Cuba* and the *Bahama-Islands*, and frequent Salt-Water only. A Man, by concealing himself from their Sight, may kill great Numbers of 'em, for they will not rise at the Report of a Gun; nor is the Sight of those killed close by them sufficient to terrify the Rest, and warn 'em of the Danger; but they stand gazing, and as it were astonish'd <note>This lack of "flight" response to gunfire made the species highly vulnerable to early hunters.</note>, till they are most or all of them kill'd.

This Bird resembles the Heron in Shape, excepting the Bill, which being of a very singular Form, I shall, in the next Table, give the Figure of it in its full Size, with a particular Description.

<column-break/>

## Flamingo <note>original French: *Flamant*</note>

*This bird is two years old before it reaches the perfection of its color. At that time it is entirely red, except for the primary feathers of the wing, which are black. When it has finished its growth, it is the same weight as a wild duck; and when it stands upright, it is five feet high. Its feet are equipped with membranes like those of geese. Its flesh is very delicate, and closely approaches that of the Partridge; but above all the tongue was highly esteemed by the most voluptuous Romans, because of the excellence of its flavor.*

*These birds make their nests in shallow waters, on small eminences; upon which they sit with their legs hanging down, like a man sitting on a stool. They produce their young on the coast of Cuba, and the Bahama Islands, and frequent only salt water. A man, by hiding himself so that they cannot see him, can kill a great number of them; for the noise of a gunshot does not make them change their place, nor is the sight of those who are killed very close to them capable of frightening the others, or of warning them of the danger they are in; but they remain with fixed eyes, and, so to speak, astonished, until they are all killed, or at least most of them.*

*This bird closely resembles the Heron in its shape, if you except the beak, of which I will give a full-size description and figure in the following plate because of its singularity.*

---

# BROWN BRANCHING HORN-PLANT <note>original Latin: *Keratophyton Dichotomum fuscum*. These are now known to be Gorgonians or Sea Fans—colonial marine animals, not plants.</note>

THIS Plant ariseth from a short Stem about two Inches round, and about the same in Height; where it divides into two larger Branches, each of which divides again into two smaller; and so generally, at the Distance of Three or Four Inches, each Branch divides in two smaller, till the whole Plant is risen to about two Feet, and the upper Branches are become not thicker than a Crow's Quill; all pliant like Horn or Whale-bone, and of a dark brown Colour. They are in great Plenty at the Bottom of the shallow Seas and Channels of the *Bahama Islands*, the Water there being exceeding clear. I have plainly seen them growing to the white Rocks in above ten Fathom Water.

<column-break/>

*This plant rises from a short stem, about two inches in circumference, and nearly the same height. It then divides into two main branches, each of which divides into two smaller ones, and so on usually at a distance of three or four inches. Each branch subdivides into two smaller ones, until the plant has risen to a height of two feet, and the upper branches have become as thin as a crow's quill. All these branches are flexible like horn or whalebone, and are a dark brown. A great number of these plants are found at the bottom of the shallow waters and channels of the Bahama Islands. As the water there is very clear, I have seen them distinctly growing on white rocks, more than ten fathoms under water.*

<summary>This page describes the American Flamingo, noting its vibrant red plumage and the historical Roman appetite for its tongue, alongside a description of a brown branching sea fan (Gorgonian) found in the clear waters of the Bahamas.</summary>
<keywords>Flamingo, Phoenicopterus, Bahama Islands, Cuba, Roman cuisine, Keratophyton, sea fan, coral, natural history</keywords>

# THE HEAD OF THE FLAMINGO IN ITS NATURAL SIZE. <note>original Latin: *Caput Phoenicopteri Naturalis Magnitudinis*</note>

## The Bill of the Flamingo in its full Dimensions:

I Need not attempt to describe the Texture of the Bill otherwise than Dr. Grew <note>Nehemiah Grew (1641–1712) was a pioneering English plant anatomist and physician who served as the curator of the Royal Society’s museum.</note> has done in his *Museum of the Royal Society* <note>original: *Mus. R. Soc.*</note> p. 67. His Words are these:
> "The Figure of each Beak is truly
> "Hyperbolical <note>In geometry, a hyperbola is a specific type of smooth curve; Grew uses this to describe the dramatic mathematical arch of the beak.</note>. The upper is rid-
> "ged behind; before, plain or flat, and pointed
> "like a Sword, and with the Extremity bended a
> "little down; within, it hath an Angle or sharp
> "Ridge, which runs all along the Middle. At the
> "Top of the Hyperbole, not above a quarter of an
> "Inch high. The lower Beak in the same Place
> "above one Inch high, hollow, and the Margins
> "strangely expanded inward, for the Breadth of
> "above a quarter of an Inch, and somewhat convex-
> "ly. They are both furnished with black Teeth,
> "as I call them, from their Use, of an unusual Fi-
> "gure; that is to say <note>original: *scil.*, an abbreviation of the Latin *scilicet*.</note> slender, numerous, and parallel, as in
> "Ivory-Combs; but also very short, scarce the
> "eighth Part of an Inch deep. An admirable Inven-
> "tion of Nature; by the Help of which, and of the
> "sharp Ridge abovementioned, this Bird holds his
> "slippery Prey the faster."

When they feed (which is always in shallow Water) by bending their Neck, they lay the upper part of their Bill next the Ground, their Feet being in continual Motion up and down in the Mud; by which Means they raise a small round Sort of Grain, resembling Millet <note>While the author describes them eating "grain," flamingos actually filter small crustaceans, algae, and larvae from the mud.</note>, which they receive into their Bill. And as there is a Necessity of admitting into their Mouths some Mud, Nature has provided the Edges of their Bill with a Sieve, or Teeth, like those of a fine Comb, with which they retain the Food, and reject the Mud that is taken in with it. This Account I had from Persons of Credit; but I never saw them feeding myself, and therefore cannot absolutely refute the Opinion of others, who say, they feed on Fish, particularly Eels, which seem to be the slippery Prey Dr. Grew says the Teeth are contrived to hold.

The accurate Dr. James Douglas <note>A Scottish physician and anatomist to the Queen, famous for his detailed anatomical studies.</note> hath obliged the World with a curious and ample Description of this Bird in *Philosophical Transactions* <note>original: *Phil. Trans.*, the world's first scientific journal.</note> No. 350.

<column-break/>

## The Beak of the Flamingo in its natural size. <note>This column translates the French version of the text: *Le Bec du Flamant de sa grandeur naturelle.*</note>

IT is not necessary that I undertake to describe the form of its beak, otherwise than Dr. Grew has done in the work entitled *Museum of the Royal Society* <note>original: *Mus. R.*</note>, p. 67. Here are his own words: "The fi-
> " gure of each mandible is truly
> " hyperbolic. That of the top is rais-
> " ed from behind; flat in front; pointed
> " like a sword, and a little curved at its extremity. It has
> " on the inside an angle, or a ridge <note>original: *filet*</note>, which
> " extends from one end to the other, and se-
> " parates it through the middle; having no more than a quarter
> " of an inch at the top of the hyperbola. The lower
> " mandible is in the same place more than an
> " inch thick, hollowed out, and having the edges extend-
> " ed toward the inside in a very strange manner,
> " the width of more than a quarter of an inch, and a little
> " convex. They are both furnished with black teeth,
> " for that is what I call them because of
> " their use. These teeth are of an extra-
> " ordinary figure, thin, in great number, and parallel
> " like those of an ivory comb; moreover, very
> " short, having barely a quarter of an inch of pro-
> " fundity; an admirable invention of Nature, by
> " the means of which, and of the ridge above mentioned,
> " this bird holds more firmly its slippery prey.

When they eat—and it is always in shallow water—by bending the neck, they make the upper part of their beak touch the ground. Their feet, however, move without ceasing up and down in the mud; and by this means they raise a small round seed which resembles millet: they receive it into their beak. And as they cannot help but receive at the same time a little silt, nature has furnished the edges of their beak with a sieve, or teeth, like those of a fine comb; by the means of which they retain their nourishment, and reject the silt which entered with it. This is what I have learned from persons worthy of faith, for I have never seen these birds eat myself. That is why I cannot absolutely refute the opinion of those who say that they feed on fish, and especially on eels; it seems that this is what Dr. Grew meant by that slippery prey, which he says their teeth are made to retain.

The exact Dr. James Douglas has published an ample and curious description of this bird in the *Philosophical Transactions* <note>original: *Phil. Trans.*</note> No. 350.

---

## BLACK SHRUB-LIKE HORN-PLANT. <note>original Latin: *Keratophyton fruticis specie, nigrum*. A species of gorgonian or sea fan, once thought to be a plant but actually a colonial animal.</note>

THIS Species differs from the former, in that it is black, and hath a large Stem like the Trunk of a Tree, which rises up through the Middle of the Plant, and sends out several larger Branches, from which arise the smaller Twigs, which are more crooked and slender than those of the preceding: So that in the Whole it resembles a Tree without Leaves.

This grows to Rocks in the same Places with the preceding.

<column-break/>

THIS species differs from the preceding in that it is black, and that it has a thick stem, like the trunk of a tree, which passes through the middle of the whole plant, and sends out several thick branches, from which come the small shoots, which are more twisted and thinner than those of the preceding species; so that this one resembles roughly a tree without leaves. It grows on rocks in the same places as the preceding. <note>This column translates the French version of the section above.</note>

# PLATE 74.

## THE HEAD OF THE FLAMINGO
<note>original Latin: *Caput Phænicopteri*</note>

## THE HORN-PLANT, ETC.
<note>original Latin: *Keratophyton &c.*</note>

->**Head of the Flamingo**<-

->**The Horn-plant**<-

This plate illustrates the head of a Flamingo in its natural size, showcasing the bird's distinctive profile. The plumage is depicted in vibrant shades of red and orange. The focus of the drawing is the specialized beak, which is heavy and bent sharply downward. Visible along the edges of the mandibles are the small, black, comb-like "teeth" <note>These are actually lamellae, filter-feeding structures that allow the bird to strain small organisms from the water.</note> described in the accompanying text.

The head is shown alongside a specimen of a **Horn-plant** <term>Keratophyton: an early botanical/zoological name for gorgonians or sea fans, so named because their skeletal structure resembles the material of a horn.</term>. This specific specimen is a black sea fan, characterized by its complex, lace-like branching pattern. The artist has arranged the composition so that the Flamingo’s head appears to be nestled within the dark, sturdy branches of the coral.

<note>In the lower-left corner of the illustration's border, there is a small artist's monogram consisting of a combined 'M' and 'C'.</note>

This illustration features the striking profile of the **White American Crane** <note>Now known as the Whooping Crane (*Grus americana*), this bird is famous for its height and its resonant, "whooping" call. In Catesby's time, it was more common, but it later became one of the rarest birds in North America.</note>. The artist has captured the bird in an upward-reaching pose, emphasizing the length of its snowy-white neck. The crown of the head is marked by a patch of dark, greyish-brown feathers, and its long, straight beak is rendered with a sharp, tapered point.

Dangling just above the crane's beak is a branch of a **Plum-tree** <term>Prunus: a broad genus of plants that includes plums, cherries, peaches, and apricots. Catesby frequently paired birds with the specific plants they were known to feed upon or inhabit.</term>. The branch is laden with clusters of round, dark-blue berries and small, oval green leaves. 

The composition is elegant and sparse, focusing on the relationship between the towering bird and the fruiting branch. 

<note>On the right side of the bird's neck, the artist has placed his hallmark monogram, 'MC,' signifying the work of Mark Catesby.</note>

<meta>This page provides the descriptive text for Plate 75, detailing the physical characteristics and habitat of the White American Crane (Whooping Crane) and the Bullet-Bush. It follows the visual illustration provided on the previous page.</meta>

# WHITE AMERICAN CRANE.
<note>original Latin: *GRUS AMERICANA ALBA*</note>

## The Whooping Crane
<note>original: "hooping Crane." The name refers to the bird's loud, resonant call.</note>

Is about the size of the common Crane. The bill is brown, and six inches long; the edges of both mandibles, towards the end, for about an inch and a half, are serrated. A deep and broad channel runs from the head more than halfway along its upper mandible. Its nostrils are very large. A broad white stripe runs from the eyes obliquely to the neck; except for this, the head is brown. The crown of the head is callous and very hard, thinly beset with stiff black hairs, which lie flat and are so thin that the skin appears bare, of a reddish flesh-color. Behind the head is a peak of black feathers. The larger wing-feathers are black. All the rest of the body is white. This description I took from the entire skin of the bird, presented to me by an Indian <note>Likely a member of the local Creek or Yamasee tribes Catesby encountered in the Southeast.</note> who made use of it for his tobacco-pouch. He told me that early in the spring great multitudes of them frequent the lower parts of the rivers near the sea; and return to the mountains in the summer. This account was afterwards confirmed to me by a white man; who added, that they make a remarkable whooping noise; and that he has seen them at the mouths of the Savannah, Altamaha <note>original: "Aratamaha"</note>, and other rivers nearer St. Augustine, but never saw any as far north as the settlements of Carolina.

<column-break/>

## White Crane of America.
<note>original French: *Grüe blanche de l'Amerique.*</note>

It is roughly the same size as the common Crane. Its beak is brown, and six inches long. The edges of the two mandibles are notched to a length of an inch and a half toward the end. A wide and deep channel extends from its head further than the middle of its upper mandible. Its nostrils are very large. A wide white stripe descends obliquely from its eyes to its neck; besides that, its whole head is brown. The crown of its head is callous and very hard, adorned with a few hard, black, sparsely scattered hairs. They are flattened and so fine that the skin appears entirely bare, and of a reddish flesh color. Behind its head there is a small tuft of black feathers. The large feathers of its wings are also black. All the rest of its body is white. I made this description from an entire skin of one of these birds, which an Indian gave to me as a gift. He used it as a bag to hold his tobacco. He told me that a great number of these birds frequent the lower parts of the rivers near the sea at the beginning of spring, and return to the mountains in summer. This account was since confirmed to me by a White man; who added, that they make a great noise with their cry; and that he has seen them at the mouth of the Savannah, Altamaha, and other rivers near St. Augustine; but that he has never seen any as far north as the settlements of Carolina.

---

# *Plum with Box-tree leaves, heart-shaped, with round black fruit.*
<note>original Latin: *Prunus Buxi folio cordato, fructu nigro rotundo.*</note>

## The Bullet-Bush.

The largest part of the stem of this shrub is seldom bigger than the small of a man's leg. The height is usually five feet. The branches shoot forth near the ground and spread. The leaves are stiff like those of the Box-tree <term>Box (Buxus): a slow-growing evergreen shrub with small, tough leaves often used for hedges.</term>, and about the same size, with notches at the ends. The berries hang to the smaller branches by stalks not half an inch long, and are globular, somewhat larger than a black cherry, of a bluish-black; and each contains a single stone.

<column-break/>

## Shrub whose fruits resemble musket balls.
<note>original French: *Arbrisseau dont les fruits ressemblent à des Balles de mousquet.*</note>

The thickest part of the trunk of this shrub rarely exceeds the size of a man's leg. Its height is usually five feet. Its branches are born near the earth and extend widely. Its leaves are stiff like those of the boxwood, and about the same size, with notches at their ends. The berries hang from the smallest branches by stems that are not half an inch in length. They are round, a little larger than a black cherry, of a black color leaning towards blue. They each contain only a single stone.

# THE BLUE HERON <note>original Latin: *ARDEA CÆRULEA*</note>

## The Blue Heron <note>original spelling: "blew Heron"</note>

This bird weighs fifteen ounces, and in size is somewhat less than a crow. The bill is blue, but darker towards the point. The <term>irides</term> <note>The colored part of the eye surrounding the pupil.</note> of the eyes are yellow. The head and neck are of a changeable purple <note>This refers to iridescence, where the color shifts as the bird moves in the light.</note>. All the rest of the body is blue. The legs and feet are green. From the breast hang long narrow feathers, as there do likewise from the hind-part of the head; and likewise on the back are such feathers, which are a foot in length and extend four inches below the tail, which is a little shorter than the wings. These birds are not numerous in Carolina <note>In the 1720s, "Carolina" referred to the English colonial territories that would become North and South Carolina.</note>; and are rarely seen but in the spring of the year.

Whence they come, and where they breed, is to me unknown.

<column-break/>

## Blue Heron <note>original French: *Heron bleu*</note>

This bird weighs fifteen ounces. It is a little less large than a crow. Its beak is blue, but darker towards the tip; the iris of its eyes is yellow. Its head and its neck are of a changing violet. All the rest of its body is blue. Its legs and its feet are green. There hang from its breast long, very narrow feathers. It has the same at the back of its head and on its back; these latter are a foot long and pass beyond its tail by four fingers <note>A "finger" was an informal unit of measurement, roughly equivalent to 3/4 of an inch.</note>. The tail is a little shorter than the wings. These birds are very few in number in Carolina; and one hardly ever sees them there except in the spring.

I do not know from where they come, or where they raise their young.

<summary>A detailed physical description of the Blue Heron (Egretta caerulea), noting its distinctive iridescent plumage and its rarity in the Carolina colony during the early 18th century.</summary>
<keywords>Blue Heron, ornithology, Carolina, Mark Catesby, plumage, migratory patterns, bird anatomy</keywords>

# THE BLUE HERON <note>original Latin: *ARDEA CÆRULEA*</note>

## The Blue Heron <note>original spelling: "blew Heron"</note>

This bird weighs fifteen ounces, and in size is somewhat less than a crow. The bill is blue, but darker towards the point. The <term>irides</term> <note>The colored part of the eye surrounding the pupil.</note> of the eyes are yellow. The head and neck are of a changeable purple <note>This refers to iridescence, where the color shifts as the bird moves in the light.</note>. All the rest of the body is blue. The legs and feet are green. From the breast hang long narrow feathers, as there do likewise from the hind-part of the head; and likewise on the back are such feathers, which are a foot in length and extend four inches below the tail, which is a little shorter than the wings. These birds are not numerous in Carolina <note>In the 1720s, "Carolina" referred to the English colonial territories that would become North and South Carolina.</note>; and are rarely seen but in the spring of the year.

Whence they come, and where they breed, is to me unknown.

<column-break/>

## Blue Heron <note>original French: *Heron bleu*</note>

This bird weighs fifteen ounces. It is a little less large than a crow. Its beak is blue, but darker towards the tip; the iris of its eyes is yellow. Its head and its neck are of a changing violet. # THE GREAT WHITE HERON <note>original Latin: *ARDEA ALBA*</note>

## The Great White Heron <note>Today commonly known as the Great Egret (*Ardea alba*).</note>

This bird is characterized by its entirely white plumage and its long, sharp beak of an orange-yellow hue. Its eyes are a bright yellow. The legs and feet are long, dark, and covered in scales, suited for wading in the shallow waters where it hunts. In this illustration, the heron is shown in profile, highlighting its elegant neck and slender form.

<column-break/>

## The Rose-Mallow <note>original Latin: *Ketmia &c.* This refers to a species of Hibiscus, likely *Hibiscus moscheutos* or *Hibiscus palustris*.</note>

The plant depicted alongside the heron features large, green leaves with deep lobes and serrated edges. Behind the bird's head, one can observe several round, brownish seed pods supported by slender stalks. The plant is further adorned with open pink flowers, each consisting of five petals and a yellow center.

<note>The monogram "MC" belonging to the artist and naturalist Mark Catesby is visible near the base of the plant's stem, signifying his authorship of both the observation and the engraving.</note>

# THE LITTLE WHITE CAROLINA HERON <note>original Latin: *ARDEA ALBA MINOR CAROLINENSIS*</note>

**The little white Heron.**

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial T featuring scrolling foliage and floral motifs.</image-desc>
THIS Bird is about the Size of the preceding <note>Referring to the Heron described on the previous page.</note>. The Bill is red. The Eyes have yellow Irides <note>The colored part of the eye surrounding the pupil.</note>. The Legs and Feet are green. The whole Plumage is white. They feed on Fish, Frogs, &c. and frequent Rivers, Ponds and Marshes, after the Manner of other Herons.

I believe, they breed in Carolina; but I have never seen any of them in Winter.

<column-break/>

**The Little White Heron.** <note>original French: *Petit Heron blanc.*</note>

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial C depicting a landscape scene with a building or tower.</image-desc>
THIS one is about the size of the preceding. Its beak is red. The Iris of its eyes is yellow. Its legs and its feet are green. All its plumage is white. It feeds on fish, frogs, etc. It frequents rivers, ponds, and marshes, like other Herons. I believe that it raises its young in Carolina; but I have never seen any there during the winter.

### *Shrubby glaucous Ketmia, with a longer, serrated leaf like the Great Maple, and a flesh-colored flower.* <note>original Latin: *Ketmia frutescens glauca, Aceris majoris folio longiore, serrato, flore carneo.* Ketmia is an archaic name for the Hibiscus genus.</note>

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial T featuring floral and leaf patterns.</image-desc>
THIS Plant rises with several Stems usually five Feet high, producing broad serrated downy Leaves, like the broad-leaved Maple, divided by six Sections. The Flowers are in Clusters, on the Top of the Stalk; of a pale red, and divided by five Segments. The Fruit is round and ribbed, about the Bigness of a large Hazel-Nut, containing many small black Seeds. They grow among the Rocks of the Bahama-Islands.

<column-break/>

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial P with floral decoration.</image-desc>
USUALLY this plant rises with several stems to the height of five feet. It produces large serrated and velvety leaves, divided into six sections, like those of the Great-leaved Maple. Its flowers are in clusters on the top of their stem. They are of a pale red, and divided into five segments. Its fruit is round, furnished with ribs, and about the size of a good hazelnut. It contains several small black seeds. This plant grows among the rocks of the Bahama Islands.

# THE AMERICAN STARRY HERON <note>original Latin: *ARDEA STELLARIS AMERICANA*; this refers to the American Bittern, *Botaurus lentiginosus*, named "starry" for its speckled plumage.</note>

## The brown Bittern.

THIS is somewhat smaller than our *English* Bittern. The bill is four inches long; the tip and upper part of it are black, and the lower part is green. The eyes are large, having gold-colored *irides* <note>the iris is the colored part of the eye surrounding the pupil</note>, surrounded by green skin. The whole body is brown, with a mixture of white feathers; the back is darker. The breast and belly are whiter. Most of the large wing feathers are white at the tips. The tail is short and of a leaden color. The legs and feet are of a yellowish green. The outer and middle toes are joined by a membrane <note>a thin skin or webbing</note>. The inner side of the middle toe is serrated <note>toothed like a saw; herons use this specialized "pectinate" claw to preen their feathers and remove fish slime</note>. These birds frequent fresh-water rivers and ponds in the inland parts of the country, far from the sea.

<column-break/>

## The Brown Bittern. <note>original French: *Butor brun*</note>

IT is a little smaller than our *English* bittern. Its beak is four inches long. The tip and the top are black; the underside is green. Its eyes are large. The iris is gold-colored, surrounded by green skin. Its whole body is brown, with some white feathers mixed in here and there. Its back is darker. Its breast and its belly are whiter. Most of the large feathers of its wings have white tips. Its tail is short and lead-colored. Its legs and its feet are of a yellow shade tending toward green. The outer toe of its foot and the middle one are joined by a membrane. The inner side of the middle toe is serrated like a saw. These birds frequent freshwater rivers and ponds in the higher parts of the country and far from the sea.

->The Starry Heron<-
<note>original Latin: *Ardea Stellaris*; the name "starry" refers to the speckled, star-like patterns on the bird's feathers.</note>

# THE AMERICAN STARRY HERON <note>original Latin: *ARDEA STELLARIS AMERICANA*; this refers to the American Bittern (*Botaurus lentiginosus*), named for its distinctive mottled plumage.</note>

## The Brown Bittern.

THIS is somewhat smaller than our *English* Bittern. The bill is four inches long; the tip and upper part of it are black, and the lower part is green. The eyes are large, having gold-colored *irides* <note>the iris is the colored part of the eye surrounding the pupil</note>, surrounded by green skin. The whole body is brown, with a mixture of white feathers; the back is darker. The breast and belly are whiter. Most of the large wing feathers are white at the tips. The tail is short and of a leaden color. The legs and feet are of a yellowish green. The outer and middle toes are joined by a membrane <note>a thin skin or webbing</note>. The inner side of the middle toe is serrated <note>toothed like a saw; herons and bitterns use this specialized "pectinate" claw to preen their feathers and remove fish slime</note>. These birds frequent fresh-water rivers and ponds in the inland parts of the country, far from the sea.

<column-break/>

## The Brown Bittern. <note>original French: *Butor brun*</note>

IT is a little smaller than our *English* bittern. Its beak is four inches long. The tip and the top are black; the underside is green. Its eyes are large. The iris is gold-colored, surrounded by green skin. Its whole body is brown, with some white feathers mixed in here and there. Its back is darker. Its breast and its belly are whiter. Most of the large feathers of its wings have white tips. Its tail is short and lead-colored. Its legs and its feet are of a yellow shade tending toward green. The outer toe of its foot and the middle one are joined by a membrane. The inner side <insert>of the middle toe is serrated</insert>. <note>The original text cuts off here; the serrated claw is a defining feature mentioned in the parallel English description.</note>

# THE AMERICAN CRESTED STARRY HERON <note>original Latin: *Ardea Stellaris Cristata Americana*; today known as the Yellow-crowned Night Heron (*Nyctanassa violacea*)</note>

## The Crested Bittern

**WEIGHS** a Pound and half. The Bill is black and strong. The Eyes very large and prominent, with red *Irides* <gloss>irises</gloss>. The Skin encompassing the Eyes is green. The Crown of the Head, from the Basis of the Bill, of a pale yellow, terminating in a Peak; from which hang three or four long white Feathers, the longest of which is six Inches; which they erect, when irritated. From the Angle of the Mouth runs a broad white List <note>a stripe or band of color</note>. The Rest of the Head is of a blueish black. The Neck, Breast and Belly dusky blue. The Back is striped with black Streaks, with a Mixture of white. From the Upper-part of the Back shoot many long narrow Feathers, extending beyond the Tail; some of which are Seven Inches long. The large Feathers of the Wing are brown, with a Tincture of blue. The Legs and Feet are yellow. These Birds are seen in *Carolina* in the rainy Seasons; but in the *Bahama-Islands*, they breed in Bushes growing among the Rocks in prodigious Numbers, and are of great Use to the Inhabitants there; who, while these Birds are young, and before they can fly, employ themselves in taking them, for the Delicacy of their Food. They are, in some of these rocky Islands, so numerous, that in a few Hours, two Men will load one of their *Capatches* <note>a small flat-bottomed boat or skiff used in the West Indies</note> or little Boats, taking them perching from off the Rocks and Bushes; they making no Attempt to escape, tho' almost full grown. They are called, by the *Bahamians*, *Crab-catchers*, *Crabs* being what they mostly subsist on; yet they are well-tasted, and free from any rank <note>an unpleasant, fishy, or "muddy" flavor common in some water birds</note> or fishy Savour.

<column-break/>

## The Crested Bittern <note>Translated from the original French: *Butor huppé*</note>

**IT** weighs a pound and a half. Its beak is black and strong. Its eyes are very large and protruding, with red *Irides* <gloss>irises</gloss>. The skin surrounding its eyes is green. The top of its head, from the base of its beak, is a pale yellow, ending in a point from which grow three or four long white feathers, the longest of which is six inches. When the bird is angry, it raises these feathers. A wide white stripe extends from the corner of its beak. The rest of its head is a black tinged with blue. Its neck, its breast, and its belly are a dark blue. Its back is striped with black and white. Several long, narrow feathers grow from the upper part of its back and extend beyond its tail. Some of these feathers are seven inches long. The large feathers of the wing are brown with a shade of blue. Its legs and its feet are yellow. These birds are seen in *Carolina* during the rainy season: But in the *Bahama Islands*, they are found in very large numbers and raise their young in bushes that grow among the rocks. The people of the country know well how to make a profit from them, for while these birds are young and before they can fly, they occupy themselves by taking them to feast upon. They are in such great numbers in some of these rocky islands that in a few hours, two men will take enough to load one of their small boats. They allow themselves to be taken right off the rocks and bushes where they are perched, without making any move to flee, even though they are already large. The *Bahamians* call them *Crab-catchers*, this shellfish being almost their only food; however, they have a very good taste and do not smell of the marsh in any way.

***

# *Shrubby Lobelia with Purslane-like leaves* <note>original Latin: *Lobelia frutescens, Portulacæ folio*; now known as the Inkberry or Beach Berry (*Scaevola plumieri*)</note>

**THIS** Plant grows usually to the Height of five or six Feet. The Leaves are, in Thickness and Form, not unlike *Purplain* <note>Purslane; a succulent plant with fleshy leaves</note>. At the End of a Stalk, growing from the Joint of a Leaf, there are set three or four monopetalous <term>a flower made of a single petal or with petals fused into a tube</term> white Flowers, divided into five pointed Sections, with a wreathed Stamen hanging out. This Flower appears in a singular Manner, as if it had been tubulous, but slit down to the Basis and laid flat open.
The Flowers are succeeded by globular Berries, of the Size of black Bullace <note>a small, wild European plum</note>, containing a Stone, covered with a smooth black Skin. These Plants grow on the rocky Shores of many of the *Bahama-Islands*.

<column-break/>

**THIS** plant usually grows to a height of five or six feet <note>Translated from the original French</note>. The shape and thickness of its leaves strongly resemble *Purslane*. At the end of a stem, which originates from the leaf axil <note>the "joint" where a leaf meets the stem</note>, are attached three or four white monopetalous flowers, divided into five pointed sections, with a twisted stamen that hangs outside. This flower appears in a peculiar way. It seems as though it were tubular, but split all the way to the base, opened and flattened. These flowers are followed by round berries, the size of black bullace plums. They contain a pit covered with a smooth, black skin. These plants grow on the rocks located along the shores of the *Bahama Islands*.

<keywords>Yellow-crowned Night Heron, Crested Bittern, Crab-catchers, Bahamas, Carolina, Inkberry, Lobelia frutescens, Scaevola plumieri, colonial natural history</keywords>
