# THE SMALLEST STARRY HERON <note>original Latin: *Ardea Stellaris Minima*; currently identified as the Least Bittern (*Ixobrychus exilis*)</note>

## The small Bittern.

THE Bill, from the Angle of the Mouth to the End, was a little more than six Inches long, and black, except some Part of the under Mandible, which was yellow. The Eyes yellow. A Crest of long green Feathers covers the Crown of the Head. The Neck and Breast of a dark muddy Red. The Back cover'd with long narrow pale-green Feathers. The large Quill-Feathers of the Wing of a very dark Green, with a Tincture of Purple. All the Rest of the Wing-Feathers of a changeable shining Green, having some Feathers edged with yellow. The Legs and Feet brown. They have a long Neck, but usually sit with it contracted, on Trees hanging over Rivers, in a lonely Manner waiting for their Prey, which is Frogs, Crabs, and other small Fish.

I don't remember to have seen any of them in Winter: Wherefore I believe, they retire from *Virginia* and *Carolina* more South.

<column-break/>

## Little Bittern. <note>original French: *Petit Butor*</note>

ITS beak, from the corner of its opening to the tip, was a little more than six inches long, and was black, except for a part of the lower mandible, which was yellow. Its eyes yellow. A crest of long green feathers covers the top of its head. Its neck and its breast are of a dull and dark red. Its back is covered with long narrow feathers, of a pale green. The large feathers of the wing are of a very dark green, with a shade of violet. All the rest of the wing feathers are of a changing and very bright green. Some are edged with yellow. Its legs and its feet are brown. These birds have a long neck; but ordinarily they retract it when they rest on trees that lean over rivers, while waiting for their prey, which is frogs, crabs, and other small fish. I do not remember having seen any of them in the winter: Thus I believe that they leave Virginia and Carolina to go further toward the south.

---

# Carolina Ash, with narrower leaves pointed at both ends, and hanging. <note>original Latin: *Fraxinus Carolinenfis, folijs angustioribus utrinque acuminatis, pendulis*</note>

THESE Trees are commonly of a mean Size <note>meaning average or moderate size</note> and Height. The Leaves are pointed at both Ends. The Seeds are winged, and hang in Clusters. They grow in low moist Places.

<column-break/>

THESE trees are ordinarily neither large nor thick. Their leaves are pointed at both ends. Their seeds are winged, and hang in clusters. They grow in low and humid places.

# THE SMALLEST STARRY HERON <note>original Latin: *Ardea Stellaris Minima*; currently identified by ornithologists as the Least Bittern (*Ixobrychus exilis*)</note>

## The small Bittern.

The bill, measured from the angle of the mouth to the tip, was slightly more than six inches long and black, except for a portion of the lower mandible which was yellow. The eyes are yellow. A crest of long green feathers covers the crown of the head. The neck and breast are a dark, muddy red. The back is covered with long, narrow, pale-green feathers. The large primary wing feathers are a very dark green with a hint of purple. All the remaining wing feathers are a shimmering, iridescent green, with some feathers edged in yellow. The legs and feet are brown. They possess a long neck, but typically sit with it pulled in close to the body. They are often found on trees overhanging rivers, where they wait in solitude for their prey, consisting of frogs, crabs, and other small fish.

I do not recall seeing any of them during the winter; therefore, I believe they migrate further south from *Virginia* and *Carolina*.

The primary feathers of the wing are of a very dark green with a shade of violet. *The Wood Pelican.* <note>original Latin: *Pelicanus Sylvaticus*; the term "sylvaticus" refers to the woods or forest. Today, this species is known as the Wood Stork (*Mycteria americana*).</note>

The Wood Pelican.

<image-desc>A hand-colored natural history engraving of a Wood Stork (historically labeled as a Wood Pelican). The bird is depicted in full profile standing on a small patch of green earth. It has white plumage with black flight feathers, long dark gray legs, and a dark, featherless, scaly-textured head and neck. Its bill is long, thick, and slightly curved downward. Superimposed in the background is a large, faint line drawing of the bird's head and neck in profile, oriented horizontally.</image-desc>

<note>In early natural history, this bird was often classified alongside pelicans due to its large size and aquatic habitat. It is actually the only species of stork that breeds in the United States. Its "scaly" head, noted in the illustration, is a patch of rough, featherless skin characteristic of adult storks.</note>

# THE AMERICAN PELICAN.
<note>original Latin: *Pelicanus Americanus*</note>

## *The Wood Pelican.*

<image-desc>Ornamental drop cap 'T' containing a scene with a classical building and landscape.</image-desc>

THIS bird is about the size of a Goose; its beak is nine and a half inches long, curved toward the end, and very thick near the head, measuring six and a half inches in circumference at that point. The front of the head is covered with a dark bluish skin, bare of feathers, while the back of the head and the neck are brown. The wings are large; the entire lower portion of them, from the shoulders to the tips—particularly the primary feathers—appears black from a distance, though they are actually shaded with green. The upper part of the wing is white; the tail is black, very short, and squared at the end; all the rest of the body is white. The legs are black and very long, with webbed feet; however, they are not as fully webbed as those of a duck. Instead, the <term>membranes</term> <note>the webbing between the toes</note> reach only to the first joint of each toe, except for the back toe, which has no membrane and is longer than usual. 

That which proves this bird belongs to the Pelican family is the <term>pouch</term> <note>original: "pouch," referring to the gular sac; in this species, the Wood Stork, it is much less prominent than in true pelicans</note> under its beak, though it is small and holds no more than half a pint. In the latter part of summer, heavy rains usually fall in Carolina, at which time numerous flocks of these birds frequent the open <term>savannas</term> <note>grassy, open wetlands that flood seasonally</note> which are then underwater; they depart before November. They are very good birds for eating, despite the fact that they feed on fish and other aquatic animals. It is a dull-witted bird and lacks fear, making it easy to shoot. They perch in great numbers on tall <term>Cypress</term> <note>the Bald Cypress tree, common in Southern swamps</note> and other trees in an upright posture, resting their heavy beaks on their necks for greater ease. I could not see any difference in the colors of the male and female.

<column-break/>

## American Pelican.
<note>original French: *Pelican Americain*</note>

<image-desc>Ornamental drop cap 'I' decorated with scrolling foliage and floral motifs.</image-desc>

IT is nearly the size of a Goose; its beak is nine and a half inches long; it is curved at its end and very thick near the head, where it measures nine and a half inches <note>Note: The French text says "nine and a half" inches in circumference, while the English says "six and a half."</note> in circumference. The front of the head of this bird is covered in a skin that is entirely bare and of a dark blue; the back of its head and its neck are brown. The wings are large; the entire lower part from the shoulders to the tips, especially the large feathers, appears black from afar, but it has nonetheless a shade of green. The top of the wings is white; the tail is black, very short, and square at the end; all the rest of the body is white. Its legs are black and very long; its feet are furnished with membranes, though not quite as much as those of a duck; the membranes only extend to the first joint of each toe, except for the one at the back, which has no membrane and is longer than usual. 

What shows that this bird is of the Pelican species is the pouch it has under its beak, although it is small and holds no more than half a pint. Toward the end of summer, heavy rains usually fall in Carolina, and it is then that numerous flights of these birds frequent the open meadows, which are then under water; they withdraw before the month of November. They are very good to eat, although they nourish themselves on fish and aquatic animals. It is a stupid bird, which is not easily frightened, and very easy to shoot. They rest in great numbers on the largest Cypress and other trees: they hold themselves quite upright, and to be more at ease, they support their heavy beaks on their necks. I have not been able to perceive any difference between the colors of the male and those of the female.

<summary>This page provides a detailed description of the Wood Pelican (now known as the Wood Stork), including its physical dimensions, its "stupid" or fearless nature, and its seasonal migration to the flooded savannas of Carolina.</summary>

<keywords>Wood Pelican, Wood Stork, Carolina, bird anatomy, seasonal migration, cypress trees, 18th-century natural history</keywords>

# THE WHITE IBIS <note>original Latin: *Numenius Albus*. Though the author calls it a "Curlew," the description and modern classification identify this as the White Ibis (*Eudocimus albus*).</note>

## The White Curlew.
<image-desc>Large decorative drop cap 'T' containing a miniature landscape with a building and trees.</image-desc>
THIS bird is about the size of a domestic pigeon; the bill is six and a half inches long and of a pale red color, with a groove or channel running from the base to the tip. The <term>iris</term> <note>the colored part of the eye</note> of the eyes is gray. The front of the head and the area surrounding the eyes are covered with a pale red skin. Four of the largest wing feathers <note>the primary feathers at the wing tips</note> have dark green ends. All the rest of the bird is white, except for the legs and feet, which are a pale red. The flesh, and particularly the fat, is very yellow, resembling the color of saffron. When the heavy rains fall, which usually happens at the end of summer, these birds arrive in Carolina in great numbers and frequent the low, watery lands.

The male and female are identical in appearance.

<column-break/>

## The White Curlew <note>Translation of the French title: *Corlieu blanc*</note>.
<image-desc>Large decorative drop cap 'I' containing a figure in a garden or field.</image-desc>
IT is approximately the size of a domestic pigeon; its beak is six and a half inches long, of a pale red, and grooved from its base to its tip. The iris of its eyes is gray; the front of its head and the area around its eyes are covered with a light red skin; four of the largest feathers of its wings have dark green tips; all the rest of the bird is white, except for its legs and feet which are a pale red. Its flesh, and especially its fat, is very yellow and the color of saffron. When the heavy rains fall, which usually happens at the end of summer, these birds arrive in large numbers in Carolina and frequent the low and marshy lands. The male and female do not differ in appearance.

->*Small aquatic Arum; or floating Virginian Arisarum with a nearly naked stalk,*<-
->*Described by Mr. Banister <note>John Banister (1650–1692), an English clergyman and naturalist who studied the flora of Virginia</note> in Plukenet’s Mantissa, page 28.*<-
<note>original Latin: *Arum aquaticum minus; S. Arifarum fluitans pene nudo Virginianum, D. Banister Pluk. Mantiss. 28.*</note>

THIS plant grows by the sides of rivers and in watery places; the root is <term>tuberous</term> <note>thick and rounded like a potato</note>, from which spring many broad, oval leaves eight or ten inches wide, on thick, fleshy, round stalks that reach a height of about four feet. From the root also shoot forth many similar stalks that produce blue flowers at the end of every stem; but as I did not have an opportunity to observe them more closely while they were in bloom, I shall only note that the flowers are followed by a bunch of green berries closely joined together in a regular pattern, similar to a <term>pine-apple</term> <note>referring here to the shape of a pine cone</note>. These berries never harden, but drop off when ripe, at which time they have the color, shape, and consistency of capers.

<column-break/>

THIS plant grows on the banks of rivers and in humid places; its root is tuberous, from which come several oval leaves, eight or ten inches wide, attached to stems four feet high that are thick and fleshy. This root also pushes out several other similar stems which produce blue flowers at their ends; but as I have not had the opportunity to observe them more exactly while they were in flower, I will content myself with noting that these flowers are succeeded by green berries, which are very tightly packed and arranged precisely like pine cones. These berries never harden, but they fall when they are ripe; they are then the color, shape, and consistency of capers.

It transitions from large waterfowl to a smaller wading bird and introduces a corresponding aquatic plant from the same wetland environment, maintaining the author’s pattern of pairing birds with the flora they frequent.</meta>

->*The White Ibis*<- <note>original Latin: *Numenius albus*</note>

# THE WHITE IBIS <note>original Latin: *Numenius Albus*. Although the author uses the Latin name for a Curlew, the physical description and modern scientific classification identify this bird as the White Ibis (*Eudocimus albus*).</note>

## The White Curlew.
<note>The decorative drop cap 'T' depicts a miniature landscape, a common artistic flourish in 18th-century natural history books.</note>
THIS bird is about the size of a domestic pigeon; the bill is six and a half inches long and of a pale red color, with a groove or channel running from the base to the tip. The <term>iris</term> <note>the colored part of the eye</note> of the eyes is gray. The front of the head and the area surrounding the eyes are covered with a pale red skin. Four of the largest wing feathers <note>the primary feathers at the wing tips</note> have dark green ends. All the rest of the bird is white <note>The illustration shows brown and grey plumage, which indicates a juvenile bird; adults are pure white with black wing tips.</note>, except for the legs and feet, which are a pale red. The flesh, and particularly the fat, is very yellow, resembling the color of saffron. When the heavy rains fall, which usually happens at the end of summer, these birds arrive in Carolina in great numbers and frequent the low, watery lands.

The male and female are identical in appearance.

<column-break/>

## The White Curlew <note>original French: *Corlieu blanc*</note>.
<note>The decorative drop cap 'I' shows a figure in a garden.</note>
IT is approximately the size of a domestic pigeon; its beak is six and a half inches long, of a pale red, and grooved from its base to its tip. The iris of its eyes is gray; the front of its head and the area around its eyes are covered in a pale red skin...

<summary>This page describes the physical appearance and seasonal migration of the White Ibis to the wetlands of Carolina. It specifically notes the bird's distinctive red beak and skin, as well as the unusual yellow color of its fat.</summary>

--- Page 267 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of North American wildlife, following the White Ibis from page 82. Here, the author describes what he believes to be a distinct species—the Brown Curlew—and pairs it with an aquatic plant found in the same marshy habitat. This reflects the 18th-century practice of documenting ecology by grouping related animals and plants together.</meta>

# THE BROWN IBIS <note>original Latin: *Numenius Fuscus*. Though the author classifies this as a "Curlew," his description and the modern understanding of the White Ibis (*Eudocimus albus*) confirm that this "Brown Curlew" is actually the juvenile form of the White Ibis described on the previous page.</note>

->The Brown Curlew.<-

THIS bird is about the same size as the White Curlew: it has the same sort of bill, with a red color around the <term>basis</term> <note>the base of the beak where it joins the head</note>, and eyes similar to the White Curlew; the rest of the head and neck are a mixture of gray. The upper part of the back, wings, and tail are brown. The lower part of the back and the <term>rump</term> <note>the area above the tail</note> are white, as is the underside of the body. The legs are reddish, like those of the White variety, as are its overall shape and size. This close resemblance made me suspect they differed only by sex, but upon dissecting them, I found <term>testicles</term> <note>male reproductive organs</note> in both kinds. <note>The author correctly observed the anatomy but was unaware that both juvenile (brown) and adult (white) birds have developed organs; they are actually the same species at different ages.</note> The flesh of this bird is dark, lacking that yellow color found in the White Curlew. They both feed and gather in flocks, yet the white ones are twenty times more numerous than the brown kind. In the <term>gizzard</term> <note>a muscular part of the stomach used for grinding food</note> were found crawfish. Both these kinds, accompanied by the Wood Pelicans, arrive annually around the middle of September and frequent the watery <term>savannas</term> <note>in this context, open marshlands or wet prairies</note> in numerous flights, staying for about six weeks before they retire and are seen no more until the same time the following year. In many of the females of the white kind, I found clusters of eggs; from this, I imagine they travel somewhere further south to breed; Carolina at that time of year would likely be too cold for that work of nature, as it is much colder at the same <term>latitude</term> <note>geographic distance from the equator</note> in this part of the world than it is in Europe. Very little or no difference appears in the feathers of the male and the female.

### *Arum with a narrow arrow-shaped leaf, with a very sharp point and lobes.* <note>original Latin: *Arum Sagitariæ folio angusto, acumine & auriculis acutissimis*. This is the Green Arrow Arum (*Peltandra virginica*), also known as Tuckahoe.</note>

THIS plant grows in ditches and shallow water to a height of three or four feet, with many arrow-headed leaves on long, fleshy stalks springing from a <term>tuberous root</term> <note>a thick, underground storage organ like a potato</note>. From this root also grow large round stalks, at the end of each of which hangs a large, roundish green <term>seed vessel</term> <note>a seed pod or capsule</note>, containing many round green berries of various sizes—some the size of musket bullets and others only half as large. This seed vessel (which is about the size of a hen's egg) opens on both sides when mature to reveal the seeds, which are green and soft when ripe. I have seen the Indigenous people boil them with their venison <note>deer meat</note>. They were excessively hot and <term>astringent</term> <note>causing a sharp, shrinking, or puckering sensation</note> in my mouth while green, but when boiled, they lost those qualities and were very pleasant to eat, and, as the people said, wholesome. They ripen in July.

<column-break/>

->The Brown Curlew.<- <note>original French: *Corlieu brun*</note>

It is nearly the same size as the White Curlew; its beak is formed in the same way at its base, and its eyes are like those of the White Curlew. The rest of its head and neck are a mixed gray; the top of its back, its wings, and its tail are brown; the lower back and the <term>rump</term> are white, as is the underside of its body. Its legs are reddish like those of the White Curlew, and of the same shape and size. The great resemblance of these birds made me suspect they differed only in sex, but upon opening them, I found testicles in both species. The flesh of this one is brown and does not have that yellow color found in the White Curlew. They feed together and join in flocks; however, the white ones are twenty times more numerous than the brown. Crawfish were found in their gizzards. These two species, along with the American Pelicans <note>referring to the Wood Stork or Wood Pelican mentioned on previous pages</note>, come every year in large flocks toward the middle of September and frequent the marshy prairies. They stay for about six weeks, then retire; and they are seen no more until the same time the following year. In several of the females of the white species, there were clusters of eggs, from which I imagine they retire a bit further South to lay them; Carolina in this season would apparently be too cold for this operation of nature, for it is much colder there than in any part of Europe located at the same latitude. There is no or very little difference between the feathers of the male and those of the female.

THIS plant grows in ditches and shallow waters to a height of three or four feet, with several leaves ending in arrow points, attached to long and fleshy stems that come from a tuberous root. From this root rise other thick, round stems, each of which carries at its end a large green capsule that contains several green berries, round and of different sizes—some like musket balls and others half as small. This capsule, which is the size of a hen's egg, opens when it is ripe and reveals the seeds, which are green and soft in their maturity. I have seen the Indians boil them with their venison; they seemed excessively hot and astringent when I held them in my mouth while green, but after they had boiled, they were changed and very good. They say that they are very healthy. They ripen in the month of July.

<summary>This page describes the "Brown Curlew" (juvenile White Ibis) and the Green Arrow Arum, noting the bird's migratory patterns in Carolina and the plant's use as a food source by Indigenous populations.</summary>
<keywords>Brown Curlew, White Ibis, Numenius fuscus, bird migration, Green Arrow Arum, Peltandra virginica, Indigenous diet, Carolina colony, natural history, botany</keywords>

# THE SCARLET IBIS <note>original Latin: *Numenius Ruber*. Although the author categorizes this bird as a "Curlew," it is known to modern science as the Scarlet Ibis (*Eudocimus ruber*).</note>

## The Red Curlew

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial 'I' containing a landscape with classical ruins.</image-desc>
[This] is a larger bird than the one previously described, being about the size of a common crow. The bill is shaped like those of other curlews and is a pale red color. On the front of the head and surrounding the eyes, the skin is the same color as the bill and is bare of feathers. The legs are likewise a pale red. Approximately one inch of the wing-tips are black, while all the rest of the bird is red.

These birds frequent the coasts of the *Bahama Islands* and other parts of *America* between the <term>tropics</term> <note>the region of Earth surrounding the Equator, known for its warm climate</note>, and are seldom seen to the north or south of the tropical regions. The <term>hens</term> <note>female birds</note> are a duller, "dirtier" red than the <term>cocks</term> <note>male birds</note>.

<column-break/>

## The Red Curlew <note>original French: *Corlieu Rouge*. This column serves as a French translation of the preceding English text for the original 18th-century audience.</note>

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial 'C' depicting a long-legged bird in a marshy landscape.</image-desc>
This bird is larger than the previous one: it is nearly the size of an ordinary crow. Its beak resembles those of other curlews in shape and is also a pale red. On the front of its head and around its eyes is a skin without feathers of the same color. Its legs are similarly a pale red. Its wings are black for about an inch at their ends; all the rest of the bird is red.

These birds frequent the coasts of the *Bahama* islands and other places in America between the tropics; they are rarely seen north or south of the tropics. The female is of a darker red than the male.

## The Red Curlew

[This] is a larger bird than the one previously described, being about the size of a common crow. The bill is shaped like those of other curlews <note>original: *Numenius*; the author refers to the curved, sickle-shaped bill characteristic of this genus</note> and is a pale red color. ## The Red Curlew <note>original French: *Le Courly Rouge*. In the original 18th-century publication, this column provided a French translation of the English text for European readers.</note>

This bird is larger than the previous one: it is nearly the size of an ordinary crow. Its beak resembles those of other curlews in its shape and is also a pale red. On the front of its head and around its eyes, the skin is devoid of feathers and is of the same color as the beak. Its legs are also a pale red. The tips of the wings are black for about the length of an inch; all the rest of the bird is red.

These birds are found on the coasts of the *Bahama Islands* and in other parts of *America* between the <term>tropics</term>, and one rarely sees them outside of these regions. The females are of a red color that is more "dirty" or dull than that of the males.

# THE OYSTERCATCHER <note>original Latin: *Haematopus*. The name is derived from the Greek words *haima* (blood) and *pous* (foot), referring to the bird's bright, reddish-pink legs.</note>

This illustration depicts the American Oystercatcher, a bird easily recognized by its long, straight, orange-red bill, which it uses with great skill to pry open the shells of mollusks. <meta>This page continues the ornithological and botanical catalog of the Americas, specifically focusing on a coastal shorebird and a Caribbean shrub. It follows the description of the Scarlet Ibis, moving from the marshes to the oyster-rich banks of the Atlantic coast and the Bahama Islands.</meta>

->**THE OYSTERCATCHER** <note>original Latin: *HÆMATOPUS*</note>. Willughby <note>Francis Willughby (1635–1672), an influential English ornithologist and ichthyologist.</note> p. 297. Belon <note>Pierre Belon (1517–1564), a French explorer and naturalist.</note> Book III. p. 203.<-

### The Oyster Catcher

Weighs one pound and two ounces; the Bill long, straight and of a bright red colour, contracted near the basis, and towards the end compress'd: the Irides of their Eyes are yellow, encompassed with a red Circle: the whole Head and Neck black, having a spot of white under the Eyes; all the under part of the Body, dusky white: the larger Quill-feathers are dusky black: the Tail is short, black towards the End, and towards the Rump white: the upper part of the Body and Wings, brown, except a broad white line, which runs along the middle of each Wing; the Legs are long and thick, and of a reddish colour. It has only three fore-toes, wanting the back-toe. Their Feet are remarkably arm'd with a very rough scaly skin. In Rivers and Creeks near the Sea there are great quantities of Oyster-banks, which at low Water are left bare: On these Banks of Oysters do these Birds principally, if not altogether, subsist; Nature having not only formed their Bills suitable to the Work, but armed the Feet and Legs for a defence against the sharp edges of the Oysters. The Hens differ from the Cocks in not having the red circle round their Eyes, and their Bellies are of a more dirty white than in the Cocks: In the Maw of one was found nothing but indigested Oysters.

This Bird seems to be the *Blood-footed Bird* <note>original Latin: *Hæmatopus*</note> of *Belon*, *Willughby* p. 297. notwithstanding there is some small difference in their description. I have seen them on the Sea Coasts both of *Carolina* and the *Bahama* Islands.

<column-break/>

### The Oyster Catcher <note>Translated from the original French: *Le Preneur d'Huitres*</note>

*This bird weighs one pound and two ounces. Its bill is long, straight and of a brilliant red: it is narrowed near its base, and flattened at the tip. The iris of its eyes is yellow, surrounded by a red circle; its entire head and neck are black: it has a white spot under the eyes; the entire underside of its body is a dull white; the large feathers of the wing are a dark black; its tail is short, black at the end and white toward the rump; the upper part of its body and its wings is brown, except for a wide white stripe that crosses each wing through the middle. Its legs are long, thick and of a reddish color. It has only three toes on each foot without a spur behind. Its feet are remarkable for the rough and scaly skin with which they are covered. In the rivers and creeks near the sea there are a great number of banks covered with Oysters, which are dry when the sea is low; it is on these banks that these birds principally find their subsistence, nature having not only given them a bill formed in a manner to accomplish this work, but having also adorned their legs and feet against the sharp edges of the Oyster shells. The females differ from the males in that they do not have the red circle around the eyes, and their belly is of a dirtier white than that of the males: in the crop of one of these birds, only oysters that were not yet digested were found.*

*It seems that this bird is the Blood-footed Bird of Belon, Willughby p. 297. although there is some small difference in the description of one and the other. I have seen them on the coasts of Carolina and the Bahama Islands.*

---

### Bahamian Shrub with oblong succulent leaves, and a roundish fruit containing a single kernel. <note>original Latin: *Frutex Bahamensis foliis oblongis succulentis, fructu subrotundo unicum nucleum continente.*</note>

This grows to the size of a small Tree; the leaves stand by pairs on foot-stalks about an inch long; they are long, thick and succulent: at the ends of the stalks grow in pairs, and sometimes singly, round flat Seed-vessels, about the breadth of a shilling: the fruit is of the substance of a Bean, and, like that, divides in the middle: it is cover'd with a thin membrane of a pale green colour. I had no opportunity of seeing the Blossoms, tho' I was told they were very small and white. The Bark of this Tree is used for tanning of Sole-leather.

<column-break/>

*It grows to the height of a small tree; its leaves are attached two by two by stalks about an inch long; they are long, thick and succulent. At the end of the stems are born in pairs, or alone, capsules which enclose the seeds: they are round, flat, about an inch in diameter: the fruit resembles a bean in its substance, and, like it, divides in two; it is covered with a thin membrane of a pale green. I did not have the convenience of seeing the flowers; however, I was told that they were white and very small. The bark of this tree serves to tan the leather from which shoe soles are made.*

# A river bird related to the Northern Gannet <note>original Latin: *ANSERI BASSANO congener Avis fluviatilis*. The Northern Gannet was historically classified as *Anser bassanus*.</note>.

## The Great Booby <note>Likely the Brown Booby (*Sula leucogaster*).</note>.

IT'S Size is about that of a Goose, the Head and Neck remarkably thick the Bill large, and almost Six Inches in length, a channel or cranny <note>a groove or furrow</note> extends from one End to the other of the Upper Mandible. The Wings extended six Feet, and when clos'd, reach to the End of the Tail. The middle Feather of the Tail was longest, the Rest gradually decreasing in length, the Eyes are large, of a hazel Colour, encompass'd with a Skin bare of Feathers. These Birds were of a dark brown colour, elegantly spotted with white on their Heads, the Spots are thick and small, on the Neck and Breast they are thinner and broader, and on the Back thinnest and broadest. The Wings are likewise spotted, except the large Quill-Feathers and the Tail, which are brown. The Belly of a dusky white. The Feet black, and shaped like those of a Cormorant. That which is most remarkable in these Birds is, that the Upper Mandible, of the Bill, two Inches below the Angle of the Mouth is jointed, by which it can raise it from the lower Mandible two Inches, without opening their Mouths <note>This refers to cranial kinesis, the ability of many birds to move their upper beak independently of the skull.</note>.

This Bird so nearly resembles the Booby (particularly in the singular Structure of the Bill) that I thought the Name of Great Booby agreed best with it. It frequents large Rivers, and plunges into them after Fish, in like Manner as the Booby does at Sea, continuing under Water a considerable Time, and there pursuing the Fish: And as I have several times found them disabled and sometimes dead on the Shore, probably they meet with Sharks and other large voracious Fishes that maim and sometimes devour them. They frequent the Rivers and Sea Coast of Florida. The Colours of the Cock <note>the male bird</note> are brighter, and more beautiful than those of the Hen <note>the female bird</note>.

---

# Whether this be a *Thymelaea* <note>a genus of shrubs in the mezereon family</note> with blunt leaves <note>original Latin: *An Thymelæa foliis obtusis*</note>.

THIS Shrub riseth to the Height of eight or ten Foot, with a small Trunc covered with a whitish Bark, the Leaves are placed alternately on Footstalks, one third of an Inch long, narrow at the Beginning, growing broader and rounding at the Ends, two Inches long, and one over, where broadest of a shining Green, with one single Rib. The Flowers are tubulous <note>tube-shaped</note>, divided at Top into four Sections, they are white, except that within the Cup there is a faint Tincture of red, they grow in Bunches at the Ends of the Branches.

These Shrubs grow in many of the Bahama-Islands, on the Rocky Shores amongst Sedge.

<column-break/>

## The Great Booby <note>original French: *Le Grand Fou*. The term "Fou" (Fool) is the French name for the Booby, referring to the bird's perceived lack of caution around humans.</note>.

THE size is nearly the same as that of a Goose; its head and its neck are of a very remarkable thickness, its beak is large and almost six inches long, the upper mandible has a channel or a groove which extends from one end to the other; its wings have a six-foot span, and when they are folded they reach to the end of its tail; the middle feather of its tail is the longest, the others decreasing as they move away from it; its eyes are large, hazel-colored, surrounded by a skin without feathers. These birds are of a dark brown, pleasantly spotted with white: on the head these spots are small and dense; on the neck and the breast they are wider and further from one another, especially on the back. The wings are also spotted, except for the large feathers and the tail which are brown. The belly is of a dark white; the feet are black, and made like those of a Cormorant. What is most remarkable in these birds is that the upper mandible of their beak, two inches below the angle of the mouth, is articulated in such a way that it can rise two inches above the lower mandible, without the beak being opened further.

This bird is so similar to the Booby, especially in the singular structure of its beak, that I believed the name Great Booby was the one that suited it best. It frequents large rivers, and plunges into them in pursuit of fish in the same way that the Booby does in the sea. It stays a considerable time under water in this pursuit: and as I have several times found them on the shore crippled or dead, they apparently encounter Sharks or other large voracious fish under the water which cripple them, or even devour them. They frequent the rivers and the coasts of Florida. The colors of the male are more beautiful and more brilliant than those of the female.

---

THIS shrub rises to the height of eight or ten feet, with a small trunk covered with a whitish bark: its leaves are placed alternately, attached to stalks three-quarters of an inch long. They are long, narrow at their beginning, widening and rounding at the extremity, two inches long, and one wide at their broadest point, of a brilliant green, with a single rib. Its flowers are tubulous, divided at the top by four sections; they are white, except that inside there is a slight shade of red; they are born in clusters at the ends of the branches.

These shrubs grow in several of the Bahama Islands on shores full of rocks and among the sea grasses.

# Plate 87: The Brown Booby

->A dark bird related to the Gannet.<-
<note>original Latin: "Anseri Bassano affinis fusca avis." Mark Catesby identifies this bird by its similarity to the Northern Gannet, known then as the "Bass Goose" (*Anser bassanus*). Today, this species is classified as the Brown Booby (*Sula leucogaster*).</note>

The illustration depicts the **Brown Booby** in its entirety, standing prominently upon a rocky outcrop. The bird is shown with dark brown, almost black plumage covering its head, back, and wings. This deep color gives way to a lighter, scale-like pattern of feathers across its underbelly. Its most striking features are the bright yellow bill and the matching patch of bare skin surrounding the eye. Its large, webbed feet—essential for a life spent at sea—are clearly visible as it grips the stone.

In the distance, a small sailing ship <note>likely representing the vessels from which these birds were frequently observed by sailors</note> sits on the horizon, emphasizing the bird's pelagic nature. 

To the left of the main figure, Catesby has included a supplementary line drawing of the bird's head. The beak is shown wide open, providing a detailed view of the mandibles and the gape, which allows the bird to swallow large fish caught while diving.

<margin>M <note>The monogram of the artist, Mark Catesby, is visible on the rock base.</note></margin>

<term>fusca avis</term> <note>Latin for "dark bird" or "dusky bird," referring to the bird's primary coloration.</note>

<meta>This page provides the descriptive text for the Brown Booby and the Man of War bird (Frigatebird), complementing the illustration on the previous page (Plate 87). Catesby provides a detailed account of the bird's appearance and its relationship with the predatory Man of War bird.</meta>

# A DARK BIRD RELATED TO THE GANNET
<note>original: "ANSERI BASSANO AFFINIS FUSCÂ AVIS." Catesby is relating the bird to the Northern Gannet, historically called the "Bass Goose" (*Anser bassanus*).</note>
## Sir Hans Sloane's History of Jamaica

### The Booby

Is somewhat less than a Goose; the basis of the Bill is yellow, and bare of feathers; in which the Eyes are placed of a light gray colour; the lower part of the Bill is of a light brown. These Birds vary so, that they are not to be distinguished by their colours only: in one of them the Belly was white, and the Back brown; in another the Breast and Belly was brown; in others all brown; nor could I perceive any outward difference in the Cock and Hen. Their Wings are very long; their Legs and Feet pale yellow, and shap'd like those of Cormorants. They frequent the *Bahama* Islands, where they breed all months in the year. They lay one, two, and sometimes three eggs on the bare rocks. *Dampier* <note>William Dampier (1651–1715), a famous English explorer and naturalist who documented many species in the West Indies.</note> says, they breed on Trees in an Island called *Bonaire* <note>original: "Bon-airy"</note>, in the *West-Indies*, which he observes not to have seen elsewhere. While young, they are covered with a white Down, and remain so till they are almost ready to fly. They subsist on Fish only, which they catch by diving. This and the great Booby are remarkable for having a joint in the upper mandible of the Bill.

It is diverting to see the frequent contests between the Booby and the Man of War Bird <note>Now known as the Frigatebird.</note>, which last lives on rapine and spoil of other Sea Birds, particularly the Booby; which so soon as the Man of War Bird perceives he hath taken a Fish, flies furiously at him, and obliges the Booby for his security to dive under water. The Man of War Bird being incapable of following him, hovers over the place till the Booby rises to breathe, and then attacks him again, and so repeats it at every opportunity, 'till the Booby at length, tired and breathless, is necessitated to resign his fish: yet not being discouraged, industriously goes to fishing again, and suffers repeated losses by fresh assaults from his rapacious Enemy.

Having had no opportunity of seeing the Man of War Bird any otherwise than in the Air, I cannot well describe it, nor say any thing more of it, except what has been related to me, which is this: While they are sitting and hatching their young, their Heads change from a brown to a Scarlet colour, which becomes brown again when they have done breeding. This was affirmed to me by many who have often seen them on their Nests; for at that time they are very tame, and will suffer one to come near to them, tho' at other times very wild. These Birds are numerous on most of the *Bahama* Islands.

<column-break/>

### The Booby <note>original: "Le Fou"</note>

It is a little smaller than a goose; the base of its bill is yellow and without feathers, where the eyes are placed, which are of a light gray; the tip of its bill is light brown. There is so much variety in the colors of these birds that one can hardly characterize them by that alone: in one the belly was white and the back brown; in another the breast and the belly were brown; others were entirely brown. Nor could I perceive any external difference between the male and the female. Their wings are very long, their legs and their feet of a pale yellow, made like those of Cormorants. They frequent the *Bahama Islands*, where they lay eggs in all months of the year. They lay sometimes one, sometimes two or three eggs, which they leave on the bare rocks. *Dampier* says that they lay on trees in an island of the *West Indies*, named *Bonaire* <note>original: "Bon-airy"</note>, which he observes not to have seen elsewhere. While they are young they are covered with a white down, and remain thus until they are almost in a state to fly. They feed only on fish, which they catch by diving. This bird and the Great Booby are remarkable for the joint <term>articulation</term> <note>refers to the kinetic skull of the bird, which allows for greater movement of the upper beak.</note> they have in the upper mandible.

It is a pleasure to see the frequent disputes which arise between this bird and the one that can be called the Pirate <note>original: "le Pirate." This is the French name given here for the Man of War Bird.</note>. This latter lives only on the prey of other sea birds, and especially on that of the Booby. As soon as the Pirate perceives that it has taken a fish, it flies with fury towards it, and obliges the Booby to dive under water for safety; the Pirate being unable to follow it, hovers over the water until the Booby reappears to breathe; then it attacks it again, and always performs the same maneuver, until the Booby, tired and out of breath, is obliged to abandon its fish: however, without being discouraged, it returns to fishing, and always suffers new losses from the assaults of its insatiable enemy.

As I have not had the opportunity to see this Pirate other than in the air, I cannot describe it exactly, nor say anything of it except what has been reported to me, namely that while they hatch their young, their heads, from being brown, become the color of fire <note>original: "couleur de feu." This likely refers to the vibrant red gular pouch of the male Frigatebird, which inflates during the breeding season.</note>, and become brown again when they have ceased hatching. This was certified to me by several people who have seen them on their nests; for then they are very gentle, and allow one to approach them, although at other times they are very wild. These birds are in great number in most of the *Bahama Islands*.

<summary>This text describes the physical characteristics and nesting habits of the Brown Booby in the Bahamas, specifically highlighting its "kleptoparasitic" relationship with the Man of War bird (Frigatebird), which steals its food.</summary>
<keywords>Brown Booby, Man of War Bird, Frigatebird, Bahamas, William Dampier, Bonaire, kleptoparasitism, natural history, ornithology</keywords>

# SMALLER SEA SWALLOW with a White Head.
<note>original Latin: "HIRUNDO MARINA MINOR Capite Albo." The bird described here is the Brown Noddy (*Anous stolidus*).</note>

->Sir Hans Sloane's <term>History of Jamaica</term>, page 31.<-
<note>Sir Hans Sloane (1660–1753) was a physician and naturalist whose collection became the foundation of the British Museum.</note>

->The Noddy<-

->Sea Swallow with a white head.<-
<note>original French: "Hirondelle marine à la tête blanche."</note>

WEIGHS four ounces. The Bill black, long and sharp: the Eyes above and below are edged with white. The Crown of the Head white, which grows gradually dusky towards the back part of the Head. All the rest of these Birds are brown, their Tails and Quill-feathers being darkest. Their Wings and Tails are of an equal length. They lay their Eggs on bare Rocks on many of the Bahama Islands, where they breed in company with Boobies. It is pleasant to see them fishing, accompanied with variety of other Sea-Birds in numerous flights, flying on the surface of the water, and continually dropping to snatch up the little fish drove in <term>sholes</term> <note>Archaic spelling of "shoals," referring to large groups of fish.</note> by larger ones to the surface of the water. This seems to be done with great pleasure and merriment, if we may judge from the various Notes and great noise they make, which is heard some miles off. The sholes of fish they follow, cause a ripling and whiteness in the water, which is a plain direction for the Birds to follow them, and may be seen from the Hills several miles off. Where the ripling appears most, there the Birds swarm thickest. This is done in breeding time, but that being past, these Noddies roam the Ocean over separately, and are seen several hundred leagues from any land, but are seldom met with without the Tropicks. They are stupid Birds, and like the Booby will suffer themselves to be laid hands on and taken from off the <term>yards</term> <note>The horizontal poles on a ship's mast used to support sails.</note> or parts of the Ships on which they alight. The Cocks and Hens differ very little in colour.

<column-break/>

It weighs four ounces. Its bill is sharp; its eyes are bordered with white above and below: the top of its head is white, and this color becomes darker by degrees toward the back of its head; all the rest of the body of these birds is brown; their tails and the large feathers of the wings are the darkest; their tails and the large feathers of the wings are of the same length. They lay their eggs on bare rocks on many of the Bahama Islands, where they raise their young in the company of Boobies. It is very agreeable to see them fishing, accompanied by several species of other sea birds in large flocks, which fly over the surface of the water and continually lower themselves to snatch up the little fish, multitudes of which are driven by the large ones toward the surface of the water. It seems that they perform this fishing with much pleasure and gaiety, if one can judge by the variety of their song and the great noise they make, which can be heard from several miles away. The multitudes of fish they pursue cause an agitation on the surface of the sea, and a whiteness, which serve as a signal for the birds to follow them, and which can be seen from the tops of mountains several miles away. Where this agitation appears most, there the birds are in greatest number. This happens during the time of egg-laying, after which these Sea Swallows roam alone upon the Ocean: they are seen more than a hundred leagues from land, but rarely beyond the Tropics. They are stupid birds, and, like the Booby, they allow themselves to be taken by hand from the yards and other places on the vessel where they rest. The male and the female differ hardly at all in color.

<summary>This page describes the appearance and behavior of the Noddy, a tropical sea bird known for its white-capped head and its tendency to follow shoals of fish in the Bahamas. Catesby notes its "stupid" nature, observing that like the Booby, it can be easily captured by hand when it lands on ships.</summary>

<keywords>Brown Noddy, Bahama Islands, sea birds, maritime ecology, fishing behavior, colonial nesting, ornithology, Hans Sloane</keywords>

# THE GREAT GULL. <note>original Latin: "LARUS MAJOR." While "Larus" remains the genus for many gulls, the Laughing Gull is now scientifically classified as *Leucophaeus atricilla*.</note>

<image-desc>The page features two ornate woodcut decorative initials: a 'T' beginning the English text block, which depicts two figures in a garden or landscape; and a 'C' beginning the French text block, which depicts a bird perched on a branch.</image-desc>

## The Laughing Gull.

This Bird weighs eight ounces. The Bill is red, hooked towards the point; the lower <term>mandible</term> <note>The lower half of a bird's beak.</note> having an angle towards the end: the Head, of a <note>original: "dusky black," meaning a dark, brownish-black color typical of this gull's breeding plumage.</note> dusky black: the Eyes are edged above and below with white: half the <term>Quill-feathers</term> <note>The primary feathers of the wing used for flight.</note> of the wing, towards the ends, are dusky black: all the rest of the Body is white, as is the Tail, the feathers of which are of an equal length, and not so long as the wings by two inches: the Legs are black, as are also the Feet, which are web-bed.

These Birds are numerous in most of the *Bahama* Islands. The noise they make has some resemblance to laughing, from which they seem to take their name. I know not whether the Hen differs from this, which is a <term>Cock</term> <note>A term used by early naturalists to denote a male bird.</note>.

<column-break/>

## The Laughing Gull. <note>original French: "La Moüette rïeufe."</note>

*This bird weighs eight ounces; its beak is red, hooked towards its end; the lower mandible making an angle in this place: its head is of a brownish black; its eyes are bordered with white above & below: half of the large feathers of the wings are towards their ends of a brownish black: all the rest of the body is white, as well as the tail, the feathers of which are of the same length, & shorter by two inches than the wings: its legs are black, as also its feet, which are equipped with membranes <note>That is, webbed feet for swimming.</note>. These birds are in great number in the majority of the* Bahama *Islands. The noise that they make resembles in some way a laugh, & it is from there that they have taken their name apparently. I do not know if the female differs from this one which is a male.*

<summary>This page describes the physical characteristics and behavior of the Laughing Gull, noting its distinctive red bill and the laughing sound that gives the species its name.</summary>

# THE GREAT GULL with an Unequal Beak. <note>original Latin: "LAURUS MAJOR Rostro inaequali." Although the Latin "Laurus" usually refers to a laurel tree, it is used here as a variant or misspelling of "Larus," the genus for gulls. The bird described is the Black Skimmer (*Rynchops niger*).</note>

## The Cut Water. <note>The "Cut Water" is a traditional name for the Black Skimmer, referring to its habit of flying low and "cutting" the water's surface with its lower beak to catch fish.</note>

THE Bill, which is the characteristic note of this Bird, is a wonderful work of Nature. The basis of the upper <term>Mandible</term> <note>The upper or lower part of a bird's beak.</note> is thick and compressed sideways gradually to the end, and terminates in a point, and is three inches long. The under Mandible is more compressed than the upper, and very thin, both edges being as sharp as a knife, and is almost an inch longer than the upper Mandible, which has a narrow Grove or Channel, into which the upper Edge of the lower Mandible shuts. Half the Bill, next the Head, is red, the rest is black; the forepart of the Head, Neck, Breast and Belly white; the hindpart of the Head, Back and Wings black, with a small Mixture of white. The upper Feather of the Tail is black, the rest white. The Legs are short and small, of a red Colour. The Feet are webbed like those of a Gull, with a small back Toe. These Birds frequent near the Sea Coasts of Carolina. They fly close to the Surface of the Water, from which they seem to receive somewhat of Food. They also frequent Oyster-banks, on which, I believe, they feed. The structure of their Bills seem adapted to that purpose. The Cocks and Hens <note>Early naturalists used "Cocks and Hens" to refer to male and female birds of any species.</note> are alike in colour.

<column-break/>

## The Water Cutter. <note>original French: "Le Coupeur d'eau."</note>

*ITS beak, which is the characteristic mark of this bird, is a surprising work of nature. The upper mandible is thick at its base, flattened by the sides gradually until its extremity; it is three inches long, and terminates in a point. The lower mandible is more flattened than the upper, and very thin; its two edges are as sharp as a knife; it is almost an inch longer than the other, which has a very narrow channel, into which the upper edge of the lower mandible enters when closing. Half of the beak towards the head is red, the rest is black. The front of the head, the neck, and the belly are white; the back of the head, the back and the wings are black, with some mixture of white. The top feather of its tail is black, the others are white. Its legs are short, small and red: its feet are furnished with membranes like those of the <term>Sea-mew</term> <note>original French: "Moüette," a general term for gulls.</note>, with a small spur behind. These birds frequent near the coasts of Carolina. They fly very close to the surface of the water, from which it seems they draw some nourishment. They also frequent the banks where there are Oysters, and I believe that they eat them. The shape of their beak appears suited to that. The male and the female have the same colors.*

# THE LARGE GULL with an Unequal Beak. <note>original Latin: "LARUS MAJOR Rostro inaequali." While Catesby's original Latin text sometimes used "Laurus" (Laurel), the intended word is "Larus," the genus for gulls. The bird shown is the Black Skimmer (*Rynchops niger*).</note>

## The Cutwater. <note>The name "Cutwater" is a traditional English name for the Black Skimmer, inspired by its unique method of foraging where it flies low and "cuts" the surface of the water with its lower beak.</note>

THE Bill, which is the defining characteristic of this Bird, is a marvelous work of Nature. The base of the upper <term>Mandible</term> <note>The upper or lower part of a bird's beak.</note> is thick and compressed sideways, narrowing gradually toward the end and finishing in a point; it measures three inches in length. The lower Mandible is even more compressed than the upper and is very thin, with both edges being as sharp as a knife. It is almost an inch longer than the upper Mandible, which has a narrow groove or channel into which the upper edge of the lower Mandible fits. 

Half of the bill, nearest to the head, is red, and the rest is black. The front of the head, neck, breast, and belly are white; the back of the head, back, and wings are black, mixed with a small amount of white. The upper feathers of the tail are black, while the rest are white. The legs are short and small, colored red. The feet are webbed like those of a gull, including a small back toe. 

These birds are frequently found near the sea coasts of Carolina. They fly very close to the surface of the water, from which they seem to obtain some form of food. They also frequent oyster banks, upon which I believe they feed, as the structure of their bills seems specifically adapted for that purpose. ->*The Grebe, etc.*<- <note>original Latin: *Podicipes &c.* The term *Podicipes* (now spelled *Podiceps*) is derived from the Latin *podex* (vent or rear) and *pes* (foot). This refers to the fact that a grebe's legs are attached very far back on its body, an adaptation that makes them powerful swimmers but leaves them unable to walk easily on land.</note>

<vocab>Grebe, grebe, bird illustration, lobed feet</vocab> <note>The <term>lobed feet</term> are the most striking feature shown in this illustration. Unlike ducks, which have fully webbed feet, grebes have individual fleshy lobes on each toe that fold back when the foot moves forward through the water to reduce resistance and open wide to provide a powerful stroke.</note>

->THE CANADA GOOSE<- <note>original Latin: *Anser Canadensis*</note>

## The Canada Goose.

THIS Bird is described by Mr. Willoughby <note>Francis Willughby (1635–1672) was an influential English naturalist whose *Ornithology* (1678) set the standard for bird classification for decades.</note> p. 361. By comparing it with his Description, and finding them agree, I conceive it sufficient to recite his Account of it as follows:

> ,, Its Length, from the point of the Bill to
> ,, the end of the Tail, or of the Feet, is forty
> ,, two inches. The Bill it self from the Angles
> ,, of the Mouth is extended two inches, and
> ,, is black of colour: the Nostrils are large.
> ,, In shape of Body it is like to a tame Goose,
> ,, save that it seems to be a little longer. The
> ,, Rump is black; but the Feathers next above
> ,, the Tail, white: the Back of a dark gray,
> ,, like the common Goose's; the lower part of
> ,, the Neck is white, else the Neck black. It
> ,, hath a kind of white Stay or Muffler <note>A "stay" or "muffler" refers to the distinctive white patch on the cheeks and throat, resembling a piece of fabric wrapped under the chin.</note> un-
> ,, der the Chin, continued on each side below
> ,, the Eyes to the Back of the Head. The
> ,, Belly is white: the Tail black, as are also
> ,, the greater Quill-feathers of the Wings.
> ,, The Eyes are Hazel-colour'd; the Edges
> ,, of the Eyelids white; the Feet black, hav-
> ,, ing the Hind-toe.”

The white Stay or Muffler before mentioned is sufficient to distinguish it from all other of the Goose kind.

In Winter they come from the Northern parts of America to Carolina, &c.

<column-break/>

## The Canada Goose. <note>original French: *Oye de Canada*</note>

*THIS bird is described by Mr. Willoughby on page 361; in comparing it with his description, I found it accurate; thus I believe it will suffice to report it; here it is.*

*Its length, from the tip of its beak to the extremity of its tail or its feet, is forty-two inches. Its beak alone, from the angles of the mouth, is two inches long, and is black. Its nostrils are wide. It is shaped like a domestic Goose, except that it appears a little longer. Its rump is black, but the feathers which are precisely above its tail are white. Its back is of a dark gray like that of ordinary geese; the bottom of its neck is white, all the rest of it is black. It has a kind of white bridle <note>original: *bride*, the French equivalent to the "stay" or "muffler" mentioned in the English column.</note> under the chin, which continues on each side under the eyes to the back of the head. Its belly is white, its tail black, as are also the largest feathers of the wings. Its eyes are hazel-colored; the edges of its eyelids are white; its feet black, with a spur <note>original: *ergot*, referring here to the hind-toe.</note> behind.*

*The white bridle mentioned above is enough to distinguish this bird from all other species of Geese.*

*In winter they come from the North of America to Carolina, etc.*

## The Canada Goose. 
<note>In Catesby's original publication, the second column typically provided a French translation of the English text for a dual-language audience. The content of this column mirrors the description provided on the left.</note>

This bird, which arrives in the southern colonies during the colder months, is easily recognized by its unique white throat patch. While it resembles the domesticated geese of Europe in its general shape, its distinct black neck and specialized plumage mark it as a native of the North American wilderness.

## The Bahama Duck and the Daisy-like Plant

->THE DAISY-FLOWERED PLANT<- <note>original Latin: *Chrysanthemum &c.* In the 18th century, the term "Chrysanthemum" was used as a broad category for many different yellow, daisy-like flowers in the sunflower family (Asteraceae). The "&c." indicates that this was a shortened version of a longer botanical description.</note>

This illustration depicts the **White-cheeked Pintail** (*Anas bahamensis*), which Catesby identified by its regional origin. The bird is characterized by its elegant, slender neck and the striking contrast of its white cheeks against its mottled brown plumage. Of particular note to naturalists of the time was the bright red patch at the base of its dark bill, a detail Catesby has rendered with great care. 

The duck is shown alongside a **Daisy-flowered plant**, a species of *Chrysanthemum*. Catesby frequently paired his zoological subjects with botanical specimens found in the same habitat, creating a primitive "ecological" portrait that showed how these species might coexist in the wild. The plant shown here features thick, fleshy leaves—suggesting a coastal or salt-tolerant environment—and vibrant yellow flowers.

<summary>This page features a hand-colored illustration of the White-cheeked Pintail and a yellow flowering plant, representing the unique biodiversity Catesby documented in the Bahamas.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Catesby’s documentation of Bahamian wildlife, following his descriptions of North American waterfowl. Here he describes the White-cheeked Pintail and a coastal succulent plant, both observed during his stay in the Caribbean.</meta>

# THE BAHAMA DUCK with a leaden beak and an orange-colored spot. <note>original Latin: *ANAS BAHAMENSIS rostro plumbeo, macula Aurantii coloris*. Today this species is known as the White-cheeked Pintail (*Anas bahamensis*).</note>

### The Eleuthera Duck. <note>Catesby writes "Ilathera," an archaic spelling for the island of Eleuthera in the Bahamas.</note>

THIS is somewhat less than the common tame Duck. The Bill is dusky blue, except on each side of the upper Mandible: next the Head is an Orange-colour'd triangular Spot; the Throat and all the fore part of the Neck, to the Eyes, is white: the upper part of the Head is of a mixed gray, inclining to yellow; as is the Back and the Belly: the upper part of the Wing and Quill-feathers are dark brown; in the middle of the Wing is a Row of green Feathers as in the common Teal, bordered towards the Quill-ends with yellow, and their ends black; below which, and next to the Quill-feathers, is a Row of yellow Feathers. The Feet are of a Lead-colour.

These Birds frequent the Bahama Islands, but are not numerous, I never having seen but one, which was a Drake.

<column-break/>

### Bahama Duck. <note>original French: *Canard de Bahama*.</note>

*IT is a little smaller than the ordinary domestic Duck: its beak is a dark blue, except that on each side of the upper mandible, near the head, there is a triangular orange-colored spot: its throat & the whole front of its neck up to its eyes, are white; the top of its head is a mixed gray, which tends toward yellow, as do its back & its belly; the top of the wing & the primary feathers are a dark brown: in the middle of the wing there is a row of green feathers like those of the common Teal; they are bordered with yellow toward the primary feathers, & black at their ends; below & right against the primary feathers is a row of yellow feathers; its feet are the color of lead. These birds frequent the Bahama Islands, but in small numbers, for I have never seen but one; it was a male.*

---

# Bermuda Chrysanthemum with thick, green, Stock-like leaves, Plukenet, Almagestum 102. <note>original Latin: *Chrysanthemum Bermudense Leucoji foliis virentibus crassis*. The reference is to Leonard Plukenet’s *Almagestum Botanicum* (1696), a famous botanical encyclopedia of the time. "Leucoji" refers to the Stock or Wallflower (*Matthiola*).</note>

THIS Plant grows on Rocks on the Sea-Shores of most of the Bahama-Islands. It grows usually to the Height of 4 or 5 Feet, with many pliant green Stems arising from the Root; the Leaves are long, increasing in width gradually to the End; and in Form resembling the Leaves of the Stock Gilly-flower; they are thick, succulent and of a shining Green, standing opposite to one another. The Flowers grow singly at the Ends of the Branches, on Foot-stalks of four inches long.

<column-break/>

*THIS plant grows on rocks on the seaside in most of the Bahama Islands; it usually rises to the height of four or five feet, with several green & flexible stems that come out of the root; its leaves are long, widening always until their extremity, & resemble in their form those of the Stock-Gillyflower; they are thick, succulent & of a brilliant green, attached one across from the other. The flowers come separately at the ends of the branches on pedicels <note>A small stalk bearing an individual flower.</note> four inches long.*

<meta>This page continues Mark Catesby’s study of North American waterfowl. # *CRESTED DUCK.* <note>original Latin: *ANAS CRISTATUS*. This species is the Hooded Merganser (*Lophodytes cucullatus*).</note>

## *The round-crested Duck.*

THIS Bird is somewhat less than a common tame Duck; the Eyes yellow: the Bill is black and narrow; the upper Mandible hooked at the end, and both Mandibles serrated <note>meaning saw-toothed or notched like a saw.</note>. This texture of the Bill shows it to be of the kind of Divers <note>original: *MERGI*, a Latin term for diving birds like mergansers.</note>. *See Willoughby, p. 335. Plate 64.* <note>Refers to Francis Willughby’s *Ornithology* (1676), a foundational text in the study of birds.</note> The Head is crowned with a very large circular Crest, or Tuft of Feathers; the middle of which, on each side, is white, and bordered round with black, which black extends to and covers the Throat and Neck. The Breast and Belly is white; the Quill-feathers of the Wings are brown; just above which are some smaller Feathers, whose exterior Vanes are edged with white, with a little white intermixed in them, as in some of the other Feathers likewise. The Tail is brown, as is also the hindmost part of the Belly near the Vent, and under the Wings; the rest of their Wings and Body is dusky black. The Females are all over of a brown colour, having a smaller Tuft of Feathers of the same colour. They frequent fresh waters, more especially Mill-ponds in *Virginia* and *Carolina*.

<column-break/>

## *Crested Duck.* <note>original French: *Canard huppé*.</note>

THIS bird is a little smaller than a *domestic Duck*; its eyes are yellow, its bill black & narrow; the upper mandible hooked at the end, and both saw-toothed like two saws. The shape of its bill shows that it is of the species of *Divers*, <note>original French: *Plongeons*.</note> (*See Willoughby p. 335. Plate 64.*) Its head is crowned with a very large & round crest of feathers, the middle of which on each side is white, & bordered with black all around; this black extends as far as the neck & the throat, & covers them. Its breast & its belly are white; the large feathers of the wing are brown; immediately above them there are some smaller ones, whose outer barbs are edged with white, with a little white mixed in some other feathers. Its tail is brown just like the underside of the belly near the vent <note>original: *anus*</note> & under the wings; the rest of the wings & of the body is a dull black <note>original: *noir sale*, literally "dirty black."</note>. The females are all brown; they have a smaller crest of the same color. They frequent fresh waters, especially the mill ponds of *Virginia* and *Carolina*.

# *CRESTED DUCK* <note>original Latin: *Anas cristatus*. The bird depicted is the Hooded Merganser (*Lophodytes cucullatus*).</note>

## *The round-crested Duck.*

THIS Bird is somewhat smaller than a common domestic Duck; the Eyes are yellow: the Bill is black and narrow; the upper Mandible <note>the upper part of the beak</note> hooked at the end, and both Mandibles serrated <note>meaning saw-toothed or notched like a saw, used for gripping slippery fish.</note>. This structure of the Bill shows it to belong to the category of Divers <note>original Latin: *MERGI*, a term Catesby uses for diving birds like mergansers.</note>. *See Willoughby, p. 335. Plate 64.* <note>Refers to Francis Willughby’s *Ornithology* (1676), one of the first scientific books on birds.</note> The Head is crowned with a very large circular Crest, or Tuft of Feathers; the middle of which, on each side, is white, and bordered round with black, which black extends to and covers the Throat and Neck. The Breast and Belly are white; the Quill-feathers of the Wings are brown; just above which are some smaller Feathers, whose exterior Vanes <note>the flat part of the feather extending from the shaft</note> are edged with white, with a little white intermixed in them, as in some of the other feathers likewise. The Tail is brown, as is also the hindmost part of the Belly near the Vent, and under the Wings; the rest of their Wings and Body is a dusky black. The Females are entirely of a brown colour, having a smaller Tuft of Feathers of the same colour. They frequent fresh waters, more especially Mill-ponds in *Virginia* and *Carolina*.

## *Crested Duck.* <note>original French: *Le Canard huppé*.</note>

THIS bird is a little smaller than a *domestic Duck*; its eyes are yellow, its bill black and narrow; the upper mandible hooked at the end, and both saw-toothed. This configuration of the bill shows that it is a type of Diver. The head is decorated with a very large round crest or tuft of feathers, the middle of which is white on both sides, bordered all around with black; this black extends to and covers the throat and the neck. The breast and the belly are white; the large wing feathers are brown; just above these are smaller feathers, the exterior edges of which are white, with a little white mixed in, as there is also in some other feathers. The tail is brown, as is the lower part of the belly near the vent and under the wings; the rest of the wings and the body are a dark black. The females are entirely brown, and have a smaller tuft of feathers of the same colour. They frequent fresh waters, and particularly mill-ponds in *Virginia* and *Carolina*.

->THE SMALLER DUCK WITH THE PURPLE HEAD<- <note>original Latin: *ANAS MINOR Purpureo Capite*. This species is known today as the Bufflehead (*Bucephala albeola*).</note>

### The Buffel's Head Duck.

<image-desc>Ornamental drop cap 'A' containing an illustration of a bird perched on a branch.</image-desc>As to the Size of this Bird, it is between the common Duck and Teal. The Bill lead-colour; on each side the Head is a broad space of white; except which, the whole Head is adorned with long loose Feathers elegantly blended with blue, green and purple: the length and looseness of these Feathers make the Head appear bigger than it is, which seems to have given it the Name of Buffel's Head <note>An archaic spelling of "Buffalo Head," referring to the American Bison</note>, that Animal's Head appearing very big by it's being covered with very thick long Hair. The Wings and upper part of the Body have alternate lists <note>stripes or bands</note> of white and black, extending from the Shoulders of the Wings and Back down to the Rump, viz. The Quill-feathers <term>Quill-feathers: the primary feathers of the wing used for flight</term> are black; next to them extends a line of white, next to which is a line of black, then a line of white, and then black, which covers the middle of the Back. The Tail is gray; the Legs are red.

The Female is all over of a brown colour; the Head smooth, and without a Ruff; the Legs and Feet brown. They frequent fresh Waters, and appear in Carolina <note>The British colonies of North and South Carolina</note> only in Winter.

<column-break/>

### The Little Duck with the Purple Head. <note>original French: *Petit Canard a la tête purpurine*</note>

*<image-desc>Ornamental drop cap 'C' containing an illustration of a bird in a naturalistic landscape.</image-desc>This bird is, in its size, between the common Duck and the Teal. Its beak is the color of lead; on each side of the head it has a large white space; besides this, its entire head is adorned with long feathers, separated from one another and pleasantly mixed with blue, green, and purple. The length and the arrangement of these feathers make its head appear larger than it is, which seems to have caused it to be given the name "Buffalo Head" <note>original French: *tête de Buffle*</note>; for the head of that animal appears extraordinarily large, being covered with very long and very thick hair. Its wings and the upper part of its body have alternating white and black stripes, which extend from the shoulder of the wing and the back down to the rump in this manner: the flight feathers <note>original French: *plumes du fouet de l'aile*, literally "whip of the wing"</note> are black; right next to them is a white stripe, then a black one, then a white one, which is followed by a black one that covers the middle of the back. Its tail is gray, its legs red. The female is entirely brown, her head smooth and without a ruff <note>original French: *fraise*, referring to the crest or collar of feathers</note>, and her legs and feet are brown. They frequent fresh water and only appear in Carolina during the winter.*

# AMERICAN DUCK *with a broad beak.* <note>original Latin: *ANAS AMERICANUS lato roftro*</note>

### *The Blue-wing Shoveler.* <note>Now known scientifically as *Spatula clypeata*, the Northern Shoveler is easily recognized by its distinctively large, spoon-shaped bill.</note>

THIS is somewhat less than a common Duck. The Eyes are yellow; the upper part of the Wing is covered with pale blue Feathers, below which is a Row of white Feathers, and below them a Row of green; the rest of the lower part of the Wing is brown; all the other part of the Body is of a mixed brown, not unlike in colour to the common Wild Duck. This Bird does not altogether agree with that described by Mr. *Willoughby <note>Referring to Francis Willughby (1635–1672), an English ornithologist whose "Ornithology" was a foundational text in the field.</note>, p. 370.* But if, as he observes, they change their colours in Winter, it is possible this may be the Bird. However, as their Bills are of the same form, and by which they may be distinguished from all others of the Duck kind, I cannot describe it in better Words than the above excellent Author.
> „ <term>It’s Bill</term> <note>The beak or rostrum</note> is three inches long, coal black
> „ (tho’ this is of a reddish brown, spotted
> „ with black) much broader toward the tip
> „ than at the base, excavated like a Buck-
> „ ler <note>A small, round shield</note>, of a round circumference. At the
> „ end it hath a small crooked Hook or Nail;
> „ each <term>Mandible</term> <note>Either the upper or lower part of the bird's beak</note> is <term>pectinated</term> <note>From the Latin 'pecten' for comb; having narrow projections like the teeth of a comb, used by the duck to filter food from water.</note> or toothed
> „ like a Comb, with Rays or thin Plates
> „ inserted mutually one into another, when
> „ the Bill is shut. The Legs and Feet are
> „ red.” I am not certain whether this was
a Male or Female.

<column-break/>

### American Duck with the large beak. <note>original French: *Canard d’Amerique au grand bec*</note>

IT is a little smaller than the common Duck. Its eyes are yellow; the top of its wings is covered with pale blue feathers; below is a row of white feathers and further down a row of green feathers; the rest of the lower part of the wing is brown; all the rest of its body is of a mixed brown, very much like the ordinary wild Duck. This bird does not entirely resemble the one that Mr. *Willoughby* described on *page 370* but if, as he remarks, it changes color in winter, it may well be that it is the same: in any case, since their <term>beak</term> is of the same shape, and since it can be sufficiently distinguished from all other species of ducks, I cannot describe it in better terms than those used by the excellent author I have cited.

Its beak is three inches long; of a jet black, (though this one is of a reddish brown spotted with black) much wider towards its tip than at its base, hollowed out like a shield; its circumference is round; it has at its end a small hook or a curved nail; each <term>mandible</term> has its edges in the shape of a saw, or furnished with teeth like those of a comb, with rays or small thin blades which enter into one another when the beak is closed. Its legs and its feet are red. I am not sure if this one was male or female.

<summary>This page provides a detailed description of the Blue-wing Shoveler, focusing on its unique plumage and the specialized, comb-like structure of its large beak. # AMERICAN DUCK *with a broad beak.* <note>original Latin: *ANAS AMERICANUS lato roftro*</note>

### *The Blue-wing Shoveler.* <note>Now known scientifically as *Spatula clypeata*, the Northern Shoveler is easily recognized by its distinctively large, spoon-shaped bill.</note>

THIS is somewhat less than a common Duck. The Eyes are yellow; the upper part of the Wing is covered with pale blue Feathers, below which is a Row of white Feathers, and below them a Row of green; the rest of the lower part of the Wing is brown; all the other part of the Body is of a mixed brown, not unlike in colour to the common Wild Duck. This Bird does not altogether agree with that described by Mr. *Willoughby* <note>Referring to Francis Willughby (1635–1672), an English ornithologist whose "Ornithology" was a foundational text in the field.</note>, *p. 370.* But if, as he observes, they change their colours in Winter, it is possible this may be the Bird. However, as their Bills are of the same form, and by which they may be distinguished from all others of the Duck kind, I cannot describe it in better Words than the above excellent Author.
