The Summer Duck . <note>A historical name for the Wood Duck, likely referring to its presence in North America during the breeding season.</note>

->Duck, etc. <note>original Latin: *Anas &c.*</note><-

# AMERICAN DUCK *with a wide beak.* <note>original Latin: *ANAS AMERICANUS lato rostro*</note>

### *The Blue-wing Shoveler.* <note>Now known scientifically as *Spatula clypeata*, the Northern Shoveler is easily recognized by its distinctively large, spoon-shaped bill.</note>

THIS bird is somewhat smaller than a common Duck. The Eyes are yellow; the upper part of the Wing is covered with pale blue Feathers, below which is a Row of white Feathers, and below them a Row of green; the rest of the lower part of the Wing is brown; all the other parts of the Body are of a mixed brown, not unlike in colour to the common Wild Duck. This Bird does not altogether agree with that described by Mr. *Willoughby* <note>Referring to Francis Willughby (1635–1672), an English ornithologist whose "Ornithology" (1676) was a foundational text in the field.</note>, *page 370.* But if, as he observes, they change their colours in Winter, it is possible this may be the Bird. However, as their Bills are of the same form, and by which they may be distinguished from all others of the Duck kind, I cannot describe it in better Words than those of the above excellent Author.

> „ <term>Its Bill</term> <note>The beak or rostrum</note> is three inches long, coal black
> „ (though this one is of a reddish brown, spotted
> „ with black) much broader toward the tip
> „ than at the base, excavated like a Buckler <note>A small, round shield; here referring to the hollowed, spoon-like shape of the bill</note>,
> „ of a round circumference. At the
> „ end it hath a small crooked Hook or Nail;
> „ each <term>Mandible</term> <note>Either the upper or lower part of the bird's beak</note> is <term>pectinated</term> <note>From the Latin *pecten* for "comb"; having fine, tooth-like serrations along the edges used to filter food from water and mud.</note>

--- Page 309 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the description of the Northern Shoveler (found on the previous page) to a detailed account of the Wood Duck (Aix sponsa), referred to here by its historical names, the "Summer Duck" and the "Elegant Crested American Duck."</meta>

# *Elegant Crested AMERICAN DUCK.* <note>original Latin: *ANAS AMERICANUS cristatus elegans*. This species is now known as the Wood Duck (*Aix sponsa*), one of the most colorful North American waterfowl.</note>

## *The Summer Duck.* <note>Historical name for the Wood Duck, likely due to its presence in the southern United States during the summer months when other ducks have migrated north.</note>

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial 'T' depicting a tall tower or structure in a rural landscape.</image-desc>

THIS is of a moderate Size, between the common Wild Duck and Teal. The Bill is red, with a black Spot on the middle of it, and a black Nail <note>A "nail" is the hard, horny tip found on the beaks of all ducks, geese, and swans.</note> or horny substance on the End; the Base of the Bill is edged about with a yellow fleshy protuberance, pointing on each Side towards the Eyes, the Irises <note>original: *Irides*</note> of which are very large and red, encompassed with a red Circle. The Crown of the Head is elegantly covered with a double Plume of long Feathers composed of blue, green and purple Colours, hanging down separately behind its Head, and divided by a narrow white Line, extending from the upper part of the Base of the Bill backward: the lower Plume is likewise bordered with a white Line, beginning at the Eyes and running parallel with the other, dividing the Plume from the under part of the Head, which is purple. The Throat is white, from each Side whereof proceed two white lines, one branching up towards the Crown of the Head, and the other below it, crossing the Neck. The Breast is of a muddy red, sprinkled thick over with white Spots, like Ermine <note>The white winter fur of a stoat, often marked with black spots.</note>. A little above the Shoulder is a broad white line, extended transversely, below which, and joining to it, runs a broad black stripe <note>original: *List*</note>. The Back and upper parts of the Wings are variously and changeably coloured with brown, blue, and purple. The small Feathers near the Vent <note>The anatomical opening for waste; the anus.</note>, are of a reddish purple, from amongst which spring two small yellow Feathers. The Tail is blue and purple. The lower part or edge <note>original: *verge*</note> of the Wings are lapped over and covered by the small downy side Feathers, extending from the Shoulders half way down the Wings, displaying alternately and in a wonderful manner black and white pointed Lines, varying in Appearance according to the Motion of the Bird, and different Position it puts its Feathers into, which adds much to the Beauty of it. The Sides of the Body below the Wings are brown, with transverse waved Lines, as in many of the Duck kind; the Legs and Feet of a reddish brown. They breed in *Virginia* and *Carolina*, and make their Nests in the Holes of tall Trees (made by Woodpeckers) growing in Water, particularly Cypress Trees. While they are young and unable to fly, the Old ones carry them on their Backs from their Nests into the Water; and at the Approach of Danger, they fix with their Bills on the Backs of the Old ones, which fly away with them. The Female is all over brown.

<column-break/>

## *Summer Duck.* <note>original French: *Canard d’Eté*</note>

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial 'I' depicting a landscape with a tree and a small building in the background.</image-desc>

IT is of a medium size between the common wild Duck and the Teal. Its beak is red with a black spot in the middle and a black nail, or a kind of horn at its end; the base of its beak is bordered all around by a fleshy protuberance of yellow color, which ends in a point on each side toward the eyes, of which the Irises are very large and red, surrounded by a red circle. The top of its head is covered with a double and beautiful crest <note>original French: *aigrette*</note> of long blue, green and purple feathers, which hang separately behind its head, and are separated by a narrow white line, which extends from the top of the base of its beak to the back of the head; the lower crest is likewise bordered by a white line which begins at the eyes and continues parallel with the other; it separates the crest from the bottom of the head, which is purple. Its throat is white; from each side there starts a white line which divides into two branches, one of which goes toward the top of the head, and the other below goes to cross the neck. Its breast is of a dull red, spotted very closely with white marks like *Ermine*: a little above the shoulders a wide black stripe extends transversely. Its back and the top of its wings are adorned with various colors, of brown, blue and purple; the small feathers near the anus <note>original French: *l’anus*</note> are of a purple pulling toward red; from between them come out two yellow feathers. Its tail is blue and purple; the edge of its wings is enveloped and covered by the small feathers or the down of the sides from the shoulders to toward the middle of the wings; this down displays stripes ending alternately in black and white in an admirable manner; these stripes seem to vary according to the movement of the bird and the different situations in which it arranges its feathers, which embellishes it greatly. The sides of its body below the wings are brown and waved transversely, as in several species of Ducks. Its feet and its legs are of a reddish brown. They have their young in *Carolina* and *Virginia*; they place their nests in the holes of tall trees (hollowed out by Woodpeckers <note>original French: *Piverts*</note>) which grow in the water, especially Cypresses. As long as they are young and incapable of flying, the old ones carry them on their backs from their nests into the water; and at the approach of some danger they attach themselves with the beak onto the back of the old ones, who flee with them. The female is entirely brown.

<summary>This page provides a detailed physical and behavioral description of the Wood Duck (Aix sponsa), highlighting its vibrant multi-colored crest and its unique habit of nesting in trees and carrying its young to water.</summary>
<keywords>Wood Duck, Summer Duck, Aix sponsa, Virginia, Carolina, Cypress Trees, Woodpeckers, ornithology, bird nesting habits</keywords>

# *THE SMALL DUCK, variegated with white and brown.* <note>original Latin: *ANAS MINOR ex albo & fusco vario*. Based on the description of the white head-patch and the "pied" male, this is the Bufflehead (*Bucephala albeola*).</note>

->*The Little Brown Duck.*<-
<column-break/>
->*The Little Brown Duck.*<- <note>original French: *Petit Canard brun*.</note>
<column-break/>

THIS Duck has a large white Spot on each side the Head, and another on the lower part of the Wing; except which, the Head and all the upper part of the Body and Wings are dark brown; the Breast and Belly light gray, the Bill black, the Irises <note>original: *Irides*</note> of the Eyes of a Hazel-colour. This was a Female. The Male was pied <note>original: *pyed*; meaning patterns of black and white patches</note> black and white; but not being able to procure it, I am necessitated to be thus short in the description. They frequent the lower parts of Rivers in Carolina, where the Water is salt, or brackish.

<column-break/>

THIS Duck has a white spot on each side of the head, and another on the lower part of the wing; otherwise its head and the entire upper part of its body and wings are a dark brown; its breast and belly are a light gray, its beak black, and the irises of its eyes the color of a hazelnut. This one was a female; the male is marked with black and white, like a Magpie, but as I was unable to obtain one, I am obliged not to describe it at greater length. They frequent the lower reaches of the rivers in Carolina where the water is salty or brackish <note>original French: *somache*, an archaic term for *saumâtre*</note>.

---

# *Box-leaved shrub with oblong leaves and pale green berries.* <note>original Latin: *Frutex Buxi foliis oblongis, baccis pallide viridibus apice donatis*. This plant is the Baycedar (*Suriana maritima*).</note>

->Soap-Wood.<-

THIS Shrub or small Tree rises to the Height of about 6 or 8 foot, and usually with one strait Stem covered with a whitish Bark; the Leaves in Size, Shape and Substance resemble those of Box <note>The Boxwood tree (*Buxus*), common in European gardens</note>, and many of them grow concave and curling, with their edges inward. At the Ends of the smaller Twigs grow Bunches of round pale green Berries of the Size of large Peas, set on Foot-stalks <note>The stalk of a leaf, flower, or fruit</note> a quarter of an Inch long with a small indented Capsule. These Berries contain an uncertain number of (four, five, and some six) small brown Seeds covered with a Mucilage <note>A thick, gluey substance produced by plants</note>. The Bark and Leaves of this Tree being beat in a Mortar produces a Lather; and is made Use of to wash Clothes and Linen, to which last it gives a yellowness. The Hunters who frequent the desolate Islands of Bahama, (where this Shrub grows on the Sea-Coast) are frequently necessitated to use this Sort of Soap to wash their Shirts, for want of better.

<column-break/>

THIS small tree rises to a height of about six or eight feet, usually from a single shoot covered in a whitish bark; its leaves, by their size, shape, and substance, resemble those of Boxwood; several grow concave and curled with their edges inward. At the ends of the smallest branches there grow clusters of pale green berries the size of a large pea, attached to stalks a quarter of an inch long, with a small indented capsule. These berries contain an uncertain number—four, five, or six—of small brown seeds covered in a mucilage. The bark and leaves of this tree, when crushed in a mortar, produce a foam that is used to wash clothes <note>original French: *hardes*, meaning old clothes or personal effects</note> and linen; it turns the latter yellow. The hunters who frequent the abandoned islands of the Bahamas, where this shrub grows on the coasts, are often obliged to use this species of soap to whiten their shirts for lack of anything better.

## THE TEAL, etc. <note>original Latin: *Querquedula &c.* The term *Querquedula* was traditionally used by early naturalists to categorize various species of small dabbling ducks, known today as teals.</note>

<meta>This page provides the descriptive text for Plate 99, continuing from the visual description of the Blue-winged Teal on the previous page. # BROWN AMERICAN TEAL <note>original Latin: *Querquidula americana fusca*. The genus *Querquedula* was used by early naturalists like Linnaeus to describe various small ducks.</note>

## The Blue-wing Teal

IS somewhat bigger than the common Teal; the Bill black, the Head, and most part of the Body of a mixt gray, like that of a Wild Duck, the Back being darker than the under part of the Body; the upper part of the Wing is of a bright blue, below which ranges a narrow Row of white Feathers; next to them a Row of green; the rest of the Wing, being the <term>Quill-feathers</term> <note>the primary and secondary feathers of the wing, essential for flight</note>, is dark brown, the Legs and Feet, brown. The Female is all brown, like a common Wild Duck.

In *August* these Birds come in great plenty to *Carolina*, and continue till the middle of *October*, at which time the Rice is gather’d in, on which they feed. In *Virginia*, where no Rice grows, they feed on a kind of *Wild Oate* <note>original: *Wild Oate*. This is almost certainly Wild Rice (*Zizania aquatica*), which was frequently called "wild oats" by early English explorers because of its appearance.</note> growing in the Marshes, and in both places they become extreamly fat.

They are not only by the Natives preferred to all other Water-fowl, but others, who have eat of them, give them the preference to all of the Duck kind for delicacy of taste.

<column-break/>

## Brown Teal of America <note>original French: *Sarcelle brune de l’Amerique*</note>

SHE is a little larger than the common Teal; her beak is black, her head & almost all of her body are of a mixed gray, like that of a wild Duck; the top of her body is darker than the bottom: the top of the wing is a brilliant blue; below is a very narrow row of white feathers, then a row of green; the rest of the wing, that is to say the large feathers, are a dark brown, her legs & her feet brown. The female is all brown, like an ordinary wild Duck.

In the month of *August* these birds come to *Carolina* in great numbers; they remain there until the middle of *October*, which is the time when the Rice they feed on has been harvested. In *Virginia*, where no rice grows, they eat a species of *Wild Oat* <note>original French: *Avoine sauvage*</note> which grows in the marshes. They grow extremely fat in these two places.

Not only the Natives of the country, but also all those who have tasted them, prefer them for flavor to all other species of Ducks.

<summary>This page describes the physical appearance, migration, and diet of the Blue-winged Teal in colonial Carolina and Virginia, noting its preference for rice and wild oats. It emphasizes the bird's high culinary value among both Indigenous peoples and settlers.</summary>

<keywords>Blue-winged Teal, Carolina, Virginia, Wild Rice, migration, colonial ornithology, waterfowl, Spatula discors</keywords>

# VARIEGATED AMERICAN TEAL <note>original Latin: *Querquedula Americana Variegata*. The genus name *Querquedula* was used by early naturalists to categorize various small dabbling ducks, or teals.</note>

## The White-Face Teal <note>This refers to the male Blue-winged Teal (*Spatula discors*), easily identified by the white crescent-shaped patch between the eye and the bill.</note>

<image-desc>An ornamental drop cap 'I' featuring an engraved landscape scene with foliage and small plants.</image-desc>

IN bigness this exceeds a common Teal; the Bill black; the Crown of the Head is black, which extends along the Basis of the Bill to the Throat, between which and the Eyes it is white; all the Rest of the Head is purple mixt with green; the Breast and Belly, in Colour, like that of a common Teal <note>original: *common Teal*. Likely the Eurasian Teal (*Anas crecca*), which served as the standard reference for English naturalists at the time.</note>; the upper Part of the Back, next the Head is brown, curiously waved like the curdling of Water <note>A descriptive term for "vermiculated" patterns—the fine, wavy lines found on the feathers of many waterfowl.</note>; the lower part of the Back is covered with long sharp-pointed Feathers of a light brown Colour. The Wings are colour'd as those of the Blue-wing Teal. The Tail is brown, and somewhat longer than the Wings; the <term>Vent Feathers</term> <note>the feathers surrounding the cloaca, located under the tail</note> under the Tail are black; the Legs and Feet yellow. The Female is all over brown. They frequent Ponds and Fresh-water Rivers in Carolina.

<column-break/>

## American Teal <note>original French: *Sarcelle d'Amerique*</note>

<image-desc>An ornamental drop cap 'E' containing an engraved landscape with a small tower or building on a distant hill.</image-desc>

*IT is larger than the common Teal; its beak is black; the top of its head is black, and this color extends all along the base of its beak to its throat; the space which is between its throat and its eyes is white; all the rest of its head is violet, mixed with green; its breast and its belly are of the same color as those of the common Teal; the top of its back from the head is brown, perfectly well-waved, the bottom of its back is covered with long pointed feathers of a light brown; its wings are of the same color as those of the brown American Teal <note>Referring to the female described on the preceding page.</note>; its tail is brown, and a little longer than its wings; the feathers situated around the anus under the tail are black; its feet are yellow. The female is entirely brown. In Carolina, they frequent ponds and freshwater rivers.*

# VARIEGATED AMERICAN TEAL <note>original Latin: *Querquedula Americana Variegata*. The genus name *Querquedula* was used by early naturalists to categorize various small dabbling ducks, or teals.</note>

## The White-Face Teal <note>This refers to the male Blue-winged Teal (*Spatula discors*), easily identified by the white crescent-shaped patch between the eye and the bill.</note>

Teal, etc. <note>original Latin: *Querquedula &c.*</note>

# VARIEGATED AMERICAN TEAL <note>original Latin: *Querquedula Americana Variegata*. The genus name *Querquedula* was used by early naturalists to categorize various small dabbling ducks, or teals.</note>

## The White-Face Teal <note>This refers to the male Blue-winged Teal (*Spatula discors*), easily identified by the white crescent-shaped patch between the eye and the bill.</note>

In size this exceeds a common Teal; the Bill is black; the Crown of the Head is black, which extends along the base of the Bill to the Throat, between which and the Eyes it is white; all the rest of the Head is purple mixed with green; the Breast and Belly, in colour, are like those of a common Teal <note>original: *common Teal*. Likely the Eurasian Teal (*Anas crecca*), which served as the standard reference for English naturalists at the time.</note>; the upper part of the Back, next to the Head, is brown, curiously waved like the curdling of Water <note>A descriptive 18th-century term for "vermiculated" patterns—the fine, wavy lines found on the feathers of many waterfowl that resemble ripples on a pond.</note>; the lower part of the Back is covered with long sharp-pointed feathers of a light brown colour. The Wings are coloured as those of the Blue-winged Teal. The Tail is brown, and somewhat longer than the Wings; the <term>Vent Feathers</term> <note>the feathers surrounding the cloaca, located under the tail</note> under the Tail are black; the Legs and Feet are yellow. The Female is brown all over. They frequent ponds and fresh-water rivers in Car...

<summary>This page describes the physical characteristics of the male Blue-winged Teal, noting its distinctive white facial markings and iridescent head plumage. It serves as a companion to the description of the female bird on the preceding page of Catesby's foundational work.</summary>
